===Original===
To my sister

**Helen Fauset Lanning**

whose persistent faith has made me

ashamed to falter

===Chinese===
献给我的姐姐

**海伦·福塞特·兰宁（Helen Fauset Lanning）**

她始终不渝的信念

令我羞于踌躇却步

===Original===
> There is confusion worse than death,

 Trouble on trouble; pain on pain﻿—

> — Tennyson

===Chinese===
> 有一种纷乱，甚于死亡，

烦恼叠着烦恼；痛苦连着痛苦——

> ——丁尼生（Tennyson）

## I / 一

===Original===
Joanna’s first consciousness of the close understanding which existed between herself and her father dated back to a time when she was very young. Her mother, her brothers and her sister had gone to church, and Joanna, suffering from some slight childish complaint, had been left home. She had climbed upon her father’s knee demanding a story.

“What sort of story?” Joel Marshall asked, willing and anxious to please her, for she was his favorite child.

“Story ’bout somebody great, Daddy. Great like I’m going to be when I get to be a big girl.”

He stared at her amazed and adoring. She was like a little, living echo out of his own forgotten past. Joel Marshall, born a slave and the son of a slave in Richmond, Virginia, had felt as a little boy that same impulse to greatness.

===Chinese===
乔安娜（Joanna）第一次意识到她和父亲之间那份深切的默契，是在她还很小的时候。那天，母亲、哥哥们和姐姐都上教堂去了，乔安娜因为一点小孩子的小病小痛，被留在了家里。她爬上父亲的膝头，吵着要听故事。

“讲个什么样的故事呢？”乔尔·马歇尔（Joel Marshall）问道，一心想哄她高兴，因为她是他最疼爱的孩子。

“讲个大人物的故事，爸爸。大得就像我长大以后要做的那种大人物。”

他望着她，又是惊叹，又是怜爱。她就像从他自己的已然淡忘的往昔里传出的一缕小小的、活生生的回声。乔尔·马歇尔生为奴隶、又是奴隶之子，出生在弗吉尼亚州的里士满（Richmond, Virginia）；可他自己还是个小男孩的时候，也曾怀着同样一股要成为大人物的冲动。

===Original===
“As a little tyke,” his mother used to tell her friends, “he was always pesterin’ me: ‘Mammy, I’ll be a great man some day, won’t I? Mammy, you’re gonna help me to be great?’

“But that was a long time ago, just a year or so after the war,” said Mammy, rocking complacently in her comfortable chair: “How wuz I to know he’d be a great caterer, feedin’ bank presidents and everything? Once you know they had him fix a banquet fur President Grant. Sent all the way to Richmond fur ’im. That’s howcome he settled yere in New York; yassuh, my son is sure a great man.”

But alas for poor Joel! His idea of greatness and his Mammy’s were totally at variance. The kind of greatness he had envisaged had been that which gets one before the public eye, which makes one a leader of causes, a “man among men.” He loved such phrases! At night the little boy in the tiny half-story room in that tiny house in Virginia picked out the stories of Napoleon, Lincoln and Garrison, all white men, it is true; but Lincoln had been poor and Napoleon unknown and yet they had risen to the highest possible state. At least he could rise to comparative fame. And when he was older and came to know of Frederick Douglass and Toussaint L’Ouverture, he knew if he could but burst his bonds he, too, could write his name in glory.

===Chinese===
“他还是个小不点儿的时候，”他母亲总爱这样告诉朋友们，“成天价缠着我问：‘妈咪，我总有一天会成了大人物，是不是？妈咪，你会帮着我成大器的，对吧？’

“可那都是老早的事喽，打完仗才一年上下，”妈妈说着，心满意足地坐在她那把舒坦的摇椅里摇着，“我哪能想到他真会成了个大宴席承办商，连银行行长们都成了他款待的主顾！你可知道，有一回他们请他去给格兰特总统置办酒席，还专程大老远赶到里士满去接他呢。他就是打那以后才在咱们纽约落的脚；可不是嘛，我儿子实实在在是个大人物。”

可是可怜的乔尔啊！他心中的伟大，和他妈妈眼中的伟大，完全是两码事。他所憧憬的那种伟大，是能把人推到万众眼前、能让人成为一番事业的首领、成为“男人中的男人”的伟大。他顶喜欢这样的字眼！夜里，弗吉尼亚那座小屋里的小小阁楼间中，这个小男孩挑出拿破仑、林肯和加里森（Garrison）的故事来读——不错，全是白人；可是林肯曾经穷苦，拿破仑曾经无名，他们不都照样登上了人所可及的至高之境。那么，他至少也能挣得一份相当的名望。等他年岁渐长，知道了弗雷德里克·道格拉斯（Frederick Douglass）和杜桑·卢维杜尔（Toussaint L’Ouverture），他便懂得：只要能挣断身上的枷锁，他同样能把名字写进荣耀里。

===Original===
This was no selfish wish. If he wanted to be great he also wanted to do honestly and faithfully the things that bring greatness. He was to that end dependable and thorough in all that he did, but even as a boy he used to feel a sick despair﻿—he had so much against him. His color, his poverty, meant nothing to his ardent heart; those were nature’s limitations, placed deliberately about one, he could see dimly, to try one’s strength on. But that he should have a father broken and sickened by slavery who lingered on and on! That after that father’s death the little house should burn down!

He was fifteen when that happened and he and his mother both went to work in the service of Harvey Carter, a wealthy Virginian, whose wife entertained on a large scale. It was here that Joel learned from an expert chef how to cook. His wages were small even for those days, but still he contrived to save, for he had set his heart on attending a theological seminary. Some day he would be a minister, a man with a great name and a healing tongue. These were the dreams he dreamed as he basted Mrs. Carter’s chickens or methodically mixed salad dressing.

His mother knew his ideas and loved them with such a fine, albeit somewhat uncomprehending passion and belief, that in grateful return he made her the one other consideration of his life, weaving unconsciously about himself a web of such loyalty and regard for her that he could not have broken through it if he would. Her very sympathy defeated his purpose. So that when she, too, fell ill on a day with what seemed for years an incurable affection, Joel shut his teeth and put his frustrated plans behind him.

===Chinese===
这不是什么自私的念头。他想成为大人物，也想老老实实、尽心竭力地去做那些能造就大人物的事。为了这个目标，他无论做什么都可靠扎实。可是哪怕还在少年时代，他也常常感到一种令人发闷的绝望——和他作对的东西实在太多了。他的肤色、他的贫穷，在他那颗炽热的心里算不得什么；那不过是造化故意围在人四周的限制，他隐约看得出，是要人拿力气来试炼的。可是，他偏偏要有一个被奴隶制压垮拖病、缠绵床榻却熬着不肯去的父亲！偏偏父亲死后，那座小房子又要烧个精光！

出那事的时候他十五岁。他和母亲双双进了哈维·卡特（Harvey Carter）家帮工。卡特是弗吉尼亚的一位富人，太太常年大宴宾客。乔尔就是在那里跟着一位手艺精湛的厨师学会了烹调。就算照那时的行情，他的工钱也少得可怜，可他还是想方设法攒钱，因为他铁了心要进神学院读书。有朝一日他要当牧师，做一个名头响亮、言语能抚慰人心的人。他一边给卡特太太的鸡浇汁上色，一边一丝不苟地调制着沙拉酱，一边做的便是这样的梦。

他的志向他母亲都知道，并且以一种细腻的、虽说多少有些懵懂的热情和信念珍爱着它们；乔尔感激图报，便把她当作自己一生中除志向之外的唯一牵挂，不知不觉在自己四周织起一张对她忠诚与顾恋的网，织得他自己纵想挣脱也挣脱不开了。偏偏就是她的这份体恤，坏了他的计划。后来她也病倒了，得的病瞧上去好几年都治不好，乔尔咬紧牙关，把受挫的计划撇到了身后。

===Original===
He drew his small savings from the bank and rented a tiny two and a half room shack in the front room of which he opened a restaurant﻿—really a little lunchstand. He was patronized at first only﻿—and that sparingly﻿—by his own people. But gradually the fame of his wonderful sandwiches, his inimitable pastries, his pancakes, brought him first more black customers, then white ones, then outside orders. In five years’ time Joel’s catering became known statewide. He conquered poverty and came to know the meaning of comfort. The Grant incident created a reputation for him in New York and he was shrewd enough to take advantage of it and move there.

Ten years too late old Mrs. Marshall was pronounced cured by the doctors. She never understood what her defection had cost her son. His material success, his position in the church, in the community at large and in the colored business world﻿—all these things meant “power.” To her, her son was already great. Joel did not undertake to explain to her that his lack of education would be a bar forever between him and the kind of greatness for which his heart had yearned.

It was after he moved to New York and after the death of his mother that Joel married. His wife had been a schoolteacher, and her precision of language and exactitude in small matters made Joel think again of the education and subsequent greatness which were to have been his. His wife was kind and sweet, but fundamentally unambitious, and for a time the pleasure of having a home and in contrasting these days of ease with the hardships of youth made Joel somewhat resigned to his fate.

===Chinese===
他从银行里取出那点微薄的积蓄，租下一座两个半房间的小破屋，在前屋开起了餐馆——其实就是个小食摊。起先光顾的只有自己的同胞，而且还寥寥无几。可是渐渐地，他那些美妙的三明治、独一无二的糕点和薄煎饼的名声传了出去，先招来更多黑人顾客，接着是白人，再后来连外面的订单也来了。不出五年，乔尔承办酒席的名声便传遍全州。他战胜了贫穷，也懂得了安逸的滋味。格兰特那件事又在纽约替他挣下了名声，他精明得很，趁着这股势头搬了过去。

迟了整整十年，医生才宣布老马歇尔太太痊愈。她始终不明白，自己这一病误了儿子多少前程。他物质上的成就，他在教会里、在整个社会上、在有色人种工商界里的地位——这一切在她听来都意味着“权势”。在她眼里，儿子早已是个大人物。乔尔也不打算向她解释：没有受过教育，将永远是一道横在他与他心底渴慕的那种伟大之间的栅栏。

乔尔是在迁居纽约、母亲去世之后才成的家。妻子从前做过小学教师，她说话的精确、对小节的一板一眼，都让乔尔重新想起那本该属于他的教育和随之而来的伟大。妻子和蔼可亲，骨子里却没什么抱负。有一阵子，有个家的快活，加上把眼前的安逸同少年时代的艰辛两两对照，倒也叫乔尔对自己的命运认了几分命。

===Original===
“Besides, it’s too late now,” he used to tell himself. “What could I be?” So he contented himself with putting by his money, and attending church, where he was a steward and really the unacknowledged head.

His first child brought back the old keen longing. It was a boy and Joel, bending over the small, warm, brown bundle, felt a gleam of hope. He would name it Joel and would instil, or more likely, stimulate the ambition which he felt must be already in that tiny brain. But his wife wouldn’t hear of the name Joel.

“It’s hard enough for him to be colored,” she said jealously guarding her young, “and to call him a stiff old-fashioned name like that would finish his bad luck. I am going to name him Alexander.”

===Chinese===
“再说，如今也太晚了，”他总这样对自己说，“我还能成个什么呢？”于是他安于攒钱、上教堂。在教堂里他是个执事，其实是位无人明言的主事者。

头一个孩子出世，又勾起了他旧日那股热切的渴望。是个男孩。乔尔俯身望着那小小的、暖乎乎的褐色襁褓，心头闪过一线希望。他要给他取名乔尔，要把志气灌输——多半只需唤醒——到他断定必定已藏在那颗小脑袋里的雄心。可是妻子连“乔尔”这个名字听都不要听。

“他生为有色人种，已经够难的了，”她说着，护崽似的警觉着，“再给他安上那么一个又生硬又老派的名字，就把他的坏运气给钉死了。我要给他取名亚历山大（Alexander）。”

===Original===
Alec, as he was usually called, did not resemble his father in the least. He was the average baby and the average boy, interested in marbles, in playing hookey, in parachutes, but with no determination to be a dark Napoleon or a Frederick Douglass. Two other children, Philip and Sylvia, resembled him, and Joel Marshall, now a man of forty, gave up his old ideas completely and decided to be a good businessman, husband and father; not a bad decision if he had but known it.

Then Joanna came; Joanna with a fluff of thick, black hair, and solemn, earnest eyes and an infinite capacity for spending long moments in thought. “She’s like you, Joel,” Mrs. Marshall said. And because the novelty of choosing names for babies had somewhat worn off, she made no objection to the name Joanna, which Joel hesitatingly proposed for her. “She certainly should have been named for you,” the mother told him a month later; “see how she follows you with her eyes. She’d rather watch you than eat.”

And indeed from the very beginning Joanna showed her preference for her father. The two seemed to have a secret understanding. After the first child, Mrs. Marshall had fretted somewhat over the time and strength expended in caring for the other little Marshalls, but she never had any occasion to worry about Joanna. Joel had his office in his residence, and after Joanna was dressed and fed, all she wanted was to lie in her carriage and later to ride about on the kiddie-car of that day in her father’s office, where she watched him with her solemn eyes.

===Chinese===
亚历克（Alec）——平时大家都这么叫他——一点也不像他父亲。他是个平平常常的婴孩、平平常常的男孩，一心迷着打弹子、逃学、玩小降落伞，可从没有打定主意要当一个黑皮肤的拿破仑或弗雷德里克·道格拉斯。后来的两个孩子，菲利普（Philip）和西尔维娅（Sylvia），都像他。于是乔尔·马歇尔——如今已是四十岁的人了——彻底丢开了旧日的想法，决意做个好商人、好丈夫、好父亲；倘若他当时能明白，这本是个不坏的决定。

后来乔安娜来了；乔安娜顶着一头浓密乌黑的绒毛般的头发，一双严肃而认真的眼睛，还有一副能对着心事呆想上老半天的能耐。“这孩子像你，乔尔。”马歇尔太太说。因为给婴儿起名字的新鲜劲儿多少淡了，乔尔迟疑着提出“乔安娜”这个名字时，她也没有异议。“她实在就该照着你的名字起，”一个月后母亲对他说，“瞧她拿眼睛追着你转的样子。她宁可看你，也不肯好好吃饭。”

而且确实，打从一开始，乔安娜就显出了偏向父亲。父女俩仿佛有一份心照不宣的默契。自打第一个孩子生下，马歇尔太太为照料其余的小马歇尔们所费的光阴和气力，不免叫她烦恼；可对乔安娜，她却从没有操过一回心。乔尔的办事处就设在家里；乔安娜一穿好衣裳、吃饱肚子，就只愿躺在她的婴儿车里，再大一点，就骑着那个年月的儿童脚踏车在父亲办事处里转来转去，用她那双严肃的眼睛瞅着他。

===Original===
Joel never forgot the first time she asked him for a story. He was in the habit of regaling his youngsters with tales of his early life, of himself, of boys who had grown up with him, of ballgames and boyish pranks. The three older children had a fine catholicity of taste. “Tell us a story,” was all they asked, its subject made no difference to them.

But on that certain Sunday before Joanna was five years old she perched herself on her father’s knee and commanded astoundingly:

“Tell me a story, Daddy, ’bout somebody great.”

===Chinese===
她头一回向他讨故事的情形，乔尔一辈子也忘不了。他惯常拿早年生活的故事给孩子们解闷——讲他自己，讲同他一起长大的男孩们，讲打球和男孩子们的淘气把戏。三个大的孩子口味极宽，什么都爱听。“给我们讲个故事吧”——他们要的只有这一句，故事讲什么，他们全不在乎。

可就在乔安娜满五岁之前的某个礼拜天，她爬上父亲的膝头，惊人地命令道：

“给我讲个故事，爸爸，讲个大人物的。”

===Original===
Joel didn’t know what she meant at first, so far removed was he from the thought of his old dream. And yet the question did seem something like an echo, faint but recognizable of a longing that had once loomed large in his life.

“Great,” he repeated. “How do you mean great, Baby? Tall, great big man, like Daddy, hey?” He stood six feet and was broad with it.

Joanna shook a dissenting head. “No, not great that way. I want to hear about a man who did things nobody else could do﻿—maybe he put out a fire,” she ended doubtfully, “but I mean something greater than that.”

Joel had her taught to read after that. She was a little frail for school, and did not start until later than the other children, though she was far the most studious. So she had three or four years of solid reading, and always her choice of subject was of someone who had overcome obstacles and so stood out beyond his fellows.

===Chinese===
乔尔起先没明白她的意思——他离那旧日的梦，早已隔着千山万水了。然而这一问又实在像一声回音，微弱，却认得出来，出自那曾经在他生命里赫然耸立的渴望。

“大人物，”他重复道，“你说的大是什么意思，宝贝？是个子高高的、块头大大的男人，像爸爸这样的，嗯？”他身高六英尺，肩宽体壮。

乔安娜摇头表示不对。“不，不是那样的大。我想听的是一个做成过别人做不成的事的人——兴许他还扑灭过一场大火，”她拿不准地补了一句，“可我的意思是，比那还要大的。”

打那以后，乔尔便请人教她认字读书。她身子单薄，上学比别的孩子晚，却远远比他们用功。于是她扎扎实实读了三四年书，而她挑的题目，从来都是那些闯过了重重阻碍、从而在同伴中出挑的人物。

===Original===
At first she thought nothing of color, and it was not until she had gone to school and learned something of discrimination that she began to ponder.

“Didn’t colored people ever do anything, Daddy?” But Joel was prepared for that. He told her himself of Douglass and Vesey and Turner. There were great women, too, Harriet Tubman, Phillis Wheatley, Sojourner Truth, women who had been slaves, he explained to her, but had won their way to fame and freedom through their own efforts.

Joanna had a fine sense of relativity. Young as she was, she could understand that the bravery and courage exercised by these slave women was a much finer and different thing from that exercised for instance by Florence Nightingale. “They were like Joan of Arc,” she thought to herself, “Joan, wonderful Joan with the name almost like mine.” Only an innate, almost too meticulous sense of honesty had kept her from changing her own name to the shorter form.

===Chinese===
起先她全不把肤色放在心上，直到上了学，多少尝到了些歧视的滋味，她才开始细细思量。

“爸爸，有色人种难道就没做成过什么事吗？”这一问乔尔早有准备。他亲口给她讲道格拉斯，讲维西（Vesey），讲特纳（Turner）。还有了不起的女人呢，他也讲给她听：哈丽特·塔布曼（Harriet Tubman）、菲莉斯·惠特利（Phillis Wheatley）、索杰纳·特鲁斯（Sojourner Truth）——他给她解释说，这些女人都当过奴隶，却凭着自己的努力赢得了名声和自由。

乔安娜有一种很好的衡量轻重的分寸感。她年纪虽小，却能懂得：这些女奴所使出的勇毅，同譬如弗洛伦斯·南丁格尔（Florence Nightingale）所表现的那种勇敢相比，是另一种远为高洁的东西。“她们像圣女贞德（Joan of Arc），”她心里想，“贞德，奇妙的贞德（Joan），名字几乎跟我的名字一样。”若不是她天生一种近乎过分认真的诚实，她早把自己的名字改成那个短一点的了。

===Original===
She used to lie in her bed at night, straight and still with her eyes fixed on the stretch of sky visible even from a house in Fifty-ninth Street and dream dreams. “I’ll be great, too,” she told herself. “I’m not sure how. I can’t be like those wonderful women, Harriet and Sojourner, but at least I won’t be ordinary.”

She spoke to her father like a little piping echo from the past, “Daddy, you’ll help me to be a great woman, somebody you’ll be proud of?”

Her words made him so happy; they renewed his life. She was so completely like himself, and he could help her. “Thank God,” he used to murmur over his books that daily showed an increase in his earnings.

===Chinese===
夜里，她常常直挺挺、静悄悄地躺在床上，眼睛望着天空的一角——哪怕从五十九街的一所屋子里也望得见的一角——做她的梦。“我也要成为了不起的人，”她对自己说，“怎么成为，我说不准。我成不了哈丽特、索杰纳那样奇妙的女人，可我至少不要做个平平常常的人。”

她对父亲说话，像一声从往昔传来的尖细的童音回响：“爸爸，你会帮我成为了不起的女人吧？一个叫你引以为荣的人？”

她的话叫他那样快活，简直等于把他的一生重新过了一遍。她活脱脱就是他自己，而这回他帮得上忙了。“感谢上帝，”他常对着账本喃喃低语——那上面的进项一天天见涨。

===Original===
He took Joanna everywhere with him. One Easter Sunday a great colored singer, a beautiful woman, sang an Easter anthem in his church, lifting up a golden voice among the tall white lilies. Afterwards she went home with Mr. and Mrs. Marshall and stayed to dinner. Joanna never moved her eyes from her during the ride home.

After dinner she stood in front of the singer in the comfortable living-room. “I can sing like you,” she said gravely, “and I can remember the tune of most of that hymn you sang this morning. Listen.”

And with no further introduction she sang most of the anthem. She was only ten then, yet her voice was already free of the shrillness of childhood and beginning to assume that liquid golden quality which so distinguished it later.

===Chinese===
他走到哪儿都带着乔安娜。有一年复活节礼拜日，一位卓越的有色人种歌唱家——一位美丽的妇人——在他的教堂里唱了一首复活节颂歌，金子般的嗓音在高高的白百合花间升起来。唱罢，她随马歇尔先生和马歇尔太太一同回家用饭。乘车回去的路上，乔安娜的眼睛一直没有离开过她。

饭后，她在那间舒适的客厅里，站到歌唱家面前。“我能像您那样唱，”她郑重其事地说，“您今早唱的那首圣歌，曲调我差不多都记得。您听。”

说罢再没有半句铺垫，她把那首颂歌唱了大半。那时她才十岁，嗓音却已经脱净了童年的尖细，开始显出那种后来令她卓然出众的水润金亮的音色。

===Original===
Madame Caldwell gasped. She had won her own laurels through bitter experience in various studios, meeting insult, indifference and unkindness with an unyielding front, which brought her finally consideration, a grudging interest, sometimes a genuine appreciation.

She was well on her way to recognition now. Colored people acclaimed her all over the country and she had some local reputation in her hometown where black and white alike were very proud of her.

“But no daughter of mine,” she used to say bitterly, “if she has the voice of an angel shall go through what I have suffered.”

Yet when she heard Joanna sing that Easter Sunday, she seized Joel Marshall’s arm. “Get her a teacher, Mr. Marshall. She has a voice in ten thousand. Poor child, how you will have to work!”

But Joanna wasn’t listening, her eyes sought her father’s. Both of them knew at once that the road to glory was stretching out before her.

===Chinese===
考德威尔夫人（Madame Caldwell）不由得倒抽了一口气。她自己的桂冠，是在一间间音乐室里含辛茹苦挣来的：侮辱、冷淡、恶言恶语，她一律以不屈的姿态相对，到头来才终于换来了礼遇、几分勉强的关注，有时还有一份真诚的赏识。

如今，她获得广泛承认已经指日可待。全国各地，有色人种无不称颂她；在故乡她也有几分名望，黑人和白人一样都以她为荣。

“可是我生的女儿，”她常愤愤地说，“不管哪一个，就算她生着一副天使般的嗓子，也绝不该去吃我吃过的这些苦。”

然而那个复活节礼拜日，一听乔安娜唱歌，她一把攥住了乔尔·马歇尔的手臂。“快给她请位老师吧，马歇尔先生。她这副嗓子是万里挑一的。可怜的孩子，往后你们可有得辛苦了！”

乔安娜却没有在听。她的眼睛在寻找父亲的眼睛。父女俩顿时都明白了：那条通往荣耀的路，正在她面前铺展开来。

## II / 二

===Original===
Joanna was like her father not only so far as ambition was concerned but also in her willingness to work. She had a fine serious mind, a little slow-moving at first, but working with a splendid precision that helped her through many a hard place. Her quality of being able to stick to a problem until she was satisfied served in the long run as well as her sister Sylvia's greater quickness and versatility. Eventually, too, Joanna's laboriousness and native exactness produced in her the result of an oft-sharpened knife. The method which she applied to one study, she remembered to apply to another, and if this failed then she was able to make combinations.

Usually she had to have things explained to her from the very beginning, either by a teacher or through directions in a book. But to offset this slowness she had a good sense of logic, a strong power of concentration, and a remarkably retentive and visualizing memory.

Sylvia and she, destined to be such perfect friends in their maturity, were not very sympathetic in their childhood. The older girl was thoughtless, quick to jump at conclusions, natively witty and strongly disinclined toward seriousness. "Joanna makes me sick," was her constant cry, "always thinking of her lessons and how important she's going to be when she's grown-up. So tiresome, too, wanting to talk about what she's going to do all the time, with no interest in your affairs."

Which was not quite true, for Joanna was mightily interested in people who had a "purpose" in life. Otherwise not at all. This was where she differed most from her father. With Joel success and distinction had been his dream, his dearest wish. But always he had realized that there were other things which might interfere. With Joanna success and distinction were an obsession. It never occurred to her that life was anything but what a man chose to make it, provided, of course, he did choose to make it something. Her brothers' and Sylvia's haphazard methods were always incomprehensible to her, and this gave her the least touch of the "holier than thou" manner.

===Chinese===
乔安娜（Joanna）像她父亲，不光在抱负上，在肯下苦功这一点上也如出一辙。她有一副上好的、严肃认真的头脑，起初运转得有些迟缓，却带着一种出色的精准，帮她闯过了许多难关。她那种不把问题琢磨到心满意足绝不撒手的劲头，从长远看，并不逊于她姐姐西尔维娅（Sylvia）的敏捷与多才多艺。而且到后来，乔安娜的勤勉与天生的精确，在她身上磨出了越磨越快的刀锋。她把用在一门功课上的方法记得挪用到另一门，倘若这一招失灵，她还能变通组合、另辟蹊径。

通常，凡事都得从头给她讲解，要么靠老师，要么靠书里的说明，她才能弄明白。不过这种迟缓自有补偿：她逻辑感极佳，专注力过人，还有一副记得又牢、又能化作图像的好记性。

西尔维娅和她，虽说成年后注定要成为莫逆之交，童年时却彼此不太投缘。姐姐这姑娘没心没肺，动辄妄下断语，天生机智俏皮，最不爱一本正经。「乔安娜真让我受不了，」她成天这么嚷嚷，「满脑子功课，成天琢磨自己长大以后要多了不起。还烦人的是，一天到晚只想谈她将来要干什么，你的事她半点不关心。」

这话倒也不全对，因为乔安娜对生活中有「志向」的人极感兴趣；除此之外的人，她便毫无兴致。这正是她与父亲最不同之处。对乔尔（Joel）而言，功成名就、出人头地是他的梦想，是他最热切的愿望；可他始终明白，世上还有别的东西会来作梗。而对乔安娜，功成名就简直成了一种执念。她从不曾想过人生还能是别的样子——人生无非是人自己选择造就的东西，当然，前提是他当真选择去造就它。她几个兄弟和西尔维娅那套漫无章法的路数，她永远无法理解，这也让她沾上了那么一丝「自命高人一等」的气。

===Original===
Her mother insisted on each child's learning to do housework. Even the boys were not exempt from this, indeed they rather liked it. Sylvia made no complaint though she occasionally bribed Alec or Philip to do her stint for her. Joanna never complained, either, yet she made up her mind early that as a woman she would never do this kind of work. Not that she despised it, she simply considered it labor lost for a person who like herself might be spending her time in more beautiful and more graceful activities. Yet in spite of her dislike, she always lingered longest over her work, and the room or the silver which she had cleaned always looked the best. It is true she never learned to iron especially well, but this was about the only thing in which she yielded place to Sylvia.

Sylvia was like a firefly in comparison with Joanna's steady beaconlike flood of light. Sylvia dashed about, worked as quickly as she thought and produced immediate and usually rather striking results. Sylvia with a ribbon, or a piece of lace and a ready needle and thread could give the effect of possessing two dresses, whereas she had only the one. Sylvia dressed the dolls, hiring Joanna's remarkable and usually disregarded assembly of these so that she might make them new clothes. She drove an honest bargain. If Joanna would let her play store with her dolls for a week, one of them could keep the new dress which Sylvia would have made for her; Joanna's dolls were usually in Sylvia's care.

Yet when Joanna did sew or knit, her stitches and pieces bore inspection much better than Sylvia's. By the same token, however, they missed Sylvia's dash.

In one thing only did Joanna show real abandon, that was in dancing. Sylvia was as light as thistledown on her feet, but Joanna was like the spirit of dancing. She had grace, the very poetry of motion, and she could dance any step however intricate if she saw it once.

===Chinese===
母亲坚持每个孩子都得学会做家务。连男孩子们也不能免，说实话他们还挺乐意。西尔维娅毫无怨言，只是偶尔塞点好处，让亚历克（Alec）或菲利普（Philip）替她干完她那份活。乔安娜也从不抱怨，却早早打定主意：将来做了女人，决不干这类活计。倒不是她瞧不起这些，她只是觉得，像她这样的人，本可以把时间花在更美好、更雅致的事情上，干这些纯属白费力气。然而尽管不喜欢，她干起活来总是耗得最久，经她收拾的房间、擦拭的银器，看上去也总是最见光彩。的确，她始终没把熨衣学得多么出色，不过这也差不多是她唯一甘拜西尔维娅下风的事了。

与乔安娜灯塔般沉稳倾泻的一片光华相比，西尔维娅就像一只飞来闪去的萤火虫。西尔维娅风风火火，手上的活跟脑子里的念头一样快，立竿见影，而且成效往往相当惹眼。西尔维娅凭一根缎带、一方花边，再备好针线，就能明明只有一条裙子、却穿出两条的效果。西尔维娅给娃娃做衣裳，把乔安娜那一大堆数目可观却常遭冷落的娃娃「雇」了下来，好给它们添置新装。她这买卖做得童叟无欺：只要乔安娜肯把娃娃交给她玩一个星期的「开铺子」，其中就有一个娃娃可以留下西尔维娅给它做的新衣；乔安娜的娃娃照例都归西尔维娅照管。

然而乔安娜当真缝纫或编织起来，她的针脚和活计可比西尔维娅的经得起细看得多了。不过话又说回来，她的活计也少了西尔维娅那股潇洒利落的劲儿。

唯有一件事能让乔安娜真正忘我，那就是跳舞。西尔维娅跳起来脚步轻似蓟花绒絮，而乔安娜简直是舞蹈的精灵。她风姿优雅，举手投足自成诗篇，再繁复的舞步，只要看过一遍，她便能跳。

===Original===
"If you want to get Joanna to play," Maggie Ellersley, Sylvia's chum and schoolmate would say impatiently, "you must start some singing or dancing game. She wouldn't play 'I Spy' or 'Pussy wants a corner' with you for worlds."

Any sort of folksong or dance, though she did not know them by that name, delighted the child. Usually she held herself aloof, but in summer down on Fifty-ninth Street Joanna was one with the children in the street, singing, dancing, jumping rope in unexpected and fancy ways.

Sylvia's and Maggie's and even her brothers' rougher scoffing affected her not at all, not only because she had the calm self-assurance which is the first step toward success, but also because of old Joel's strong belief in her.

Joel believed that all things were possible. "Nothing in reason," he used to tell Joanna, "is impossible. Forty years ago I was almost a pauper in Richmond. Look at me today. I spend more on you in a month, Joanna, than my mother and I ever saw in a five-year stretch. One hundred years ago and nearly all of us were slaves. See what we are now. Ten years ago people would have laughed at the thought of colored people on the stage. Look at the billboards on Broadway."

===Chinese===
「你要是想让乔安娜跟你玩，」西尔维娅的密友兼同窗玛吉·埃勒斯利（Maggie Ellersley）总不耐烦地说，「就得起个唱唱跳跳的游戏。你就是拿全世界跟她换，她也不肯陪你玩『捉间谍』（I Spy）或『猫咪要角落』（Pussy wants a corner）。」

任何一种民歌或民间舞——虽然她当时还叫不上这些名目——都能让这孩子满心欢喜。她平日总是自持疏离，可一到夏天，在五十九街那头，乔安娜便和街上的孩子们打成一片，唱歌、跳舞，还会出其不意地跳出种种花样的绳来。

西尔维娅的讥诮、玛吉的取笑，甚至连几个兄弟更粗鲁的嘲弄，都丝毫奈何她不得——这不仅因为她怀有一种沉静的自信（那正是迈向成功的第一步），更因为老乔尔对她坚信不疑。

乔尔相信一切皆有可能。「只要合乎情理，」他常对乔安娜说，「就没有办不成的。四十年前，我在里士满（Richmond）差不多就是个穷光蛋。瞧瞧我今天。乔安娜，我如今一个月花在你身上的钱，比我母亲和我当年五年里见过的加起来还多。一百年前，我们几乎人人都是奴隶。看看我们现在成了什么样子。十年前，谁要是想到有色人种能登台演戏，人们准会笑掉大牙。去瞧瞧百老汇街上的海报吧。」

===Original===
It was in the first part of the century when Williams and Walker, Cole and Johnson, Ada Overton and others were at their zenith. Old Joel believed them the precursors of greater things. Since Joanna's gifts were those of singing and dancing, he hoped to make her famous the country over. Of course he would have preferred a more serious form of endowment. But such as it was, it was Joanna's, and must be developed. Joel Marshall believed in using the gifts nearest at hand.

"And don't think anything about being colored," he used to say.

"It might be different if you lived in some other part of the country, but here in this section it may not interfere much more than being poor, or having some slight deformity. I have often noticed," said Joel, who had used his powers of observation to no small advantage, "that having some natural drawback often pushes you forward, that is if you've got anything in you to start with. It might even happen," he added, launched now on his favorite theme, "that your color would add to your success. Depend on it if you've got something which these white folks haven't got, or can do something better than they can, they'll call on you fast enough and your color will only make you more noticeable."

Joanna used to listen interestedly. Not that in those early years she always understood fully everything her father said, but his talk created for her a kind of atmosphere which created in turn a feeling of assurance and self-confidence which was really superb.

===Chinese===
那正是本世纪初叶，威廉斯与沃克（Williams and Walker）、科尔与约翰逊（Cole and Johnson）、艾达·奥弗顿（Ada Overton）等人声名如日中天的年代。老乔尔相信，他们是更辉煌事业的开路先锋。既然乔安娜的天赋在歌舞，他便指望让她名扬全国。当然，他原本更希望她有一份更庄重些的天资。可天赋如此，它就是乔安娜的，就得加以施展。乔尔·马歇尔（Joel Marshall）信奉的是：手边现成的天赋就要用足。

「还有，别把你是有色人种这回事放在心上。」他总这么说。

「你要是住在国里别的地界，情形也许不同；可在这一带，它未必比贫穷或一点小小的残疾更碍事。我常常发现，」乔尔说——他那双善于观察的眼睛可没少给他出力——「天生的短处往往反倒推着你往前赶，当然，前提是你自己肚里得有点东西。甚至没准儿，」他接着说，这时已扯到他最得意的题目上了，「你的肤色反倒会给你添彩。记住我的话：只要你手里有这些白人没有的东西，或者什么事做得比他们好，他们抢着来找你都来不及，你的肤色只会让你更加显眼。」

乔安娜总是听得津津有味。倒不是小小年纪的她总能把父亲的话句句听透，而是他的言谈为她营造出一种氛围，这氛围又在她心里生出一种笃定与自信——那实在是一种极其恢宏的自信。

===Original===
Another theory of Joel's which he had worked out for himself, and which in no small degree contributed to Joanna's education was his early understanding of the natural rights of men inherent in the mere fact of living. He told Joanna that no class of men remained static throughout the ages—he had not used these words, it is true, but he had come pretty near it. Somewhere in those early days of his in odd scraps of reading he had learned that Greece had once been enslaved; that Russia had but recently freed her serfs; that England possessed a submerged class.

"All people, all countries, have their ups and downs, Joanna," he would tell her gravely, "and just now it's our turn to be down, but it will soon roll round for our time to be up, or rather we must see to it that we do get up. So everyone of us has something to do for the race. Never forget that, little girl."

Joanna was a memorable type in these days. A grave child, brown without that peculiar luminosity of appearance which she was to have later on, and which Sylvia already possessed. She had a mop of thick black hair which was actually heavy, so much so that the back of her head bulged. Joanna knew next to nothing at this time of those first aids to colored people in this country in the matter of conforming to average appearance. If she had known them, it is doubtful if she would have used them, for she had the variety of honesty which made her hesitate and even dislike to do or adopt anything artificial, no matter how much it might improve her general appearance. No hair straighteners, nor even curling kids for her.

"Joanna's ways are so straight, they almost sway back," Sylvia used to say aptly. And indeed Joanna wanted one to see her at her very worst. She did not like to take people by surprise. But as her worst included a pair of very nice brown eyes, with thick, if somewhat short, and curling lashes, an unobtrusive nose, small square hands and exquisite feet, it was not hard to look at. She was always intensely susceptible to beautiful people and to beautiful things. It was the beauty inherent in Joel's ideals, and in all ideals which really underlie success, that most attracted her. And this passion for beauty while informing and indeed molding her character, yet by a strange twist influenced adversely and warped her sympathies.

===Chinese===
乔尔还有一套自己琢磨出来的道理，对乔安娜的成长教育影响不小：他很早就领悟到，人的种种天然权利，就蕴含在「活着」这一单纯的事实之中。他对乔安娜说，古往今来，没有任何一个阶层会原地不动地停在老地方——话虽不是这么说的，意思却也相去不远。早年间，在东零西碎的断章阅读里，他得知希腊曾沦为奴役之邦，俄国前不久才解放了农奴，英国也蓄着一个沉在社会底层的阶级。

「凡是人，凡是国，都有起有落，乔安娜，」他会郑重地告诉她，「眼下轮到我们跌在低处，可风水轮流转，早晚会轮到我们起来——不，更该说，我们必须自己设法站起来。所以我们每个人都要为这个种族做点什么。永远别忘了，小姑娘。」

那些日子里，乔安娜是个令人过目难忘的孩子。一个神情肃穆的小姑娘，一身棕色皮肤，还没有她日后将有的那种独特的光彩——那种光彩西尔维娅已然具备。她一头浓密的黑色乱发，沉甸甸的，压得后脑勺都凸了出来。这时候的乔安娜，对本国有色人种为使容貌趋近寻常模样而用的那些最初的小法门，几乎一无所知。就算知道，她多半也不会用，因为她天生有一种实诚，凡属弄虚作假之举，无论能把她的仪容改观多少，她都迟疑不肯，甚至心生厌恶。直发药剂她不碰，连卷发用的假发绺也不要。

「乔安娜这人性子直得太厉害，简直直得都往后仰了。」西尔维娅从前常这么说，倒也说得贴切。的确，乔安娜巴不得人家看到的就是她最不济的样子。她不喜欢让人猝不及防。不过她那「最不济」里，也有一双很好看的棕色眼睛，睫毛虽略短，却浓密卷曲，鼻子生得不抢眼，小手方正，双足精巧，实在并不难看。她对美丽的人、美丽的事物，向来最易动心。而最吸引她的，正是蕴藏在乔尔的理想之中、蕴藏在一切真正托举着成功的理想之中的那种美。这份对美的热望，在滋养乃至塑造她品格的同时，却因一种奇特的扭曲，反而妨害了她的同情之心，使它变了形。

## III / 三

===Original===
It was Joanna’s love for beauty that made her consciously see Peter Bye. It is true that almost as soon as she saw him she lost sight of him again, for the boy did not come up to her requirements which, even at the early age at which these two met, were quite crystallized. Joanna liked first of all fixity of purpose. The phrase “When I grow up, I’m going to be” was constantly on her lips. She got into the habit of measuring people, “sizing them up” Joel would have said, in accordance with the amount of steadfastness, perseverance and ambition which they displayed. She had little time for shiftless or “do-less” persons. Sylvia used to say, half angrily, “Joanna, when the bad man gets you, he isn’t going to torture you. He’s just going to shut you up with lazy, good-for-nothing folks. That will be torture enough for you.”

Peter Bye, in spite of the dark arresting beauty which first drew Joanna’s glance to him across the other white and pink faces in the crowded schoolroom, was undoubtedly shiftless. “Not lazy,” Joanna said to herself, looking at him from under level brows before she dismissed him forever from her busy mind. “It’s just that he doesn’t care; he just doesn’t want to be anybody.”

She was too young to understand the power of that great force, heredity. She had no notion of the part which it played in her own life. Peter was the legitimate result of a heredity that had become a tradition, of a tradition that had become warped, that had gone astray and had carried Peter and Peter Bye’s father along in its general wreckage.

It is impossible to understand the boy’s character without some knowledge of the lives of those who had gone before him.

===Chinese===
正是对美的热爱，让乔安娜（Joanna）真正注意到了彼得·拜（Peter Bye）。不错，她几乎刚一看见他，便又把他丢在了脑后，因为这孩子够不上她的标准——虽说两人相遇时年纪都还小，她的标准却早已定型。乔安娜头一条看重的，便是目标专一。「等我长大了，我要当……」这句话成天挂在她嘴边。她养成了衡量人的习惯——照乔尔（Joel）的说法，是给人「掂掂分量」——掂量的，是对方身上表现出多少坚定、坚韧和进取心。她对懒散懈怠、不思进取的人向来没有耐性。西尔维娅（Sylvia）常半带恼意地说：「乔安娜，等坏人把你抓了去，他不会折磨你的。他只消把你和一群好吃懒做的窝囊废关在一起，对你来说就是够受的酷刑了。」

彼得·拜无疑是懒散没出息的，尽管正是那股黝黑逼人的俊美，最初把乔安娜的目光从挤挤挨挨的教室里那些白里透粉的脸蛋中间引到了他身上。「不是懒，」乔安娜从平直的眉线下打量着他，在把他从自己那颗忙碌的脑袋里永远打发掉之前这样想道，「他只是满不在乎；他就是压根不想成个人物。」

她年纪太小，还不懂得遗传这股伟大力量的威力，也全然不知它在自己的生命里扮演着怎样的角色。彼得正是一种遗传的顺理成章的产物——那遗传已化作一种家传，那家传又走了样、入了歧途，在总体的倾覆之中，把彼得和彼得·拜的父亲一并裹挟而去。

若不知道些先行者的生平，便无从理解这孩子的品性。

===Original===
As far back as the last decades of the eighteenth century there had been white Byes and black Byes in Philadelphia. The black Byes were known to be the chattels of Aaron and Dinah Bye, Quakers, who without reluctance had set free their slaves, among them black Joshua Bye, the great-grandfather of Peter. This was done in 1780 according to the laws of Pennsylvania, which thus allowed the Quakers to salve their consciences without offending their thrifty instincts.

Aaron Bye, most people said, was unusually good to his slaves. He had something of the patriarchal instinct and liked to think of himself as ruler over the destiny of many people, his wife’s, his children’s and more completely that of his slaves. Certainly he was very kind to Joshua’s mother, Judy. She was a tall, straight, steely, black woman with fine inscrutable eyes, a thin-lipped mouth and a large but shapely nose. She bore about her a quality of brooding, of mystery, embodying the attraction which she exercised for many men, white and black. But apparently she knew little of this. Her only weakness, if such it might be called, was an inexplicable attachment to the white Bye family. She married, a few years before receiving her freedom, a man named Ceazer, a proud, surly, handsome individual, who refused to adopt the surname of his master; he had belonged to white people named Morton. Since even after freedom Judy would not hear to leaving the Bye family, Aaron Bye greatly pleased by this loyalty offered the position of coachman to Ceazer, which the latter, with his customary surliness, accepted. Later he not only threw up his job, but ran away, vanishing finally into legend.

His was a strange truculent character; he hated slavery, hated all white people, hated particularly the Mortons, hated ineffably Aaron Bye. He wanted nothing at his hands. Once he knocked down another Negro who referred to him as “Mist’ Bye’s man.” He was no man’s man, he assured the stricken narrator, least of all the man of that damn Quaker. His enmity went to ridiculous lengths. Aaron Bye taught Joshua how to write and gave him a little black testament for a prize. In it he wrote “The gift of Aaron Bye.” Joshua, delighted, wrote his own name under the inscription and ran and showed it to his mother. She, it turned out, had not been watching his making of pothooks without purpose. Underneath her boy’s name she fashioned in halting crazy characters her single attempt at writing, her own name, Judy Bye. Nothing would serve Joshua then but that he must have Ceazer’s name in the book, too. Remembering that his father could not write, Joshua wrote out himself with a fine flourish “Ceazer Bye” and showed the name to its owner, entreating him to make his mark beside it. Ceazer took up the pen in his strong, wiry fingers.

===Chinese===
早在十八世纪最后几十年，费城（Philadelphia）便有白人拜家与黑人拜家之分。黑人拜家原是亚伦·拜（Aaron Bye）与黛娜·拜（Dinah Bye）夫妇名下的动产；这对贵格会教徒毫不勉强地释放了自己的奴隶，其中便有黑人约书亚·拜（Joshua Bye），彼得的曾祖父。这件事依据宾夕法尼亚的法律办在 1780 年，如此一来，贵格会教徒既宽慰了良心，又不曾委屈他们那副节俭的天性。

大家都说，亚伦·拜待自己的奴隶好得出奇。他身上颇有几分族长式的天性，喜欢把自己看作主宰众人命运的人——妻子的、儿女的，而最完全的，是奴隶们的。他对约书亚的母亲朱迪（Judy）确实极为和善。她是个高挑、挺直、钢筋铁骨般的黑人女子，有一双细巧而深不可测的眼睛，两片薄唇，一只大而端正的鼻子。她周身透着一股沉沉的郁结之气、一股神秘，这正是她令许多男人——无论白的黑的——倾倒的魅力所在。可她自己对此似乎浑然不觉。她唯一的弱点——倘若这也能叫弱点——是对白人拜家一种说不清道不明的依恋。获释前几年，她嫁了一个名叫西泽（Ceazer）的男人；此人生得俊美，却高傲乖戾，拒不改随主人的姓——他原属于姓莫顿（Morton）的白人人家。朱迪即便得了自由，也死活不肯离开拜家；亚伦·拜对这份忠心大为受用，便提出让西泽来当车夫，后者带着他惯常的乖戾应下了。再后来，他非但撂了差事，还索性逃走，终于遁入传说之中。

他的性子古怪而凶悍：他恨奴隶制，恨一切白人，尤其恨莫顿家，对亚伦·拜更恨得无以言表。从亚伦手里，他什么都不想要。有一回，另一个黑人唤他「拜老爷的人」，被他一拳撂倒。他向那个挨了打、吓懵了的发话人郑重声明：他不属于任何人的手下，尤其不属于那个该死的贵格会教徒。他的仇视达到了荒唐的地步。亚伦·拜教约书亚写字，还奖了他一本小小的黑色《新约》，并在里面题了「亚伦·拜敬赠」。约书亚喜出望外，在题词底下写上自己的名字，跑去给母亲看。原来，她平日里看儿子描那些弯钩笔画，并非全无用心。她在儿子的名字底下，用踉跄歪斜的字迹，完成了此生唯一一次书写——她自己的名字：朱迪·拜。这下子约书亚说什么也非要让西泽的名字也进这本书。想到父亲不会写字，约书亚便自己大笔一挥，写下「西泽·拜」几个字，拿给这名字的主人看，求他在旁边画押。西泽用那双筋结有力的手指拈起了笔。

===Original===
“Which one ob dese did you say were mine?”

Joshua pointed it out, waiting for the cross. Ceazer made a mark, it was true, but it was a thick broad line drawn through his name with a fury which almost tore the thin page. *He* was no Bye!

It was not long after this that he disappeared, a strange, brooding, intractable figure.

===Chinese===
「你说这堆里头，哪一样是我的来着？」

约书亚指给他看，等着他画那个十字。西泽倒是画了一道——却是怒气冲冲横贯自己名字的一笔，又粗又宽，险些把薄薄的书页划破。*他*可不姓拜！

这事之后不久，他便消失了，一个古怪、阴沉、不服管束的身影。

===Original===
Joshua, although born in slavery, had never known the institution in its more hideous aspects. He had been a very little boy when his freedom came to him. And Ceazer, old Judy told him, had fought in the Revolution! So that Joshua knew more of warfare to set people free than of slavery for which war was later to be waged. From him his son Isaiah heard almost nothing of the old regime, though there were many vestiges of it on all sides. All he knew was that Joshua had kept on working for Dinah and Aaron Bye after his emancipation, and that they had given him on the occasion of his marriage to Belle Potter a huge Family Bible, bound in leather and with an Apocrypha. On the title page was written in a fine old script: To Joshua and Belle Bye from Aaron and Dinah Bye. “By their fruits ye shall know them.”

For a long time to Isaiah, who used to pore absorbedly as a boy over this book with its pictures and long old-fashioned *S*, this inscription savored of vineyards and orchards. The white Byes, as a matter of fact, were the possessors of very fine peach-orchards in the neighborhood of what is now known as Bryn Mawr, and Isaiah, even as a little fellow, had been taken out there to pick peaches.

His father Joshua had spent his life in making those orchards what they were; a born agriculturist, he had an uncanny knowledge of planting, of grafting, of fertilizing. Many a farmer tried to inveigle him from Aaron Bye. But although Joshua’s wages were small, he had inherited his mother’s blind, invincible attachment for the Byes. His place was with Aaron.

===Chinese===
约书亚虽生在奴隶制下，却从未领教过这制度最狰狞的一面。自由来到他身上时，他还是个很小的娃娃。老朱迪还告诉他：西泽当年打过独立战争！于是约书亚对于解放人的战争，所知竟多过对奴隶制的所知——为废除奴隶制而打的仗，还是后来的事。儿子艾赛亚（Isaiah）从他那里几乎听不到什么旧制度的情形，尽管旧时代的遗迹在四下里比比皆是。他知道的只是：约书亚获释之后一直替黛娜和亚伦·拜做工；还知道在他娶贝尔·波特（Belle Potter）的时候，两位主人送了他一部硕大的家用《圣经》，皮面精装，附有《次经》。扉页上以一手古雅的好字题着：亚伦·拜与黛娜·拜，赠约书亚·拜与贝尔·拜。「凭着他们的果子，就可以认出他们来。」

艾赛亚小时候常捧着这部大书看得入迷——看里面的图画，看那些老式的长尾巴 *S*。很长一段时间里，这句题词在他闻来满是葡萄园和果园的气味。事实上，白人拜家在如今叫做布林莫尔（Bryn Mawr）的地方一带，拥有极好的桃园；艾赛亚小小年纪，就被带去那里摘过桃子。

他父亲约书亚一辈子的心血都花在那些果园上，才造就了它们后来的光景；他天生是个农艺家，于播种、嫁接、施肥，都有一种近乎神妙的本事。多少农户想把他从亚伦·拜手里挖走。然而约书亚工钱虽薄，却继承了母亲对拜家那种盲目而打不破的依恋。他的位置，就在亚伦身边。

===Original===
It was young white Meriwether Bye, youngest son-of Aaron’s and Dinah’s ten children, who told Isaiah what the inscription meant. Joshua had not married until he was nearly fifty and his single son, black Isaiah, and white Meriwether were boys together. Meriwether used to come to the Bye house at Fourth and Coates Streets, which is now Fairmount Avenue, as often as Isaiah used to appear at the Bye house at Fourth and Spruce.

Isaiah showed the inscription to Meriwether, “By their fruits ye shall know them.”

“Yes,” said young Merry tracing the letters with a fat finger, “that’s our family motto.” Isaiah wanted to know what a motto was.

“Something,” Meriwether told him vaguely, “that your whole family goes by.” The black boy thought that likely.

===Chinese===
把题词的真正意思讲给艾赛亚听的，是白人梅里韦瑟·拜（Meriwether Bye），亚伦和黛娜十个子女中最小的儿子。约书亚年近五十才成婚，他的独子——黑人艾赛亚——和白人梅里韦瑟是同年的玩伴。梅里韦瑟常到第四街与科茨街口——如今已叫费尔芒特大道——的拜宅来，正如艾赛亚常到第四街与斯普鲁斯街口的拜宅去一样。

艾赛亚把题词指给梅里韦瑟看：「凭着他们的果子，就可以认出他们来。」

「是啊，」小梅里（Merry）用胖乎乎的指头描着那几个字说，「这是咱们家的家训。」艾赛亚想知道家训是什么东西。

「就是，」梅里韦瑟含糊地告诉他，「你们全家都凭它行事的那样东西。」黑孩子觉得多半就是如此。

===Original===
“Everybody knows Bye peaches, ain’t that so? Cause of that everybody knows the Byes.”

Meriwether, though impressed by this logic, didn’t think that that was what was meant. A subsequent conversation with his father confirmed his opinion.

“It means this, Ziah,” he said one hot July afternoon walking home with the colored boy from the brickyard where Isaiah worked, “it means it shows the kind of stuff you are. It means—now—you see a bare tree in the wintertime don’t you, and you don’t know what it is? But you do perhaps know an apple blossom when you see it, or a peach blossom. In the spring you see that tree covered, let’s say, with apple blossoms. Well, you know it’s an apple tree.”

“But what’s that got to do with us?” Isaiah wanted to know. He was interested, he could not tell why, but his slow-working mind clung to its first idea. “Your father wrote it in the book he gave my father. My father hasn’t any fruit trees.”

===Chinese===
「拜家的桃子，谁人不认得？是不是？就凭这个，谁人都认得拜家。」

梅里韦瑟虽然被这套逻辑折服，却觉得题词的意思不在此。后来同父亲的一番交谈，证实了他的看法。

「它的意思是这么回事，赛亚（Ziah），」七月里一个酷热的午后，他同那个有色人种男孩从艾赛亚干活的砖窑一路回家，说道，「意思是，它能显出你是块什么材料。意思是——呃——你见过冬天里光秃秃的树吧？你不知道它是什么树。可你见到苹果花、桃花，多半是认得的。到了春天，你看见那棵树开满了——比方说——苹果花。得，你就知道那是一棵苹果树。」

「可那跟我们有什么相干？」艾赛亚想知道。他说不清为什么，只觉得这话有意思；可他那转得慢悠悠的脑子，还死死抱着头一个念头。「是你父亲把它写在他送给我父亲的那本书上的。我父亲一棵果树也没有。」

===Original===
Isaiah never forgot the answer Meriwether made him in the unconscious cruelty of youth. “When it comes to people,” said the young Quaker, “it means pretty much the same thing. Now when I grow up, I’m going to be a great doctor,” his chest swelled, “but nobody will be surprised. They’ll all say, ‘Of course, he’s the son of Aaron Bye, the rich peach-merchant. Good stock there,’ ” he involuntarily mimicked his pompous father; “and I’ll be good fruit. That’s the way it always is: good trees, good fruit; rich, important people, rich important sons.”

“What’ll I be?” asked Isaiah Bye, grotesquely tragic in his tattered clothes, the sweat rolling off his shiny face, so intent was his interest.

“Well,” Meriwether countered judicially, “what could you be?” He pondered a moment, his own position so secure that he was willing to do his best by this serious case. “Your father and your father’s father were slaves. ’Course your father’s free now but he’s just a servant. He’s not what you’d call his own man. So I s’pose that’s what you’ll be, a good servant. Tell you what, Isaiah, you can be my coachman. I’ll be good to you. And when you’re grown up,” said Meriwether with more imagination than he usually displayed, “I’ll point you out to some famous doctor from France and say, ‘His father was a good servant to my father, and he’s been a good servant to my father’s son.’ How’ll you like that?” Meriwether tapped him fondly if somewhat condescendingly on the arm.

“You’ll never,” said Isaiah Bye, drawing back from the familiar touch, “you’ll never be able to say that about me.” And he turned and ran down the hot street, leaving Meriwether Bye gaping on the sidewalk.

===Chinese===
艾赛亚一辈子也忘不了梅里韦瑟给他的这个回答，那是少年人浑然不觉的残忍。「说到人身上，」这位贵格会少年说，「意思也差不多是一回事。等我长大了，我要当一位了不起的名医，」他胸膛挺了起来，「可是谁也不会觉得稀奇。他们全会说：『那当然，他是亚伦·拜的儿子，那个阔气的桃子商人。好人家出来的。』」他不自觉地学起了父亲那副自命不凡的腔调；「我就是好果子。事情向来如此：好树结好果；有钱有势的人家，就有有钱有势的儿子。」

「那我会是什么？」艾赛亚·拜问道。他一身破衣烂衫，汗珠子从油亮的脸上滚滚而下，那份专注让整个人透出一副怪诞的悲剧相。

「唔，」梅里韦瑟以裁判的口吻回道，「你能是什么？」他沉吟了片刻；自己的地位稳稳当当，倒也愿意尽心料理这桩郑重的案子。「你父亲、你父亲的父亲，都是奴隶。当然喽，你父亲如今是自由人了，可他不过是个仆役，算不上你说的那种自己当家做主的人。所以我估摸着，你将来也就是那么回事——做个好仆役。这么着吧，艾赛亚，你来给我当车夫，我会好好待你。等你长大了，」梅里韦瑟这话说得比平素多了几分想象力，「我就把你指给哪位法国来的名医看，说：『他父亲给我父亲当过好仆人，他又给我父亲的儿子当了好仆人。』你说好不好？」梅里韦瑟亲热地——虽然多少带着点居高临下——拍了拍他的胳膊。

「你永远，」艾赛亚·拜闪身躲开那亲昵的一拍，「你永远没法那样说我。」说罢他转身顺着滚烫的街道跑去，撇下梅里韦瑟·拜张着嘴愣在人行道上。

===Original===
After that his father could never persuade him to enter again the Bye house, or the Bye orchards. Fortunately his mother upheld him here. “ ’Tain’t as though he had to work for them old Byes,” she said straightening up her already straight shoulders. “He makes just as much and more in the brickyard and in helpin’ Amos White haul.”

“I know that,” Joshua would reply impatiently, “but old Mist’ Aaron says—now—he likes to have his own people workin’ roun’ him. And I don’t like to disappoint him.”

Belle Bye told Isaiah. “I’m not one of his own people, Ma,” he answered stubbornly, “and after that I’m not ever goin’ back.” Belle was rejoiced to hear this. She would have been an insurgent in any walk of life. Joshua was the genuine peasant type—the type, black or white, which believes in a superior class and yields blindly to its mandates. But Belle had seen too many changes even in her thirty-five years—she was far younger than Joshua—not to know that many things are possible if one just has courage.

===Chinese===
打那以后，他父亲再也劝不动他踏进拜家宅子或拜家果园一步。所幸在这件事上，母亲给他撑腰。「又不是说，他非得给那些老拜家的人做工不可，」她说着，把本就笔直的肩膀又挺了挺。「他在砖窑里做活，再帮阿莫斯·怀特（Amos White）拉货，挣的只多不少。」

「这我知道，」约书亚总不耐烦地回道，「可老亚伦老爷说了——呃——他喜欢让自己人在跟前做事。我不想叫他失望。」

这些话，贝尔·拜都一五一十讲给了艾赛亚听。「我不是他什么自己人，妈，」他倔头倔脑地答道，「打那以后，我再也不回去了。」贝尔听了这话，满心欢喜。她这种人，无论干哪一行，都会是个造反的料。约书亚则是地道的庄稼人类型——这种人无论黑白，都笃信世上有高一等的阶级，并对这阶级的号令盲目服从。可贝尔纵然才三十五岁——她比约书亚年轻得多——也已见惯了太多世事的变迁，深知只要有胆量，许多事情都办得到。

===Original===
Isaiah, on being questioned, told his mother with considerable reluctance about his conversation with Meriwether. Belle, while regretting the breach, understood. She had been glad to have her boy the associate of young white Bye. Without expressing it to herself in so many words she had realized that association with Meriwether was an education for Isaiah. Already he was talking more correctly than other colored boys in his group, his manners were good, and though his work was of the roughest kind, his vision was broad, he knew there were other things.

“I don’t believe,” his mother told him wisely, “that you kin go as fur as you dream. Too many things agin you fur that, boy. But you kin die much further along the road than when you was born. Never forget that.”

So Isaiah was saved from the initial mistake of aiming too high and of coming utterly to smash. Yet he accomplished wonders. Who shall say how he increased his slender store of knowledge? How he learned to read wise books borrowed and bought as best he might? How he learned geography and history that made his heartbeats go wild since it told him of the French Revolution and how a whole nation once practically enslaved arose to a fuller, richer life?

===Chinese===
艾赛亚被问起来，才老大不情愿地把同梅里韦瑟的那番对话讲给母亲听。贝尔虽惋惜这段交情断了，却是理解的。她原本乐见自家儿子同白人拜家的小少爷做伴。虽不曾对自己明明白白说出，她心里明白：同梅里韦瑟来往，对艾赛亚是一种教育。比起他那一伙里的其他有色人种男孩，他说话已经规矩周正得多，举止也有礼数；虽说干的活计再粗重不过，他的眼界却宽——他知道世上还有别样的东西。

「我不信，」母亲明智地告诉他，「你能走到你梦的那么远。拦着你的东西太多啦，孩子。可你能死在比你出生时远得多的路途上。这道理，永远别忘了。」

就这样，艾赛亚躲过了志向悬得过高、摔得粉身碎骨的初错。然而他仍创造了奇迹。谁能说得清，他是怎样添起自己那点单薄的学识家底的？怎样靠着东借西买、想方设法，读懂了一本本充满智慧的书？又怎样学会了使他心跳狂奔的地理和历史——因为那里面讲到法国大革命，讲到一整个几乎尽遭奴役的民族如何奋起，走向一种更充实、更丰盈的生活？

===Original===
The inspiration for all this lay in those careless words of young Meriwether. Although Isaiah met the young fellow many times after that incident, and apparently with friendliness, he never in his heart forgave him. Like Ceazer he developed a dislike for white people and their ways which developed, however, into a sturdy independence and an unyielding pride. No amount of contumely ever made him ashamed of his slave ancestry. On the contrary, to measure himself against old Ceazer and Judy gave him ground for honest pride. “See what they were and how far I’ve gone,” he used to say, pleasantly boastful.

He resented as few sons of freedmen did the assurance with which the white Byes took their wealth and position and power. “Hoisted themselves on the backs of the black Byes.” He resented especially the ingratitude of Aaron Bye to Joshua. For himself he asked nothing; being content to fight his own way “through an onfriendly world.”

The white Byes had gone far, but the black Byes having now that greatest of all gifts, freedom, would go far, too. They would be leaders of other black men.

The upshot of all this was that Isaiah Bye opened a school for colored youth down on Vine Street. No name and no figure in colored life in Philadelphia was ever better beloved and more revered than his.

===Chinese===
这一切的灵感，正埋在那少年梅里韦瑟随口说出的话里。那件事之后，艾赛亚虽同这位少爷又见过许多回，表面上也算友好，心里却始终没有原谅他。像西泽一样，他滋长出对白人及其做派的厌恶；只是这厌恶在他身上，长成了一种坚实的自立和一副不屈的自尊。再多的羞辱，也从未让他为自己的奴隶家世害臊。恰恰相反，拿自己同老西泽和朱迪两相比较，倒给了他堂堂正正自豪的凭据。「瞧瞧他们当年是什么光景，再瞧瞧我如今走到了哪里，」他常这样夸口，语气却招人喜欢。

白人拜家安享着财富、地位与权势，那份心安理得，获释者之子中鲜有人像他这样愤懑难平。「他们是踩着黑人拜家的脊背爬上去的。」他尤其怨恨亚伦·拜对约书亚的忘恩负义。至于他自己，则一无所求，只满足于独力闯出一条路，「穿过一个不友好的世界」。

白人拜家已经走得很远；而黑人拜家如今拥有了那份最伟大的馈赠——自由，也终将走得很远。他们要做其他黑人的带头人。

这一切的结局是：艾赛亚·拜在瓦因街（Vine Street）上开办了一所面向有色人种青年的学校。在费城有色人种的生活里，再没有哪个名字、哪个人物，比他的名字更受爱戴、更受尊崇。

## IV / 四

===Original===

Isaiah did not marry until he was thirty-one, which was an advanced age for his times. Even then he had married earlier than his father. Old Joshua, who died long before Isaiah’s marriage, had been inordinately proud of his one son.

“Jes’ wouldn’t work fer white folks,” Joshua used to say, “that weren’t good enough fer him.”

Isaiah and Miriam Sayres Bye had one son. “Meriwether,” Isaiah wrote in Aaron and Dinah Bye’s old gift, and under it in a script as fine and characteristic as that of the original inscription: “By *his* fruits shall ye know﻿—*me*.” It was a strange but not unnatural bit of pride, the same pride which had made him name this squirming bundle of potentialities, “Meriwether﻿—Meriwether Bye,” a boy with the same name which old white Aaron Bye’s son had borne and with as good chances. The Civil War was on the horizon then and Isaiah Bye, with that calm expectation of the unexpected which was his mother’s chiefest legacy, was sure that in that grand melee all his people would know freedom. So black Meriwether Bye, born like himself in freedom, would know nothing but that estate when he began to have understanding.

Isaiah had accumulated a little, though how that was possible, no one aware of his tiny stipend could guess. It is true he not only taught school, but he had outside pupils, ex-slaves, freedmen, men like himself born in freedom, but unable through economic pressure to enjoy it except in name﻿—all these crowded his home at night on Vine Street, and sweated mightily over primers and pothooks and the abacus. Twenty-five cents an hour he charged them, giving each a meticulous care such as would bring a modern tutor many dollars. He wrote letters, pamphlets, too, for that marvelous organization already well established, the A.M.E. Church. His wife had a sister whose husband kept a secondhand shop and from this source he earned an occasional dollar. When Meriwether was eight, Isaiah owned two houses in Pearl Street, the house in Vine Street, a half interest in his brother-in-law’s store and a plot in Mount Olivet Cemetery.

===Chinese===

艾赛亚直到三十一岁才娶亲，搁在那个年代已算是晚婚了。饶是如此，比起他父亲来，他成婚还算早的。老约书亚早在艾赛亚娶亲之前多年便已去世，生前对这独子骄傲得了不得。

「就是死活不肯给白人做工，」约书亚生前常说，「嫌那配不上他。」

艾赛亚和米里亚姆·塞尔斯·拜（Miriam Sayres Bye）只生了一个儿子。艾赛亚在亚伦·拜和黛娜·拜传下的那件旧赠物上写下「梅里韦瑟」，又在下面用一手与原有题词同样精妙、同样见性情的字迹写道：「凭着*他*的果子，就可以认出——*我*。」这是一种古怪却并非不近情理的骄傲，也正是这份骄傲，促使他给这团手脚乱蹬、蕴着无限可能的小家伙起名「梅里韦瑟——梅里韦瑟·拜」，与白人老亚伦·拜的儿子同名，前程也一样地好。那时南北战争已隐隐在望，艾赛亚·拜带着母亲传给他的头一份遗产——对意外之事从容以待的期许——深信在那场大混战里，他的族人全都将尝到自由的滋味。于是黑人梅里韦瑟·拜同他一样生而自由，待到渐通人事，所知的便唯有自由这一种境况。

艾赛亚攒下了一份小小的家业，虽说知道他那份微薄薪俸的人，实在猜不出这怎么可能。不错，他不仅在学校教书，还有校外的学生——昔日为奴的、被解放的自由民，还有像他一样生而自由、却因经济所迫除了名义之外无从消受这份自由的人——这些人夜里挤满他瓦因街的家，对着识字课本、描红笔画和算盘埋头苦熬。他每人每小时收两毛五分钱，那份细致入微的照拂，放在今天足以让一位家庭教师挣来大把美元。他还为那个当时已根基稳固的了不起的组织——非洲卫理公会（A.M.E. Church）——捉刀书信和小册子。他妻子有个姐妹，嫁的丈夫开着一爿旧货铺，他由此也间或挣得一元半块。到梅里韦瑟八岁那年，艾赛亚已坐拥珀尔街（Pearl Street）的两幢房子、瓦因街的那一幢、连襟铺子里的一半股份，还有橄榄山公墓（Mount Olivet Cemetery）的一方墓地。

===Original===

From the very beginning Meriwether knew he was to be a great man﻿—a doctor, his father had said emphatically. And Meriwether repeated it by rote. He was a clever enough child though without his father’s solid trait of concentration. But he liked the idea of greatness﻿—that and the profession of medicine came to be synonymous with him as it was already with his father. Otherwise it is likely that both of them would have seen earlier the boy’s inaptitude for the calling thus thrust upon him.

Meriwether went to his father’s school, to Mr. Jonas Howard’s catering establishment, which he loved, to Sunday-School and to his Uncle Peter’s secondhand store. In any one of these places he was at home. He might have made a good teacher, caterer, minister or storekeeper. Yet he meandered on, doing absolutely mediocre work, never failing, never shining, and always rather purposely waiting the day which should bring him to the Medical School.

He was waiting for something else, too, though this Isaiah never guessed. He was waiting for some sign of help or recognition from the white Byes. His father had told him of the slaveholder’s great debt to old Joshua; he had taken him riding past the Bryn Mawr peach orchards. “By rights part of them ought to belong to us. But I don’t mind, no sir‑ee! Let ’em have ’em. See where we are today without their help. Think of it!”

Meriwether did think of it and did mind it. He learned that he had been named after the son of his grandfather’s patron and somehow it seemed impossible to him that that mere fact should not result in something tangibly advantageous. He lacked the imagination to understand the pride which actuated Isaiah to name his boy as he had. The year before Meriwether was to enter medical school, Isaiah, fortunately, for himself, died.

===Chinese===

打从一开始，梅里韦瑟就知道自己将来要成了不起的人物——当医生，他父亲斩钉截铁地说过。梅里韦瑟便有口无心地把这话挂在嘴边念叨。这孩子够聪明，只是没有他父亲那种脚踏实地的专注。可他喜欢「伟大」这个念头——伟大与行医这两样，在他心里渐渐成了同义语，一如早已在他父亲心里那样。要不然，父子俩多半早该看出，这孩子于这份硬塞给他的行当实在并不相宜。

梅里韦瑟上他父亲的学校，去乔纳斯·霍华德（Jonas Howard）先生承办酒席的铺子——那地方他顶喜欢——上主日学校，还去他叔父彼得（Uncle Peter）的旧货铺。这几处地方，随便哪一处他都如鱼得水。他本可以成为一位出色的教师、宴席承办商、牧师或者店主。可他就这么有一搭没一搭地晃荡着，做出来的事一律平平，从不砸锅，也从不冒尖，只一门心思等着那个把他送进医学院的日子。

其实他还在等一样别的东西，只是艾赛亚从未想到。他在等白人拜家施以援手、另眼相看的某种迹象。父亲跟他讲过那位奴隶主欠老约书亚多大一笔情分；还带他驱车经过布林莫尔的桃园。「论理，那些园子有一部分本该归咱们。可我不计较，才不呢，先生！随他们留着去吧。瞧瞧咱们今天不靠他们帮衬走到了哪一步。你想想！」

梅里韦瑟还果真想了，也果真计较了。他得知自己的名字袭自祖父那位恩主的儿子，不知怎么，在他看来，单凭这一层，就不可能不生出些看得见摸得着的好处来。他缺少那种想象力，去领会驱使艾赛亚给儿子取这样一个名字的骄傲。就在梅里韦瑟该进医学院的前一年，艾赛亚死了——于他自己而言，实属万幸。

===Original===

A few months later Miriam died, too. Meriwether was left sole heir to the three houses and two or three hundred dollars. He was tired of school and not at all displeased with the idea of being his own master. He would like a little vacation, he fancied, and a chance to see the world. Somebody told him of a good way to do this﻿—why not get a job as train porter? The idea pleased him; there was travel, easy money, besides his little property in Philadelphia. And afterwards perhaps there would be the patron for whom he had been named, Dr. Meriwether Bye of Bryn Mawr.

Isaiah’s mother, Belle Bye, used to say, “Things you do expect and things you don’t expect are sure to come to pass.” It took Isaiah many years to see the reasonableness of this apparently unreasoned statement. Certainly one of the things he never expected to come to pass was that his boy Meriwether should, first, give up altogether his project of studying medicine and, second, that bit by bit, through sickness, gambling, and a hitherto unsuspected penchant for sheer laziness, he should run through his Philadelphia property, thus wiping away all that edifice of respectability and good citizenship which Isaiah Bye had so carefully built up.

Colored Philadelphia society is organized as definitely as, and even a little more carefully than, Philadelphia white society. One wasn’t “in” in those old days unless one were, first, “an old citizen,” and, second, unless one were eminently respectable﻿—almost it might be said God-fearing. Meriwether having been born to this estate suffered all the inconveniences coming to a member of a group at that time small and closely welded. His business was everybody’s business. His Uncle Peter had upbraided him for not studying medicine. Jonas Howard, the caterer, knew about his first real estate transfer. The young Howards and his cousins knew about his gambling and rebuked him admiringly. On one of his “runs” Meriwether spent a week in New York. This was in 1889. Not a single colored person knew him or cared about him. He rented a room in Fifty-third Street and made that his headquarters. Later he rented two rooms and married a young seamstress who died in 1891 when her boy was born.

Meriwether did do two things after that. First he wrote to Dr. Meriwether Bye telling him who he was and implying he would not disdain a little aid. It is doubtful if the doctor, who at that time was traveling in Europe with his tiny grandson, ever received the letter. Second, he took to drink. More than anything else he fell into a deep, ineluctable mood of melancholia. Here he was, Meriwether Bye, destined to be a great man, a famous physician. Why, he had been a man of property once, with money in the bank! And now he was just a poor nobody, picking up odd jobs, paying his room rent fearfully from week to week, sometimes pawning Isaiah Bye’s chased gold watch.

===Chinese===

几个月后，米里亚姆也故去了。梅里韦瑟成了三幢房子和两三百块钱的唯一继承人。他早已念书念腻了，一想到从此自己当家做主，非但没有半点不乐意，反倒正中下怀。他想，不妨先放个短假，见见世面。有人指点了他一条好路子——何不到火车上谋个搬运工的差事？这主意正合他意：既能四处跑，钱又来得容易，何况费城还守着他那份小小产业。再往后，兴许还能指望上那位让他得了这个名字的恩主——布林莫尔的梅里韦瑟·拜医生。

艾赛亚的母亲贝尔·拜生前常说：「你盼着的事和没盼着的事，都准会来。」这句看似全无道理的话，艾赛亚过了许多年才咂摸出它的在理。当然，他万万没盼到的一件事是：他的儿子梅里韦瑟，其一，干脆彻底撂下了学医的打算；其二，又靠着生病、赌钱，加上一种前所未见的纯粹偷懒的癖性，一点一点把费城的产业败了个精光——就这样，把艾赛亚·拜苦心孤诣垒起的那座体面人家、良善公民的大厦夷为了平地。

费城有色人种的社会，组织之严整不亚于费城白人的社会，甚至还要更讲究几分。在往昔那些年头，一个人要想算「场面上的人」，头一条得是「老市民」，第二条得极其体面——几乎可以说，还得敬畏上帝。梅里韦瑟生就这份身份，也就把当时那种人数寥寥、抱团紧密的群体中一员要吃的种种不便，尝了个遍。他的事就是大家的事。他叔父彼得责备过他不好好学医；承办酒席的乔纳斯·霍华德对他的头一笔房产过手一清二楚；霍华德家的年轻人和他的表亲们晓得他赌钱，一边数落，一边又透着艳羡。有一趟「跑车」，梅里韦瑟在纽约待了一个星期。那是 1889 年。没有一个有色人种认得他，也没有一个把他放在心上。他在五十三街（Fifty-third Street）赁了个房间当作落脚点，后来又赁了两间，娶了个年轻的女裁缝——1891 年，她生下男孩时便死了。

打那以后，梅里韦瑟倒实实在在做了两件事。第一，他给梅里韦瑟·拜医生写了封信，自报家门，暗示自己并不嫌弃一点资助。那位医生当时正带着年幼的孙子在欧洲游历，这封信到底收没收到，殊可怀疑。第二，他喝上了酒。比这更甚的是，他陷进了一种深沉的、无可逃遁的忧郁。瞧瞧他吧——梅里韦瑟·拜，命中注定要成了不起的人物、有名的医生；嘿，他也曾是有产业的人，银行里存过钱！可如今不过是个无足轻重的穷汉，打着零工度日，一礼拜一礼拜提心吊胆地付着房租，有时还得把艾赛亚·拜那块雕花金表送进当铺。

===Original===

How he worked it out he himself could not have told. But he saw himself a martyr, “driven by fate” from the high eminence of his father’s dreams to his own poor realities. Think how he had struggled, sacrificed﻿—he believed it﻿—the fun and freedom of youth to come to this! “How,” said Meriwether Bye harking back to Sunday-School days, “how are the mighty fallen!” And how easily might they have remained mighty.

He named his boy Peter after his Uncle Peter, in whose secondhand shop in Philadelphia he had spent delightful hours.

Now see the perversity of human nature. Just as his father Isaiah Bye had talked to his son Meriwether about the reward of effort and faithful toil, just so Meriwether talked to Peter about the futility of labor and ambition. And in particular he talked to him about the ingratitude of the white Byes﻿—of all white people.

“It makes no difference, Peter, what you do, or how hard you work. The rewards of life are only for such or such. You may pour your heart’s blood out,”﻿—he had a fine gift of rhetoric﻿—“and still achieve nothing. Think of your great-grandfather. Fate favors those whom she chooses. Blessed is he who expects nothing, for he shall not be disappointed.”

Or, “Peter, if life has any favors for you, she’ll give them to you without your asking for them. The world owes you a living, let it come to you, don’t bother going after it.”

===Chinese===

这里头的账他自己怎么算平的，他说不上来。反正他把自己看成一个殉道者，「被命运驱赶着」，从父亲梦想的高处跌进了自己寒碜的现实。想想他曾怎样挣扎、怎样牺牲——他信这个——连青春年少的玩乐与自由都牺牲了，才落到这般田地！「怎么，」梅里韦瑟·拜翻出主日学校年月的老话，念叨道，「大英雄何竟仆倒！」而想当年，他们要保持那份英雄气概，本是何等容易的事。

他给儿子取名彼得，袭的是叔父彼得的名字——在费城，在那位叔父的旧货铺里，他曾度过多少快活的时辰。

且看人性何其乖谬。当年他父亲艾赛亚·拜怎样对儿子梅里韦瑟大谈勤勉奋斗的报偿，如今梅里韦瑟便怎样对彼得大谈劳碌与雄心的虚妄。尤其少不了要数落的，是白人拜家的忘恩负义——是一切白人的忘恩负义。

「彼得，你干什么、下多大功夫，全不相干。人生的报偿，从来只归某一类人所有。你就是把心血都呕尽，」——他很有几分雄辩的天才——「也照样一事无成。想想你的曾祖父。命运只青睐她选中的人。凡事不抱指望的人有福了，因为他必不至于失望。」

再不然便是：「彼得，要是命里有什么好处等着你，不用你开口，它自会送上门来。这世界欠你一口饭吃，就让饭来找你，犯不着你去追它。」

===Original===

How completely his son might be absorbing all this, Meriwether never knew, for Peter, vocal enough with his playmates and others, maintained an owlish silence when his father thus harangued him.

But his aunt knew. She was a tall, stout, yellow woman, with that ineffable look of sadness in her eyes characteristic of a certain type of colored people. She was the sister of Peter’s mother, and when Peter’s father died, suddenly, inconsequently, she accepted uncomplainingly his son along with her other burdens.

Peter was then twelve; extraordinarily handsome, vivid and alert. Miss Susan Graves riding home from the cemetery reflected that he might be not such a burden after all. Clearly he would soon want to be taking care of himself.

“Peter,” she said thoughtfully, “what do you want to do when you grow up?”

“Oh, I don’t know,” her nephew replied, temporarily removing his gaze from the windowpane where it had been glued for twenty minutes. “I’m not bothered about that, Aunt Susan. You see the world owes me a living.”

She noticed in him then the first fruits of his father’s shiftlessness. But far more deeply rooted than that was his deep dislike for white people. He did not believe that any of them were kind or just or even human. And although he could not himself have told what he wanted from the white Byes, if indeed he wanted anything, he grew up with the feeling that he and his had been unusually badly treated. His grandfather’s connection with white people resulted in pride, his father’s in shiftlessness; in Peter it took the form of a constant and increasing bitterness.

===Chinese===

儿子把这一套囫囵吞下了多少，梅里韦瑟从不知晓，因为彼得跟玩伴们和别人在一起时话并不少，每逢父亲这样训诫，却像猫头鹰似的一本正经、一言不发。

可他的姨妈心里有数。她是个高挑壮实的浅肤色女人，眼里带着一种难以言说的哀愁——那是某一类有色人种特有的神情。她是彼得母亲的姐妹；及至彼得的父亲猝然弃世——死得突兀，也没留下什么交代——她便毫无怨言地把他这个儿子连同自己肩上别的担子一并接了下来。

彼得那年十二岁，俊俏得出奇，鲜活而机警。苏珊·格雷夫斯小姐（Susan Graves）从墓园坐车回家，一路上寻思：这孩子到头来未必是多大的累赘。显然，他很快就该想着自食其力了。

「彼得，」她若有所思地说，「你长大了想做什么呀？」

「哦，不知道，」她外甥答道，目光在窗玻璃上足足粘了二十分钟，这才临时挪开。「我不为这个操心，苏珊姨妈。你瞧，这世界欠着我一口饭吃呢。」

她当即在他身上看出了他父亲那副懒散撒手的作风结出的头一批果子。可比这扎根深得多的，是他对白人深深的厌恶。他不信他们中间有谁善良、公道，甚至不信他们算得上人。而且，尽管他自己也说不出想从白人拜家那里得到什么——倘若当真想得到什么的话——他是怀着一种感觉长大的：他和他的家人受了异乎寻常的亏待。他祖父与白人的那段纠葛，结出的是骄傲；他父亲结出的是懒散；到了彼得身上，则化作了一种经久不息、与日俱增的怨愤。

## IX / 九

===Original===
She played her cards with an odd mixture of deliberation and spontaneity.

"Maggie adores you, Sylvia," said Joanna.

"I think she does," Sylvia replied modestly. "I don't know why, I'm sure. She certainly is nice to me."

Maggie's obvious admiration and Sylvia's naive acceptance made the way easy. It is hard not to be nice to someone who shows plainly that you are her ideal, your company her supreme satisfaction. Maggie wore her hair like Sylvia's, she copied when she could her manner of dressing, she spent half her time at the Marshall house.

===Chinese===
她打自己的牌，既深思熟虑，又率性而为，两样奇特地融在一处。

「玛吉崇拜你呢，西尔维娅。」乔安娜说。

「我想是吧。」西尔维娅谦和地答道，「要说为什么，我实在不明白。她对我确实很好。」

玛吉的倾慕写得明明白白，西尔维娅又天真地坦然领受，这条路便好走了。人家明明白白表露出你是她的偶像、与你作伴便是她无上的满足，你再想对她不好也难。玛吉梳着西尔维娅一样的发式，一有机会便模仿她的穿衣路数，半个日子都泡在马歇尔家里。

===Original===
She saw the value of absolute honesty. No need to pose when telling the exact truth brought what one wanted without the strain of living up to a false position. The Marshalls soon knew of Maggie's poverty, of the quick wit and determination which had brought them from that "dump-heap"—Maggie's word—to the respectable and comfortable if still cheap boardinghouse. Sylvia used to talk to her mother about it. Mrs. Marshall suggested that she hand over to Maggie one or two of her perfectly good but discarded dresses.

But Sylvia objected with a very real delicacy. "She goes to the same school I go to and to Sunday-School. I wouldn't want the other children to see her in my things, she'd feel so badly."

Her mother saw the justice of that. "I suppose I have one or two things. There's that old brown Henrietta of mine and the silk poplin. How'll she get them made over though, Sylvia? Now don't expect me to help."

"Oh, mamma, you darling! You really are a brick! That poplin is old rose, isn't it? She ought to look lovely in it. I can fix them. You know how I love to fix things over and Maggie knows how to sew on the machine. If she stayed here three or four days, the rest of this week, we could finish them."

===Chinese===
她明白绝对诚实的好处。既然照实说出真相便能换来想要的东西，还不必绷着一副假体面活受罪，又何必再去装模作样。马歇尔家很快便知道了玛吉的穷困，知道了那股机灵劲儿与决断——正是凭着这两样，她们才从那处「垃圾堆」——这是玛吉的原话——搬进了虽说仍然廉价、却体面舒适的寄宿公寓。西尔维娅常跟母亲念叨这些。马歇尔太太提议，把自己一两件完好无损、只是不穿了的裙子送给玛吉。

西尔维娅却出于一种实实在在的体贴表示反对。「她和我上同一所学校，还一起上主日学校。我可不想让别的孩子看见她穿着我的衣裳，那样她会很难受的。」

母亲承认这话在理。「我大概有一两件：那件旧的棕色亨利埃塔呢裙，还有那条丝毛葛的。不过她要怎么把它们改出来呢，西尔维娅？可别指望我帮忙。」

「噢，妈妈，亲爱的！你可真是个大好人！那条丝毛葛是暗玫瑰色的，对吧？她穿上一定好看极了。我会改衣裳，你知道我最爱把旧衣翻新，玛吉又会使缝纫机。要是这礼拜剩下的三四天她都住在这儿，我们就能把它们都改完。」

===Original===
Mrs. Marshall agreed, Maggie's mother was consulted, Maggie came in an ecstasy. Her first sojourn away from home! And what a sojourn! Naturally neat though she was, she learned of toilet mysteries, of rites of which she had never dreamed. Nightly hair-brushings and the discovery that of course each one had her own brush and comb! Frequent washings of both, talcum powders! Joanna the ascetic used scentless ones, but Sylvia's were highly fragrant. These Maggie preferred. A bath every night.

"If you don't mind," said Sylvia, "I'll take mine first and then you can stay in as long as you like. I hope that pig Joanna hasn't used up all the hot water!"

Delicacies for breakfast, lunch, and dinner! Dinner at six instead of the middle of the day! Mrs. Marshall complained of a headache Saturday morning and Joanna took her breakfast up to her on a silver tray. Mr. Marshall kept box on box of cigars in his den. Sandy and Philip wore superlatively blackened shoes.

Maggie looked, listened, stored in her memory. The dresses were a success. The rose poplin, being the prettier, was finished first; Sylvia had longed so to get her hands on it. Maggie put it on Saturday morning and stood in front of the cheval mirror in Mrs. Marshall's room admiring her own and Sylvia's handiwork, and herself with it.

===Chinese===
马歇尔太太答应了，又征询了玛吉母亲的意见，玛吉欣喜若狂地来了。这是她头一回离家小住！何等样的小住啊！她原本就爱整洁，这回才算识得了梳妆盥洗的种种奥秘、识得了连做梦都想不到的规矩。每夜例行的梳发，外加一个发现——原来人人都有自己单独的发刷和梳子！两样还得常常洗，又用爽身粉！苦行僧似的乔安娜用的是不带香气的，西尔维娅的却香气袭人。玛吉偏爱后者。还有每夜一个澡。

「你要是不介意，」西尔维娅说，「我先洗，然后你爱泡多久就泡多久。但愿乔安娜那头猪没把热水全用完！」

早饭、午饭、晚饭，餐餐有精美吃食！晚饭挪到六点吃，不再摆在晌午！星期六早晨马歇尔太太说头疼，乔安娜便用银托盘把早餐给她送上楼去。马歇尔先生的书房里存着一盒又一盒的雪茄。桑迪和菲利普脚上的皮鞋总擦得乌黑锃亮。

玛吉看在眼里，听在耳里，一一记在心上。两条裙子改得十分成功。那条玫瑰红的丝毛葛更漂亮，便先改好了——西尔维娅早就巴不得亲手摆弄它了。星期六早晨玛吉把它穿上，站在马歇尔太太房里的穿衣镜前，欣赏自己和西尔维娅的手艺，也连同欣赏穿着它的自己。

===Original===
"It's too pretty to wear in the house. Oh, don't let's have to wait till tomorrow. Mamma, couldn't the boys take us to the matinée? Maggie, have you seen *Peter Pan*?"

Maggie, it transpired, had seen nothing, had never been inside a theater.

"What fun!" Sylvia's native delicacy hit on the right expression. "Fancy going to your first matinée. Can you spare us, Mother dear?"

The party could be arranged. Philip and Alexander expressed their willingness. Joanna did not care to go, to Maggie's astonishment, which increased when she saw how wonderful the theater was. But there were other things. The girl never forgot the thrill that came over her as Philip took her arm and led her over dangerous crossings, arranged her seat and program for her, took off her coat. He held it during the performance and wrinkled it shamelessly. Sylvia scolded him.

===Chinese===
「这裙子太好看了，光在家里穿可惜了。噢，别让我们非得等到明天。妈妈，不能让男孩子们带我们去看日场戏吗？玛吉，你看过《彼得·潘》（Peter Pan）吗？」

一问才知道，玛吉什么都没看过，连剧院的门都没进去过。

「多有意思呀！」西尔维娅天生的细腻让她挑中了恰到好处的说法。「头一回看日场戏呢，想想都美。亲爱的妈妈，放我们走一趟行吗？」

这一趟是张罗得起来的。菲利普和亚历山大都表示愿意去。乔安娜却不想去，这已经让玛吉吃惊不小；及至亲眼见到剧院何等堂皇，她更惊奇了。不过更要紧的还在别处。菲利普挽起她的手臂，领她走过危险的路口，替她安排座位、递上戏单，又帮她脱下外套——那股涌遍全身的战栗，这姑娘一辈子也忘不了。整场演出他把她那件外套抱在怀里，毫无顾忌地抱出满脸褶皱。西尔维娅数落了他。

===Original===
"You ought to be ashamed of yourself, Phil."

"It doesn't matter," Maggie interposed happily. She was beginning to have her good time like other people. Oh, God bless John Howe!

The acquaintanceship progressed. All through the high school the two were nearly inseparable. It is true, Maggie sought Sylvia more than Sylvia sought her, but on the other hand Maggie's presence was taken as a matter of course by the Marshalls and their friends. Maggie went to parties with Sylvia, the two escorted by Brian Spencer and Philip. Often she slept at her house after the parties and at Christmas time and weekends. Once, when Mrs. Marshall took Joanna to visit relatives in Philadelphia, Maggie stayed with Sylvia a whole month.

In her junior year in the high school she had a long talk with Mr. Marshall. Of course they were still poor, the house just kept them in comfort. Maggie had become addicted to the wearing of good clothes. Her mother was getting older. They needed help from time to time. If Mr. Marshall would assist her in getting some work. She was young and strong and willing.

===Chinese===
「你该自己害臊的，菲尔（Phil）。」

「不要紧的。」玛吉快活地插了一句。她也总算像旁人一样，开始过上好日子了。噢，愿上帝保佑约翰·豪！

这份交情一路发展下去。整个高中阶段，两人几乎形影不离。诚然，玛吉找西尔维娅的时候多，西尔维娅找她的时候少；但另一方面，马歇尔一家和他们的朋友早把玛吉的存在当作理所当然。玛吉同西尔维娅一道去参加舞会，两人由布莱恩·斯宾塞和菲利普陪同。舞会散了，她常在那座宅子里留宿，圣诞节和周末也去。有一回马歇尔太太带乔安娜去费城走亲戚，玛吉便陪着西尔维娅住了整整一个月。

高中第三学年那年，她同马歇尔先生作了一次长谈。她们当然还是穷，那栋房子也仅够维持一份起码的舒适。玛吉穿好衣裳已经穿上了瘾。母亲年纪渐长，母女俩时不时需要帮衬。要是马歇尔先生肯帮她寻份工作就好。她年轻，有力气，也肯干。

===Original===
"No, no, Mr. Marshall!" she objected as Joel—they were sitting in his office—spoke of a loan and reached for his checkbook. "Not that! When could I ever pay you back? No, I mean work, real work. I could take orders, count the silver, look after the napery, pay off the men if you'd care to trust me."

Perhaps a man of another race might have stopped to consider such a proposition coming from the lips of a young and dainty girl. He might have been suspicious and realized that his younger son was working in the business with him just then and the boy and girl would be bound to be thrown together. But colored men of old Joel's type are obsessed with the idea of a progressing younger generation. "They must advance," thinks the older man, "I must do all in my power to help them. This is my contribution to mine own."

Joel taught her his simple system of bookkeeping and installed her. She proved herself efficient, willing, and—her mother's teachings spoke here—absolutely honest. Her energy and interest were surprising. "You might think it was her own business," said Joel. He had no desire to see either her or any of his children become caterers, but he did like to see a job well done. Philip was the only one who had evinced any interest in the business, and that was only during his last year before entering college. He had to make some extra money somehow—both he and Sandy had a healthy dislike of burdening their father with their college expenses—and since he had to work he preferred to spend his time and energy in his father's interests.

His chief work consisted in directing his father's various squads of waiters. He met them at the house where Joel was catering, started them off, checked over necessities, looked after the thousand details which lent to Joel's service the perfection that so justly brought him fame. Maggie often accompanied Philip on these trips. Sometimes she went to one house and he to another, and he would call for her and take her home. She pondered deeply over the possibility of these meetings.

===Chinese===
「不，不，马歇尔先生！」乔尔刚一提起借款、伸手去拿支票簿——两人当时正坐在他的办公室里——她就表示反对。「不是那个！我哪年哪月还得清呢？不，我是说工作，正经的工作。您要是信得过我，我可以接订单、清点银器、照管桌布餐巾，还能给伙计们发工钱。」

换作别个种族的男人，听见一个年轻娇秀的姑娘提出这样的请求，也许会停下来掂量掂量。他也许会起疑心，想到自己的小儿子那时正在买卖里跟着他做事，这一对青年男女难免抬头不见低头见。可是像老乔尔这一类有色人种男人，心里念念不忘的是年轻一代要不断上进。「他们必须往前走，」这位长者想，「我必须竭尽所能帮助他们。这是我对自家族人的一份贡献。」

乔尔把自己那套简单的记账法教给她，安插她上了工。她证明自己能干、肯干，而且——这里显出了她母亲的教诲——绝对诚实。她的干劲和热心令人惊奇。「你简直会当那是她自己的买卖。」乔尔说。他并不指望她、也不指望自己哪个孩子将来做宴席承办商，但他确实乐见一件事办得漂漂亮亮。孩子们里头，只有菲利普对这门生意露过一点兴趣，那也只是在进大学前的最后一年。他总得想法子挣些外快——他和桑迪都打心底不愿意拿上大学的开销去加重父亲的负担，这份心思再正常不过——既然横竖要做事，他宁可把时间和力气花在父亲的事业上。

他的主要差事，是调度父亲手下的一队队侍者。他到乔尔承办酒席的宅子同他们会合，派他们各就各位，清点必备物件，照料千百样细务——正是这些，让乔尔的服务臻于完美，也让他名不虚传。玛吉常跟着菲利普跑这些差事。有时她去一家，他去另一家，完了他再接她回家。这些会面里头的种种可能，她在心里翻来覆去掂量得很深。

===Original===
He was usually jolly, unsentimental, almost brotherly. Maggie took care to follow his lead. But to her great surprise she was beginning to be conscious of a deep affection for him. At first she had only yearned for respectability and comfort, and Philip represented such a convenient shortcut to her heart's desire. But now things were different.

Sometimes when they came home quite late he would take her arm and the two would walk slowly and silently down the strangely quiet streets. A curious little sense of intimacy used to brood over them at times like these. Philip would laugh a little nervously.

"Awfully jolly being out late like this by ourselves, isn't it, Maggie?"

She would nod him a smiling yes. "Some day," she thought, "he must say more."

===Chinese===
他向来快快活活，不来儿女情长那一套，几乎像个兄长。玛吉也小心拿捏着同样的分寸。可是大大出乎自己的意料，她渐渐觉出心里对他生出了一片深情。起初她渴望的不过是体面和安逸，而菲利普恰似一条通往心头愿想的便捷近路。如今却不一样了。

有时候两人回来得很晚，他便挽起她的手臂，两人缓缓地、默默地走过静得出奇的街道。这样的时刻，一种说不清的小小亲昵便会悄然笼罩他们。菲利普会带几分紧张地笑起来。

「就咱们俩这么晚在外头，痛快极了，是不是，玛吉？」

她便含笑冲他点点头，算是应了一声。「总有一天，」她想，「他总得再说出点别的。」

===Original===
Her studies, her work and these trips with Philip took up most of her time just now. She and Sylvia of course still saw a great deal of each other and once in a while went out together. She went to the theater still more rarely, or to a church festival with Henderson Neal, one of her mother's boarders. A mysterious tall brown figure of a man, twenty years older than Maggie and a thousand years older in experience, he caught and not infrequently held her attention. He had lived with them two years, paid his bills regularly, asked no questions and vouchsafed no explanations.

Maggie wondered what he did. Whatever his occupation, it certainly paid him well. More than once she had seen him display without ostentation a huge roll of bills, which apparently was static in bulk. His speech was often ungrammatical, but so deliberate that one thought he must be speaking correctly. He had a rather grand air, and listened to both Mrs. Ellersley and her daughter with a somewhat ponderous attention. Maggie thought he was rather interesting for such an old man—he must be nearly forty! She was a little afraid of him, though, and decided it would be rather unpleasant for anyone who chanced to make him angry.

Once he met Sylvia and Maggie on the street and offered to take them to the matinée. His interest was clearly in Maggie but he politely included her friend. Sylvia later told Philip about it.

"I hope you didn't go," he replied quickly.

===Chinese===
这阵子，她的功课、她的活计，还有同菲利普的这些外出，占去了她大部分时间。她和西尔维娅自然还是常来常往，偶尔也一道出门。上剧院就更要少了，倒是偶尔同亨德森·尼尔（Henderson Neal）去一回教堂节庆——他是她母亲的一名房客。这是个谜一样的高个子棕色皮肤男人，比玛吉年长二十岁，论阅历却老出一千年；他不时引得她注目，有时还让她看得挪不开眼。他在她们家住了两年，账目按期付清，从不发问，也从不解释。

玛吉猜不透他是干什么营生的。不管操的是哪一行，进项显然丰厚。不止一次，她见他毫不炫耀地露出一大卷钞票，那卷钞票的厚度仿佛从不增减。他说话常常文理不通，却字斟句酌、慢条斯理，叫人只当他必定说得在理。他颇有几分大人物的气派，听埃勒斯利太太母女俩说话时，那份专注未免郑重得过分。玛吉觉得，这么一个上了年纪的男人——他准有四十上下了！——倒挺有意思。不过她也有点怕他，心里认定：谁要是碰巧惹恼了他，日子怕是不会好过。

有一回他在街上碰见西尔维娅和玛吉，提出带她们去看日场戏。他的心思明明在玛吉身上，却也有礼有节地捎上了她的朋友。西尔维娅后来把这事告诉了菲利普。

「我希望你们没去。」他立刻答道。

===Original===
"No, I didn't, Maggie didn't, either. But there's no reason why I shouldn't have. She goes with him sometimes."

"But that's different. Maggie's known different people from any you've ever known. She can take care of herself."

"What's the matter?" Joanna asked, putting her head in the door. "What's old Phil so excited about?"

"You might just as well hear this, too, Jan. I won't have you and Sylvia going about with a man like Henderson Neal. Maggie can go with men that my sisters can't afford to associate with."

===Chinese===
「是没去，玛吉也没去。可我要是去了，也没什么不该的。她有时就跟他出门。」

「不过那不一样。玛吉见识过的人，跟你见识过的全然不同。她能照顾好自己。」

「怎么回事？」乔安娜把头探进门来问道。「老菲尔这么激动，是为了什么呀？」

「小乔，这事你也不妨听听。我不能让你和西尔维娅跟着亨德森·尼尔这样的男人到处走。玛吉能交往的男人，是我妹妹们交往不起的。」

## V / 五

===Original===
It may seem a cold-blooded thing to say, but the dying of Meriwether Bye was about the best thing he could have done for his son, Peter. Certainly that was what Miss Susan Graves thought as she viewed rather grimly the small and motley collection of belongings which Peter transferred to her home in his little express wagon from his father’s former landlady, Mrs. Reading. The collection consisted of a well-worn extra suit of clothes, another pair of shoes, some underwear in sad need of patching, some books chiefly on physiology and anatomy, the Bye Family Bible, a little old black testament, and a box of letters. There was also a big railroad map which Peter lugged along under his arm and from which he stubbornly refused to be parted. Meriwether, in his brighter moods, used to refer to his “runs” as “business-trips” and would point out to Peter just where he would go on such and such a date. The boy learned a lot of geography in this way, and was talking to his playmates about Duluth and Jacksonville, Sacramento and Denver, before most of them knew that they personally were living in the country’s metropolis.

The books on medicine and anatomy had been well thumbed by Peter, too. Meriwether had received them from old Isaiah, his father, and had carried them around on his runs to impress his co-workers in the Pullman service.

Later he got into the habit of reading from them to Peter who always listened in the grave silence which he usually reserved for his father’s effusions. For some reason the little boy’s brain retained the various and amazing things which his father read to him from the dry old books. Long before he knew his multiplication tables he knew the names of the principal bones of the body and the course of the food. In fact these books were his first readers, for Meriwether, more interested in this dry stuff, now that it was too late to profit him anything, taught his boy how to pronounce the difficult names, so that the latter could read to him. Perhaps the poor fellow, dissolute and weak failure though he was, thought that some of the old “greatness” might still accrue to him by this fiction of studying at medicine.

===Chinese===
这话听来未免冷酷，可梅里韦瑟·拜一命归西，对他儿子彼得来说，差不多算得上他此生做过的最有益的一件事了。苏珊·格雷夫斯小姐（Susan Graves）心里肯定正是这么想的：她神情颇为严峻地望着那一小堆五花八门的家当——彼得用他那辆小小的运货板车，把它们从亡父昔日的房东雷丁太太（Mrs. Reading）那里拉到了她家。那堆东西计有：一套穿得半旧的换洗衣裳，又一双鞋，几件亟待缝补的内衣，若干书籍——多半是生理学和解剖学的——拜家的家传《圣经》，一本黑色小《新约》，还有一匣信件。此外还有一张大大的铁路地图，是彼得夹在腋下一路拖来的，他死活不肯同它分开。梅里韦瑟兴致高的时候，总把自己的一次次「跑车」称作「出差」，还会向彼得一一指点：某月某日他要到哪些地方去。这孩子就这样学得了不少地理，早在多数小伙伴还没弄明白自己就住在全国第一大都会的时候，他已经在跟玩伴们大谈德卢斯（Duluth）和杰克逊维尔（Jacksonville）、萨克拉门托（Sacramento）和丹佛（Denver）了。

那些医学与解剖学的书，彼得也翻得卷了边。这些书原是梅里韦瑟从父亲老艾赛亚手里接过来的，跑车时随身携带，好在普尔曼（Pullman）卧铺列车上的同事面前充一充场面。

后来他养成了念这些书给彼得听的习惯。彼得总是一脸凝重的沉默听着——那是他一贯留给父亲高谈阔论的礼遇。不知怎的，父亲从那些干巴巴的旧书里念出的种种奇奇怪怪的东西，小家伙的脑袋竟都留住了。他还没背会乘法口诀，就已经晓得人体主要骨骼的名称，晓得食物下肚后走的路径。其实，这些书就是他的启蒙读本——梅里韦瑟如今再学这些干巴巴的学问已无从得益，反倒来了兴致，教会孩子那些难词的读音，好让孩子念给他听。这个可怜的家伙虽然放浪形骸、软弱潦倒，或许还想着，借这番研习医学的假戏，分沾几分旧日的「荣光」。

===Original===
The Bible was the one thing that Peter knew least about. He looked into it once or twice and hitting on Isaiah Bye’s tragically proud inscription: “By *his* fruits ye shall know﻿—*me*,” spelled it out laboriously﻿—he always had trouble in reading script﻿—and asked his father with some natural perplexity what it meant. But Meriwether snatched the book away from him with such a black look and took such pains to put it out of his reach, that Peter for a long time thought the Bible, or at any rate that inscription, must be something decidedly off color. He waited until his father had gone on his next “business-trip” before investigating again, but finding the book nowhere as exciting as his beloved Anatomy, he gave up the puzzle and attributed his father’s defection to the inscrutable whims and vagaries of the genus called parents. He valued that old Bible the least of all his possessions. That was the bitterest day of his life when he found out what it ought to mean to him.

Miss Susan, though not an “old Philadelphian” herself, knew something of colored Philadelphia’s pride in the possession of family and tradition. She would have been glad of course if Meriwether Bye had left Peter some money. But of the two she would very much rather have had the Bible with its absolute assurance of the former standing and respectability of the black Byes. She had a family tradition of her own, for she was a member of the Graves family of Gravestown, New Jersey, a clan well known to colored people not only in that vicinity, but also throughout Pennsylvania.

The story is that two white sisters in the middle of the eighteenth century fell in love with two of their father’s black slaves.
