The Negroes may have been African Princes for all anyone knows to the contrary. Since nothing they could do or say would win their father’s consent to such a union, the girls ran away with their lovers, and married them, with or without benefit of clergy it is impossible to relate. Nature and God alike, instead of being disconcerted at this utter contravention of the laws of man, presented each couple with numerous children. When these reached mating age, finding themselves out of favor with both black and white of their community, the cousins solved the problem by marrying each other. The children of each generation did the same, whether driven to it by like necessity or not, history does not say. But by the time the next brood appeared a precedent had been established, and Graves married Graves not only as a matter of course, but as a matter of pride. They were able to do this, being automatically rendered free by the fact that a white woman had married a black man.

===Chinese===
彼得最摸不着头脑的，偏偏是那部《圣经》。他翻开过一两回，撞上了艾赛亚·拜那句悲怆而高傲的题词：「凭着*他*的果子，就可以认出——*我*。」他费劲地逐字拼读出来——他认手写体向来吃力——又带着几分自然而然的困惑，问父亲这话是什么意思。可梅里韦瑟黑着脸一把将书夺了过去，又千方百计把它放到他够不着的地方，以致彼得在很长一段日子里都以为，那部《圣经》，至少那句题词，准是样很不体面的东西。他一直等到父亲再度「出差」上路，才重新查究起来，可是发现这本书远不如他心爱的《解剖学》来得带劲，便丢下了这个谜，把他父亲的这一反常，归因于名叫「父母」的那一族生灵莫测高深的心性与反复无常。在他全部的家当里，那部旧《圣经》是他最不放在眼里的。等到他弄清这部书本该对他意味着什么的那一天，成了他一生中最苦的日子。

苏珊小姐自己虽算不得「老费城人」，却也深知费城的有色人种是怎样以家世与门风自矜。梅里韦瑟·拜若能给彼得留下几个钱，她当然求之不得；可两样之中，她宁可要那部《圣经》——它确凿无疑地担保着黑人拜家昔日的门第与体面。她自己也有家世可恃，因为她出身新泽西州格雷夫斯敦（Gravestown, New Jersey）的格雷夫斯家——这一族不仅闻名于左近一带的有色人种，而且声名遍及整个宾夕法尼亚。

传说是这样的：十八世纪中叶，有两位白人姐妹，同她们父亲的两名黑奴相爱了。谁也拿不出半点反证，说不定那两个黑人还是非洲的王子呢。两个姑娘做什么、说什么都无法换得父亲对这桩姻缘的首肯，索性跟着心上人私奔了；至于成婚时究竟有没有牧师证婚，就无从稽考了。造化与上帝对于这桩全然违逆人间法度的结合非但毫无愠色，反倒赐给每对夫妻一大群儿女。待这些儿女长到婚嫁之龄，发现自己在本地的黑人和白人两头都不受待见，这些表亲便索性彼此通婚，把难题解决了。往后每一代子孙照此办理——是出于同样的迫不得已，还是出于自愿，史无可考。不过等再一代人丁出世时，先例已经立定：格雷夫斯家嫁格雷夫斯家，不但顺理成章，更引以为荣。他们之所以能这样行事，靠的是这一点：白人女子既嫁了黑人，生下的儿女便自动是自由之身。

===Original===
Miss Susan Graves had not married for the simple and sufficient reason that in her day there were not enough male Graves to go around. She would as soon have thought of marrying outside her family as a Spanish grandee would have thought of marrying an English cockney. In those days the position of old maid had its decided disadvantages﻿—few people if any gave her the benefit of the doubt that she might have remained single from choice. Yet Miss Susan Graves, in spite of three other offers, soared on family pride above all this and made her career that of housekeeper for the family of a wealthy merchant on Girard Avenue, in Philadelphia. (You must marry a Graves, but obviously you obtained work where you could find it.)

There was a younger sister, Alice Graves, not as direct in purpose as Susan, yet in some respects curiously strong. She had always considered the Graves’ tradition silly: it was so unexciting marrying someone whom you had known and seen all your life. What was marriage for if not for a change?

When the oldest son of Merchant Sharples of Girard Avenue married and went to New York, Susan Graves went along as housekeeper. And thither Alice Graves followed shortly to do sewing for that intricate but orderly household. Meriwether Bye, who had known both ladies in Philadelphia﻿—for Miss Susan Bye was a frequent visitor both at his father’s and his Uncle Peter’s house﻿—came to see them in his rare fits of loneliness, and between runs courted Alice Graves in Central Park. Of course it would have been better if Alice could have married a Graves, but Susan resigned herself easily to the matter﻿—for Bye belonged to old stock and must, she thought, make good eventually. But she developed a strong dislike for him before his death, and took Peter not only for his mother’s sake but also to dispel if possible his father’s doubtless harmful influence.

===Chinese===
苏珊·格雷夫斯小姐终身未嫁，理由简单而充分：在她那个年代，格雷夫斯家的男人不够分配。要她嫁到外姓人家去，就好比要一位西班牙大贵族去娶个伦敦东头的市井姑娘——想都别想。在那个年头，做老姑娘有着明摆着的坏处——几乎没有人肯发善心，往好处想她是自愿独身。然而苏珊·格雷夫斯小姐尽管另有三桩提亲，仍凭着家族的骄傲凌驾于这一切之上，为自己择定的前程，是到费城吉拉德大道（Girard Avenue）一位富商家里当女管家。（夫婿嘛，非格雷夫斯家的人不嫁；可饭碗嘛，显然是有哪儿找得着就上哪儿。）

她还有个妹妹，艾丽斯·格雷夫斯（Alice Graves），行事不像苏珊那样目标明确，可在某些方面却出奇地强硬。她一向认为格雷夫斯家的传统蠢透了：嫁给一个打小认得、看了一辈子的人，多没意思。若不是图个改变，结婚还有什么意思？

后来，吉拉德大道的富商夏普尔斯（Sharples）家的大少爷娶亲迁居纽约，苏珊·格雷夫斯便以女管家的身份随同前往。不多时，艾丽斯·格雷夫斯也跟了过去，替那户人情纷纭却井然有序的人家做针线活。梅里韦瑟·拜在费城就认识这姐妹俩——因为苏珊·格雷夫斯小姐当年是他父亲和他叔父彼得两家的常客——如今在他难得袭来的孤寂里，便来看望她们，并在一次次的跑车之间，到中央公园（Central Park）向艾丽斯·格雷夫斯大献殷勤。艾丽斯若是能嫁给一个格雷夫斯家的人，自然再好不过，但苏珊对这桩事倒也轻易想开了——拜家毕竟是老根底的人家，她想，他终归会有出息的。可是到他去世之前，她已对他生出深深的厌恶；她收留彼得，一半是看在他母亲的份上，一半也是想尽可能驱散他父亲那毋庸置疑的有害影响。

===Original===
Peter was a surprise to his aunt. She found him kind but thoughtless, industrious on occasions but unspeakably shiftless, not too proud, not very grateful and with no sense of responsibility. His father of course spoke there. Yet the boy was indubitably charming, never complained, and usually did as he was told. Miss Susan found herself between two minds﻿—she had an impulse to work her fingers to the bone and thus spare Alice’s beautiful son the tussle with poverty which he must know, and again a desire to speak and act forcibly and drive him into an acknowledgment of what her loyalty to her sister was leading her to do for a homeless, friendless lad. Actually she struck a medium, made him keep clean, insisted on his regular attendance at school, took him to Sunday-School and Church entertainments and induced him to work on Saturdays and holidays by refusing pocket-money to “a boy as big as you.”

She could not understand why he chose a job in a butcher’s shop. Doubtless Peter hardly knew himself. “I like to watch the man saw the bones,” he would have said vaguely. “I can do it, too. I can cut up a chicken or a rabbit just as neatly!”

===Chinese===
彼得让这位姨妈大出所料。她发现这孩子心善却不用心思，偶尔勤快，却懒散得没治，架子不算大，也不大懂得感恩，全无责任感——这上头，自然是他父亲在说话。然而这孩子又有着无可置疑的可爱之处：从不抱怨，叫他做什么，多半也就做了。苏珊小姐发现自己左右为难——她一时恨不得累断十指地做工，好让艾丽斯这个俊美的儿子免于他势必要经历的那场与贫穷的缠斗；一时又想疾言厉色地逼他认清：她正是出于对妹妹的一片忠心，才为一个无家可归、无亲无故的孩子做下这一切。实际上她取了个中道：逼他拾掇得干干净净，坚持要他按时上学，带他去主日学校和教会的游艺会，还拿对「像你这么大个儿的孩子」一概不给零花钱的办法，劝得他星期六和节假日都出去做工。

她弄不懂他干吗偏挑了家肉铺去做工。恐怕彼得自己也未必说得清。「我爱看人家锯骨头，」他多半会含含糊糊地这么说。「这我也会。一只鸡、一只兔，我能剖得一样利索！」

## VI / 六

===Original===
It was Joanna who first acquainted Peter with himself. But neither of the children knew this at the time. And although Peter came to realize it later it was many years before he told her so. For, though he went through many changes and though these two came to speak of many things, he kept a certain inarticulateness all his lifetime.

Joanna and all the older Marshalls went to a school in West Fifty-second Street, one after another like little steps, with Joanna at first quite some distance behind. They were known throughout the school. “Those Marshall children, you know those colored children that always dress so well and as though they had someone to take care of them. Pretty nice looking children, too, if only they weren’t colored. Their father is a caterer, has that place over there on Fifty-ninth Street. Makes a lot of money for a colored man.”

Peter, unlike Joanna, had gone to school, one might almost say, all over New York, and nowhere for any great length of time. Meriwether had stayed longest at Mrs. Reading’s but as, in later years, he more and more went off on his runs without paying his bills, Mrs. Reading frequently refused to let Peter leave the house until his father’s return.

===Chinese===
最先让彼得认识他自己的，是乔安娜。不过当时两个孩子谁也不知道这一点。虽然彼得后来明白了过来，却也隔了许多年才对她提起。因为，尽管他一生经历过许多变化，尽管这两人后来无话不谈，他到老都保持着某种讷于言辞的沉默。

乔安娜和马歇尔家所有比她年长的孩子，一个接一个地进了西五十二街（West Fifty-second Street）上的一所学校，像一级级小小的台阶，起初乔安娜落在后面老远一段。全校都认得他们。「就是马歇尔家那几个孩子——你知道吧，就是那几个有色人种的孩子，穿得总是那么齐整，一看就像有人悉心照料似的。孩子长得也挺体面，要不是有色人种就好了。他们父亲是承办酒席的，五十九街上那家铺子就是他开的。就一个有色人来说，钱挣得可真不少。」

彼得和乔安娜不一样，他上过的学校几乎可以说遍布整个纽约，哪一所也没待长久。梅里韦瑟在雷丁太太（Mrs. Reading）的寄宿处住得最久；可后来那几年，他越来越多地出门跑活儿、欠账不结，雷丁太太便常常不许彼得出屋，非等到他父亲回来不可。

===Original===
“For all I know he may be joinin’ his father on the outside and the two of them go off together. Then where’d I be? For them few rags that Mr. Bye keeps in his room wouldn’t be no good to nobody.”

This enforced truancy was the least of Peter’s troubles. He did not like school—too many white people and consequently, as he saw it, too much chance for petty injustice. The result of this was that Peter at twelve, possessed it is true of a large assortment of really useful facts, lacked the fine precision, if the doubtful usefulness, of Joanna’s knowledge at ten. When Miss Susan settled in the Marshalls’ neighborhood and brought Peter to the school in Fifty-second Street he was found to be lacking and yet curiously in advance. “We’ll try him,” said the principal doubtfully, “in the fifth grade. I’ll take him to Miss Shanley’s room.”

Miss Shanley was Joanna’s teacher. She greeted Peter without enthusiasm, not because he was colored but because he was clearly a problem. Joanna spied him immediately. He was too handsome with his brown-red skin, his black silky hair that curled alluringly, his dark, almost almond-shaped eyes, to escape her notice. But she forgot about him, too, almost immediately, for the first time Miss Shanley called on him he failed rather ignominiously. Joanna did not like stupid people and thereafter to her he simply was not.

===Chinese===
「谁知道呢，他没准儿就在外头跟他爹碰了头，爷儿俩搭伙一块儿跑了。到那时候我可怎么办？拜先生搁在屋里那几件破衣裳，抵给谁也顶不了账呀。」

这种被迫的逃学，在彼得的种种烦恼里还算最轻的一桩。他本就不喜欢上学——白人太多，因而在他看来，鸡毛蒜皮的不公平也就太多。结果是：彼得十二岁上，肚子里固然装着一大堆真正顶用的见识，却缺少乔安娜十岁时的功课那种精细严整——虽说那种功课究竟有多大用处，还在两可之间。待到苏珊小姐（Miss Susan）在马歇尔家一带安顿下来，把彼得送进五十二街那所学校，人们发现他底子有欠缺，却又奇怪地处处超前。「那就让他试试，」校长将信将疑地说，「进五年级吧。我带他去尚利小姐（Miss Shanley）的班上。」

尚利小姐是乔安娜的老师。她迎见彼得时并不热络，倒不是因为他是有色人种的孩子，而是因为他一望而知就是个麻烦。乔安娜一眼就瞥见了他。红棕色的皮肤，乌黑柔亮、鬈得诱人的头发，黑黑的、近乎杏形的眼睛——他实在太俊，想躲过她的眼睛也难。可她几乎同样立刻就把他忘了，因为尚利小姐头一回叫他答问，他就败得相当不体面。乔安娜不喜欢笨人，打那以后，他在她眼里干脆就不存在。

===Original===
On the contrary, Joanna had caught and retained Peter’s attention. She was the only other colored person in the room and therefore to him the only one worth considering. And though at that time Joanna was still rather plain, she already had an air. Everything about her was of an exquisite perfection. Her hair was brushed till it shone, her skin glowed not only with health but obviously with cleanliness, her shoes were brown and shiny, with perfectly level heels. She wore that first week a very fine soft sage-green middy suit with a wide buff tie. The nails which finished off the rather square-tipped fingers of her small square hands, were even and rounded and shining. Peter had seen little girls with this perfection and assurance on Chestnut Street in Philadelphia and on Fifth Avenue in New York, but they had been white. He had not yet envisaged this sort of thing for his own. Perhaps he inherited his great-grandfather Joshua’s spiritless acceptance of things as they are, and his belief that differences between people were not made, but had to be.

Joanna clearly stood for something in the class. Peter noted a little enviously the quality of the tone in which Miss Shanley addressed her. To other children she said, “Gertrude, can you tell me about the Articles of Confederation?” Usually she implied a doubt, which Gertrude usually justified. But she was sure of Joanna. The tenseness of her attitude might be seen to relax; her mentality prepared momentarily for a rest. “Joanna will now tell us—” she would announce. For Joanna, having a purpose and having been drilled by Joel to the effect that final perfection is built on small intermediate perfections, got her lessons completely and in detail every day.

It was at this time and for many years thereafter characteristic of Peter that he, too, wanted to shine, but did not realize that one shone only as a result of much mental polishing personally applied. Joanna’s assurance, her air of purposefulness, her indifference intrigued him and piqued him. He sidled across to the blackboard nearest her—if they were both sent to the board—cleaned hers off if she gave him a chance, managed to speak a word to her now and then. He even contrived to wait for her one day at the Girls’ entrance. Joanna threw him a glance of recognition, swept by, returned.

===Chinese===
反倒是乔安娜，抓住并留住了彼得的注意。她是教室里除他之外唯一的有色人种，因此在彼得眼里，也是唯一值得留意的人。虽说那时乔安娜模样还相当平常，却已自有一股气度。她浑身上下透着一种精致的完美：头发梳得油光水滑，肌肤不仅焕发着健康，更一望而知洁净无尘；棕色皮鞋锃亮，两只鞋跟平平正正、一般齐高。开学头一个礼拜，她穿一身细软的鼠尾草绿水手服套装，系一条宽宽的浅黄领巾。那双方方正正的小手，指尖也是方方的，指甲修得匀净、圆润、发亮。这样的完美与自信，彼得在费城的栗树街（Chestnut Street）上、在纽约的第五大道（Fifth Avenue）上都见过，可那些小姑娘都是白人。他还从不曾把这种气派同自己人联系在一起。也许，他继承了曾祖父约书亚的脾性——对世事一概逆来顺受，认定人与人的差别不是谁造出来的，而是生来如此、非有不可。

乔安娜在班里显然占着一种分量。彼得略带妒意地留意到，尚利小姐对她说话时口气便不一样。对别的孩子，她是这样问的：「格特鲁德（Gertrude），你能给我讲讲《邦联条例》吗？」话音里通常暗含着疑虑，而格特鲁德也多半把这疑虑坐实。可她对乔安娜有十足的把握。她那紧绷的神情会眼看松弛下来，头脑暂且歇上一口气。「下面由乔安娜来讲——」她会这样宣布。因为乔安娜是有志向的人，又受过乔尔的反复调教，懂得最终的完美是由一桩桩细小的完美垒起来的道理，所以每天的功课都准备得完完整整、细细致致。

从这个时候起，往后许多年，彼得都有一个特点：他也想出挑，却不明白人只有靠自己下苦功把头脑打磨又打磨，才能放出光来。乔安娜的自信、她那股目标明确的神气、她的淡漠，既让他着迷，又让他气恼。两人都给派到黑板跟前去的时候，他就侧着身子蹭到离她最近的那块黑板；她若给他机会，他就替她把黑板擦干净，还不时设法搭上句话。有一天，他甚至设法在女生入口外等她。乔安娜瞥了他一眼算是认出了他，一阵风似的走过，又折了回来。

===Original===
His heart jumped within him.

“If you see my sister Sylvia—you know her?—tell her not to wait for me. I have to go early to my music-lesson. She’ll be right out.”

Sylvia didn’t appear for half an hour and Peter should have been at the butcher’s, but he waited. Sylvia and Maggie Ellersley came out laughing and glowing. Peter gave the message.

“Thanks,” said Sylvia prettily. Maggie stared after him. She was still the least bit bold in those days.

“Ain’t he the best looker you ever saw, Sylvia? Such eyes! Who is he, anyway? Not ever Joanna’s beau?”

“Imagine old Joanna with a beau.” Sylvia laughed. “He’s just a new boy in her class. He *is* good looking.”

===Chinese===
他的心在胸腔里猛地一跳。

「要是你看见我姐姐西尔维娅——你认识她吧？——告诉她别等我。我得早点儿去上音乐课。她马上就出来。」

西尔维娅过了半个钟头才露面，而彼得本该早到肉铺去了，可他还是等着。西尔维娅和玛吉·埃勒斯利说说笑笑、红光满面地走了出来。彼得把话带到。

「谢谢。」西尔维娅漂漂亮亮地道了声谢。玛吉却目送着他的背影。那时候，她身上还带着一点儿大胆劲儿。

「他是不是你一辈子见过的最俊的一个，西尔维娅？那双眼睛！他到底是谁呀？总不会是乔安娜的相好吧？」

「想想乔安娜那丫头也会有相好。」西尔维娅笑了。「不过是她班上新来的一个男生。人*确实*长得俊。」

===Original===
Some important examinations were to take place shortly and Miss Shanley planned extensive reviews. She was a thorough if somewhat unimaginative teacher and she meant to have no loose threads. So she devoted two days to geography, two more to grammar, another to history, one to the rather puzzling consideration of that mysterious study, physiology. Perhaps by now the class was a bit fed up with cramming, perhaps the children weren’t really interested in physiological processes. Joanna wasn’t, but she always got lessons like these doggedly, thinking “Soon we’ll be past all this,” or “I’m going to forget this old stuff as soon as I grow up.” Poor Miss Shanley was in despair. She could not call on Joanna for everything. Pupil after pupil had failed. Her eye roved over the room and fell on Peter’s black head.

She sighed. He had not even been a member of the class when she had taught this particular physiological phenomenon. “Can’t anyone besides Joanna Marshall give me the ‘Course of the Food?’ ”

Peter raised his hand. “He looks intelligent,” she thought. “Well, Bye you may try it.”

“I don’t think I can give it to you the way the others say it,”—the children had been reciting by rote, “but I know what happens to the food.”

She knew he would fail if he didn’t know it her way, but she let him begin.

===Chinese===
不久就要举行几场重要考试，尚利小姐筹划了大面积的复习。她是个一丝不苟、只是略欠想象力的老师，打定主意不留下一条没缝死的线头。于是地理花两天，语法再花两天，历史又一天，还有一天用来费劲地琢磨那门神秘的学问——生理学。也许到这时全班对填鸭式的死记硬背已经有点倒了胃口，也许孩子们对生理过程压根儿不感兴趣。乔安娜就不感兴趣，可这类功课她总是一门死功夫啃下来，心里想着「这些咱们很快就熬过去了」，或者「等我长大，这些老古董马上就忘个精光」。可怜的尚利小姐绝望了。她总不能事事都点乔安娜。一个学生接一个学生地答砸了。她的目光扫过教室，落在彼得那颗黑脑袋上。

她叹了口气。讲这个生理现象的时候，他甚至还没进这个班呢。「除了乔安娜·马歇尔，就没人能给我讲讲『食物的通路』了吗？」

彼得举起了手。「他看着还算聪明。」她想。「好吧，拜，你来试试。」

「我想，我没法照别的同学那个说法讲给你听」——孩子们一直是在死背的——「可我知道吃下去的东西是怎回事。」

她明白，若不照她那一套来讲，他准得砸锅，可她还是让他开了头。

===Original===
This was old ground for Peter. “Look, I can draw it. See, you take the food in your mouth,” he drew a rough sketch of lips, mouth cavity and gullet, “then you must chew it, masticate, I think you said.” He went on varying from his own simplified interpretation of Meriwether Bye’s early instructions, past difficult names like pancreatic juice and thoracic duct, and while he talked he drew, recalling pictures from those old anatomies; expounding, flourishing. Miss Shanley stared at him in amazement. This jewel, this undiscovered diamond!

“How’d you come to know it, Peter?”

“I read it, I studied it.” He did not say when. “But it’s so easy to learn things about the body. It’s yourself.”

She quizzed him then while the other children, Joanna among them, stared open-eyed. But he knew all the simple ground which she had already covered, and much, much beyond.

“If all the children,” said Miss Shanley, forgetting Peter’s past, “would just get their lessons like Peter Bye and Joanna Marshall.”

===Chinese===
这可是彼得烂熟于心的地界。「看，我能画出来。瞧，食物先进入嘴里」——他随手勾出嘴唇、口腔和食道的草图——「然后你得把它嚼碎，『咀嚼』——我记得您是这么说的。」他一路讲下去，跟梅里韦瑟·拜早年那套教导相比，自有他一番简化的理解；一路越过胰液、胸导管这些难念的名词，一面讲，一面画，把那些旧解剖学书里的图样凭记忆描出来，讲得滔滔，笔下生风。尚利小姐惊愕地盯着他。这块宝石，这颗未经识得的钻石！

「你怎么知道的，彼得？」

「我读过，也钻研过。」他没说是什么时候读的。「不过，学身体的事太容易了，那就是你自己呀。」

接着她盘问他，其余的孩子——乔安娜也在其中——都睁大了眼睛瞧着。她已经教过的那些浅显内容，他全知道，而且还远远、远远超出。

「要是所有的孩子」——尚利小姐把彼得的从前忘到了一边——「都能像彼得·拜和乔安娜·马歇尔这样把功课准备好，就好了。」

===Original===
She had coupled their names together! And after school Joanna was waiting for him. He walked up the street with her, pleasantly conscious of her interest, her frank admiration.

“How wonderful,” she breathed, “that you should know your physiology like that. What are you going to be when you grow up, a doctor?”

“A surgeon,” said Peter forgetting his old formula and expressing a resolve which her question had engendered in him just that second. He saw himself on the instant, a tall distinguished-looking man, wielding scissors and knife with deft nervous fingers. Joanna would be hovering somewhere—he was not sure how—in the offing. And she would be looking at him with this same admiration.

“My, won’t you have to study?” Joanna could have told an aspirant almost to the day and measure the amount of time and effort it would take him to become a surgeon, a dentist, a lawyer, an engineer. All these things Joel discussed about his table with the intense seriousness which colored men feel when they speak of their children’s futures. Alexander and Philip were to have their choice of any calling within reason. They were seventeen and fifteen now and the house swarmed with college catalogues. Schools, terms, degrees of prejudice, fields of practice—Joanna knew them all.

“Yes,” said Peter, “I suppose I will have to study. How did you come to know so much—did your father tell you?”

===Chinese===
她把两个人的名字连在了一起！放学以后，乔安娜果然在等他。他同她一道沿街走去，心里美滋滋地觉出她的兴致，她那坦率的钦慕。

「真了不起，」她轻声赞叹，「你的生理学得这么好。你长大了打算做什么，当医生吗？」

「当外科医生。」彼得说，忘了自己从前的老套话，说出的正是她这一问在这一瞬间催生于他心中的决心。他登时看见了自己：一个高大出众的男子，用灵巧有力的手指操着剪子与手术刀。乔安娜会在不远的地方徘徊——怎么个徘徊法，他还说不准——而她会用此刻这样的钦慕望着他。

「哎呀，那你可得下多少功夫学啊！」对于一个有志向的人，乔安娜几乎能掐算到日子，说出他成为外科医生、牙医、律师或工程师要花多少时间、多少气力。这些事，乔尔都在自家饭桌上认认真真地议论过，那份郑重劲儿，正是有色人种的父亲们谈起子女前途时共有的心情。亚历山大和菲利普将来可以在情理之中任选职业。他们一个十七、一个十五，家里各大学的招生简章已经堆得满屋都是。学校啦，学制啦，偏见深浅的程度啦，开业行医的天地啦——乔安娜无一不了然于心。

「是啊，」彼得说，「我想我是得用功念书。你怎么懂得这么多——是你爸爸告诉你的？」

===Original===
“Why, I get it out of books, of course.” Joanna was highly indignant: “I never go to bed without getting my lessons. In fact, all I do is to get lessons of some kind—school lessons or music. You know I’m to be a great singer.”

“No, I didn’t know that. Perhaps you’ll sing in your choir?”

Then Joanna astonished him. “In my choir—I sing there already! No! Everywhere, anywhere, Carnegie Hall and in Boston and London. You see, I’m to be famous.”

“But,” Peter objected, “colored people don’t get any chance at that kind of thing.”

“Colored people,” Joanna quoted from her extensive reading, “can do everything that anybody else can do. They’ve already done it. Some one colored person somewhere in the world does as good a job as anyone else—perhaps a better one. They’ve been kings and queens and poets and teachers and doctors and everything. I’m going to be the one colored person who sings best in these days, and I never, never, never mean to let color interfere with anything I *really* want to do.”

===Chinese===
「哪里，当然是我从书上看来的。」乔安娜一脸的愤愤然：「我每天不做完功课从不上床睡觉。说真的，我干的就只有做功课——不是学校的功课，就是音乐。你知道的，我要当个大歌唱家。」

「这我倒不知道。也许你会在你们的唱诗班唱歌吧？」

这一下乔安娜让他大吃一惊。「在唱诗班唱——我早就在那儿唱了！不！要到处唱，哪儿都唱，卡内基音乐厅，还有波士顿，还有伦敦。你瞧着吧，我是要成名的。」

「可是，」彼得反驳道，「干那一行，有色人种是捞不着机会的。」

「有色人种，」乔安娜搬出了她博览群书得来的道理，「别人能做的事，他们样样都能做。而且都已经做到了。世界上总会有某一个有色人种的人，在哪一行上干得不比任何人差——说不定还更好。他们当过国王和王后，当过诗人、教师、医生，什么都当过。我要做当今时代里歌唱得最好的那一个有色人，而且我永远、永远、永远不让肤色妨碍我去做任何我*真正*想做的事。」

===Original===
“I dance, too,” she interrupted herself, “and I’ll probably do that besides. Not ordinary dancing, you know, but queer beautiful things that are different from what we see around here; perhaps I’ll make them up myself. You’ll see! They’ll have on the billboard, ‘Joanna Marshall, the famous artist,’﻿—” She was almost dancing along the sidewalk now, her eyes and cheeks glowing.

Peter looked at her wistfully. His practical experience and the memory of his father inclined him to dubiousness. But her superb assurance carried away all his doubts.

“I don’t suppose you’ll ever think of just ordinary people like me?”

“But you’ll be famous, too—you’ll be a wonderful doctor. Do be. I can’t stand stupid, common people.”

“You’ll always be able to stand me,” said Peter with a fervor which made his statement a vow.

===Chinese===
「我还跳舞呢，」她打断了自己的话，「往后八成也要走这一条。你知道，不是普普通通的跳舞，是些奇异的、美的东西，跟我们这一带见到的都不一样；说不定我自己来编。你瞧着吧！到那时候招贴上会写着：『乔安娜·马歇尔，著名艺术家』——」这会儿她简直是在人行道上舞着走了，眼睛发亮，双颊绯红。

彼得怅然地望着她。他自己的实际阅历，还有对父亲的记忆，都让他倾向于怀疑。可她那股超凡的自信，把他的疑虑一扫而空。

「像我这号普普通通的人，你大概永远不会放在心上吧？」

「可你也会成名的呀——你会是个了不起的医生。一定要成为啊。又蠢又俗的人我可爱不了。」

「那你永远都受得了我。」彼得说，那股热切劲儿，让这句话成了一句誓言。

## VII / 七

===Original===
Sylvia and Joanna, walking through Sixty-third Street on an errand for their mother, came upon groups of children playing games. Italians, Jews, colored Americans, white Americans were there disporting themselves with more or less abandon, according to their peculiar temperament.

“Look,” said Joanna suddenly, catching at Sylvia’s hand. “See those children dancing! Wait, I’ve got to see that!”

Out in the middle of the street a band of colored children were dancing and acting a game. With no thought of spectators they joined hands, took a few steps, separated, spun around, smote hands sharply, and then flung them above their heads. One girl stood in the middle, singing too, but with an attentive air. Presently she darted forward, seized a member of the ring:

> “Say, little Missy, won’t you marry me?”

===Chinese===
西尔维娅和乔安娜替母亲出门办事，正走过六十三街（Sixty-third Street），迎面遇见一群群做游戏的孩子。意大利人的孩子、犹太人的孩子、有色人种的孩子、白人的孩子，都在那里嬉戏耍闹，各按各的脾性，撒欢儿的程度也各有深浅。

「看哪，」乔安娜忽然叫了一声，一把抓住西尔维娅的手，「看那些孩子跳舞！等等，我得瞧瞧这个！」

街道正中，一伙有色人种的孩子正连唱带跳地做一个游戏。他们全不在乎有没有人看：手拉手走上几步，散开，打个转，啪地击一记响掌，随即双臂高高扬过头顶。一个姑娘站在当中，也唱着，神情却很专注。忽然她一个箭步窜上前，一把抓住圈上的一个人：

> 「喂，小姑娘，你肯不肯嫁给我呀？」

===Original===
Their voices were treble and sweet, though shrill, and rang with a peculiar, piercing quality above the street noises and the sounds of the other children’s games. The little players were absorbed, enraptured with the spirit of the dance and the abandon of the music. Joanna, too, was in a transport. She watched them going through the motions several times. Presently she caught all the words:

> “Sissy in the barn, join in the weddin’,
 

 Sissy in the barn, join in the weddin’.”

===Chinese===
她们清亮的童声又尖又甜，带着一种特别的穿透力，压过街上的嘈杂和其他孩子游戏的声响。小小的玩角们全副身心都沉浸其中，被舞蹈的兴致和音乐的狂欢迷住了。乔安娜也心醉神迷。她看着他们把整套动作做了好几遍，不多时便把歌词全都听全了：

> 「谷仓里的小妞儿，来呀来参加婚礼，
 

 谷仓里的小妞儿，来呀来参加婚礼。」

===Original===
The child in the center here chose a partner. The others sang:

> “Sweetest l’il couple I ever did see.
 

 Barn! Barn!”

They stamped here.

> “Arms all ’round me!
 

 Barn!”

===Chinese===
唱到这里，当中的孩子挑了一个舞伴。其余的孩子唱道：

> 「多好的一对小乖乖，我从没见过这么般配的。
 

 谷仓！谷仓！」

唱到这儿，孩子们跺了跺脚。

> 「胳膊都来搂住我！
 

 谷仓！」

===Original===
The two children in the center embraced each other while the rest sang:

> “Say, little Missy, won’t you marry me?”

Then the two in the center pointed fingers at each other, shrilling:

> “Stay back, girl, don’t you come near me
 

 All them sassy words you say!”

===Chinese===
当中的两个孩子相互搂住，其余的孩子唱道：

> 「喂，小姑娘，你肯不肯嫁给我呀？」

接着，当中的两个彼此用手指点着对方，尖声唱：

> 「站开点，丫头，别往我跟前凑

 你那满嘴的冲话少来说！」

===Original===
Then all:

> “Oh, Barn! Barn!
 

 Arms all ’round me!
 

 Say, little Missy, won’t you marry me?
 

 Marry me?”

The last line came as a faint echo.

===Chinese===
随后大家齐唱：

> 「哦，谷仓！谷仓！
 

 胳膊都来搂住我！
 

 喂，小姑娘，你肯不肯嫁给我呀？

 嫁给我呀？」

最后一句唱出来，像一声淡淡的回音。

===Original===
Joanna rushed forward: “I can play it! Girls let me play it, too!”

The children stared at her a moment, then, with the instinct of childhood for a kindred spirit, two of them unclasped hands and took Joanna in. She outdid them all in the fervor and grace of her acting. Two white settlement workers stopped and looked at her.

“Come on, Joanna,” Sylvia called impatiently.

Joanna came running, a string of the children after her. She bade them goodbye. “I must go now, but I’m coming back sometime soon, to learn some more.” She blew them a kiss, “goodbye, oh, goodbye!”

She came up to Sylvia flushed and excited. “We’ll play it home, Sylvia! Wasn’t it lovely and dear? Oh, I could dance like that forever!” She went almost all the entire remaining distance on tiptoe.

===Chinese===
乔安娜冲上前去：「我会玩这个！姑娘们，让我也来玩吧！」

孩子们怔怔地看了她片刻，随即凭着孩童认出同类的那种本能，有两个孩子松开手，把乔安娜接进了圈里。论表演的热切与身姿的美妙，她把所有人都比了下去。两位白人社区工作者停下脚步，望着她。

「走吧，乔安娜。」西尔维娅不耐烦地催她。

乔安娜跑回来，身后还跟着一串孩子。她同他们道别：「我现在得走了，不过过不了几天我还要再来的，再多学一点儿。」她给他们飞去一个吻，「再见啦，哦，再见啦！」

她跑到西尔维娅跟前，兴奋得满脸通红。「咱们回家也玩这个，西尔维娅！多可爱呀，你说是不是？哦，我真想这样跳一辈子！」剩下的一路，她几乎全是踮着脚尖走完的。

===Original===
Life in Joel Marshall’s house was not always a serious discussion of the Marshall children’s future. Like many of the better class of colored people, the Marshalls did not meet with the grosser forms of color prejudice, because they kept away from the places where it might be shown. This was bad from the standpoint of development of civic pride and interest. But it had its good results along another line. The children took most of their pleasures in their house or in those of their friends and devoted their wits and young originality to indoor pastimes.

The Marshall house was a great center for this kind of thing, and already Friday and Saturday nights were being regularly set apart for the children’s amusement and for the reception and entertainment of the various young people who dropped in.

Joanna taught her dance. Sylvia and Philip and Alexander were willing pupils; Joanna was magnetic when in this kind of mood. By the time Harry Portor and Maggie Ellersley arrived, they were all singing and stamping and twirling. Peter came in late, held up by the butcher. “Had to go on an errand for the grand white folks,” he explained briefly.

===Chinese===
乔尔·马歇尔家的生活，并不总是一本正经地讨论马歇尔家孩子们的前程。同许多有色人种中的上等人家一样，马歇尔一家人从不涉足可能显露歧视的场所，因此也就不曾领教过那类露骨的肤色偏见的厉害。从培养公民自豪感和公共意识来看，这固然是要不得的；可在另一方面却也不无好处。孩子们的乐子大多取自自己家里或朋友家里，把他们的机灵劲儿和少年的别出心裁，都投注在室内消遣上。

马歇尔家是这类活动的大本营，每逢星期五、星期六的晚上，已经照例专门腾出来给孩子们取乐，也用来接待、款待各色登门来访的年轻人。

乔安娜教她那个舞。西尔维娅、菲利普和亚历山大都是肯学的好学生；乔安娜一到这种兴头上，浑身都带着磁性。等哈里·波托尔（Harry Portor）和玛吉·埃勒斯利到来时，大伙儿已经又唱又跺脚又打转了。彼得来迟了，被屠户家给耽搁住了。「得给那些尊贵的大白人老爷们跑趟腿。」他三言两语地解释道。

===Original===
“You’ll wear out my carpet tonight for sure,” said Mrs. Marshall, but she loved the dancing as much as any of them, and got up and took a turn. Joanna taught the tune to Peter, who had a good ear, and he ran over to the old-fashioned square piano and rattled it off to a wild thumping accompaniment. When Brian Spencer came in, who even in those days was pretty sure to be where Sylvia was﻿—the fun was at its height. Peter, strumming a haunting, atavistic measure; Joanna, dancing like a faun, instructed Maggie Ellersley.

“Now, Maggie, dance up to one of them. All right, take Philip. You point your finger at him﻿—no both of you. Yes, you’re right, Peter. I forgot that. See, Phil, Peter’s learned it already. Here I’ll do it by myself; all of you stand back.”

She went through an elaborate pantomime, stretching out her hands as though clasping a partner on each side. She described an imaginary circle for the ring and ran into the midst of it. An imaginary partner was before her and she drew him in, pointed a slim, brown finger at him, rested both hands on her young hips, pirouetted, sang to him gayly:

“Stand back, boy, don’t you come near me!”

“My,” laughed Brian Spencer, clapping loudly. “Can’t you see it all just as plainly? Really, Jan, you ought to go on the stage as an impersonator, I don’t believe you could be beat.” He was a tall dark boy with fine proud features that looked chiseled. He and Alexander were home from college for the Easter vacation.

===Chinese===
「今晚我的地毯准要给你们踩烂了。」马歇尔太太嘴上这么说，心里爱这舞蹈却不亚于他们任何一个，于是也起身跳了一轮。乔安娜把那支曲子教给彼得——他耳朵灵——他便跑到那架老式方形钢琴前，噼里啪啦地弹奏起来，配着一阵狂野的重击节拍。等布莱恩·斯宾塞（Brian Spencer）进门时——哪怕在那些年头，西尔维娅在哪儿，他就准保在哪儿——热闹正到顶点：彼得弹着一支萦回不去、带着返祖气息的调子；乔安娜舞得像个小牧神，正指点玛吉·埃勒斯利。

「喏，玛吉，朝她们中间的一个舞过去。好，就找菲利普。你用手指着他——不，你们俩都要指。对，你说得对，彼得，这个我忘了。瞧见没有，菲尔，彼得已经学会了。来，我一个人做一遍给你们看；你们都退后。」

她做了一套繁复的哑剧动作：伸出双手，仿佛左右各挽着一位舞伴；又比划出一个想象中的圆圈当作圈子，自己跑进圈中央。一位想象中的舞伴立在她面前，她把他拉进来，用一根纤细的棕色手指点着他，双手叉在少女的腰胯上，踮脚打了个旋儿，快活地冲他唱：

「站开些，小子，你可别往我跟前凑！」

「哎呀，」布莱恩·斯宾塞大声鼓掌笑道，「这不全都活灵活现吗？说真的，小乔（Jan），你真该上台去做个模仿艺人，我不信有谁能胜得过你。」他是个高挑黝黑的少年，五官端正矜贵，如同刀刻斧凿。他和亚历山大正从大学回家过复活节假期。

===Original===
Maggie Ellersley, as it happened, had been at a matinée the week before. “It was vaudeville, Joanna, and there was an actress there who took off different people and then she did some Irish folk dances, but she couldn’t hold a candle to you. Too bad we’re colored.”

“It’s not going to make any difference to me,” said Joanna determinedly. “Mother and father are willing. If I want to go on the stage I’ll get there.”

“Joanna has the faith that moves mountains,” laughed Peter. “If anybody can make it she can.”

===Chinese===
碰巧，玛吉·埃勒斯利前一个星期刚看过一场日场。「那是歌舞杂耍（vaudeville），乔安娜，里头有位女演员，一会儿学这个人的做派、一会儿学那个人的，后来又跳了几支爱尔兰民间舞——可她连给你提鞋都不配。只可惜我们是有色人种。」

「这可拦不住我，」乔安娜斩钉截铁地说，「爸爸妈妈都点了头。只要我想上舞台，我就一定上得去。」

「乔安娜有移山的信心呢。」彼得笑道，「要是有人能成，那准是她。」

===Original===
Peter was a regular visitor at the Marshall home now. Ever since that day four years before when he had told Joanna of his newborn determination to be a surgeon, he had spent all his spare time near her. Miss Susan Graves did not like this at first, not that she resented Peter’s absence from her so much, but he was a Bye and she did not choose to have him associate too much with people whom she did not know. It was no part of her plan for Peter to retrograde into the wreck which Meriwether had become. She made it her business to meet Mrs. Marshall at a church affair.

“I think,” said Miss Graves, eyeing Joanna’s mother with her clear, square gaze, “that my boy has spoken to me of you.”

Mrs. Marshall looked puzzled. She thought this was a *Miss* Graves.

===Chinese===
如今彼得已是马歇尔家的常客。自四年前那一天他对乔安娜说出自己新立的志向——要当一名外科医生——起，他就把全部闲暇都消磨在她身边。起初苏珊·格雷夫斯小姐并不乐意，倒不是多么怨彼得不在自己跟前，而是他姓拜，她不愿他同她不认识的人家过分来往。她可从没打算让彼得退回到梅里韦瑟那副烂掉的样子去。于是她特意在一个教会活动上结识了马歇尔太太。

「我想，」格雷夫斯小姐说，用她那双清亮方正的眼睛端详着乔安娜的母亲，「我家这孩子跟我提起过您。」

马歇尔太太一脸茫然。她本来还当这位是位*格雷夫斯小姐*呢。

===Original===
“Peter Bye,” his aunt continued, “he’s my nephew. He often speaks of Joanna Marshall.”

“Oh, Peter! Yes, we like to have him at the house. The girls find him great fun. So you’re his aunt. You must come to see us, too. Get him to bring you.”

Miss Graves came and was impressed enough to let Peter continue, though he would have continued without her permission. But Miss Susan, like Belle Bye nearly a century ago, recognized atmosphere when she saw it. She was poor; Peter was penniless. These were the sort of people her nephew ought to know. She liked Joel’s success, his pride, his air of being somebody. She estimated rightly the correctness of the old-fashioned walnut furniture, the heavy curtains, the kidney table in the parlor, the massive silver service and good linen. It is true Sylvia changed much of this﻿—except the silver﻿—for cretonnes and wicker chairs and gay rugs. But as Miss Susan went to the house only a few times she did not know of this.

===Chinese===
「彼得·拜，」他的姨妈接着说，「是我的外甥。他常提起乔安娜·马歇尔。」

「哦，彼得呀！可不是，我们喜欢他来家里玩。姑娘们都觉得他有趣极了。原来您是他姨妈。您一定也要来我们家坐坐，让他带您来。」

格雷夫斯小姐来了，印象好到足以容许彼得继续往来——尽管没有她的允许他也会照来不误。不过苏珊小姐就像近一个世纪前的贝尔·拜一样，一望便识得门第气象。她穷；彼得身无分文。而这一类人家，正是她外甥应当结交的。她喜欢乔尔的成就、他的自尊、他那种算个人物的气派。老式胡桃木家具、厚重的帘幔、客厅里的肾形桌、成套的厚重银器和上等亚麻桌布，其合乎体统之处，她都估量得丝毫不差。诚然，这些后来大半被西尔维娅换掉了——银器除外——换上了印花装饰布、藤椅和色彩明快的地毯。只是苏珊小姐统共只来过几回，并不知情。

===Original===
What she especially liked was the spirit of life, of ambition and hopefulness that pulsed in that household. As Miss Graves grew older, she began to see that her younger sister had had some pretty good views after all, that it did not do to stick to settled views﻿—“this for me, and that quite other thing for you.” The great things of life were for the taking, it was true, but the result of deliberate planning. One did not simply stumble into success. She had lived too long with “the best white people” not to find that out.

Joel knew this, too, she realized. His whole life was devoted to the mapping out of his children’s future. His own and Joanna’s high enthusiasms had borne fruit. Of late the boys, Philip and Alexander, had talked good solid man-talk.

“Colored people will be going big pretty soon. We’ll have to get in it, too, Pa.”

===Chinese===
她格外看重的，是那户人家里搏动着的生气、抱负与希望。格雷夫斯小姐年事渐长，开始明白她妹妹当年的见识终究是不错的：抱着定死的成见不放是要不得的——「这个归我，那个才归你」。人生的大事业固然人人可取，却是深谋远虑的结果。人不会糊里糊涂就跌进成功里去。她在「最好的白人」家里待得实在太久，不会不明白这个道理。

她还看出，乔尔也深谙此道。他一辈子都在为儿女的前程谋划布局。他自己和乔安娜那一腔高远的热情已经结出了果子。近来，菲利普和亚历山大这两个小子谈起话来，已是结结实实的男子汉口吻。

「有色人种眼看就要干出名堂来了。咱们也得插上一脚，爸。」

===Original===
Miss Susan decided this was a good place for Peter. Even if she had the money to do so, she could not send him to a school where he would meet with more inspiration in both precept and actual concrete example. Already in the lesser things this association was bearing fruit. Peter was too handsome, too graceful, too charming ever to be considered a boor. But he had lacked finish, that fine courtliness of manner which Miss Susan noted could convert a man of most ordinary appearance into a prince. She had marked it among Jacob Sharples’ grandsons. Peter had not possessed a knowledge of that delicacy, of that attention to trifles which, once gained by a man, gives him passport everywhere. Miss Susan had noticed, to her regret, the boy’s tendency to let her carry bundles, to look after even the heavier household duties. It had never occurred to him if the weather were cold or stormy, to offer to go errands for her. And his aunt, practical though she was, shrank from calling his attention to these things. She did not want him to think of her as exacting a return for her kindness.

Now the Marshall boys were fine gentlemen. Joel had made them so by teaching, as well as by his attitude toward their mother and sisters. Joanna and Sylvia, particularly Sylvia, helped the boys out with an occasional stitch, an occasional sewing on of a button. When Alexander was getting ready for college, and was working at nights to help with his expenses, Sylvia used to arrange sandwiches and milk for him when he came in late. And Joanna had recopied his chemistry and history notes. These were only kind trivialities, but the boys treated their sisters like queens. Philip was a little like Sylvia, only neither as handsome nor as lithe and quick. Alexander﻿—Alec, Sandy, the girls called him variously﻿—was slower, like Joanna. Both boys were tall and well set-up. The girls used to thrill a little﻿—sisters to them though they were﻿—over the very real and thoughtful gallantry of these two young men.

===Chinese===
苏珊小姐断定，这地方对彼得大有好处。就算她出得起钱，她也送不了他去一所比这里更能激励他的学校——无论在道理教训上，还是在活生生的实例上都是如此。单在细微小事上，这种交往已经结出果实。彼得太俊雅、太倜傥、太招人喜欢，谁也不会把他当成粗人。可他还欠着火候——缺那一份精细的礼数；苏珊小姐注意到，这份礼数能把一个长相最平平的男子变成一位王子。这种风范，她曾在雅各布·夏普尔斯（Jacob Sharples）家的孙儿们身上见识过。彼得还不懂得那种细腻，那种对琐事的留意——一个男人一旦习得，便可以通行天下。苏珊小姐遗憾地注意到，这孩子总是让她自己提着包袱，连家中较重的杂务也由她照管；天气寒冷或刮风下雨的时候，他从来想不到主动替她跑腿。而他这位姨妈虽讲究实际，却不肯开口点破这些事让他留意。她不愿让他觉得，她是在索取她那份慈爱的回报。

马歇尔家的两个小子恰恰是规规矩矩的绅士。乔尔把他们教养成人，一靠调教，二靠他对孩子们的母亲和姐妹的态度。乔安娜和西尔维娅——尤其是西尔维娅——也常帮兄弟们缝上一针、钉个扣子什么的。亚历山大准备上大学那阵子，夜里做工贴补学费，回来晚了，西尔维娅总给他备好三明治和牛奶；乔安娜则替他誊抄过化学和历史笔记。这些不过是些微不足道的小恩小惠，可两个小子把姐妹们当王后一般相待。菲利普有点像西尔维娅，只是既不及她俊美，也不及她轻盈敏捷。亚历山大——姑娘们有时叫他亚历克，有时叫他桑迪（Sandy）——性子要慢一些，像乔安娜。两个男孩都个子高挑、体态挺拔。姑娘们虽是他们的姐妹，却不免为这两位青年那实实在在、体贴入微的骑士风度而微微心折。

===Original===
Miss Susan had remarked this quality as soon as she met them. And she was beginning now to see its reflection in Peter. And as he had beauty and great personal charm to go with it, it distinguished him even more than the Marshall boys. She halfway suspected a conscious assumption of this on his part.

“But if he keeps it up, it will become part of him,” she thought to herself, “and then﻿—girls be careful.” She would have been a little fearful for Joanna had she not noticed immediately in the young girl that indomitable desire for distinction. “Joanna will never fall in love with anybody,” she said once to a common friend of herself and the Marshalls. “She’ll never be able to take her mind off long enough from her highfalutin dreams.”

===Chinese===
苏珊小姐一见到他们，便留意到这种品质。如今她开始看到它在彼得身上的映影。何况他又有相貌和出众的个人魅力相配，这使他比马歇尔家的两小子还要出众。她隐隐疑心，这是他有意识装出来的。

「不过只要他坚持下来，这就会长成他身上的一部分，」她暗自想道，「到那时——姑娘们可要当心了。」若不是她一眼就看出这少女身上那股不肯服输的求名之心，她本要为乔安娜捏一把汗的。「乔安娜永远不会爱上任何人，」她有一次对她和马歇尔家共同的一位朋友说，「她那套好高骛远的梦，她一辈子也放不下来。」

===Original===
Of course Peter had no conception why his aunt liked him to visit the Marshalls. He was only too glad that she didn’t disapprove. He was seventeen now and beginning to know himself in some ways pretty well. He liked Sandy and Philip and Sylvia Marshall﻿—liked them very well, and Joanna! It could hardly be said that he loved her at this time. But he knew that what he liked best of all in the world was to be near her, to watch her, and to listen to her plans. She had little shadowy gleams in her dark thick hair, glints of light that ended abruptly in wavy blackness. He would like to touch it. He remembered that he had once pulled her hair. He had done it often! But now, though she was only fifteen, he did not dare. Yet he often touched Sylvia’s.

The night that Joanna taught them all the barn dance, Peter maneuvered until he got Harry Portor at the piano, and said:

“How does that part go, Joanna? Here I am in the center. Then I take you in. Then﻿—”

“Put your arms around her,” said Sylvia. “That’s it. Now﻿—

> Barn! Barn!”

He went home and fairly babbled to his aunt about it. “Joanna is the most wonderful!”

===Chinese===
彼得自然全然想不到，姨妈为什么喜欢他上马歇尔家去。她不反对，他就再高兴不过了。他如今十七岁，有些方面已经开始相当了解自己。他喜欢桑迪、菲利普和西尔维娅·马歇尔——非常喜欢——还有乔安娜！要说这时他已经爱上了她，倒也谈不上。可他知道，这世上他最喜欢的，就是待在她身边，望着她，听她讲她的种种打算。她乌黑浓密的头发里透出一绺绺浅浅的光影，一点微光乍然一闪，便没入起伏的黑波里。他想摸一摸它。他记得自己从前扯过她的头发。何止一次，常扯呢！可如今，虽说她才十五岁，他竟不敢了。倒是西尔维娅的头发，他还常常碰。

乔安娜教大家跳谷仓舞（barn dance）的那个晚上，彼得想方设法把哈里·波托尔支到钢琴前，然后说：

「那一段是怎么走的，乔安娜？我站在这儿，就在当中。然后我把你拉进来。然后——」

「把胳膊搂住她呀，」西尔维娅说，「对啦。来——

> 谷仓！谷仓！」

他回到家，缠着姨妈叽叽喳喳说个没完。「乔安娜真是妙极了！」

## VIII / 八

===Original===
If Peter was unconscious of the utter desirability of association with the young Marshalls, Maggie Ellersley was not. Ever since her childhood when she had overheard a conversation between a cousin and her mother, she had made up her mind to attach herself to some such family and see what came of it.

The cousin and her mother worked together for some wealthy white people. Maggie’s mother was a laundress, a spare hardworking woman to whom life had meant nothing but poverty and confusion. On Thursdays and Fridays of each week she washed and ironed and gossiped with “my cousin Mis’ Sparrow” who was cook at the house on Madison Avenue. Maggie used to come there for dinner and go home with her mother.

“Mis’ Sparrow,” small and spidery, had a perpetual complaint against the world. In particular she experienced envy toward those who were better off than herself. Her jaundiced disposition may be excused, however, when one reflects that hers was a lot which had been hard ever since she could remember. She was poor, she was weak, she was ignorant. Add to that the fact that she was black in a country where color is a crime and you have her “complex.” Some people would say she had really done well in one sense with her life. She had attained by her own unaided efforts to a comfortable, even if menial, position, where she had heat, light and enough to eat. They would ask: Considering her beginnings what more could she want? Alas, in that dull soul unknown aspirations stirred, amazing questions took form. “Why, why, why?” asked Mis’ Sparrow in her own peculiar dialect, “are all the sweetness and light of life showered on some and utterly denied to me?”

===Chinese===
若说彼得全然不觉同马歇尔家的年轻一辈交往是何等令人向往，玛吉·埃勒斯利可不然。打小时候偷听到母亲和一位表亲的那番谈话起，她就拿定了主意：要攀附上这样一户人家，且看能有什么结果。

那位表亲和玛吉的母亲一同给几户有钱的白人做工。玛吉的母亲是个洗衣妇，精瘦而勤苦，对她来说，生活从来就只有贫穷和纷乱。每逢星期四、星期五，她一边洗衣熨衣，一边同「我家表姐斯派罗太太（Mis' Sparrow）」闲磕牙，这位表姐在麦迪逊大道（Madison Avenue）上一户人家当厨娘。玛吉常去那儿吃晚饭，再随母亲一道回家。

斯派罗太太个子矮小，瘦得像只蜘蛛，对这个世界怀着一腔至死方休的怨气。她尤其嫉恨那些境况比自己好的人。不过，她那副偏狭乖戾的性子倒也可以原谅——只要想一想，打从记事时候起，她的命就一直苦。她穷，她弱，她无知。再加上一条：在一个把肤色当罪过的国度里，她是个黑人——她的「情结」便凑齐了。有些人会说，从某种意义上讲，她这辈子其实混得不坏。她单枪匹马挣到了一个虽属仆役、却也安稳的位子，有火取暖，有灯照亮，饭也吃得饱。他们会问：想想她的出身，她还能奢求什么呢？唉，偏偏在那颗迟钝的灵魂里，翻搅着无名的想望，凝成了惊人的疑问。「为什么，为什么，到底为什么？」斯派罗太太用她那口独特的土话问道，「生活里所有的甜和光，凭什么尽往一些人头上浇，到我这里就一滴也不给？」

===Original===
At present Mis’ Sparrow had fastened a resentful eye on Mrs. Proctor, the bride of the son of the “white folks” for whom she worked. Edmonia the maid had told her about the newcomer, and over the supper table she retailed it to Mrs. Ellersley.

“She wan’t nobuddy. Jes’ a little teeny slip of ole white gal. No money, no fambly, no nuthin’.”

“Where’d he meet her then?” asked Mrs. Ellersley, uninterested but polite.

“Young Mr. Proctor’s sister met her in boardin’-school, poorest thing there,” replied Mis’ Sparrow, wiping a puckered mouth with her apron. “ ’Monia says Miss Dorothy sorry for her and got her a job in her father’s office. Mr. Harry was jes’ home f’um college; he saw her, took a fancy to her and jes’ married her. Jes’ wouldn’t listen to nobuddy a-tall.”

“Don’t it beat all,” pondered Mrs. Ellersley, “how some people have all the luck? Now if that kind of thing could just happen to my Maggie.”

===Chinese===
这阵子，斯派罗太太正把一腔怨愤盯在普罗克托太太（Mrs. Proctor）身上——她东家「白人主子」家少爷新娶的媳妇。女仆埃德莫尼娅（Edmonia）把这位新人的底细讲给她听，她便在饭桌上转述给埃勒斯利太太（Mrs. Ellersley）。

「她算个啥人物。就一小条又瘦又小的小白人丫头。没钱，没家底，没这没那。」

「那他打哪儿碰上她的呢？」埃勒斯利太太问，兴致缺缺，倒还客气。

「普罗克托家少爷的妹妹是在寄宿学校认识她的，全校数她顶穷，」斯派罗太太答道，拿围裙揩了揩瘪瘪的嘴。「莫尼娅说，多萝西小姐（Miss Dorothy）可怜她，在她爹的事务所里给她寻了个差事。哈里先生（Mr. Harry）那阵子刚从大学回到家；见了她，相中了，说娶就娶了。就是谁的劝也半句听不进。」

「真是没天理，」埃勒斯利太太寻思道，「怎么有的人就把好运全占尽了？我们玛吉要是也能摊上这么一回就好了。」

===Original===
Mis’ Sparrow was unmoved by the irrelevant allusion to Maggie. Where would she get such a chance?

“ ’Monia says she don’t even love him. Liked some young travelin’ salesman she’d known all her life. ’Monia declares she cries about him when she’s by herself.”

“What she marry him for then?” asked Maggie Ellersley, aged twelve, and an interested listener.

“H’m child, wouldn’t you do anything to get away f’um hard work, an’ ugly cloes an’ bills? Some w’ite folks has it most as bad as us poor colored people. Only thing is they has more opporchunities.”

===Chinese===
对这句不相干的感慨，斯派罗太太无动于衷——玛吉上哪儿去摊这样的机会？

「莫尼娅说她压根儿不爱他。心里喜欢的是个跑外的年轻推销员，两人打小就认识。莫尼娅咬定，她独自一个人的时候还为他哭呢。」

「那她干吗嫁给他？」玛吉·埃勒斯利问道。她十二岁，正听得津津有味。

「嗨，孩子，要是能躲开累死人的活计、丑衣裳、还不清的账，你还有啥不肯干的？有些白人的日子，过得跟我们穷有色人种差不多苦。只有一条——人家机会多。」

===Original===
Maggie, visualizing the life which she and her mother endured, thought she probably would. She thought it again after they had reached the tenement in Thirty-fifth Street where the two of them lived. It was the famous “Tenderloin” of those days and Maggie’s spirit revolted with a revulsion of feeling which never ceased to amaze her mother against the sordidness of that place. There were three rooms. The front one looked on the street and so was well lighted, but the other two got light only from the air-shaft. Mrs. Ellersley, a widow who considered herself fortunate to be one, rented the front room out, usually to trainmen (perhaps some of Meriwether’s acquaintances were among them), occasionally to a married couple.

She and Maggie slept and lived in the two wretchedly ventilated rooms, in a perpetual gloom penetrated ever so slightly by a flickering blue flame. A confusion of clothes, obscene old furniture, boxes, stale newspapers was littered about them. For some reason the rooms were everlastingly damp, perhaps because, although rain could get down the air-shaft, the sunlight never could. The rooms gave Maggie a constantly eerie feeling, which in later more fortunate years she was always able to recall by the sight of a gas-flame burning low and blue.

They never, in those days, enjoyed a really bright flame. Saving was Mrs. Ellersley’s insistent because necessary fetish. Maggie’s tea was always weak, and never sweet enough. The bread﻿—baker’s with holes in it, yesterday’s, two loaves for five cents﻿—was always stale; the meat usually salt and sometimes tainted.

===Chinese===
玛吉把自己和母亲熬着的日子在脑子里过了一遍，心想自己多半什么都肯干。等她们回到三十五街（Thirty-fifth Street）那座出租屋——母女俩就住在那儿——她把这念头又想了一遍。那一带正是当年大名鼎鼎的「坦德洛因」（Tenderloin）区；面对那地方的藏污纳垢，玛吉心里激起的反感之强烈，始终令她母亲暗暗称奇。屋子共三间。临街那间朝着大街，采光倒好；另外两间却只能从通风井里漏进些光。埃勒斯利太太是个寡妇——还自认为守寡是桩幸事——把临街那间租了出去，房客通常是列车乘务员（说不定梅里韦瑟的相识也在其中），偶尔也租给小两口。

她和玛吉就睡在、也活在那两间通风糟透了的屋子里，四下是经年不散的幽暗，只有一豆摇曳的蓝色火苗，勉勉强强刺进一丝亮。一堆乱七八糟的衣裳、不堪入目的旧家具、纸箱子、隔宿的报纸，狼藉地堆在她们身边。不知为什么，那两间屋子常年潮湿——也许因为雨水下得进通风井，日光却永远下不进去。那两间屋子给玛吉一种时时袭来的阴森之感，以致后来境况转好的岁月里，她只要看见一盏燃得低而发蓝的煤气火苗，那感觉便能立刻召回到眼前。

那些日子，她们从未享受过一团真正亮堂的火苗。节省是埃勒斯利太太念兹在兹的迷信——也是不得不尔的迷信。玛吉的茶永远是稀薄的，甜头永远不够；面包——面包房买的、带着窟窿眼的隔夜货，五分钱两只——永远是陈的；肉多半腌得齁咸，有时还发了点味儿。

===Original===
Out of it all Maggie bloomed﻿—a strange word but somehow true. She was like a yellow calla lily in the deep cream of her skin, the slim straightness of her body. She had a mass of fine, wiry hair which hung like a cloud, a mist over two gray eyes. Her lips, in spite of her constant malnutrition, persisted unbelievably red. When she met excitement those gray eyes darkened and shone, her cheeks flushed a little, her small hands fluttered. And she was nearly always excited. Something within her frail bosom pulsed in a constant revolt against the spirit of things that kept her in these conditions.

“I will not always live like this, Ma﻿—I’ll get out of it some way.”

And her mother, though always scoffing, believed her with a dreary hopefulness. “If there’s a way to be found out, Maggie’ll find it.”

===Chinese===
就在这一切之中，玛吉竟出落得花一般——「出落」这个词儿怪，却不知怎的贴切。她像一枝黄马蹄莲，开在深奶油色的皮肤和纤细笔直的身段里。她有一头又细又韧的浓发，披下来像一片云、一层雾，罩着两只灰眼睛。两片嘴唇，尽管长年营养不足，却偏偏红得叫人不敢相信。一逢兴奋，那双灰眼睛便转黑、发亮，两颊微微泛红，一双小手扑棱棱地颤。而她几乎无时无刻不在兴奋。她那纤弱的胸脯里，有什么东西一搏一搏地跳个不停，永远反抗着把她摁在这种境况里的世道。

「我不会一辈子这么过的，妈——我总要想法子跳出去。」

而她母亲虽总嘲弄她，却怀着一种凄楚的指望信她。「只要天底下寻得着路子，玛吉准寻得着。」

===Original===
Maggie found early that one avenue of escape lay through men. They were stronger than women, they made money. They did not give the impression of shrinking from spending the last penny lest when that cent was gone there should be no more. All the trainmen liked her. She could not get much order in that abominable home, but she could and did keep herself clean and neat. She washed her few garments over night; she wound a stray ribbon, from a box of cigars or a box of candy, through her hair. Some of the men, young students, “on the road” during their summer vacations, used to flirt with her.

“Hurry and grow up, Mag. When I get through school I’ll come back and marry you. How’d you like to live in a little house﻿—not like this!﻿—in Washington?” Or Wilmington or Savannah as the case might be. “I’d give you pretty dresses.”

Poor Maggie. Her calla-lily charm visibly lessened in those days when she opened her pretty mouth. She disclosed herself then for what she was, a true daughter of the Tenderloin.

“Aw quit your kiddin!”

===Chinese===
玛吉早早便发现，脱身的一条道儿打男人身上过。男人比女人强，男人挣得起钱。他们不像女人那样，花最后一枚铜板也缩手缩脚，生怕这一文花掉，后头就再没有一文。乘务员个个都喜欢她。那个糟糕透顶的家里，她整治不出什么名堂；可她自己身上的干净齐整，却是能办到、也果真办到了的。她那几件衣裳连夜洗出来；再拣一条零零碎碎的缎带——雪茄盒里或是糖果盒里来的——绕进头发里。有些男人是年轻的学生，暑假里「跑外」做工，常跟她调情。

「快快长大呀，玛吉。等我念完书，就回来娶你。住进一幢小房子——可不是这样的房子！——在华盛顿（Washington），好不好？」要不就是威尔明顿（Wilmington）或萨凡纳（Savannah），看各人说的情形。「我还给你买漂亮衣裳。」

可怜的玛吉。那些日子，只要她一张开那张俏丽的嘴，马蹄莲似的魅力便眼见着减了成色。那时她便现出本来面目——坦德洛因区地地道道的女儿。

「去你的，少耍贫嘴！」

===Original===
But she came slowly to realize that here was a way out. If she could only grow up﻿—if she were﻿—say﻿—seventeen.

She was persistently frail, else her mother might have put her to work. As it was she was sent to school very regularly﻿—to save fuel and gas. Evenings she went to the houses where her mother worked and got her dinner.

On the night after she had listened to Mis’ Sparrow’s comments about young Mrs. Proctor, she sat thoughtful a long time. She had sense enough to know that very often these trainmen stayed poor. They made pretty good money﻿—they did, too, in those days﻿—but not enough to save their wives from labor. Maggie did not want to wash and iron, to go through the dreary existence which had been her mother’s when her father was living; he had run on the road.

Suppose, just suppose, there were some colored men who were fortunate, successful, who had enough to eat, who could give their wives help. Her mother knew of ministers like that. There were colored doctors and lawyers somewhere. Their very titles connoted prosperity.

===Chinese===
但她慢慢懂得了，这里头正有一条出路。只消她快快长大——假如她——比如说——已经十七岁就好了。

她偏偏一直孱弱，不然母亲早就送她去做工了。就这样，她还被天天按时送到学校——为的是省煤省煤气。傍晚，她上母亲做工的人家去吃晚饭。

听完斯派罗太太议论小普罗克托太太的那天夜里，她坐着怔怔地想了半天。她脑子够用，知道这些乘务员往往到老都穷。他们挣钱不算少——那年月也确实如此——却还不够让老婆免于操劳。玛吉不想洗衣服熨衣服，不想重过母亲在父亲活着时过的那种愁惨日子；父亲生前就是常年在外跑车的。

假如，单单是假如，有一些有色男人走运、发迹、吃得饱穿得暖，还能给老婆雇上帮手呢？她母亲就认得那样的牧师。天南地北还有有色人的医生和律师。单看那些头衔，便透着股殷实气。

===Original===
“Ma,” she spoke out of her brown study, “are there any very rich colored men?”

“Not any very rich ones, I don’t think,” Mrs. Ellersley replied thoughtfully, “but lots very well off, comfortable, with servants to wait on ’em.” She sighed.

“I’m going to meet one,” said Maggie solemnly, and henceforth she thought, she dreamed of nothing else.

===Chinese===
「妈，」她从凝神苦想里开了口，「有没有非常有钱的有色男人？」

「非常有钱的，怕是没有，」埃勒斯利太太若有所思地答道，「不过宽裕的、日子过得舒坦的、家里仆人伺候着的，可着实不少。」她叹了口气。

「我要去结识一个这样的人。」玛吉一本正经地说。从此她想的、梦的，再没有别的。

===Original===
When she was fourteen young John Howe, who was occupying the front room, came down with a spell of typhoid fever. He begged Mrs. Ellersley not to send him to the hospital, and it was impossible to get him to his home in Oklahoma. He had enough money to see him through, and he put his fortunes and his case into her withered hands. All the trainmen knew of Mrs. Ellersley’s absolute honesty. She did what she could for him, sat up long nights, gave him his medicine faithfully, “counted out his money.”

But it was Maggie who gave real service. She stayed out of school to attend him. The doctor gave her a list of directions which she followed with meticulous care. In that shabby house down in that terrible district John Howe met with an attention, a devotion from the humble woman and her delicate daughter, such as no money could have bought him in the seats of the mighty.

John Howe was a Lincoln divinity student, intermittently working his way through college. He sat up gaunt and weak in the scratched bed of cheap cherry wood and picked with long skeleton fingers at the thin blue and white checked coverlet.

===Chinese===
她十四岁那年，租着临街屋子的年轻人约翰·豪（John Howe）害起了伤寒。他央求埃勒斯利太太别送他进医院，而要把他送回俄克拉何马（Oklahoma）的家中又断无可能。他的钱足够对付这场病，于是把自己的财产和病况一并交到她那双干瘦的手里。乘务员们全都知道埃勒斯利太太的诚实毫厘无欺。她尽力照料他，熬过一个又一个长夜，按时按顿给他服药，还「把钱一五一十点数清楚」。

但真正尽心出力的，是玛吉。她辍了学来侍疾。医生开了一张嘱咐单，她一丝不苟地照着办。在那样可怕的地段、那样寒酸的房子里，约翰·豪受到的那份关切与照拂——出自那卑微的妇人和她纤弱的女儿——是在朱门显贵之地花多少钱也买不来的。

约翰·豪是林肯大学（Lincoln）的神学学生，断断续续地半工半读。他在那张抓痕累累的廉价樱桃木床上坐起身来，形容枯瘦而虚弱，用嶙峋的长手指一下一下捻着薄薄的蓝白格床罩。

===Original===
“Maggie, you and your mother’ve been mighty good to me. Look here, I’ve got to pay you back somehow. After this illness I’ll have to stay out of school a year. What do you want?”

Maggie stared at him, her gray eyes going black in the yellow oval of her face.

“There’s only one thing I want, Mr. Howe, and you couldn’t give me that.”

“I could try. What is it?”

“Oh Mr. Howe, if you could just get us out of this awful place, this house, this street! If I could just get to know some decent folks﻿—”

===Chinese===
「玛吉，你和你娘待我实在没得说。听着，我总得想法子报答你们。这场病过后，我不得不休学一年。你想要什么？」

玛吉直愣愣地望着他，在她那张鹅蛋形的黄脸盘上，一双灰眼睛渐渐转成了黑。

「我只想要一样东西，豪先生，可那样东西您给不了。」

「我可以试试。是什么？」

「唉，豪先生，您要是能把我们从这个可怕的地方、这所房子、这条街弄出去就好了！我只要能结识几位体面人——」

===Original===
“Well, I don’t see how I could arrange about the folks. Where do you want to live, if you go from here? There’re not many places for colored folks in New York.”

“There are houses for colored people up in Fifty-third Street, and decent folks living in them.”

“But my goodness, Maggie, it costs a fortune to rent one of those houses.”

“I know, oh, I know. But if we could just get started. Mother could fill the house with roomers. Why there’ve been twelve men here for this room since you’ve been sick. The rest of the rooms aren’t much, but mother always keeps this room tidy, and we’re honest. They all know that. Never missed a penny here, any of them. And they tell their friends about us. Lots of times they tell Ma if she only had more room she’d have all the roomers she wanted.”

“But you’ve no furniture.”

“We could buy on the instalment plan.” She had her scheme all worked out. Clearly she had nursed her project. “Mr. Howe, if you could just help us to begin.”

===Chinese===
「呃，体面人那一节，我看我无从安排。你们要是搬，想住到哪儿去？纽约给有色人住的地方可不多。」

「五十三街（Fifty-third Street）那头有有色人住的房子，里头住的都是体面人。」

「可是我的天，玛吉，租那样一幢房子可要一大笔钱呢。」

「我知道，唉，我知道。可只要能起个头。母亲能把屋子都住满房客。您瞧，打您病倒起，单这间屋就住过十二个男人了。其余几间屋子不怎么样，可母亲一向把这间收拾得利利索索，我们又诚实，他们谁都知道。谁在这儿也没短过一枚铜子儿。他们还把我们的名声讲给朋友听。多少回他们对妈说，只要屋子再多几间，她要多少房客有多少房客。」

「可你们没有家具。」

「我们可以按分期付款的法子买呀。」她的盘算早打得妥妥帖帖，这桩图谋显然孕育已久了。「豪先生，您只要帮我们起个头。」

===Original===
He would, he told her, convinced by her earnestness. “What exactly do you want me to do, Maggie?”

She wanted him to make his headquarters with them for the year, and to pay as much as he could in advance. It was still early summer. He must write and tell other men, who would want rooms, and get a few of them to pay in advance, too. “Trainmen won’t mind that,” she told him shrewdly, “they’ll like to know they have some place to go to when they’ve cleaned themselves out at cards, or whatever it is they do. That will pay a month’s rent, and leave something, and with what we pay on this﻿—this *hole*, we’ll have something to put on the furniture.”

“I guess you’re right,” said Howe, “I’ll speak to your mother about it.”

===Chinese===
他答应帮她，他告诉她，是被她的恳切打动了。「你要我做的，到底是什么呢，玛吉？」

她要他把那一年的落脚点安在她们家，并尽其所能预付房租。时令还只是初夏。他还得写信告诉别的要租房子的男人，再劝上几个也预先付款。「乘务员不碍这个的，」她精明地告诉他，「他们反倒乐意知道有这么个地方，等打牌输光了口袋——或者不管干下了什么——总还有个去处。这就付得出一个月房租，还能剩下一点；再加上我们如今在这——这个*烂窟窿*上交的房钱，添置家具的款项也就有了。」

「我看你说得对，」豪说，「我去跟你母亲说。」

===Original===
But that was useless. Mrs. Ellersley was sure of her livelihood, her mere existence here, but she was doubtful about a great venture. “Of course, for Maggie’s sake I’d like to get away.”

“Oh, Ma, do﻿—do, Ma,” Maggie had pleaded in an ecstasy of longing. “This is our one chance. You see if we don’t take this we’ll never get away.”

Fortunately she had Howe to back her. “She’s right, Mrs. Ellersley, and this is no place for a young girl to grow up. You can count on me. I’ll go look for a house, and see about some furniture. I know plenty of fellows would be glad to come.”

===Chinese===
可是这没用。眼下的营生、眼下这条活命的路，埃勒斯利太太是稳拿的；对一桩大的冒险，她却狐疑不定。「自然，为了玛吉，我也想搬。」

「哦，妈，搬吧——搬吧，妈，」玛吉恳求过，恳盼得如痴如狂。「这是咱们唯一的一回机会。您瞧，这回不抓住，咱们就一辈子走不脱了。」

幸而有豪替她撑腰。「她说得对，埃勒斯利太太，这地方本不是个年轻姑娘长大的去处。你尽管信我。我去找房子，再张罗些家具。我认得好些小伙子都巴不得来呢。」

===Original===
Miraculously the scheme worked. It gave Maggie her first insight into the workings of life. If you wanted things, you thought and thought about them, and when an opportunity offered, there you were with your mind made up to jump at it.

Of course they were poor, but at least they were decent. John Howe, staying for that year in New York, realizing more and more how truly he was indebted to Maggie and her mother, took a proprietary interest. He laid the cheap rugs, he set up the cots, three in a room, he did mysterious jobs in the bathroom which to Maggie was always so marvelous. He bought tools and fixed window-cords which the landlord neglected. Maggie darned his socks for him, and he bought some wallpaper, cheap but clean and virginal, a soft yellow, and papered her square box of a room. A good job he made of it, too. Another roomer at his instigation made a dressing table out of a packing box which Mrs. Ellersley, reinvigorated, covered with scrim.

Gradually, word of her rooming-house spread among the better class of transients. All her lodgers gave her their mending to do, she washed for some of them, gave breakfast to a few chosen spirits, and they paid willingly and well.

===Chinese===
这一着竟奇迹般地成了。这让玛吉头一遭窥见生活的运转之道：你若想望什么东西，便想了又想，待到机会一露面，你早已主意拿定，扑上去就是。

她们当然还是穷，但至少体面了。约翰·豪那一年留在纽约，越来越明白自己欠玛吉母女的情分有多实在，便以一种近乎当家自居的心肠关照起这个家来。他铺开廉价的地毯，支起折叠床，一间屋搭三张；他在浴室里做了几桩神秘的活计，玛吉瞧着只觉得妙不可言。他买来工具，修好房东懒得过问的吊窗绳。玛吉替他织补袜子；他便买来墙纸——便宜，却干净素洁、新得像未沾纤尘，一种柔柔的黄色——把她那间四四方方盒子似的房间裱糊了一遍。活儿干得还着实不赖。另一位房客受他撺掇，用一只包装箱打成一张梳妆台，埃勒斯利太太重振精神，给它蒙上细纱。

渐渐地，她这爿供宿宅子的名声，在上等一些的过往客商中间传开了。房客们把缝补活计都交给她做；有几个人的衣裳也归她洗；几位挑得中的雅客还搭她一顿早饭——他们付钱付得心甘情愿，出手也着实可观。

===Original===
Maggie was in transports. This was something like a home. Of course, she had to attend school in the district. Her mother took her as soon as matters were settled. She looked fresh and neat in a dark blue serge dress trimmed with black braid, the gift of melancholy Mis’ Sparrow who in turn had had it from young Mrs. Proctor. The dress was worn, but it was whole, and Maggie had tacked a tiny turnover of white lace in the high collar.

She was assigned to the eighth grade. There were two of them in the school. Her star was in the ascendant, for she was assigned to the one of which Sylvia Marshall was a member. She would have fared differently if it had been Joanna, for unless she were markedly clever, Joanna, who was intellectually a snob, would probably never have seen her. But Sylvia spied her at once. She came over to Maggie at recess.

“You’re a new girl, aren’t you? Want me to show you your way around?”

===Chinese===
玛吉欢喜得心花怒放。这才有点儿家的样子了。当然，她还得在本学区上学。事情一落定，母亲就把她送了过去。她穿一条藏青哔叽连衣裙，镶着黑辫带，看着清清爽爽、齐齐整整——那是愁眉苦脸的斯派罗太太送的礼，而斯派罗太太又是从小普罗克托太太手里得来的。裙子旧了，却完好无破，玛吉还在高领上缝了一圈小小的白蕾丝翻边。

她被编入八年级；全校八年级共有两个班。她的星运正冉冉上升——分的正是西尔维娅·马歇尔所在的那一班。若是换了乔安娜，她的境遇便会两样：乔安娜在学问上是个势利眼，除非谁聪明得出挑，否则她多半永远不会看见这个人。可西尔维娅一眼就瞅见了她。课间休息时，她朝玛吉走了过来。

「你是新来的同学，对吧？要不要我带你认认地方？」

===Original===
Maggie looked at the pretty girl, charming in a soft dark red cashmere dress made with a wide pleated skirt. She had on little patent leather, buttoned shoes with cloth tops, and a big red bow perched butterfly fashion on her dark head. Joanna wore her hair rather primly back from her face, but Sylvia’s was parted and rolled in waves over her ears, then it was caught up and confined by the bow. She had a thin gold bracelet on one arm. And about her hung the aura of well-being and easy self-assurance which marked all the Marshall children.

“I wish you would,” said Maggie.

===Chinese===
玛吉打量着这个俊俏的姑娘：一身柔润的深红色羊绒连衣裙，裙摆宽宽地打着褶，说不出的动人。她脚上是一双小小的漆皮扣带鞋，帮子是布的，乌黑的头顶上栖着一只大红蝴蝶结，恰像落了只蝴蝶。乔安娜的头发总是端端正正地从脸上往后拢，显得拘谨；西尔维娅的却中分开来，一波波卷过耳畔，再向上拢起，让那只蝴蝶结束住。她一只手腕上戴着细细的金镯子。她周身笼着一层安乐富足、从容自信的光晕——那是马歇尔家孩子个个带有的标记。

「那敢情好。」玛吉说。

===Original===
Sylvia in those days was an ardent worker in Old Zion Sunday School and had promised to help in a campaign for more students. She told Maggie about it within the next two or three days.

“My mother is going to entertain the new folks whom I get to join. Will you join?”

Maggie would and so went to Sylvia’s home as her mother’s guest.

===Chinese===
西尔维娅那阵子是老锡安主日学校（Old Zion Sunday School）的热心干事，还答应帮着搞一场多招学生的活动。过后不出两三天，她便对玛吉讲起了这件事。

「我母亲要设宴款待我拉来入会的新朋友。你也入吧？」

玛吉肯入，于是便作为西尔维娅母亲的客人，去到了西尔维娅家里。

===Original===
She never forgot that home with its quiet dignity and atmosphere of prosperity. The Marshall children were a revelation to her. She had not known of colored people like these.

“At last I’m getting to know decent people,” she told her mother.

She had a passion for respectability and decency quite apart from what they connoted of comparative ease and comfort, though she coveted these latter, too, and meant some day to have them.

“Two months ago,” she thought, “I was still in that horrible house on Thirty-fifth Street, and I got away. If that could happen, anything could happen.” She lay in her bed at nights in the little yellow room and saw visions.

===Chinese===
那个家她一辈子也忘不了——安安静静的尊贵气度，殷实富足的氛围。马歇尔家的孩子们让她大开眼界。她从来不知道世上还有这样的有色人。

「我总算认识体面人了。」她告诉母亲。

她热切地渴慕体面正派，倒不全是为着这两样所意味着的相对宽裕舒适——虽说后两样她也眼热，还打定主意迟早要弄到手。

「两个月以前，」她想，「我还窝在三十五街那所可怕的房子里呢，可我逃出来了。连那样的事都能成真，还有什么事成不了真？」夜里，她躺在那间小小的黄色房间里自己的床上，望见一个个幻景。

## X / 十

===Original===
Sunday was always an important day in the Marshall household. Its importance, it is true, took on a different character as the years sped. In the early days Mr. Marshall looked forward to it as the outward and visible sign of an inward worth. He was a steward in his church, Old Zion, and on Sundays in a long frock coat with a correct collar, a black Ascot tie surmounted by a gold horseshoe, he passed the collection box from pew to pew. He liked to bend his rather stately iron-gray head in recognition of various greetings. He felt he looked the part, as indeed he did, of an upright, ambitious, aspiring citizen.

Many a small boy unconsciously stored away a memory of the erect wholesome figure as a possible exemplar for future consideration.

His wife found Sunday a rather distracting day. It was eminently satisfying, doubtless, to be able to show off such a number of stylish costumes. Joel had always been able to have her dress well. There was one wine-colored cashmere with a polonaise and bustle which she had considered particularly fetching.

"I never put the dress on in the old days," she said to her girls, showing them the truly awe-inspiring picture, "without thinking to myself: 'I certainly am glad I married Joel.' I always did love fine clothes. Sylvia, I think you must have inherited my taste."

===Chinese===
在马歇尔家，星期天从来是个要紧的日子。诚然，随着岁月飞逝，这份要紧的分量也变换了性质。早年间，马歇尔先生把星期天看作内在德操的外在表征。他是老锡安教堂的执事，每逢星期天，他身穿长礼服，衬着一丝不苟的衣领，系一条缀着金马掌饰物的黑色阿斯科特领带，托着捐献匣挨排传递。他喜欢微微颔首，用那颗颇为庄重的铁灰色头颅应答四面八方的问候。他觉得自己看上去正是一副正派、上进、有抱负的公民模样——事实上也的确如此。

多少小男孩把这个挺拔康健的身影不知不觉收进记忆，当作日后可以效法的楷模。

他太太却觉得星期天是个颇叫人心烦意乱的日子。能炫耀这么多身时髦衣裳，无疑极尽满足。乔尔一向供得起她穿得体面。有一件酒红色的开司米长裙，配着波罗乃兹式上衣和裙撑，她一直觉得那身衣裳格外动人。

「早年间，我一穿上那身衣裳，」她给女儿们看那张确实令人叹为观止的照片，「心里总要想：『嫁给了乔尔，我真是高兴。』我一向就爱漂亮衣裳。西尔维娅，我看你这口味准是承自我的。」

===Original===
Sylvia groaned. "Oh, no, mamma, I don't deserve that!"

Clothes, however, had not quite compensated Mrs. Marshall for the arduousness otherwise entailed in the observance of the Sabbath. There was always company. Joel, a caterer, knew "how it ought to be done." Then there were the four children to dress and get off, and the constant oversight of them when they came home to see that they did not break the thousand inhibitions which made the day sacred.

"I used to hate it so," Sandy laughed. "Remember, Phil, how we used to try to find those awful sailor collars﻿—I think they're called Buster Browns now﻿—and see if we couldn't hide them or mark them up before the next Sunday? Mother must have had a million of them, for we were never able to exhaust her supply."

"Weren't you sights!" Sylvia teased. "You were fat, Alec, and your face rose large and round over your collar like a full moon. You had two eyes set away back from your fat cheeks and you had to bend your head way over to look down﻿—"

===Chinese===
西尔维娅哀叹一声。「哎哟，别，妈，我可担不起这份抬举！」

然而，衣裳的乐趣远不足以补偿马歇尔太太守安息日的种种辛劳。家里总归有客。乔尔是承办酒席的，深谙「该怎么办才像个样」。再者，四个孩子要穿戴齐整打发出门，他们回家后还得时时盯着，唯恐他们触犯那使这一天神圣不可侵犯的千百条清规。

「我从前恨透了这一天，」桑迪笑道，「还记得吗，菲尔，我们老是去找那些可怕的海军服领子——如今好像管它叫巴斯特·布朗（Buster Brown）领了——想法子在下一个星期天之前把它们藏起来或者涂花？母亲准备了上百万条，我们怎么也糟蹋不完她的存货。」

「你俩那模样可真够瞧的！」西尔维娅打趣道。「亚历克，你那会儿胖乎乎的，脸从领子上鼓出来，又大又圆，活像一轮满月。两只眼睛深深缩在胖脸蛋后面，得把脑袋使劲往下弯才看得到东西——」

===Original===
"And you wore a grayish-brownish-greenery-yallery round straw hat," interposed Joanna.

"Don't you talk," Philip jeered at them, "I remember two poke bonnets, reddish, with fuzzy stuff sticking up over them."

"Astrakhan. Yes, I remember," Sylvia told him. "Weren't they awful? And the deadliness of Sunday afternoon! You boys sitting around knocking your feet against the rungs of the chairs. Such glooms!"

"Yes, and you," said Sandy, assuming a solemn countenance. "Looking dejectedly out of the window, your face propped in your hands!"

===Chinese===
「还有你，戴一顶灰不溜秋、黄绿夹杂的圆草帽。」乔安娜插嘴道。

「你还别说人家，」菲利普嘲弄她们道，「我记得有两顶罩檐软帽，红彤彤的，帽顶上竖着一圈毛茸茸的东西。」

「阿斯特拉罕羔皮的。是的，我记得。」西尔维娅对他说。「那两顶帽子何其难看！还有星期天下午那份要命的沉闷！你们男孩子围坐着，脚老在椅子横档上磕来磕去。一个个愁眉苦脸！」

「可不是，还有你呢，」桑迪装出一副庄严的面孔说，「趴在窗前怅然外望，双手托着腮帮子！」

===Original===
"Joanna was the only one who got anything out of those Sundays," Philip mused. "I can see her now flat on her tummy reading the life of some exemplary female."

"Notable women of color," laughed Joanna. "I adored Sunday."

Certainly no flavor of those past days spoiled the Marshalls' enjoyment in these later years. Rather remarkably the whole family still went to church, Mr. and Mrs. Marshall from years of long habit, Sylvia because she rather liked to please her mother and because it amused her to have Brian Spencer, whom churchgoing bored to the point of agony, obey her wish that he should go. Sandy, now in the real estate business, thought it gave him standing in the community.

===Chinese===
「那些星期天，只有乔安娜一个人得到了些好处。」菲利普沉吟道。「我现在还能看见她趴在地上，读某位女楷模的生平。」

「《有色人种杰出女性》。」乔安娜笑答。「我最爱星期天了。」

当然，往日那些滋味丝毫没有败坏马歇尔一家后来这些年里的兴致。颇为难得的是，全家仍旧上教堂：马歇尔先生和太太是出于多年的积习；西尔维娅一来乐意讨母亲欢心，二来觉得有趣——布莱恩·斯宾塞被上教堂无聊得苦不堪言，却偏偏肯听她的话奉命前往；桑迪如今做房地产生意，觉得上教堂让他在社区里有头有脸。

===Original===
Philip's reasons were various. Chiefly he went to church as he went to many meetings, because he was interested in seeing groups of colored people together. He had a strong desire to sense the social consciousness, for he was trying to learn just what stirred mass feeling and into what channels it could be directed. A minister of the poorest type was an unfailing source of study to him. How would this man sway the people? And what would he ask of them once he had them ready to listen to his will? Philip always dreamed of a leader who should recognize that psychological moment and who would guide a whole race forward to the realization of its steadily increasing strength.

He dreamed many dreams sitting crosswise in the far corner of his pew, his back partly against the wall, partly against the seat, his lean, brown, slightly haggard face bent forward. He had already the somewhat remote glance of the thinker, though his firm chin pronounced him no less the man of action. Maggie Ellersley, sitting across the church from him, watching him a little covertly under her drooping hat brim, used to think he looked like a man who would take what he wanted.

"If only he knew *what* he wanted," she half sighed. Joanna was the soloist of the choir these days, sole raison d'être of *her* churchgoing. Her mezzo voice full and pulsing and gold brought throngs to the church every Sunday.

===Chinese===
菲利普的理由则多种多样。他上教堂，同他赴许多集会一样，主要是出于观察有色人种聚在一起的情形的兴趣。他极想体察那种群体的社会意识，因为他正力图弄明白，究竟是什么搅动起大众的情绪，这情绪又能导入怎样的河道。一个最蹩脚类型的牧师，在他也是取之不竭的研究素材。此人会如何鼓动民众？等民众俯首听命之后，他又会向他们索取什么？菲利普始终梦想着一位领袖，一位能认清那个心理时机的领袖，由他引领整个种族向前，去实现其日益壮大的力量。

他斜坐在座位最里头的角落里，背一半抵着墙，一半靠着椅座，那张瘦削的棕色面孔微微前倾、略带憔悴，做过许许多多的梦。他眼里已有了思想家那种略显辽远的神色，而那坚定的下巴又宣告他同样是个实干家。玛吉·埃勒斯利坐在教堂另一侧，在低垂的帽檐下悄悄打量他，总觉得他看上去像个想要什么便会径直去取的人。

「只可惜他不知道自己要的究竟是什么。」她半是叹息地想。如今乔安娜是唱诗班的独唱，这是她上教堂唯一的存在理由。她的女中音饱满、悸动、金光灿灿，每逢星期天都把成群的人引进教堂。

===Original===
"There is a green hill far away," she sang, and the puzzled, groping congregation turned its sea of black and brown, yellow and white faces toward her and knew a sudden peace. Even Philip stopped his restless inner queries.

At times like these Peter Bye felt his very heart leap toward her.

Joanna with her cool eyes and steady head cared almost nothing about this. She never saw herself in this scene. Always in her mind's eye she was far, far away from the church, in a great hall, in a crowded theater. There would be tier on tier of faces rising, rising above her. And tomorrow there would be the critics.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
「远处有一座青山」，她唱道，那满堂困惑迷茫的会众便转过一片黑与棕、黄与白的面孔朝向她，骤然感到一种安宁。连菲利普也停下了内心永无休止的追问。

每逢这样的时刻，彼得·拜都觉得自己的心整个儿向她飞去。

乔安娜双眼冷静、头脑沉稳，对此几乎毫不在意。她从不在眼前这幅图景里看到自己。她心里的眼睛所见的，永远是远离教堂的、很远很远的地方：一座大厅，一家满座的剧院。一层又一层的面孔在她上方升腾、升腾。而明天，就轮到乐评人们。……

===Original===
The Sundays passed thus weekend to weekend. One of them stood out in Joanna's memory. Philip, a Harvard junior, was home on his summer vacation, but he and Sylvia and Sandy had gone to visit their mother's folks in Philadelphia.

Joanna, Brian Spencer, Peter, and Maggie Ellersley stepped out of church and walked down the torrid street. It was early June, but the weather was that of August.

"Our children are growing up," said Mrs. Marshall to Mrs. Ellersley, lingering a moment in the shady vestibule. "See how tall Joanna and Maggie have grown. They were the littlest things!"

Mrs. Ellersley followed the group with a wistful eye. Of late she had begun to have some idea of Maggie's unspoken desires. She wished it were Philip instead of Brian walking down the street with her daughter. She was very tired, tired enough to die, but she could not, she felt, leave Maggie alone, unplaced in the world.

===Chinese===
星期天就这样一个周末接一个周末地过去。其中有一个，在乔安娜的记忆里格外分明。菲利普已是哈佛三年级学生，回家过暑假，但他同西尔维娅、桑迪动身去费城看望母亲娘家的亲戚了。

乔安娜、布莱恩·斯宾塞、彼得和玛吉·埃勒斯利走出教堂，沿着灼热的街道走去。时方六月初，天气却已是八月的天。

「咱们的孩子们都长大了，」马歇尔太太在阴凉的门廊里流连片刻，对埃勒斯利太太说。「瞧乔安娜和玛吉长得多高了。她们那会儿还是顶顶小的小不点儿呢！」

埃勒斯利太太怅然望着那一行人远去。近来她渐渐咂摸出玛吉心底不曾出口的愿望。她巴不得同女儿并肩走在街上的不是布莱恩，而是菲利普。她已经疲惫不堪，疲惫得足以死去，可她觉得她不能撒手，不能把玛吉孤零零丢在世上，无依无着。

===Original===
The four young people turned the corner and prepared to separate.

"Brian is coming to the house for dinner," said Joanna. "You coming, Maggie and Peter?"

Maggie had an engagement for the afternoon. Peter refused, too, sulkily, to Joanna's vast satisfaction.

"Jealous," she thought with some pride. It was an exhibition with which she seldom met. Most of the young men of her acquaintance were a little afraid of Joanna with her intent and serious air. "Highbrow" they called her and she knew it, liked it, too, though it had its inconveniences.

===Chinese===
四个年轻人拐过街角，准备分道。

「布莱恩要来家里吃晚饭，」乔安娜说，「玛吉、彼得，你们来吗？」

玛吉下午另有约会。彼得也怏怏地推辞了，这叫乔安娜大为得意。

「吃醋了。」她带着几分得意想道。这种表现她可是难得领教。她认识的年轻人多数有点怕乔安娜，怕她那副专注而正经的神气。他们管她叫「女学究」，她心里有数，还挺喜欢，尽管这称呼也有不便之处。

===Original===
"Peter's mad," she laughed as the two moved off, "because I told him I was going to the benefit concert with you, Brian, and so he couldn't come tonight."

"Sorry if I spoked his wheel," said Brian, "but you just have to take pity on me, Jan, I'm so lonely without Sylvia."

"Of course. Isn't it funny that he doesn't realize that? He thinks you are making up to me. As though I would come between you and Sylvia. Great chance I'd have."

"About as much as *I'd* have, trying to come between you and Peter. Not that I know anything about you, Janna. Heaven only knows what you mean to do with the boy. But I wouldn't want to face Peter, if I were aiming to be his rival. Wonder what he'll do when he goes to the University in Philadelphia. What's he going off there for, anyway? Can't he do just as well here?"

===Chinese===
「彼得气着呢，」两人走开时她笑道，「因为我告诉他，今晚我要陪你去看义演音乐会，布莱恩，所以他来不成了。」

「要是我搅了他的局，那真对不住，」布莱恩说，「可你只好可怜可怜我，小乔，西尔维娅不在，我闷得慌。」

「那是自然。他不明白这一点，你说怪不怪？他以为你在向我献殷勤呢。好像我会插到你和西尔维娅中间似的。我哪有这个机会。」

「同我想插到你和彼得中间的机会也差不多。我对你可摸不着底，小乔。天才知道你打算拿这小子怎么办。不过我要是想做他的情敌，可不愿意去面对彼得。不知道他去费城读大学以后会怎么样。他到底跑那么远去做什么？难道在这儿念不好吗？」

===Original===
"The penalty of being colored," said Joanna soberly. "He can get much better hospital work in Philadelphia. Of course he could take his pre-medic work here, but he thinks it best to begin where he plans to finish."

"How long will he be away?"

"Forever and ever, six or seven years, I think. Of course, we both have relatives in Philadelphia. His great-uncle Peter, for whom he was named, is still there, you know. Peter's counting on living with him. It will save expense."

"Six or seven years!" said Brian disregarding anything else. "Golly what a wait! It would kill any girl but you, Janna."

===Chinese===
「身为有色人种的代价，」乔安娜正色道。「在费城，他能找到好得多的医院工作。医学预科当然在这儿也能念，可他觉得最好是从打算终局的地方起步。」

「他要去多久？」

「永永远远，我看得六七年吧。当然，我们俩在费城都有亲戚。他那位叔祖父彼得——他的名字就是照着他取的——还住在那儿，你是知道的。彼得指望住到他家里去，能省一笔开销。」

「六七年！」布莱恩顾不上别的，嚷道。「老天，这得等多久啊！换了别的姑娘准得熬死，也就你熬得住，小乔。」

===Original===
"Sylvia didn't die while you were in Harvard," Joanna returned meanly.

"Not much she didn't! But she kept me in the back of her head, I'll swear. While you with your singing and dancing and your wildcat schemes of getting on the stage! Better stick to your own Janna, and build up colored art."

"Why, I am," cried Joanna, astonished. "You don't think I want to forsake﻿—*us*. Not at all. But I want to show *us* to the world. I am colored, of course, but American first. Why shouldn't I speak to all America?"

"H'm, I suppose you're right. You ought to win out if anyone can. You work hard enough, Janna. You're eighteen now, aren't you? Well, you've got a good ways to go. How old is Peter?"

===Chinese===
「你在哈佛那几年，西尔维娅也没死啊。」乔安娜刻薄地回敬。

「她才不是没事呢！可我敢发誓，她一直把我搁在心底。倒是你，又是唱又是跳，还打着一堆登台演戏的异想天开的主意！你最好守好自己人这一边，小乔，去建设有色人种的艺术。」

「咦，我正是这个意思啊，」乔安娜讶然叫道，「你总不至于以为我想背弃——咱们自己吧。完全没有的事。可我要把咱们展示给全世界看。我当然是有色人种，但首先是美国人。我为什么不该对全体美国人发声呢？」

「嗯，我想你说得对。只要有谁能成，就该是你。你也够刻苦的了，小乔。你现在十八了，是吧？好嘛，你前头的路还长着呢。彼得多大了？」

===Original===
"Twenty. He lost a lot of time when he was little. That's why he's so late entering college."

"Well look here, what are you going to do with him?"

"I may not have a chance to do anything with him, Mr. Busybody."

"Phew, isn't it hot! Thank goodness here's the house. Run along and get your brother-in-law a long, cold drink."

===Chinese===
「二十。他小时候耽误了不少年头，所以上大学才这么晚。」

「哎，我说，你打算把他怎么办？」

「我未必有机会把他怎么样呢，爱管闲事先生。」

「嚯，这天可真热！谢天谢地，到家了。快去给你姐夫弄一大杯清凉的长饮来。」

===Original===
He stayed after dinner﻿—they had it on Sundays at three﻿—and talked away the rest of the afternoon to Joel in the long dark dining room.

"It's cool here," said Joel, handing him a cigar. "Light up and tell me how's Harlem?"

"Great, sir. It's the place for colored people. Let us get you a house up there. Pick you up something fine in One Hundred and Thirty-first Street." Brian, too, had gone into real estate as Alexander's partner.

===Chinese===
饭后他留了下来——星期天的饭三点开——在那间狭长幽暗的餐厅里，陪着乔尔把下半晌的时光都聊了过去。

「这儿凉快，」乔尔递给他一支雪茄说，「点上，跟我讲讲哈莱姆近况如何？」

「好极了，先生。那才是有色人种该待的地方。让我们上那边给您寻所房子，在一百三十一街（One Hundred and Thirty-first Street）给您挑套好的。」布莱恩也已入了房地产生意，做了亚历山大的合伙人。

===Original===
Joel rolled his cigar from one side of his mouth to the other. "Don't know but what I might. This neighborhood's gone down. Let me see your house."

"Yes, sir, I will. Has﻿—er﻿—Sylvia said anything to you about me? I'm getting along pretty well now, sir."

"What should she say? Here Joanna, come take this lovesick boy off my hands!"

Joanna came, serene and cool, a little prim in her pale yellow dress and soft floppy hat of tan chiffon. She handed Brian his Panama.

===Chinese===
乔尔把雪茄从嘴的一边碾到另一边。「也不是不能考虑。这一带是败落了。把你们的房子带来给我瞧瞧。」

「一定，先生。西尔维娅——呃——跟您说起过我什么吗？我如今混得相当不错了，先生。」

「她能说什么？喂，乔安娜，来把这个害相思病的小子从我手里领走吧！」

乔安娜来了，安详而冷静，一身淡黄色衣裙，头上一顶棕褐色雪纺绸软塌帽，显得有几分拘谨。她把巴拿马草帽递给布莱恩。

===Original===
"I'm ready, Brian."

Joel stopped them for a moment, clapped the boy on the shoulder. "It's all right as far as her mother and I are concerned, Brian."

The two went off and heard a gracious, mellow-voiced woman fill a hall with sound that made them forget the heat.

===Chinese===
「我准备好了，布莱恩。」

乔尔把他们叫住片刻，拍了拍小伙子的肩膀。「就她母亲和我这边而言，这事算是成了，布莱恩。」

两人出了门，去听一位仪态雍容、嗓音圆润的女歌唱家演唱，满堂歌声使他们忘却了暑热。

===Original===
"My collar's wringing wet, and I never thought of it. Wonderful how music can make people forget."

"Even color," said Joanna thoughtfully. "Did you see that white woman next to me edge away when I sat down? But when she heard me humming after it was over, she leaned over and asked me if I knew the words."

"I wondered what you were talking about. Awfully jolly of you to have taken pity on me tonight, Janna dear. You marry Peter and all four of us will go to these concerts and sit in the gallery and come home praising God from whom all blessings flow."

===Chinese===
「我的衣领湿得能拧出水来，我却浑然不觉。音乐竟能叫人忘忧，真是奇妙。」

「连肤色也能忘掉，」乔安娜若有所思地说，「你看见我坐下时，旁边那个白人女人往边上躲了吗？可等散场后她听见我哼那曲子，又凑过来问我知不知道歌词。」

「我还纳闷你们在聊什么呢。今晚多亏你发善心可怜我，陪我来了，亲爱的小乔。你嫁给彼得，往后咱们四个就都能来听这样的音乐会，坐在顶层楼座里，回家一路赞美上帝——赞美那万福之源。」

===Original===
"It certainly sounds nice. Only we mustn't forget Philip. Don't ring the bell, here's the key."

He took it. "All right about Philip. Maggie is fond of music, too."

Joanna, in the act of entering the door, stepped back and faced him sharply. "What's Maggie got to do with it?"

===Chinese===
「听上去确实美得很。只是咱们可不能把菲利普忘了。别按铃，我这儿有钥匙。」

他接过钥匙。「菲利普嘛，没说的。玛吉也喜爱音乐。」

乔安娜一只脚正跨进门，闻言退了回来，疾言厉色地面对着他。「玛吉跟这有什么相干？」

===Original===
"Well, she and Phil. They've always paired off together, haven't they? Just like you and Peter, just like Sylvia and me."

"She wouldn't dare," said Joanna fiercely. "Why, Philip﻿—he's going to be somebody great, wonderful, a Garibaldi, a Toussaint! And Maggie, Maggie's just nobody, Brian. Why, do you know what she's taking up? Hair work, straightening hair, salves and shampoos and curling-irons."

"Joanna, you're an utter snob. I always knew you looked down on people unless they were following some mad will o' the wisp. Maggie's as good as any of us. Why look here, she graduated from high school with Sylvia. You can't look down on her."

"Sylvia's my sister, thank you. She's Joel Marshall's daughter. She has background, she knows good music and pictures and worth while people."

===Chinese===
「呃，她同菲尔呗。他们俩一向配成一对，不是吗？就像你和彼得，就像西尔维娅和我。」

「她胆敢！」乔安娜勃然道，「菲利普——他将来是要做伟人的，了不起的伟人，一个加里波第（Garibaldi），一个杜桑！可玛吉，玛吉算个什么人物，布莱恩。哼，你知道她在学什么吗？做头发的手艺，把头发拉直，什么发膏、洗发水、烫发钳。」

「乔安娜，你是个彻头彻尾的势利眼。我早知道，人家要不是在追什么疯狂的鬼火，你就要看低人家三分。玛吉同咱们任何一个人一样出色。你瞧，她可是同西尔维娅一道高中毕业的。你可没资格看低她。」

「西尔维娅是我姐姐，谢了。她是乔尔·马歇尔的女儿。她有家世，她懂好音乐、好画，结识有分量的人。」

===Original===
"You talk like a silly book. What's that got to do with it? And, anyway, you can't stop it now."

"What's the reason I can't?"

"Well, good Lord, it must be as good as settled. Why Maggie thinks﻿—only today﻿—Oh﻿—here, I've said enough. Thanks awfully for a nice evening, Jan﻿—"

"What'd she say, Brian?"

===Chinese===
「你说话像本蠢书里出来似的。这跟那有什么相干？再说，事到如今你也拦不住了。」

「凭什么拦不住？」

「哎呀，老天爷，这事八成早定下了。玛吉她想——就在今天——噢——得，我说得够多了。多谢你今晚陪我，度过了一个愉快的夜晚，小乔——」

「她说了什么，布莱恩？」

===Original===
"Well, you know we were coming home from church and you and Bye were ahead and I said, 'Look at the lucky pair.'"

"Yes, never mind me. Well, well?"

"And she said, 'You miss Sylvia, don't you, Brian?' 'You bet,' I told her.

And she looked at me﻿—you know how Maggie can look﻿—she said, 'Just like I miss Philip, I guess.'"

===Chinese===
「是这样，你知道，我们一块儿从教堂回来，你和拜走在前头，我就说：『瞧这对幸运儿。』」

「是，别管我了。快说，怎么样？」

「她就说：『你想西尔维娅了，是不是，布莱恩？』『可不是嘛，』我告诉她。」

「她看了我一眼——你知道玛吉那种眼神——她说：『大概就像我想菲利普那样吧。』」

===Original===
Joanna grew visibly taller. "You let her say that, Brian Spencer?"

"Well, how could I stop her? Of course she misses Phil. And quit acting 'offended pride,' Joanna. Heavens, doesn't Sylvia sometimes do sewing?"

"Oh, but that's different, she creates, she's an artist﻿—"

"Artist your grandmother! Sleep it off, Janna. Good night." He went off, striding down the quiet street.

===Chinese===
乔安娜分明是拔高了身子。「你竟容她说出这种话，布莱恩·斯宾塞？」

「唉，我怎么拦得住她？她自然是想念菲尔的。还有，别摆你那副『自尊受伤』的架势了，乔安娜。天哪，西尔维娅难道就从不做针线活吗？」

「哦，那可不一样，她是在创造，她是艺术家——」

「艺术家，见你的鬼！回去睡一觉，把这股气性睡没了，小乔。晚安。」他大步流星地走进静谧的街道，去了。

===Original===
Joanna closed the door and crept quietly up to her room. Seated in a wicker armchair in a stream of gold summer moonlight, she spent a long time in deep thought.

Maggie and Philip! Maggie! Of late she and Philip had had many a long talk. He'd lean against the mantelpiece﻿—his restless fingers caressing a little black statuette:

"Jan, I'll talk to you, because you've always cared about this kind of thing. Raise a monument﻿—more-enduring-than-bronze sort of business, you know. When I graduate﻿—by the way, I think I'll be elected Phi Beta Kappa next year﻿—I've got a scheme, I've got a plan that will work all right. Father will be proud of me, you'll see. And you, too, old girl, you've always been a bright beacon light. You stick to this stage business, you'll win out. There'll be a twin star constellation.
