'The well known Marshalls, Joanna and Philip.' We'll make the whole world realize what colored people can do. Nothing short of 'battle, murder or sudden death' is to interfere."

===Chinese===
乔安娜关上门，悄悄溜回自己房里。她坐在一张藤靠椅上，沐着一道金色夏夜的月光，沉思了许久。

玛吉和菲利普！玛吉！近来她和菲利普有过许多次长谈。他会倚在壁炉架旁——不安分的手指摩挲着一尊小小的黑色雕像：

「小乔，我愿意同你谈，因为你一向关心这类事。立一座纪念碑——比青铜更恒久的那一类事业，你懂的。等我毕业——对了，我看明年我准能入选优等生荣誉学会（Phi Beta Kappa）——我有一个构想，有一个准能行得通的计划。父亲会为我骄傲的，你瞧着吧。还有你，老姑娘，你一向是一盏明亮的灯塔。你守住登台这一行，你会成功的。到时候便是一对双星同辉。『鼎鼎大名的马歇尔兄妹，乔安娜与菲利普。』咱们要让全世界看看有色人种能做出什么来。除了『刀兵、凶杀与暴毙』，什么也阻挡不得。」

===Original===
He, too, had been bitten by the desire to make the most of his life. And now here was Maggie Ellersley.

"What ambition has she?" Joanna asked herself fiercely, forgetting to measure the depth of the abyss of poverty and wretchedness from which Maggie had sprung. "She shan't spoil my brother's chances."

Rushing over to her little desk, she pulled out a piece of tan stationery and began a note.

"Dear Maggie﻿—"

===Chinese===
他也同样被那份把生命用到极致的欲望咬住了。可如今偏偏冒出个玛吉·埃勒斯利。

「她有什么抱负？」乔安娜狠狠地问自己，全忘了去掂量玛吉从中挣扎出来的那个贫穷困苦的深渊有多深。「她休想毁了我哥哥的前程。」

她奔到小书桌前，抽出一张棕黄色信纸，动笔写信。

「亲爱的玛吉——」

## XI / 十一

===Original===
Peter had accompanied Maggie as far as the subway station. “You won’t mind if I don’t go all the way home with you, Maggie? Fact is, I don’t feel so well today, so if you’ll excuse me—” His voice trailed indeterminately.

Maggie smiled at him nicely. She was oddly happy at this moment. Linking her name with Philip’s, as she had, gave her an odd sense of freedom, of sureness. “And Brian didn’t seem at all surprised,” she kept thinking to herself over and over.

She answered out loud, “That’s all right, Peter. Go home and rest. I’m going to be in the house only a minute, anyway.” She looked at him meaningly. “I guess both of us have a lot to think of. Goodbye.” She flashed down the steps, looked back; a second later a slender golden hand waved to him from the gloom of the subway.

“Now I don’t know what she meant,” thought Peter, pushing his hat back from his hot forehead, and immediately turning to another idea. “I’d like to punch that fresh Brian’s head. Oh, Janna, how could you go off with him?”

===Chinese===
彼得只把玛吉送到了地铁站。「玛吉，我不送你到家门口了，你别介意吧？不瞒你说，我今天身上不大舒服，你若肯体谅我——」他的声音含含糊糊地低了下去。

玛吉和颜悦色地朝他一笑。这一刻，她心里有种说不出的快活。像方才那样，把自己的名字同菲利普的连在一起，给了她一种奇异的自由感、笃定感。「布莱恩好像一点儿也不吃惊呢。」这句话在她心里翻来覆去地念叨。

她出声答道：「不打紧，彼得。回家歇着去吧。反正我进屋待不了一会儿。」她意味深长地看了他一眼。「我想，咱们俩都有一肚子事要想。再见。」她一闪身奔下台阶，又回过头来；一秒钟后，一只纤细的金色小手从地铁站的幽暗里朝他挥了挥。

「她这话到底什么意思，我倒不明白。」彼得想着，把帽子从滚烫的额头上往后一推，随即又转上另一个念头。「我真想照那张轻狂的布莱恩的脑袋揍上一拳。哦，乔娜，你怎么能跟他走呢？」

===Original===
Down in the subway train Maggie sat smiling a little inanely. Of late, her feeling for Philip had taken a definite form; she wanted, as always, desperately to marry, and to marry well in order to secure for herself the decent respectability for which those first arid fourteen years of her life had created an almost morbid obsession. But she knew now that the one man through whom she wanted to secure that respectability was Philip Marshall. She loved him.

“If the way I wanted him at first, dear God, was a sin, you must forgive me. Oh, Philip, Philip, have a good time in Philadelphia today. I bet you’re at a meeting of some kind this minute.” The picture of his favorite attitude came before her, and she smiled more broadly.

A white man sitting opposite mistook the smile and leaned forward, leering a little. She turned her head quickly, noting as she did so that something about his build made her think of Henderson Neal, her mother’s roomer.

===Chinese===
地铁车厢里，玛吉坐着，唇边挂着一点痴痴的笑。近来，她对菲利普的感情已然有了个明确的形状；她一如既往地拼命想结婚，而且要嫁得好，好为自己挣得那份体面人的尊荣——她生命最初那十四年干涸的岁月，叫她对这体面生了一种近乎病态的执念。而如今她明白了，她要借以挣得这份尊荣的男人，唯有菲利普·马歇尔一个。她爱他。

「亲爱的上帝，假使我起初想要他的那个念头算是罪过，你务必饶恕我。哦，菲利普，菲利普，今天在费城好好玩吧。我敢打赌，你这会儿准是在开什么会。」他最得意的那副姿态浮上她的心头，她的笑意又深了一层。

对面坐着一个白人，会错了这笑的意思，身子前倾，一脸邪气地瞅过来。她急忙扭过头去，一面留意到，那人身形上有几分什么，叫她想起了亨德森·尼尔（Henderson Neal）——她母亲招住的房客。

===Original===
She was to go motoring with him this afternoon. He had asked her very often of late. Usually she spent Sundays with Philip and Sylvia and Brian, sometimes with Joanna and Peter. But since the first two were away, she might just as well spend the time with Mr. Henderson. He would have a nice car, she knew; twice before he had taken herself and her mother out. It had really been very nice. She rather fancied he must work in a garage, he came riding up to the house so often. She wished a little nervously that she hadn’t promised to go, it would be nice to sit quietly in her room or in the long, sparsely furnished parlor and think.

Still it was hot, and if there were any air to be got they’d catch it in an automobile.

She ran up the subway steps and hurried toward Fifty-third Street. Somehow she didn’t care to keep Mr. Neal waiting.

===Chinese===
今天下午，她要同他去兜风。近来他三番五次地邀她。往常星期天，她总是同菲利普、西尔维娅和布莱恩一起过，有时同乔安娜和彼得。可既然前两位出了远门，这点时光，倒不如拿来陪陪亨德森先生。他准会有一辆好车，这她知道；先前他已载过她和她母亲两回。实在是挺痛快的。她倒有点自作聪明地认定，他必是在汽车行里做事——不然哪能三天两头驱车往她家门口凑。她又微微有些心慌地懊悔，不该答应去的；安安静静坐在自己房里，或是坐在那间狭长而陈设寥寥的客厅里想一想心事，该有多好。

不过天到底热，倘若还有一丝凉风可寻，也只有坐上汽车才追得着。

她奔上地铁台阶，匆匆朝五十三街赶去。不知怎么，她不愿教尼尔先生久等。

===Original===
There was still a quarter of an hour before he might be expected. She bathed her face, shook out her short, thick hair, twisted it back from her forehead. Next she crowned her oval, deep-cream face with a wide black hat, whose somberness was repeated in a broad velvet ribbon around the waist of her white dress.

But she looked anything but somber as she ran to the door at the whirr of the motor.

“Going, Ma,” she called back. Mr. Neal climbed out of the car and helped her in.

He didn’t look so old—elderly—today, she thought to herself, noting the straightness of his flat back and the smooth bronze of his closely shaven cheek. Evidently his beard was very strong and this had lent hitherto a somewhat heavy cast to his face. But today he was shaven to the blood. Maggie was used to studying men. It was a legacy from the old days, when failure to analyze a prospective roomer’s appearance might jeopardize a week’s rent. She noticed Neal’s hands at the wheel, powerful and sinewy with broad square fingertips. He was still baffling, but not so bad, she thought.

===Chinese===
离他该到的钟点还剩一刻钟。她洗了脸，抖开又短又厚的头发，从前额往后一拧。接着，一顶宽大的黑帽加冕似的端落在她那张鹅蛋形的深奶油色脸庞上；帽色的那份沉肃，又在白裙腰间一条宽宽的丝绒缎带上应和了一遍。

可马达嗡地一响、她奔向门口的时候，那副模样哪里还有半点沉肃。

「走了，妈。」她回头喊道。尼尔先生下了车，扶她上了车。

今天他瞧着不那么见老——不那么一副上了年纪的样子了，她心里想，一面留意着他扁平的背脊挺得笔直，剃得干干净净的面颊泛着光滑的古铜色。想必他的胡须生得极重，以往这才给他脸上蒙了几分沉滞之气。可今天，他把脸刮得精光，几乎刮到见血。玛吉惯于打量男人。这是旧日留下的本事——那时候，若不会相一相前来租房的房客的模样，兴许就要赔上一礼拜的房钱。她看住尼尔握在方向盘上的那双手：有力，虬劲，指尖方方正正、宽宽大大。他仍旧叫人摸不透，不过也不算糟，她想。

===Original===
“Of course, not like Philip, but nice enough to go around with, and this is a dandy car.” She looked at him again sideways. He caught her glance.

“Thinkin’ I ain’t so bad maybe, Miss Maggie?”

She blushed, confused, not so much at his catching her eye as at the completeness with which he had read her thought.

“You certainly look nice in that suit, Mr. Neal. It’s different from what most men wear, isn’t it?”

===Chinese===
「当然喽，比不得菲利普，可也够体面、够一块儿出门的了，何况这是辆呱呱叫的车。」她又侧过眼去瞟他。他捉住了她的目光。

「这会儿是不是在心里寻思，我兴许还不算太糟，玛吉小姐？」

她红了脸，心下慌乱——倒不甚为他瞧见她瞟他，更为他把她心里那点念头读了个通通透透。

「您这身西装真是体面，尼尔先生。跟寻常男人们穿的不大一样，是不是？」

===Original===
“Likely as not. I picked it up in London last time I crossed the big pond.”

“You’ve been to Europe?” asked Maggie all ears.

“Yes to England, France, Spain, Germany *and* Italy. They was a time,” he said in his deliberately incorrect way, “when I thought I’d stay in them parts forever, but I come back. Used to valet for a rich white fellow. Took me everywhere with him. Wanted to carry me to Africa lion-hunting. But I quit him cold. If you want to hunt lions, go to it. Me, I’m a-goin’ t’stay right here.”

He spoke with a heavy emphasis on the last word which lent a curious whimsicality to his speech.

===Chinese===
「八成是吧。上回我过大水塘上伦敦，捎带手买下的。」

「您去过欧洲？」玛吉竖起了耳朵问。

「去过。英国、法国、西班牙、德国——还有意大利。」他说着，用的是那副故意不合文法的腔调，「有那么一阵子，我还想着就在那些地方待上一辈子呢，可我回来了。从前伺候一个有钱的白人老爷，当贴身跟班。他带我四处走。还想捎我去非洲猎狮子。可我干脆利落把他给辞了。他要猎狮子，随他去猎。我呢，我就守在这儿不动窝儿。」

他把末尾那个字咬得极重，给他这话平添了一股古怪的诙谐劲儿。

===Original===
“This is the first time you’ve ever talked about yourself, Mr. Neal. Tell me some more, it’s mighty interesting.”

He had been everything from a farmer to a chauffeur, he told her, confirming her idea that his present occupation was concerned with the manipulation of cars.

“And I’ve been a lot of places and I’ve seen a lot of people. But you don’t want to hear about me, Miss Maggie. They ain’t nothing in me to interest a little lady like you. Now, on the other hand, seems to me, you might make real interestin’ talkin’.”

He had a nice smile, Maggie thought.

===Chinese===
「这可是您头一回讲起自己的事，尼尔先生。再讲点儿吧，有意思得很。」

他告诉她，从农夫到汽车司机，他什么都做过——这倒印证了她的猜想：他眼下的营生，总归是同摆弄汽车打交道的。

「我到过好些地方，见过好些人。可你不会想听我这个人的事，玛吉小姐。我这个人肚里没什么货色，入不了你这样一位小姑娘的眼。倒是反过来，依我看，你讲起话来才真叫有味儿呢。」

他笑起来挺受看，玛吉想。

===Original===
“There isn’t much to tell,” she smiled back at him. “There’s just my mother and me. I’m twenty-one and I’ve been out of school three years. I work in the office of Mr. Marshall, the caterer; you know him?”

“Know of him, Miss Maggie, know of him. Son’s a real-estate agent, ain’t he?”

“Yes. Well, I’m a sort of overseer-bookkeeper. In my spare time I’m taking up a course in hairdressing. You know there’s a Madame Harkness who’s invented a method of softening hair, and of taking the harshness out of your folks’ locks.” She laughed at him. “You know I think there’s a big future in it. It ought to mean a lot to us. Everybody wants to be beautiful, and every woman looks better if her hair is soft and manageable.”

“Reckon you don’t need to use no such preparation, Miss Maggie.”

===Chinese===
「我没什么可讲的，」她含笑回他，「家里就母亲和我两个。我二十一岁，出校门三年了。我在宴席承办商马歇尔先生的办事处做职员；您认得他吧？」

「知道他，玛吉小姐，听说过。他家儿子是做房地产生意的经纪人，对吧？」

「对。我呢，算是半个管事、半个账房。空下来的工夫，我在修一门美发的课程。您知道有位哈克尼斯夫人（Madame Harkness），发明了一门把头发变软的手艺，专治你们这号人又硬又倔的头发。」她笑着打趣他。「您知道吗，我觉得这里头大有前程。这桩事对我们该顶要紧的。人人都想长得美，头发一柔顺听话，哪个女人都更好看。」

「我寻思你自个儿用不着使这些个方子，玛吉小姐。」

===Original===
“No, I don’t, fortunately, but I’ll be glad to help those that do. I love to see people look nice; like to look nice myself.”

“You sure do, you’re like a little yellow flower, growin’ in that house.” He gave her a keen level glance whose boldness was softened by his serious manner.

“Let’s stop talking about me,” said Maggie with sudden confusion. “Don’t you want to hear about my mother?”

“Well, not as much as about some others.”

“Anyway, she’s been a wonderful mother. My father died when I was about eight, and left us nothing. Mother has been hard put to it at times. That’s why I want to learn the hair-trade. I want to set up a business for myself some day. If I succeed, both mother and I can live on easy street.”

===Chinese===
「可不是嘛，幸而用不着。可我乐意帮衬那些用得着的人。我就爱瞧见人人齐齐整整的；自个儿也爱齐整。」

「你就是嘛。你就像一朵开在那宅子里的小黄花。」他朝她投来锐利而平直的一瞥，那股孟浪劲儿，叫他一本正经的做派冲淡了几分。

「别说我的事了，」玛吉蓦地一阵慌乱，说道，「您不想听听我母亲的事吗？」

「想倒也想，可比不上想听另外一些人的。」

「反正，她一直是个了不起的母亲。我八岁上下，父亲就没了，什么也没给我们留下。母亲有些时候，真是撑得艰难。所以我才要学这门头发的营生。我想有朝一日，自己开起一爿生意。倘若成了，我和母亲就都能过上安生富泰的日子了。」

===Original===
“You’d ought to be living there now. A delicate little girl like you’s got no business having to worry her pretty head about taking care of herself.” He bent on her a long considering look. “There’s many a man would be willing to take that job off your hands. I bet I know of one.” An odd bashfulness seemed to descend upon him.

“Perhaps he’s going to propose,” thought Maggie innocently enraptured, “wouldn’t that be great?” She pictured Sylvia’s surprise when she should tell her. His clumsy circumlocution, his heavy deference, delighted her. Philip of course was wonderful, but he was inclined, like all the Marshalls, to be a little superior. Well, why shouldn’t they be?

She sighed.

===Chinese===
「你这会儿就该过着那样的日子才是。像你这样娇嫩的小姑娘，本犯不着操这份心，愁着那颗好看的小脑袋怎么养活自己。」他朝她俯下长久端详的目光。「有的是男人，情愿把这桩差事从你手里接过去。我敢说，我就认得这么一个。」一种古怪的腼腆，仿佛落到了他身上。

「莫非他要开口求婚了，」玛吉天真烂漫，心头狂喜，「那可有多好？」她想象着自己说给西尔维娅听时，西尔维娅那副吃惊的样子。他那笨拙的兜圈子，他那沉沉的恭敬，都叫她欢喜。菲利普当然好得很，只是他跟马歇尔家所有人一样，难免有点儿居高临下。唉，人家凭什么不该呢？

她叹了口气。

===Original===
Her silence seemed to put an end to his sentimental maunderings, for he began to talk about the car, explaining its mechanism. Once, too, he turned and swore fluently at a motorist who passed him too closely. At the sudden passion which convulsed his face Maggie drew back, a little frightened. He noticed it, and immediately ironed out the lines of anger.

“You must forgive me, Miss Maggie. It made me so angry to think that that fool might have caused an accident which would have injured you.”

She thought with the ignorant pride of a young girl that it would be very easy for her to manage him. Shortly after that they turned around and came home. Maggie was glad when they reached the house, for she had many things to think about. Shutting off the motor, he followed her into the hall and they stood there a minute, his powerful dark figure looming over her.

She thanked him prettily. “It was very nice of you, Mr. Neal. You’ve been most kind to mother and me.” As she sped lightly up the stairs she forgot him completely.

===Chinese===
她的沉默，似乎叫他那番多愁善感的絮叨收了场，因为他转而谈起这辆车来，讲解它的机关构造。有一回，他还回过头去，冲一个贴他贴得太近的司机，劈里啪啦骂出一长串。他脸上骤然痉挛起的那股狂怒，吓得玛吉往后一缩。他察觉了，当即把满脸的怒纹熨得平平整整。

「你务必饶我这一遭，玛吉小姐。一想到那个蠢货险些闯出大祸、伤着了你，我这火就压不住。」

她怀着年轻姑娘那种不谙世事的骄傲想道：要驾驭这个男人，于她易如反掌。此后不久，他们便掉头回了家。到家时玛吉是欢喜的，因为她有太多的事要想。他熄了马达，随她进了门厅，两人立了片刻，他那健壮的黑影，黑塔似的罩在她头顶。

她客客气气谢过了他。「您真是太好心了，尼尔先生。您待我和母亲，一向周到得很。」她脚步轻盈地飞奔上楼，转瞬之间，就把他忘了个干净。

===Original===
Her windows were open and a full moon flooded her room with light. “Oh, Philip if I only knew how you felt,” she murmured, getting up and leaning out the window, gazing into the still, hot air. The people next door were in their backyard; one of their boys was playing an accordion. A little thin tinkle of voices floated up to her. How content other people seemed!

Her mind was feverish—she had concentrated so on her other desires, a decent home, a reasonable education, the means of making a little extra money. It seemed to her she couldn’t find the strength to focus the flame of her ambition on Philip’s kind but immobile attitude. He was so uncomprehending. She turned back to the room again and stretched her arms to the shadowy wall.

“If you’d only say one word, Philip. I’d wait forever.” It was the uncertainty that sickened her spirit. “Yet,” she thought, growing suddenly cold, “suppose I should be made certain—the wrong way. Perhaps you’ve met a girl in Philadelphia.”

She determined the suspense was best. “You’ve been my hope so long, if you should fail me what would I do? Besides, I love you, Philip.”

She lay half the night, very still and very wakeful in her white iron bed. The morning brought back her old sanguineness, she was to have a very full day; until early forenoon there was work in Mr. Marshall’s office, and in the late afternoon Madame Harkness’ Method of Hair Culture claimed her.

===Chinese===
窗子开着，一轮满月，把她的房间泡在光里。「哦，菲利普，我要是知道你怎么想就好了。」她喃喃着，起身伏在窗口，朝外面凝滞而燥热的夜色里望去。隔壁人家在自家后院里；他们家一个男孩在拉手风琴。一缕细细碎碎的人语声，飘上她窗口来。别人家的日子，显得多么安稳自足！

她的脑子烧得慌——她那点力气，早叫别的种种欲望占满了：一个体面的家，一份说得过去的教育，一点挣外快的法子。如今她像是再也聚不起力气，把满腔心气的火焰，拢到菲利普那和善却纹丝不动的态度上去。他太不解人了。她转回房里，朝着那面幽暗的墙，张开了双臂。

「你只要说一个字，菲利普。等多久我都甘愿。」正是这份悬而未决，熬得她心口发苦。「可是，」她突然浑身发冷，想道，「万一那确定，是以不对的路子来的呢。说不定，你在费城遇上了一位姑娘。」

她拿定了主意，还是这样悬着最好。「你做我的指望这么久了，你若负了我，我可怎么办？再说，我爱你，菲利普。」

她在那张白色的铁床上躺了半夜，一动不动，毫无睡意。清晨又带回她旧日的那股子乐天劲儿——这一天，她要过得满满当当：一早到上午，在马歇尔先生的办事处做工；傍晚时分，哈克尼斯头发养护法（Method of Hair Culture）又要来占用她了。

===Original===
She came home, hot and deliciously tired.

“There’s a letter for you,” her mother told her. “Wash your face and eat your supper first. I want to get through’s quick as I can. Mis’ Sparrow and me, we’re going to a meeting.”

Maggie spied the letter in the gloom of the hall. It was from Sylvia probably; her heart hoped it was from Philip. But she put the thought away from her as too audacious. “Now just for that,” she told herself whimsically, “I won’t let you touch that letter till after supper.” Smiling, she washed her face and changed into something cool and old that she could lounge in later up in her room, while she read Sylvia’s letter.

Supper over, the dishes washed and her mother started in the direction of Mis’ Sparrow’s residence, Maggie went for her letter. Even in the half gloom she descried with a sudden pang that the superscription was unfamiliar. “Not from Philip, not even from Sylvia. Well, why should they write me?” she chided herself bravely.

===Chinese===
她回到家来，一身燥热，乏得舒坦。

「有你一封信。」母亲告诉她。「先洗把脸，先吃晚饭。我想赶紧吃完了好动身。我和斯派罗太太，还要去赴一个会呢。」

玛吉在门厅的昏暗里瞥见了那封信。多半是西尔维娅来的；她心里却巴望是菲利普来的。可她把这念头撵开了，嫌它太不知轻重。「就冲这个，」她孩子气地对自己说，「晚饭之前，偏不许你碰那封信。」她笑吟吟地洗了脸，换上一身凉快的旧衣裳——待会儿好在楼上自己房里窝着，读西尔维娅的信。

晚饭吃罢，碗也洗净了，母亲也朝斯派罗太太家那头去了，玛吉这才去取她的信。纵在半昏的光线里，她也心口猛地一沉，看出信封上的字迹眼生。「不是菲利普的，连西尔维娅的也不是。也是，人家凭什么给我写信呢？」她给自己壮着胆，数落自己。

===Original===
In the waning but clear light in her room she could see plainly that the letter must be from a stranger. Yet there was something vaguely familiar about the writing after all.

She slit the envelope.

> “**Dear Maggie**: [the letter ran]
> “You’ll be surprised to get this letter, yet something tells me I should write it. It’s about you and Philip. [‘What’s this?’ said Maggie, startled.] I have learned, Maggie, that you are taking Philip’s kindnesses to you too seriously, that perhaps you are thinking of marrying him.
> “I think you ought to know that such an arrangement would not be at all pleasing to our family, nor would it be good for Philip. I’ve often heard my mother say that only people of like position should marry each other, and I hardly think that would be true in the case of you and Philip. Then you must consider the future. My father is very ambitious for us and lately Philip has shown that he means to embark on a real career. You can see that a girl of your lowly aims would only be a hindrance to him. Philip Marshall cannot marry a hairdresser!”

===Chinese===
她房里的天光将尽未尽，倒还晴亮，她看得明明白白：这信必是个陌生人写来的。可那笔迹，到底又有几分说不出的眼熟。

她裁开了信封。

> 「**亲爱的玛吉**：〔信上这样起头〕
> 「你收到这封信一定纳闷，可有个声音叮嘱我，这信非写不可。说的是你和菲利普的事。〔『这是什么话？』玛吉吃了一惊〕玛吉，我得知了，你把菲利普待你的好意看得过于认真，说不定，你正盘算着嫁给他。
> 「我想你应当明白，这样的安排，我们全家断然不会乐意，对菲利普也没有好处。我常听母亲说，唯有门第相当的人，才该彼此结亲；而你和菲利普之间，我怕算不上相当。再者，你总得想想前程。我父亲对我们寄望极高，近来菲利普也显出，他是有心闯出一番真正的事业的。你瞧得出，像你这样志向低微的姑娘，只会拖累他。菲利普·马歇尔不能娶一个美发师！」

===Original===
The childish cruel words ran on:

> “Then, too, I am sure he does not care for you in the way you care for him. Don’t you go around sometimes with a Mr. Henderson, or somebody like that? Sylvia met him somehow and Phil didn’t like it and raised a big fuss. Sylvia told him that you knew him and went out with him and Philip said ‘That’s different. Maggie Ellersley can do things that my sisters mustn’t do.’ That doesn’t sound as though he had any serious feeling for you, does it?
> “I guess this will be sort of hard for you to read, but I believe” [Joanna wrote virtuously] “that some day you will thank me for these words.
> “Wouldn’t it be just as well if you didn’t see him for some time after his return?
> “Yours, 

 “**Joanna Marshall**.”
> “P.S. Papa is thinking of buying a house in One Hundred and Thirty-first Street, in Harlem, you know. So we may move after Sylvia and the others come back from Philadelphia. Papa would still keep his office in Fifty-ninth Street. That puts us pretty far away, so if you shouldn’t come up so often, no one would think anything of it.”

===Chinese===
这封信句句稚气，句句刻毒，兀自往下淌：

> 「再者，我敢断定，他对你的情意，远不及你对他的深。你不是有时同一位亨德森先生，或是诸如此类的什么人，一道出门吗？西尔维娅不知怎么碰见过他，菲尔不乐意，还大闹了一场。西尔维娅告诉他，你认得这人，还同他出去过，菲利普便说：『那另当别论。玛吉·埃勒斯利做得的事，我妹妹们就做不得。』这话听上去，可不像他对你有什么正经的情意，是不是？
> 「我料想，这信你读来怕不好受，可我相信」〔乔安娜满心得意地写道〕「有朝一日，你会为这些话感激我。
> 「等他回来以后，你索性有一阵子不去见他，岂不也一样？
> 「你的，

 「**乔安娜·马歇尔**」。
> 「又及：爸爸正盘算着在哈莱姆（Harlem）的一百三十一街买所房子呢，你是知道的。所以等西尔维娅他们几个从费城回来，我们兴许就要搬了。爸爸的办事处，还照旧设在五十九街。那样一来，两家就离得挺远了，往后你不常上我们那边去，也没人会多心。」

===Original===
Maggie folded the letter carefully and put it on her mantelpiece. Then, fully dressed as she still was, she lay down on her bed.

“You poor idiot,” she thought to herself, “you simpleton, you fool, why should the Marshalls want you? They’re rich, respected! Mr. Joel Marshall—you see the name at the head of every committee of colored citizens, and you are nobody, the daughter of a worthless father, and a poor ex-laundress!”

Her mind dwelt briefly on her mother. “Poor Mamma, she expected so much of me! Yet if Philip really cared about me, he wouldn’t care a rap if they did object.” She remembered then his slighting words.

“I hate him,” she said fiercely, “and Joanna and her everlasting ambitions and the pride of all of them. Why, you’re just a beggar to them.” She resumed her merciless self-attack.

===Chinese===
玛吉仔仔细细把信叠好，搁在壁炉台上。然后，连衣裳也没脱，就往床上一躺。

「你这可怜的傻子，」她心里数落自己，「你这呆子，你这蠢货，马歇尔家凭什么要你？人家又有钱，又受人敬重！乔尔·马歇尔先生——有色公民的哪一个委员会里，不是他的名字高居头名？可你是无名小卒，是一个没出息的爹的女儿，是一个穷苦的前洗衣妇的闺女！」

她的心思，在母亲身上稍稍停了停。「可怜的妈妈，她从前对我指望多着呢！可菲利普若真心待我，就算他们反对，他也不会放在心上。」这时，她才想起他那番轻慢的话来。

「我恨他，」她狠狠地说，「恨乔安娜，恨她那没完没了的抱负，恨他们全家的傲气。哼，你在他们眼里，不过是个乞儿。」她又继续那场毫不留情的自我鞭挞。

===Original===
Presently she began to cry great, scalding tears that burned her cheeks and hurt her throat. At eleven o’clock she heard her mother’s step and forced herself to an aching quiet. About midnight she realized that her head ached, that her throat was so dry and parched that it almost rasped.

“To think I should care like this,” she told herself. “Oh, Maggie, Maggie, they’re proud, can’t you copy their pride?”

There were some lemons on the table in the dining room, she remembered. At least she could ease her tortured throat. Hot though it was she put on her felt bedroom slippers, so that her step on the creaking stairs might not disturb her mother.

===Chinese===
不多时，她哭起来了，大颗大颗滚烫的泪，烧着她的脸颊，苦着她的喉咙。十一点钟，她听见母亲的脚步声，硬逼着自己归于一种发疼的静默。约莫半夜，她觉出自己头也在痛，喉咙干得冒烟，几乎要嘶哑出声来。

「想不到，我竟这样放不下。」她对自己说。「哦，玛吉呀玛吉，他们傲气，你就不能学一学人家的傲气吗？」

她记起来，饭厅的桌上有几只柠檬。至少，能润一润她那受苦的喉咙。天虽说热，她还是趿上了毡子的睡鞋，免得脚步踩得楼梯吱呀作响，搅了母亲。

===Original===
The quiet lower rooms struck her with their awful solemnity, added to her woe. She sat there at the dining room table, one hand clutching the forgotten lemon, the other flung on the red-checked table cloth, above her dark bowed head.

Two conflicts were raging within her. A twofold stream of disappointment overwhelmed. Not only had Philip not made love to her but he had despised her, not considered her the peer of his sisters. And how was she to mend her precarious fortunes? She was not strong, her mother was aging; suppose, before she got on her feet, she should fall back into the old hateful abyss. As it was she would never enter Mr. Marshall’s office again.

===Chinese===
楼下几间屋子静得吓人，一片森然的肃穆，愈发添了她的悲哀。她在饭厅的桌边坐下，一只手攥着那只早被忘却的柠檬，另一只手摊在红格子的桌布上，乌黑的头，深深垂在臂弯上面。

两重争斗，在她心里翻腾。失望的双股洪流将她没顶。菲利普非但不曾向她求爱，反而看轻了她，不曾把她当作他妹妹们的同辈。况且，她那朝不保夕的境况，又拿什么去收拾？她身子不结实，母亲一年老似一年；倘若她还没能站稳脚跟，就先跌回那旧日可恨的深渊里去，可怎么好。事到如今，马歇尔先生的办事处，她是无论如何不会再进了。

===Original===
Her shame and despair heavy upon her, she buried her face deeper on her arm. Someone seemed to say, “Miss Maggie!”

She imagined it, she knew, but even if it were real she did not want to lift that heavy, heavy head.

A powerful but kind hand strove to lift it for her. She looked up then, a blinking figure of misery in the flickering gas flame.

“But Miss Maggie, t’aint ever you. Was you asleep or—was you crying?” Henderson Neal had come in, and spying the light in the dining room had come to investigate.

===Chinese===
羞耻与绝望沉沉压在身上，她把脸更深地理进臂弯。恍惚间，有人唤道：「玛吉小姐！」

她知道是自己听岔了，可就算是真的，她也不想抬起那颗沉甸甸、沉甸甸的头。

一只有力而和善的手，却来替她抬它。她这才抬起头来，成了煤气灯摇曳光焰里一个眨着眼的苦人儿。

「可是玛吉小姐，这可太不像你了。你是睡着了，还是——是在哭啊？」原来亨德森·尼尔进来了，望见饭厅里的灯，特过来瞧个究竟。

===Original===
She blinked at him stupidly.

“Little Miss Maggie, what’s happened to you? You ain’t in trouble?”

“In awful trouble.” Her lips shaped the words stiffly.

His mind, accustomed to the ways of men, jumped to one dread conclusion. “You mean some good for nothin’ feller’s took advantage of you?”

She didn’t understand him at first. “What? Oh, that! No, of course not!” A spasm of horrible amusement crossed her tightly drawn features. “He—he wouldn’t touch me.”

===Chinese===
她怔怔地朝他直眨眼。

「小玛吉小姐，你这是怎么啦？碰上难处了？」

「天大的难处。」她的嘴唇僵僵地吐出这几个字。

他那颗惯见世故的头脑，一步跳到了一个可怕的结论上。「你是说，哪个没出息的家伙，占了你的便宜？」

她起初没听明白。「什么？哦，那个呀！不，当然没有！」一阵可怕的、哭也似笑的抽搐，掠过她紧绷绷的脸。「他——他才不会碰我呢。」

===Original===
She broke into passionate yet stifled weeping. Her mother must not hear her.

Neal’s face twitched. He picked her up in his steely arms, sat down in an old cavernous morris chair and held her back against him like a baby.

“Tell me about it, Miss Maggie; some of them tony fellers bothering you to marry them?”

The supposition was balm to her spirit, but she had schooled herself to honesty. “No, not that—one of them, oh, he never knew—I hoped, oh, Mr. Neal, you see I wanted him to like me—”

“And he doesn’t, and he’s been leading you on? The damned skunk. I’d like to kill him.”

===Chinese===
她放声痛哭起来，却又死死抑着。万万不能教母亲听见。

尼尔的脸抽搐了一下。他把她抱进钢铁似的臂弯，在一把旧而宽大的安乐椅里坐下，让她像婴孩一样，背靠在自己怀里。

「说给我听听吧，玛吉小姐；是哪一位阔气的公子哥儿缠着要娶你，叫你烦心了？」

这猜想，于她受创的心是一味香膏，可她早已把自己调教得诚实。「不，不是那样——其中有一个，唉，他从没知道过——我盼过的，哦，尼尔先生，您明白，我要他喜欢我——」

「他偏不喜欢，还一直吊着你？这该死的畜生。我真想宰了他。」

===Original===
“Don’t say that. He was just being kind. He’d probably be all right if he ever thought about me. You see, it’s his sisters, his sister,” she corrected herself, “she doesn’t consider me good enough.”

“Well, what’s she got to do with it? Can’t the feller speak for himself?”

“That’s just it, I used to go to see them, they don’t come to see me. If the sisters don’t want me, there’s no way I can reach him, particularly since he isn’t interested. I had just hoped that if he kept on seeing me, some day he would grow to like me.”

Neal was nonplussed. This was a puzzle.

“What are you going to do now?”

===Chinese===
「别那么说。他不过是心善罢了。他若哪天把我放在心上，多半也会好好的。您明白，坏就坏在他妹妹们，他一个妹妹，」她改口道，「她觉得我配不上。」

「嗐，那跟她什么相干？那小子自己没长嘴吗？」

「坏就坏在这里。从前，总是我上他们家去，他们从不来我家。做妹妹的若不接纳我，我就够不着他，何况他本来也没那个心思。我不过是指望，他若肯常常见我，兴许哪一天，就会渐渐喜欢上我了。」

尼尔没了主意。这道题，他解不开。

「那你往后打算怎么办？」

===Original===
“Oh, I don’t know. And I’m losing my job now. I got it through them.”

“I see.” He sat silent, studying her a moment. “Look here, Maggie, whyn’t you marry me? I’m old and I’m rough and you see I ain’t no book-learnin’. But I can take care of you—you and your mother, too, and I can dress you pretty, like you’d ought to be, and with money and fine clothes you can do a little lordin’ on your own.”

She hated to offend him. He was so kind. “Mother would never hear of it,” she quavered for lack of a better answer.

“You don’t have to let her know about it,” he said, encouraged by her failure to refuse him flatly. “I’ll get a license in the morning and we’ll slip out after she goes to work. You won’t be sorry. I’ll be kind to you Maggie—girl. I’ve always wanted you to give me a chance.” He added a cunning afterthought.

===Chinese===
「哦，我也不知道。再说，我这饭碗眼看也要丢了。当初还是托他们家的情分才得的呢。」

「我明白了。」他坐着不作声，把她端详了一会儿。「听我说，玛吉，你咋不嫁给我呢？我老，我粗，你也瞧见了，我肚里没一点儿书本学问。可我能养活你——连你母亲一块儿——我还能把你打扮得漂漂亮亮，像你本该有的那样；有了钱，有了好衣裳，你自个儿也能抖一抖威风。」

她不忍叫他下不来台。他心肠这样好。「母亲断断不会答应的。」她想不出更好的答话，声音发着颤。

「不叫她知道就得了。」她没把他一口回绝，他受了鼓舞。「明儿一早我去领结婚证，等她上了工，咱俩就溜出去。你不会后悔的。我会好好待你，玛吉——丫头。」他又转了个狡黠的念头，添上一句：「我早巴望着，你能给我一个机会呢。」

===Original===
“Show these stuck-up friends of yourn, and show ’em quick that you don’t have to go beggin’ for favors. There’s others, yes, not a man that comes into this house that wouldn’t be proud to marry you.”

She began to toy with the idea. Marriage with Neal was not what she wanted, but it represented to her security, a home for herself and her mother, freedom from all the little nagging worries that beset the woman who fights her own way through the world. Perhaps she had aimed too high. This was the sort of person with whom she had grown up; he would not, because he could not, look down on her lowliness. On the contrary, he would place her on a pedestal.

“I’ll think about it,” she promised him finally.

But he knew if she did not take him now, she would never take him. She knew it, too.

===Chinese===
「叫你那帮眼高于顶的朋友瞧瞧，赶紧叫他们瞧瞧：你犯不上上赶着去讨他们的好。天下有的是人，可不是嘛——但凡进这宅子一眼的男人，哪个不想娶你？娶了还觉得体面呢。」

她开始拿这个念头在手里把玩起来。嫁给尼尔，本不是她想要的，可在她眼里，这代表着安稳，代表着她同母亲的一个家，代表着一劳永逸地甩脱那无数琐碎磨人的愁烦——一个独自在世上打拼的女人，总要受这些愁烦的纠缠。兴许，是她把目标立得太高了。他本是同她一块儿长大的这一路人；他不会看轻她的卑微，因为他没本事看高。恰恰相反，他会把她供上高台。

「我考虑考虑吧。」她最后这样应承他。

可他心里明白：她此番若不跟了他，往后就再也不会跟了。她心里，也明白。

===Original===
He set her gently in the chair, and knelt in front of her, barring her escape with his powerful body.

“Listen, Maggie, marry me now, tomorrow. We’ll go to Atlantic City for a few weeks, and come back and go to housekeeping. I don’t have to live here. I just stayed on, first because it was clean and your mother was honest and then because I liked you. I ain’t no lawyer, nor doctor, nor in none of the fine positions your friends hold, but I handle a good bit of money and I’ll get you everything you want.”

He did have money, she knew that. She supposed she ought to find out exactly how he made it. But of course he was honest. And anyway she was too tired, too weak to bother. She could feel his strong will impinging on her own, beating hers down.

“I’ll do it, Mr. Neal.”

===Chinese===
他把她轻轻放回椅子里，跪在她跟前，用他那健壮的身躯，拦死了她的退路。

「听我说，玛吉，这就嫁给我吧，就明天。咱们上大西洋城（Atlantic City）去待上几个礼拜，回来就自立门户过日子。我不必住在这儿。我当初赖着没走，一是图这儿干净、你母亲为人实诚，二嘛，是我喜欢你。我不是律师，不是大夫，也不在你那些朋友的高贵位子上；可我手里过手的钱不算少，你要什么，我就给你置办什么。」

他确实有钱，这她知道。她原该问个明白，这钱他到底是怎么挣下的。可他当然是个实诚人。何况，她也太乏了、太软了，懒怠去计较。她能觉出，他坚强的意志正压上她的意志，把它一分一分压下去。

「我依您，尼尔先生。」

===Original===
“My name’s Henderson, Maggie. You will, you mean it?”

“Yes, tomorrow. But I ought to let my mother know.”

“Oh, no, she might object—mothers hate to see their daughters leave them. But after she sees how well fixed and happy you are, she won’t mind.”

“I guess you’re right. I—I don’t see how I can ever pack. I’m so tired.” Her figure slacked weakly against the chair.

“You don’t need to. Just wear something dark and quiet. We’ll get everything you want in Atlantic City, or maybe Philadelphia.”

“No, no—not in Philadelphia, we won’t stop there now,” she told him feverishly.

===Chinese===
「我的名字叫亨德森，玛吉。你肯的？你是当真的？」

「当真，就明天。可我总该让母亲知道一声。」

「哎，使不得，她兴许不依——当妈的，见不得闺女离开自己。可等她瞧见你过得多么富足、多么如意，她就不会计较了。」

「您说得许是对。我——我真不知道，行李可怎么个收拾法。我太乏了。」她的身子软软地瘫靠在椅背上。

「用不着收拾。只管穿一身素净的深色衣裳。你要的东西，咱们到大西洋城去置办，兴许上费城去买。」

「不，不——别去费城，这回别在费城落脚。」她急煎煎地对他说。

===Original===
“All right. Now run up to bed. Kiss me, Maggie.”

She gave him her cold, stiff lips.

“Good girl! Tomorrow at ten. You ain’t foolin’ me?”

“Oh, no, Mr. Neal!”

“Henderson’s my name. Good night, little girl.”

Shaking, she got up to her room to lie vacant-eyed across the bed, watching the darkness deepen, shade into gray, vanish. The sun came bringing a new day, to her a new life.

===Chinese===
「行。上楼睡去吧。亲亲我，玛吉。」

她把自己冰冷僵硬的嘴唇，递给了他。

「好姑娘！明天十点。你可别诓我？」

「哦，不会的，尼尔先生！」

「我的名字是亨德森。晚安，小姑娘。」

她抖抖索索地回到楼上自己房里，两眼空空地横躺在床上，望着黑夜一点点深浓，转成灰白，又消散无踪。太阳升起来，带来新的一天——于她，是一段新的生活。

===Original===
She wrote her mother a note, then dressed herself carefully in a little tan poplin suit, a small brown hat and a white veil. “Brides wear veils,” she thought to herself numbly. “Oh, I didn’t think I’d be a bride like this!”

Well, it was too late now. At quarter of nine she went downstairs. Her mother had left long since. Presently she heard a taxi drive up and Neal, heavy but immaculate, got out. He was coming for her. She walked stiffly to meet him; they entered the cab together and were whirled away.

“This was marriage,” she thought, murmuring some words later to a Justice of the Peace. They entered the waiting taxi again and drove to the Pennsylvania station. A surprising number of the redcaps seemed to know Mr. Neal—her husband. Well, of course, of course why shouldn’t they? They walked down the steps past car after car. Neal ushered her finally into a drawing-room. She had never dreamed of traveling like this. As the train pulled out Neal hailed a passing waiter. “Bring us something to eat as soon as possible.”

===Chinese===
她给母亲留了张字条，然后细细地装扮起来：一身小小的米色府绸套装，一顶小小的棕色帽，一方白色的头纱。「新娘子要戴头纱，」她木然地想，「哦，我没想到，我会是这样做新娘子！」

唉，如今说什么都迟了。九点差一刻，她下了楼。母亲早已出门去了。不多时，她听见一辆出租汽车驶到门前，尼尔下了车——身躯沉甸甸，衣冠却一尘不染。他是来接她的。她僵硬地迎上前去；两人一同坐进车里，风也似的去了。

「这就是结婚了，」她想——过后，她对着一位治安法官（Justice of the Peace），喃喃念了几句什么话。他们重新坐进候着的出租汽车，驶往宾夕法尼亚车站。好大一帮红帽搬运工，似乎都认得尼尔先生——她的丈夫。可不是嘛，可不是，凭什么不认得呢？两人顺着台阶往下走，走过一节又一节车厢。尼尔末了把她让进一间豪华包厢。她做梦也不曾想过这样旅行。火车开动时，尼尔唤住一个走过的侍者：「尽快给我们弄点吃的来。」

===Original===
He sat down beside her, immaculate in a gray suit, gray tie, carefully brushed low shoes. His tan overcoat rested in the corner of the seat. He put his arms around her.

“Poor, sleepy, frightened Maggie,” he said tenderly.

She burst into sharp, strangling sobs, burying her head against his shoulder.

So she left New York, weeping, to return to it one day dry-eyed but with a bitterness that was worse than tears.

===Chinese===
他在她身旁坐下，一身灰色西装、灰色领带，低帮皮鞋刷得仔仔细细，通体一尘不染。他的黄褐色大衣，搁在座位的角落里。他伸出胳膊，搂住了她。

「可怜的、困乏的、受惊的玛吉。」他温存地说。

她骤然迸出一阵噎人的抽泣，把头深深埋进他的肩膀。

她就这样离开了纽约，一路哭着；有朝一日再回来时，眼里虽没了泪，心头的苦味，却比泪更甚。

## XII / 十二

===Original===
“Really, Joanna, you ought to treat me better. You know I’m staying in New York just on account of you!”

“How do you want me to treat you, Peter?”

“Oh, hang it all. Why can’t you be nicer to me? When Brian comes to see Sylvia she runs to meet him, puts her arms about his neck.”

“But Sylvia and Brian are engaged. You and I are just friends.”

“Just friends! Joanna, have a heart. What do you think I spend all my spare time with you for? You know how I feel.”

===Chinese===
「说真的，乔安娜，你该对我好一点。你可知道，我留在纽约，就是为了你！」

「你要我怎样对你好呢，彼得？」

「唉，见鬼。你为什么不能对我亲热些？布莱恩来看西尔维娅的时候，她会跑出去迎接他，搂住他的脖子。」

「可西尔维娅和布莱恩是订了婚的。你和我只是朋友。」

「只是朋友！乔安娜，你发发善心吧。你以为我把全部闲暇时间都花在你身上，是为了什么？你知道我的心意。」

===Original===
Joanna raised a slim, protesting hand. “None of that, Peter! You come to see me because both of us are interested in the same things. Each of us is going to be an artist in different ways. What other girl is there in New York who would let you talk to her about the joys of surgery?”

“What other girl would want me to?”

Joanna, looking at the long brown figure lying full length on the grass, thought it highly improbable that any other girl would. She had seen other girls in the company of Peter, and watched quite without jealousy their ways with him. She rather prided herself on her own aloofness from such tactics. Of course, some day she might let Peter talk to her about things other than work and art, and she might answer him, but at present the big things of life must be arranged. Love was an after consideration, she felt, and as far as she knew she meant it.

===Chinese===
乔安娜举起一只纤细的手，表示抗议。「别说这些，彼得！你来看我，是因为我们俩兴趣相投。我们各自都要以不同的方式成为艺术家。纽约还有哪个姑娘，肯听你讲外科学的乐趣？」

「又有哪个姑娘愿意要我陪呢？」

乔安娜望着那个修长的棕色身影整个儿舒展在草地上，心想别的姑娘怕是万难愿意的。她见过别的姑娘同彼得在一起，也全无妒意地看过她们对付他的种种手段。她颇为自得的是，自己从不屑于那一套。当然，将来有一天，她也许会让彼得同她谈谈工作和艺术以外的事情，也许会回应他，可眼下，人生的几件大事必须先安排妥当。她觉得，爱情是以后再考虑的事——就她自己所知，她这话是真心实意的。

===Original===
It was a Saturday afternoon in July and the two were in Van Cortlandt Park. Peter was to go to school in Philadelphia in the fall, and it was important for him to earn as much money as possible for his expenses. He might have gone with a group of other boys to one of the watering places and worked in a hotel. But that took him too far away from Joanna. Ragtime was coming into vogue then, and Peter proved himself an adept at it. The butcher shop was of course a thing long since of the past.

“Here’s where I put my gift of strumming to some use,” he laughed to Joanna. “You ought to see how glad they were to take me on at that cabaret.”

“I hope you won’t learn anything you shouldn’t in that atmosphere,” she had answered primly.

===Chinese===
那是七月的一个星期六下午，两人在范科特兰公园（Van Cortlandt Park）。彼得秋天要去费城上学，尽量多挣些钱应付开销，对他来说很重要。他本可以同另外几个男孩结伴去哪处避暑胜地，在旅馆里做工。可那样就离乔安娜太远了。那时拉格泰姆（ragtime）正开始流行，而彼得证明了自己是此道高手。肉铺当然是早已过去的事了。

「我这瞎弹乱敲的天赋总算派上了用场，」他笑着对乔安娜说。「你真该看看，那家卡巴莱（cabaret）歌舞厅收下我的时候有多高兴。」

「我希望你在那种环境里别沾染上不该学的东西。」她当时一本正经地答道。

===Original===
“Oh, of course I won’t,” he returned, thinking how amazed she would be if she ever looked down from her pinnacle long enough to understand what life really was. He would have liked her to see that cabaret with its jostling crowds and blaring lights, and the host of noisy good-hearted dancing girls. He tried to give her some description of it. But Joanna turned away.

“Men and women are like that, just the same,” he protested. “Everybody isn’t living on the mountaintops like you, Janna. I can’t live there of my own accord myself. That’s why I haunt you so because you do keep me on the heights, dear.” She liked that.

“But just the same,” he resumed, rolling over on the short grass like a lithe handsome animal, “all the big things of life smack of the earth. Your poet has to eat, or he can’t write poetry. Well, so does the commonest laboring-man. The queen has children, in agony, Janna, just like the poorest charwoman. And love is the—the driving force for both of them.” He mused a little. “Love is the most natural and ordinary thing in the world.”

But Joanna didn’t believe that. “Love is a wonderful, rare thing, very beautiful, very sweet, but you can do without it.”

===Chinese===
「噢，我当然不会。」他答道，心想她若是肯从那高不可攀的宝座上低头看一看，弄明白生活究竟是什么样子，不知该多么惊讶。他倒真愿意让她看看那家卡巴莱：拥挤推搡的人群，炫目刺耳的灯光，还有一大群吵吵嚷嚷、心肠热乎的舞女。他试着向她描绘一番。可乔安娜把脸转开了。

「男男女女就是那样过日子的，没什么两样。」他分辩道。「并不是人人都像你一样住在山顶上，乔娜（Janna）。我自己也待不住那儿。我之所以这样缠着你，就因为你确实把我留在了高处，亲爱的。」她喜欢听这话。

「不过说到底，」他接着说，像一头柔韧漂亮的动物，在矮草地上翻了个身，「人生的一切大事都带着泥土气。你的诗人也得吃饭，不然就写不出诗来。最普通的苦力也一样。王后生孩子也要受分娩之苦，乔娜，跟最穷的打杂女工没有两样。而爱情——正是他们两个人共同的驱动力。」他沉吟片刻。「爱情是世上最自然、最平常不过的事。」

可乔安娜不信这个。「爱情是奇妙而稀罕的东西，很美，很甜，但没有它你也照样过活。」

===Original===
“Not much you can’t. Better not try it, Joanna. You have to found your life on love, then you can do all these other things.”

“Don’t talk like a silly, Peter. You know perfectly well that for a woman love usually means a household of children, the getting of a thousand meals, picking up laundry, no time to herself for meditation, or reading or—”

“Dancing! That’s through poor management. Marry a man who understands you, Janna, and he’ll see that you have time for anything you want. Where is such a man? Behold him!” He struck his chest dramatically.

“Peter Bye! How you talk!”

===Chinese===
「哪有的事，你做不到的。最好别去尝试，乔安娜。你得把生活奠基于爱情之上，然后才谈得上做其他一切事情。」

「别傻里傻气的，彼得。你完全清楚，对女人来说，爱情通常意味着一屋子孩子，一千顿饭要做，要收洗晾晒衣物，没有属于自己的时间静思、读书，或者——」

「还有跳舞！那是安排不当的缘故。嫁一个懂你的男人，乔娜，他会保证你有时间做任何想做的事。这样的男人在哪里？请看！」他戏剧性地捶了捶自己的胸膛。

「彼得·拜！瞧你说的！」

===Original===
“All right, I’ll choose something else. Tell me why is it that though I’ve elected to stay in New York in all this hot weather just to be at your side, I see less of you than at any time since I’ve been coming to your house.”

“Does seem queer, doesn’t it? It must be because I have so much work to do. I am taking extra singing lessons from Brailoff now. And my dancing takes up a lot of my time; my classes come at such inconvenient hours, 7:30 to 10:00 three times a week.”

“That *is* bad. Funny time to give dancing lessons. Where’d you say you took them?”

“At Bertully’s.”

===Chinese===
「好吧，那我换个话头。告诉我，我大热天特意留在纽约，就为了待在你身边，可如今见到你的次数，反倒比自我上你家来以后的任何时候都少，这是为什么？」

「确实奇怪，不是吗？准是因为我的功课太多。我现在跟布赖洛夫（Brailoff）加学声乐课。跳舞也占去我许多时间；课的时段又那么别扭，每周三次，从 7:30 上到 10:00。」

「那可真不妙。这个时间上舞蹈课，也是稀奇。你说过在哪儿上吗？」

「在贝图利（Bertully）那里。」

===Original===
“Bertully’s! That’s in Twenty-ninth Street, isn’t it? How’d you ever make it? I didn’t suppose a colored girl got a chance to stick her nose in there.”

“She wouldn’t ordinarily. Bertully refused Helena Arnold last year. ‘I’m sorry, Mees, but the white Americans like not to study with the brown Americans. Vair seely, but so. I am a poor man, I must follow the weeshes of my clients!’ ” Joanna shrugged her shoulders, spread her hands.

“You’re a born impersonator, Jan. I can see that little Frenchman now. How’d you ever get in, then?”

“Helena and I went back this year and asked if he would take a separate class of colored girls, if we got it up for him. He was very decent, said he’d be glad to. So we got up a class of eight, he only asked for six. Of course, we had to take his hours.”

===Chinese===
「贝图利那里！那不是在二十九街吗？你是怎么进去的？我还以为有色人种姑娘压根没机会往那儿伸鼻子呢。」

「平常是没机会。去年贝图利就拒绝过海伦娜·阿诺德（Helena Arnold）。『对不起，米斯，可是白种的美国人不喜欢和棕种的美国人一起学。蠢得很哪，可没有办法。我是个穷人，只好顺着客户们的意思办！』」乔安娜耸了耸肩，摊开双手。

「你真是天生的模仿家，小乔。那小个子法国人我现在就能想见。那你是怎么进去的？」

「今年我和海伦娜又去了，问他愿不愿意单独收一个有色人种姑娘的班，条件由我们替他张罗。他人很厚道，说非常乐意。于是我们凑成了一个八个人的班，他只要求六个。当然，上课时间得听他的。」

===Original===
“Who are in it besides you and Helena?”

“Oh, all our crowd.” She named the daughters of several prominent colored men, a physician, a lawyer, a journalist, a real-estate man among them. “There’s Gertrude Moseley, Vera and Alice Manning, Elizabeth Beckett, Sylvia, Helena, and I.”

“That’s seven.”

“Oh, yes, Sylvia meant to ask Maggie Ellersley.”

“H’m, she had other things in her head without bothering about fancy dancing, hadn’t she? Funny how she went off and married without telling any of us about it, wasn’t it?”

“Yes,” said Joanna uneasily.

===Chinese===
「除了你和海伦娜，还有谁？」

「噢，都是我们那一伙的。」她说出了几位有声望的有色人种人士的女儿，其中有医生、律师、记者，还有一位房地产商的千金。「有格特鲁德·莫斯利（Gertrude Moseley）、维拉和艾丽斯·曼宁（Vera and Alice Manning）、伊丽莎白·贝克特（Elizabeth Beckett）、西尔维娅、海伦娜，还有我。」

「这是七个人。」

「啊，对了，西尔维娅本打算邀请玛吉·埃勒斯利的。」

「哼，她脑子里装着别的大事，哪还顾得上花哨舞蹈，是不是？她一声不响就出走结了婚，我们谁都没告诉，这事也够奇的，不是吗？」

「是啊。」乔安娜不安地说。

===Original===
“You’d have thought she’d have let old Phil in on it. I wonder if they had a falling out of any kind! Philip seemed rather hard hit when he heard the news.”

“Not a bit of it. Why should he be?” Joanna spoke stoutly. But her tone belied her convictions. She hadn’t forgotten Philip’s expression the day Sylvia had come rushing in with the astounding news:

“What do you think? I just met Mrs. Ellersley. Maggie’s married—married—think of it! She ran away with that man at her house, that Mr. Neal. And they’re going to live in Philadelphia.”

Philip’s haggard face had turned a trifle more wan, Joanna had thought. “Has she written to you, Sylvia?” he asked her quickly.

“Not a word. I can’t imagine why she said nothing to me about it. She must have planned it for ages. If that isn’t the funniest!”

===Chinese===
「照理你会以为，她总该让老菲尔知道一声。真不知道他们俩是不是闹了什么别扭！菲利普听到消息时，看上去受的打击不小。」

「一点也没有。他为什么会呢？」乔安娜说得斩钉截铁。可她的语气泄露了心底的动摇。西尔维娅冲进屋来带来那个惊人消息的那天，菲利普的神情，她一直没有忘记：

「你猜怎么着？我刚才碰见了埃勒斯利太太。玛吉结婚了——结婚了——你想想！她跟住在她家的那个男人私奔了，就是那位尼尔先生。他们要到费城去住。」

乔安娜当时觉得，菲利普那张憔悴的脸又添了几分苍白。「她给你写信了吗，西尔维娅？」他急急地问她。

「一个字也没有。我想不通她怎么对我说都没说一声。她准是筹划了好久了。这不是再稀奇不过的事吗！」

===Original===
Later Joanna heard Philip asking his mother if she were sure she had given him all the mail that had come for him while he was in Philadelphia. Still later he had announced his intention of teaching summer school in South Carolina.

“Fellow whose place I’m going to fill is sick. They’ve been at me a long time to come. I think I ought to go, father. It will give me a chance to see the South.”

Joanna’s throat constricted a little at the thought of Philip’s look, his general listlessness. She wished she hadn’t written that letter. Though that couldn’t have brought about the marriage. People don’t arrange to be married over night. As Sylvia said, it must have been on Maggie’s mind long since. And then, anyway, Philip couldn’t really have cared for a girl like Maggie.

“I don’t believe Philip was the least bit interested in Maggie,” she voiced her thought to Peter. “Well, anyway, Mr. Bye, that’s why my company is so scarce. Goodness, what are you frowning about?”

===Chinese===
后来，乔安娜听见菲利普问他母亲，他在费城期间寄来的信件，她是否肯定全都交给了他。再后来，他宣布自己打算去南卡罗来纳（South Carolina）教暑期学校。

「我要顶替的那位先生病了。他们一直劝我去，劝了好久。父亲，我想我该去。这也能让我有机会见见南方。」

一想到菲利普的神情、他那份全面的委顿，乔安娜的喉咙便有些发紧。她后悔写了那封信。虽说那封信总不至于促成了这桩婚事。人是不会一夜之间安排好婚事的。正如西尔维娅所说，这念头必定在玛吉心里盘桓已久。再说，不管怎样，菲利普总不会真的爱上玛吉这样的姑娘。

「我不相信菲利普对玛吉有一丁点儿意思。」她把自己的想法说给彼得听。「好了，不管怎样，拜先生，这就是我为什么难得奉陪的缘故。天哪，你皱着眉头干什么？」

===Original===
“Well, I’m mad to think you swallowed that Frenchman’s insult. To think of your taking lessons from him after that!”

“But, Peter, he didn’t insult us. He can’t help this stupid prejudice. ‘In my country, Mademoiselle Maréchal,’—he always calls me that—‘you’d be an honor to any class.’ He says I’ve got a great future. That if there’s anything that will break down prejudice it will be equality or perhaps even superiority on the part of colored people in the arts. And I agree with him.”

“But to be set apart like that!”

“What do I care?” asked Joanna, the practical. “You’ve got to take life as you find it, Peter. The way I figure it is this. If all I needed to get on the stage was the mastery of a difficult step, I’d get there, wouldn’t I? For somehow, sometime, I’d learn how to overcome that difficulty.”

“You bet you would.”

“Very well, then. Now my problem is how to master, how to get around prejudice. It *is* an awful nuisance; in some parts of this country it is more than a nuisance, it’s a veritable menace. Philip says he’s going to change all that some day. First, I’m going to get my training up to the last notch, then I’m going to watch for an opportunity and squeeze in.”

===Chinese===
「唉，一想到你把那法国人的侮辱咽了下去，我就来气。想想看，那之后你居然还跟他上课！」

「可是彼得，他并没有侮辱我们。他拿这愚蠢的偏见没有办法。『在我的国家，马雷夏尔小姐（Mademoiselle Maréchal）』——他总是这样称呼我——『你会给任何一个班增光。』他说我前程远大。他说，若说有什么能打破偏见，那就是有色人种在艺术上取得平等、甚至取得优势。这一点我赞同他。」

「可是被人那样另眼分开！」

「我在乎什么呢？」凡事讲实际的乔安娜问道。「人只能接受生活本来的样子，彼得。我是这么盘算的：倘若我上舞台只需要攻克一个高难的舞步，那我总能成功，不是吗？因为不管怎样，总有那么一天，我会学会怎样克服那道难关。」

「那当然，你准能。」

「那不就结了。现在我的问题是怎样练到登峰造极，怎样绕过偏见。它确实讨厌极了；在这个国家的某些地方，它还不止是讨厌，简直是实实在在的威胁。菲利普说，他将来有一天要改变这一切。首先，我要把自己的功底练到十成十，然后我盯着机会，见缝就钻。」

===Original===
“You’ll never get it.”

“Oh, yes, I will. Some white people are kind, some of them are so truly artistic that they’ll put themselves to great trouble for the sake of art. Look at Bertully. It works him much harder than it does us to hold those extra classes.”

“Bertully’s one man in a thousand. Besides, he’s a foreigner. Where’ll you find a white American like that?”

“You blessed pessimist. I know of people like that already. That’s how Helena Arnold got to Bertully in the first place. A Miss Sharples—why, they’re the people your Aunt Susan works for, aren’t they? Your aunt told Miss Sharples about Helena, and Miss Sharples took her, herself, to Bertully.”

===Chinese===
「你永远等不来机会的。」

「噢，不，我会等到的。有些白人心肠好，有些人对艺术是真爱，肯为了艺术自找大麻烦。看看贝图利吧。多开那些班，他费的力气比我们大得多。」

「贝图利是千里挑一的人物。再说，他是个外国人。你上哪儿再找一个那样的美国白人？」

「你这可气的悲观派。这样的人我早就有耳闻。海伦娜·阿诺德当初能进贝图利的门，就是这么来的。有位夏普尔斯小姐（Miss Sharples）——咦，那不就是你苏珊姨妈帮工的那户人家吗？你姨妈跟夏普尔斯小姐提起了海伦娜，夏普尔斯小姐便亲自把她带到了贝图利那里。」

===Original===
“That was awfully decent, I must say. Of course, the Sharples are Philadelphia Quaker stock. Not that that makes much difference. The white Byes were Quakers, and see how they left us stranded, though my father told me old black Joshua Bye practically coined them their money. Not many people like those Sharples.”

“There doesn’t need to be. The point is there’s *one*. Miss Sharples’ family, by the way, may have been Quakers, but there’s nothing Quakerish about her. Helena says she goes with the Greenwich Village group all the time, and for all their craziness, they’ve got some mighty big ideas.”

“Can’t get anything to eat, if you’re colored, down in their dinky old restaurants.”

“Awful, isn’t it? Well, we’ll let some other colored person pound away at that side of it. Me, I’m going to break into art. The public wants novelty, and *I* want fame, I’ve got to have it, Peter.”

===Chinese===
「我得说，那真是厚道极了。当然了，夏普尔斯家是费城的贵格会世家。不过这也算不得什么大分别。白皮肤的拜家就是贵格会，瞧瞧他们怎么把我们扔下不管的，虽然我父亲告诉我，老黑人约书亚·拜差不多是替他们挣下那份家业的。像夏普尔斯家这样的人可不多。」

「不需要多。要紧的是有这么一个。顺便说一句，夏普尔斯小姐的祖上也许是贵格会，可她身上没有一点贵格会的味儿。海伦娜说她成天同格林尼治村（Greenwich Village）那帮人往来，那伙人疯归疯，有些念头可真是了不起。」

「你要是有色人种，在他们那些又小又破的馆子里，连口饭都吃不上。」

「可怕吧？那一面嘛，就让别的有色人种去死磕好了。至于我，我要闯进艺术这一行。公众要新奇，而我要的是名声，我非得到它不可，彼得。」

===Original===
“You talk about going on the stage as though you had a signed contract in your hand. How’ll you get the stage-presence?”

“I’m to go on a recital tour next fall among colored people. I’m used to singing in the choir. If I can stand before them I can stand before any audience in the world.”

“Yes, we are mighty critical.”

“I should say so. Get up, Peter Bye. We’ve got to go home.”

They started on the long trip back.

===Chinese===
「你说起登台，倒好像手里已经攥着签好的合同似的。你的台风又从哪儿来？」

「明年秋天我要在有色人种中间做巡回演唱。我唱诗班唱惯了。要是能在他们面前站得住，那天下什么样的观众我都能应付。」

「可不是，我们这些观众可挑剔得很。」

「谁说不是呢。起来吧，彼得·拜。我们该回家了。」

两人踏上漫长的归途。

===Original===
“But see here, Joanna,” Peter pleaded when they reached the house, “you will give me a little more time, won’t you? I don’t have to work in the morning, you know. And I don’t work Wednesday nights. Promise me that, won’t you?”

“Yes,” said Joanna, her heart warming to his glowing beauty. “We’ll remember this summer, Peter, the last before we go off trying our wings for further flights.”

That was an enchanted season. Peter used to call for her in the morning, and the two would go off exploring. Joanna liked the foreign quarters, but she had never cared to stand around too long in those teeming, exotic streets. She was too conspicuous, attracted too many inquiring glances. With Peter she felt safe to stand for long moments watching the children play, to enter queer dark shops, to taste strange messes. Sometimes she spoke to the women about their dresses, their headgear. One Spanish woman, grown used to the sight of this dark American girl and the good-looking boy at her side, took them into her quarters one day and showed Joanna how she dressed her hair. Another time she taught her an intricate Spanish dance.

“I’m going to do a dance representing all the nations, some day,” Joanna told Peter.

===Chinese===
「不过听着，乔安娜，」到了家门口，彼得恳求道，「你会多给我一点时间的，对吧？你知道，我上午不用干活。星期三晚上我也没有工。答应我，好吗？」

「好。」乔安娜说，他那容光焕发的俊美让她心里发热。「我们会记住这个夏天的，彼得——这是我们振翅远飞之前，最后一个夏天了。」

那是一个如痴如醉的季节。彼得常在早晨来接她，两人便出门去探险。乔安娜喜欢那些外国人聚居的街区，可她从不愿在那些熙熙攘攘、异国情调的街巷里久留。她太惹眼，招来太多探询的目光。有彼得在身边，她就觉得安稳，可以久久伫立看孩子们玩耍，钻进古怪幽暗的铺子，尝些稀奇古怪的吃食。有时她同那些女人聊聊衣裳、头巾。一位西班牙女人看惯了这黑皮肤的美国姑娘和她身边俊俏的男孩，有一天把他们让进自己屋里，教乔安娜怎样梳她的头发。还有一回，她教了她一套繁复的西班牙舞。

「将来有一天，我要跳一支表现天下万国的舞。」乔安娜对彼得说。

===Original===
They planned for Wednesday nights very carefully at first, but gradually as the torrid weather increased, Joanna’s desire for the theater and other indoor forms of amusement yielded to the desire to be cool at any cost. Central Park claimed them then, and later Morningside, since it was just a few moments’ stroll from the Marshalls’ new house.

Morningside was usually crowded. The seats were always taken when they arrived.

“I wonder what time the people come,” Joanna murmured. But they didn’t mind. The grass, the sloping hillside, was good enough for them. Joanna would sit down, her dainty summer dress spread around her, her splendidly poised head turned at first so she could see the passersby. She was forever studying types, and eyed them with a grave deliberation.

===Chinese===
起初，他们为星期三的晚上精心筹划，可随着暑气日盛，乔安娜对剧院和其他室内消遣的兴致，渐渐让位于不惜一切代价图个凉快的愿望。于是中央公园（Central Park）把他们招了去，再后来是晨边公园（Morningside），因为从马歇尔家的新居到那里，不过几步路的事。

晨边公园通常很挤。他们到的时候，座位总是已被人占了。

「真不知道这些人都是几点来的。」乔安娜喃喃道。但他们并不在意。草地，那片缓缓倾斜的山坡，对他们来说就够好了。乔安娜会坐下来，娇俏的夏裙向四周铺开，那颗端丽沉稳的头起初还转着，好看看来往的行人。她永远在琢磨人的类型，用一种庄重的审慎打量他们。

===Original===
“You’ll get your head knocked off yet, Joanna,” Peter would remonstrate, “staring at people so.”

He liked it better when later on in the evening she turned toward the slope of the hill and looked down at the city, laughing in its myriad twinkling lights. Her face at that time took on a grave wistfulness which he could not analyze. Joanna herself could not define the feeling which prompted that expression.

Peter, leaning on his elbow, would lie beside her, his curly black head bent toward her, one slender brown hand touching her dress ever so lightly. He would have given the world to believe she was thinking about him, but he knew she was not. He would have been astounded if he could have dreamed of the maze of her thoughts. Joanna was really most human at moments like these. Through her mind was floating a series of little detached pictures. She saw a glittering stage, Peter, herself, some little children. She felt a hazy, nebulous, mystical joy.

Peter adored her at moments like these, but he was afraid of her, too.

===Chinese===
「你这么盯着人瞧，早晚要被人把脑袋敲掉，乔安娜。」彼得总要这样规劝她。

他更喜欢的是，入夜之后她转向山坡那面，俯瞰那在万千灯火中欢笑的城市。那时她的脸上会浮起一种他分析不出的、庄重的怅惘。乔安娜自己也说不清是何种心绪催生了那种神情。

彼得支着臂肘躺在她身旁，一头黑色卷发朝她倾着，一只纤细的棕色手若有若无地搭着她的裙子。哪怕倾尽全世界，他也愿意相信她想着的是他，可他知道并非如此。他若是能梦见她那思绪的迷宫，准会惊得目瞪口呆。在这种时刻，乔安娜其实是最有人情味的。一幕幕细小零散的画面正从她心头浮过。她看见一座流光溢彩的舞台，彼得，她自己，还有几个小孩。她感到一种朦胧、混沌而近乎神秘的喜悦。

这种时刻，彼得崇拜她，可也怕她。

===Original===
One night she astonished him. “Peter,” she said suddenly, “sit up. So. I’m tired. I’ve had a hard day. Do you mind if I rest my head on your shoulder?”

Would he mind if she offered him a king’s estate?

He was too ecstatic, too—yes—scared, to speak. He sat as she directed, he stretched his thin tense arm around her fine young body. He even put up one hand and pressed her head closer against his shoulder, touched her hair, let his fingers trail ever so lightly over her cheek. Joanna in his arms! Joanna!

She felt him trembling. “Am I too heavy, Peter?”

He could hardly articulate, but she heard his ardent “no” and moved imperceptibly closer.

His breath stirred her thick, dark hair. He let it caress his chin. Its soft heaviness was a revelation to him, a rapture.

===Chinese===
一天晚上，她让他吃了一惊。「彼得，」她突然说，「坐起来。就这样。我累了。今天累了一整天。我把头靠在你肩上，你不介意吧？」

她若是要把一座王者的领地赏给他，他会介意吗？

他狂喜得，也太——是的——害怕得，说不出话来。他依着她的话坐好，把那条瘦而绷紧的手臂环上她美好的年轻身子。他甚至抬起一只手，把她的头往自己肩上按得更紧，抚过她的头发，让指尖极轻极轻地从她脸颊上滑过。乔安娜在他怀里！乔安娜！

她觉出他在发抖。「我是不是太重了，彼得？」

他几乎说不出话来，可她听见了他那一声炽热的「不」，便又不易察觉地偎近了些。

他的呼吸拂动她浓密的黑发。他任那发丝爱抚自己的下巴。那份柔软的沉甸甸，对他是一种启示，一种狂喜。

===Original===
She lay so quietly against him he thought she must be asleep. So he whispered, “Are you asleeep, Joanna?”

“No,” she whispered back, “only very, very tired.”

“Oh, Joanna, Joanna,” he breathed, “be tired forever.”

Somewhere out of the heavenly silence, a girl’s voice, a foreign voice, broke into song high and shrill. Russian, Peter thought. It was just a snatch, poignant and sweet, that died away leaving a faint lingering sadness.

She put her head back then. She opened her dark eyes and looked full into his.

Their lips were so near, so near. In a second he had pressed his against hers, briefly yet with passion. She sat up and drew a little away from him, dazed. But he put his arms around her and held her close. Presently they walked home, speechless. When they came to an arc-light, they looked at each other’s faces, eager to study and to reveal these new selves. Their glances met and clung with a sweet enchantment. Something leaped, something fluttered within their hearts, like a fettered, struggling wing. And it was beautifully, it was magically, first love!

===Chinese===
她偎着他，那么安静，他以为她准是睡着了。于是他低声问：「你睡着了吗，乔安娜？」

「没有。」她低声答道，「只是很累，很累。」

「噢，乔安娜，乔安娜。」他轻声说，「永远这么累下去吧。」

在那天堂般的静寂中，不知何处，一个姑娘的嗓音，一个外国口音的嗓音，高亢尖亮地唱起歌来。是俄语，彼得想。那只是一小段，凄婉而甜美，渐渐消逝，留下一缕淡淡的、萦绕不去的哀愁。

于是她仰起了头。她睁开那双黑眼睛，直直地望进他的眼睛。

他们的嘴唇那么近，那么近。转瞬之间，他已把自己的唇压上了她的，短暂，却满含激情。她坐起身，微微从他身边撤开，有些迷乱。可他伸出双臂，把她紧紧搂住。过了一会儿，他们一言不发地走回家去。走到一盏弧光灯下，他们端详彼此的脸，急切地想要探究、也想要展露这崭新的自我。他们的目光相遇了，黏住了，带着一种甜蜜的迷醉。有什么在跃动，有什么在扑腾，在他们心里，像一只被缚住的、挣扎的翅膀。而这，是那么美，那么奇妙——这就是初恋！

## XIII / 十三

===Original===
The vacation sped as vacations will. Peter played in the awful cabaret, saved his money and adored Joanna. Joanna practiced trills, danced, thought of Peter and allowed him to adore her. As the early September days spread their golden haze over Harlem and Morningside Park, she actually shivered a little when she realized that when the month was over she and Peter would be miles apart.

It is hard to say just how much Joanna cared for Peter at this time. Certainly the boy worshipped her. He dreamed wordless dreams of her at night sitting in the noisy cabaret. His visit to her was the one objective point in his day. When the inexorable moment of separation came it cost him actual physical pain to bid her goodbye.

Joanna was hardly like that. She had a very real, very ardent feeling for Peter. But it was still small, if one may speak of a feeling by size. Her love for him was a new experience, a fresh interest in her already crowded life, but it had not pushed aside the other interests. At nineteen she looked at love as a man of forty might—as “a thing apart.” This was due partly to her hard unripeness, partly to her deliberate self-training. Joanna had read of too many able women who had “counted the world well lost for love,” until it was too late. “Poor, silly sheep,” she dubbed them.

She could not, it is true, bundle up her thoughts of Peter and say, “I’ll think of you tomorrow at three,” but she did achieve a concentration in her work that made it almost impossible for him to remain too long in her thoughts. And at nights when he tossed sleepless on his bed, dreaming fragrant dreams and seeing golden visions, she was sleeping the perfect sleep of healthy weariness.

===Chinese===
假期飞驰而去，假期总是这样。彼得在那家糟糕的卡巴莱歌舞厅里弹琴，攒着钱，崇拜着乔安娜。乔安娜练颤音，练跳舞，想着彼得，也容许他崇拜自己。九月初的日子把金色的薄霭铺满哈莱姆和晨边公园，她一想到月底一到，她和彼得就要相隔数英里，竟不由得微微打了个寒噤。

很难说这时候乔安娜对彼得到底有多少情意。这男孩子确实是崇拜她的。夜里坐在喧闹的卡巴莱里，他会做无言的梦，梦里全是她。上她家去，是他一天里唯一明确的目标。等到那无可回避的分别时刻来临，道一声再见竟叫他肉体上真实地疼痛。

乔安娜却难得这样。她对彼得怀有一种非常真实、非常炽热的感情。但这份感情还小——如果感情可以论大小的话。对他的爱是她的一桩新体验，是她本已拥挤的生活里一份新鲜的兴味，可它并没有把别的兴味挤到一边。十九岁的她看待爱情，就像一个四十岁的男人那样——把它当作「身外之物」。这一半由于她生性冷硬、未经世事，一半由于她有意的自我训练。乔安娜读过太多能干女人的故事，她们「为了爱情，把全世界丢了也在所不惜」，直到悔之已晚。「可怜的蠢羊。」她这样称呼她们。

诚然，她无法把对彼得的思念打个包，说一句「我明天三点再想你」，但她确实做到了功课上的专心致志，使他几乎不可能在她心里停留太久。而当夜里他在床上翻来覆去睡不着，做着芬芳的梦、看着金色的幻象时，她正睡着健康疲倦者那完美的酣眠。

===Original===
The last days were hard for her, however, as they were for Peter. For Joanna was doomed by her very makeup to a sort of perpetual loneliness. Sylvia had her own interests, she had Brian and many, many friends. She was the most popular of all the Marshalls. Alec and Joanna had never been thrown much together. Philip, once her great confidant, was usually away from home. And on his return he was apt to relapse during these days into a rapt sadness.

It followed, then, that while Joanna was Peter’s sweetheart, his heart’s dear queen, Peter was at once her lover whom she didn’t need very much—at least she did not realize that need—and more than that her companion and friend whom she needed greatly. The prospect of the days stretched long and dreary before her. Even the concert tour, a remarkable booking for one so young, did not entirely console her.

===Chinese===
然而，最后的日子对她是难熬的，正如对彼得一样。因为乔安娜的天性注定了她要承受某种恒久的孤独。西尔维娅有自己的天地，她有布莱恩，还有许许多多朋友。她是马歇尔一家里人缘最好的。亚历克和乔安娜从不常聚在一起。菲利普，她昔日倾诉衷肠的人，多半不在家。即便回来，这些日子里他也往往陷入一种出神的忧伤。

于是事情便成了这样：乔安娜是彼得的心上人、他心尖上亲爱的女王，而彼得于她，一半是情人——她并不怎么需要情人，至少她没有意识到那种需要——另一半更是她极需要的同伴和朋友。眼前的日子铺展开来，漫长而阴郁。就连那趟巡回演出——对这么年轻的人来说是了不起的预约——也不能完全安慰她。

===Original===
The two talked about it on the day before Peter left for Philadelphia. They were in Van Cortlandt Park in a little tangled grove. It was noon and the September sun streamed down on them making the green wooden bench on which they sat pleasantly warm. But the leaves about them were going a little sere; in the shade the air felt chill, and the sunshine, though warm, was thin and white.

“ ‘The summer is ended.’ ” Joanna quoted softly; she sighed. Peter looked at her, there were tears in her eyes.

“Dear, beautiful Joanna,” said Peter, and his own beautiful face was full of the woe of parting, “how can I leave you tomorrow? Janna, don’t send me away, tell me I’m not to go.” He put his arms around her and she clung to him.

“Peter, you must go, you must, really. We—we can’t go on like this. We’ve got to prepare ourselves while we’re young for the future.”

“Yes,” said Peter and his ardor chilled a little at the touch of her cool practicality. But a moment later her light touch rekindled him.

===Chinese===
彼得动身去费城的前一天，两人谈起了这件事。他们在范科特兰公园一处枝柯缠绕的小树丛里。正是中午，九月的阳光倾泻在他们身上，把他们坐的那条绿漆木凳晒得暖融融的。可四周的叶子已微微发枯；背阴处空气生凉，阳光虽暖，却稀薄而苍白。

「『夏天结束了。』」乔安娜轻声引了这一句；她叹了口气。彼得看着她，她眼里含着泪。

「亲爱的、美丽的乔安娜，」彼得说，他自己那张俊美的脸上满是离别的哀伤，「我明天怎么能离开你？小乔，别打发我走，告诉我我不用去了。」他张开双臂搂住她，她也紧紧依偎着他。

「彼得，你必须走，你必须，真的。我们——我们不能这样下去了。我们得趁着年轻，为将来作好准备。」

「好吧。」彼得说，她那冷静的实际劲儿使他的热情凉了一分。可片刻之后，她轻轻的一触又把他重新点燃。

===Original===
“You love me, Janna? You know I love you?”

“Yes, Peter dear, but we mustn’t say anything more about it.”

“I know, Joanna, I’m not going to worry you any more just now, but you’ll let me speak sometime?”

“Yes, oh, yes!”

“Dearest girl! Kiss me, Joanna.”

She touched his lips with a light, lingering kiss. He looked at her, his face haggard with his gusty, boyish passion.

“Ah, Joanna, I’ll never forget that kiss.”

Neither would she, her heart told her. It was the first time she had ever kissed him.

===Chinese===
「你爱我吗，小乔？你知道我爱你的？」

「是的，亲爱的彼得，可我们不能再多说这件事了。」

「我知道，乔安娜，眼下我不会再烦你了，可你会容我以后某一天说出来的，对吗？」

「会的，噢，会的！」

「最亲爱的姑娘！亲亲我，乔安娜。」

她用一个轻柔而缠绵的吻碰了碰他的唇。他望着她，那张脸因少男一阵阵狂放的炽情而显得憔悴。

「啊，乔安娜，我永远不会忘记这个吻。」

她也不会，她的心这样告诉她。这是她有生以来第一次吻他。

===Original===
They walked through the deserted park, their arms frankly about each other, like children. The dry grass and brittle leaves crackled beneath their feet, the air hung over them like a thin, misty veil. Joanna sang a bit from an old Italian song:

> “If from Heaven we could but borrow
> One day longer of fond affection
> It would lessen then our sorrow,
> Give fresh joys for recollection.”

She hummed a line here, then her voice rose again in the thin, shimmering air:

> “—The future, dark and lonely!
> Dearest Loved One, dearest Loved One
> Parting makes these joys so dear!
> Ah!—”

===Chinese===
两人穿过空寂的公园，彼此坦坦荡荡地互相搂着，像一对孩子。干草和脆叶在脚下咯吱作响，空气像一层薄薄的雾纱笼罩着他们。乔安娜唱起一支意大利老歌的片段：

> 「倘若能向天堂借来
> 再多一日缱绻深情，
> 我们的悲哀便会稍减，
> 还添新欢，供回忆重温。」

她哼了一句，随后那嗓音又在稀薄、微颤的空气里扬起：

> 「——前路黑暗又孤单！
> 最亲爱的人，最亲爱的人，
> 离别使这些欢乐如此珍贵！
> 啊！——」

===Original===
“Don’t, Joanna; it’s too sweet. You’ll make me cry.”

“I know it. Oh, Peter, go away and come back great and when you come back, speak to me.”

She went with him to the train next morning and to his amazement no less than her own, broke down and sobbed into her handkerchief.

He bent over her. “To think of your crying for me, Joanna! Goodbye, goodbye, my sweet. Remember, I’ll be back Christmas.”

===Chinese===
「别唱了，乔安娜；太甜了。你要把我唱哭了。」

「我知道。噢，彼得，走吧，带着一身成就回来，等你回来时，再对我说。」

第二天早晨她送他上火车，而令他不亚于令她自己吃惊的是，她竟抑制不住，捂着手帕呜咽起来。

他俯下身来。「想想看，你居然为我哭，乔安娜！再见，再见，我亲爱的。记住，我圣诞节就回来。」

===Original===
He vanished through the gates, was borne out of her vision. A strange exaltation possessed him. He was sad, but his sadness was as nothing to his joy, his sense of satisfaction. Joanna loved him. She had been unusually capricious since that night in Morningside Park. But now he was sure of her. He smiled steadily from Manhattan Transfer Station to North Philadelphia.

His cousin Louis Boyd met him at Broad Street Station and took him to his great-uncle Peter’s in South Eighteenth Street. The old man almost cried over him.

“You’re Meriwether’s son, but you’re more like your grandfather, Isaiah. He was darker than you, but he held his head high like yours, and you’re going to do what he wanted his son to do. It’s good to see you, boy.”

===Chinese===
他穿过检票口消失了，被载出了她的视野。一种奇异的激昂攫住了他。他难过，可他的难过比起他的欢喜、比起他的满足感来，根本算不得什么。乔安娜爱他。自晨边公园那一夜之后，她一直喜怒无常得出奇。可现在，他对她有把握了。从曼哈顿换乘站到北费城，他一路笑容不断。

他表哥路易斯·博伊德（Louis Boyd）到布罗德街车站接他，把他带到南十八街叔祖父彼得家里。老人见到他，差点儿掉下泪来。

「你是梅里韦瑟的儿子，可你更像你祖父艾赛亚。他的皮肤比你黑，但他也像你一样高昂着头，而你要去做的，正是他当年想让儿子做的事。见到你真好，孩子。」

===Original===
He registered at the University the next day, consulted catalogues, met professors, wrote a glowing letter to Joanna. By the end of the week he was desperately homesick. He would have gone over to New York if he had not been so ashamed, and if he had not been expected to dinner at Louis Boyd’s.

“Tell you what’s the matter with you, fellow,” said Louis when Peter had told him of his nostalgia, “you want to meet a few girls. We’ll start out after dinner.”

Peter did not think this would help much. He wanted Joanna, though he said nothing about that to Louis. Astonishingly, however, the cure worked.

===Chinese===
第二天他去大学注册，翻查课程目录，拜见教授，给乔安娜写了一封热情洋溢的信。到了周末，他已经想家想得要命。若不是实在太难为情，又受邀要去路易斯·博伊德家吃晚饭，他早就跑回纽约去了。

「我来告诉你你是怎么了，伙计，」彼得把自己的乡愁对他讲了之后，路易斯说，「你是想认识几个姑娘。晚饭后我们就出发。」

彼得觉得这不会有多大用处。他想要的是乔安娜，不过这话他对路易斯只字未提。然而出人意料的是，这药方竟灵验了。

===Original===
Louis seemed to know half of colored Philadelphia. “Mighty nice girls in this man’s town, I can tell you. They’ll take to you, Peter, because, of course, you’re a Bye. Mentioned your name to old Mrs. Viny the other day and she told me to be sure to bring you around. She’d like to meet an ‘old Philadelphian,’ even if he had been living a while in New York.”

The girls deserved the nice things Louis said about them. They were pretty, nicely dressed and a shining contrast to the dingy streets and old-fashioned houses in which most of them lived. Peter was pleasantly struck, too, by the apparent lack of aspiration on the part of most of them. They seemed to be pretty well satisfied with being girls. A few were able to live home, many sewed, a number of others taught. There was no talk of art, of fame, of preparation for the future among them. Peter spoke of it to Arabelle Morton, the last girl to whose house Louis took him.

“Well, of course we want to get married, and we’re not spoiling our chances by being highbrows. Wouldn’t you like to come and play cards next Friday night, Mr. Bye? There’ll be just two tables, then afterwards we might dance. I’m sure you’d like it.”

Peter thought so, too. He liked Arabelle already and her friendly shallowness. He wrote to Joanna:

===Chinese===
路易斯似乎认识半个费城的有色人种圈子。「我可以告诉你，这个城里的姑娘好得很。她们会喜欢你的，彼得，因为嘛，你是拜家的人。前几天我跟老薇妮太太（Mrs. Viny）提了你的名字，她叮嘱我务必带你过去一趟。她想见一位『老费城人』，哪怕他在纽约住了些日子。」

这些姑娘配得上路易斯对她们的美言。她们漂亮，衣着讲究，同她们大多数人身处的灰暗街道和老式房屋形成鲜亮的对照。同样令彼得暗暗称奇的，是她们大多数人显然并无什么抱负。她们似乎对做姑娘这件事相当心满意足。少数几个住在家中，许多做针线活，另一些教书。她们中间不谈艺术，不谈名声，也不为将来做准备。彼得把这话对阿拉贝尔·莫顿（Arabelle Morton）说了——路易斯带他登门的姑娘里，她是最后一位。

「这个嘛，我们当然想结婚，我们才不会因为做女学究而坏了好事。下星期五晚上你愿意来打牌吗，拜先生？就两桌，打完之后我们还可以跳舞。我敢保你会喜欢的。」

彼得也这么想。他已经喜欢上阿拉贝尔了，连同她那份友善的浅薄。他给乔安娜写信：

===Original===
> “Tell you what, Jan, I think I’m going to like Philly very much. Being Isaiah Bye’s grandson seems to help me no end. They actually consider me an ‘old Philadelphian’ and on the strength of that alone I’ve had four dinner invitations from elderly people to meet other ‘old Philadelphians.’ Some of them old enough, too, I’ll say. However, the dinners are fine and come in very handy for a struggling student. I don’t board at Uncle Peter’s, you see.
> “There’re lots of jolly girls here. Of course, they’re not like yours and Sylvia’s crowd, bent on climbing to the top of a profession—well, Sylvia wasn’t that way so much—but they’re a very nice bunch and they have been most kind to your humble servant. …
> “Do you remember that day in the Park? Joanna darling, what are you going to say to me when I come back Christmas?
> “Peter.”
> “N.B. These *x*’s are kisses.” [There was a long string of them.]

===Chinese===
> 「告诉你吧，小乔，我想我准会非常喜欢费城。做艾赛亚·拜的孙子，似乎让我受益无穷。他们竟然把我当成『老费城人』，单凭这一条，我就收到了四位老人家发出的晚餐邀请，去结识别的『老费城人』——有几个也确实够老的了，我敢说。不过那些晚餐好极了，对挣扎求学的穷学生实在帮了大忙。你知道，我并不在叔父彼得家搭伙。
> 「这里有许多快活的姑娘。当然啦，她们不像你和西尔维娅那一伙人，一心要攀上某个行业的顶峰——嗯，西尔维娅倒也不尽然——不过她们是一帮非常好的姑娘，待在下也客气极了。……
> 「你还记得在公园里的那一天吗？亲爱的乔安娜，等我圣诞节回来，你打算对我说什么？
> 「彼得。」
> 「注：这些 *x* 都是吻。」［足足长长的一串。］

===Original===
His letters to Joanna reacted to his own advantage. He felt he must be able to tell her truthfully of his success in his studies, of his ability to fit into this new life. Joanna was interested in him with a deep personal interest such as she had never exhibited before, and he meant to keep it alive. These were with one exception the most wholesome, most formative days of Peter’s life. He had youth, he had inspiration, he had the promise of love, with much hard labor to keep it.

Many of the colored boys lived in West Philadelphia. They had a fraternity, and though according to their laws he could not be taken in during his freshman year, it was plain that this honor would be extended to him as soon as he became a sophomore. He was pretty well liked, and was constantly receiving invitations to spend the night across the river. One or two of the boys lived in the dormitories and he was frequently offered a chance to see something of this side of college life.

===Chinese===
他写给乔安娜的信反过来促成了他自己的长进。他觉得自己必须能够如实地告诉她学业上的成功，告诉她自己有能力融入这新生活。乔安娜对他怀着一种深切的个人关切，那是她从未表露过的，而他打算让这份关切一直保持下去。除了一桩例外，这是彼得一生中最健康、最塑造人的日子。他有青春，有灵感，有爱情的许诺——还有守护这份爱情的许多苦工。

许多有色人种同学住在西费城。他们有一个兄弟会，虽然按会章他在一年级时不能入会，但显然等他一升上二年级，这份荣誉就会落到他头上。他人缘颇好，不断收到去河对岸过夜的邀请。有一两个同学住在宿舍里，人家也常常邀他去见识见识大学生活的这一面。

===Original===
But his steadiness surprised himself. He got his meals in a restaurant on Woodland Avenue, worked faithfully in the Library between classes, and completed the rest of his assignments at night in his Uncle’s sitting room. The old fellow loved to see him there. He pictured in Peter the restoration of the Bye family in Philadelphia.

To eke out his scanty bank account, he played three nights a week in a dance hall at Sixteenth and South Streets. Saturday afternoons he did track work. Friday and Sunday he spent at Arabelle Morton’s or at Lawyer Talbert’s on Christian Street. This latter and his family consisting of two sons and two daughters, were the relatives with whom the Marshalls stayed on their visits to Philadelphia. He found them very enjoyable. One of the boys was an undertaker but with a disposition far less lugubrious than his calling. The other was in the Wharton School of Finance at Pennsylvania and was to read law later at Harvard. Both girls were young and both were engaged. They were very much in love, but as their fiancés were studying medicine at Howard University, they welcomed Peter with much acclaim.

Thanks to them and Louis, he was soon enrolled in the social calendar, and if he chose to be lonely, it was his own fault.

===Chinese===
但他的沉稳连自己都感到意外。他在伍德兰大道上一家餐馆吃饭，课间在图书馆里踏实用功，晚上在叔父的起居室里完成其余的功课。老人家就爱看他待在那儿。他在彼得身上，看到了拜家在费城重振的图景。

为了贴补菲薄的银行存款，他每周三个晚上在十六街与南街口的一家舞厅里弹琴。星期六下午他练田径。星期五和星期天，他泡在阿拉贝尔·莫顿家，或是在克里斯蒂安街的塔尔伯特律师家。后者一家——两个儿子、两个女儿——正是马歇尔一家来费城时借住的亲戚。他发现他们极好相处。两个小伙子，一个是殡葬商，性情却远不像他那一行那么阴郁；另一个在宾夕法尼亚大学的沃顿金融学院（Wharton School of Finance）读书，日后还要去哈佛读法律。两个姑娘都还年轻，也都订了婚。她们情意正浓，可未婚夫们都在霍华德大学（Howard University）学医，于是她们对彼得的到来无不欢天喜地。

多亏他们和路易斯，他很快就被排进了社交日程，他若要自甘寂寞，那只能怪自己。

===Original===
At Christmas he went back to New York; Joanna met him at the station and took him home in her father’s car. Joel was one of the first ten colored men in Harlem to possess an automobile. The distance between his house and his business rendered it almost a necessity, and he was old enough to deserve release from the noise of the subway and the weary climbing to the elevated.

Joanna had grown very good-looking, Peter thought. More than that, she looked even distinguished. Her purposefulness gave her a quality which he had missed in the Philadelphia girls. His ardor had not cooled in the least, but he had had to force it into second place. Now it surged uppermost in his heart again.

He was glad that he had been in another city, had seen so many other girls. It only confirmed his conviction that Joanna was the only woman in the world for him. He hoped she possessed the same singleness of desire for him.

===Chinese===
圣诞节他回了纽约；乔安娜到车站接他，用她父亲的车送他回家。乔尔是哈莱姆最早拥有汽车的前十位有色人种之一。住宅与营业场所之间的距离，使汽车几乎成了必需，况且他年纪也够大了，理当免受地铁的喧嚣和攀爬高架车站的劳顿。

乔安娜出落得很好看了，彼得想。不止如此，她看上去甚至气度不凡。她那种志在必得的劲头赋予她一种气质，是他在费城姑娘身上所错过的。他的热情丝毫没有冷却，只是不得不强压到第二位。此刻，它又涌上心头，占了首位。

他庆幸自己去过另一座城市，见过那么多别的姑娘。这只会更坚定他的信念：乔安娜是这世上唯一属于他的女人。他希望她对他也怀有同样专一的渴望。

===Original===
“There’s lots going on,” Joanna told him, sitting arm in arm with him in the car. “Sylvia and Brian are to be married Easter, so mother’s formally announcing it now. There’ll be luncheons—not for you I’m afraid, Peter. Then our dancing class is giving a benefit for the Pierce Day Nursery. There’ll be fancy dancing on the stage, in which your humble servant will star. And we’re to have a Christmas tree at our house and a house party. I’m asking you now, Peter. Isn’t it great being grown up?”

“You bet. Which of these functions comes off first?”

“Sylvia’s engagement party.”

“So she and Spencer are actually going to pull it off. They’ve waited a long time, haven’t they?”

“Yes, that’s because Brian insisted on getting a good start before he married. Sylvia would have married him the day after they became engaged. But I think Brian’s right.”

“They’re both right, but Sylvia’s way is the best. That’s the only attitude for anyone to have towards marriage. I’m afraid you lack it, my child. You want to begin with a mansion and three cars.”

===Chinese===
「热闹事儿多着呢，」乔安娜同他手挽着手坐在车里，对他说。「西尔维娅和布莱恩复活节结婚，所以妈妈现在要正式宣布了。会有几场午宴——恐怕没有你的份儿，彼得。然后我们的舞蹈班要为皮尔斯日间托儿所办一场义演。台上会有花式舞蹈，领衔主演的就是在下。我们家还要立圣诞树，还要办一场宾客盈门的连日聚会。我现在就邀请你，彼得。长大了多好，是不是？」

「那还用说。这些活动哪一场最先来？」

「西尔维娅的订婚宴。」

「这么说，她和斯宾塞真要把婚事办成了。他们等了很久了，不是吗？」

「是啊，那是因为布莱恩坚持结婚前先打下个好底子。要是依西尔维娅，订婚第二天就嫁他了。不过我觉得布莱恩是对的。」

「他们俩都没错，不过西尔维娅的想法最好。对待婚姻，人人只该抱这种态度。可惜你缺的就是这个，我的孩子。你倒好，一上来就要一座宅邸外加三辆汽车。」

===Original===
“You mean thing! I don’t care about money as money one bit and you know it. But I do care about success. And a house or a car usually implies that. Any girl likes her man to look well in the eyes of other men.”

“This man’s going to look well.” He yearned toward her. “Kiss me, sweetheart.”

“Sir, you insult me. People shouldn’t kiss unless they’re engaged.”

“Then be engaged to me, dearest Joanna. Great Scott, are we here?”

Joanna evaded him after that. Christmas was Tuesday, but as he had saved his cuts for Saturday classes, he had managed to come away the preceding Friday night. On Christmas morning he caught her before daybreak. They had arranged to go to an early service in a large Episcopal church where Joanna had recently been engaged as a soloist. He was waiting for her in the dark hall.

===Chinese===
「你真坏！钱本身我一丁点儿也不在乎，这你是知道的。可我确实在乎成功。而房子也好汽车也好，通常就意味着成功。哪个姑娘都喜欢自己的男人在别的男人眼里有头有脸。」

「这个男人会有头有脸的。」他朝她倾过身去。「亲亲我，宝贝。」

「先生，你这是冒犯我。没订婚的人不该亲嘴。」

「那就跟我订婚吧，最亲爱的乔安娜。哎呀天哪，我们到了？」

那以后乔安娜总躲着他。圣诞节那天是星期二，不过他把缺的课都折算到星期六去补，总算得以提前在星期五晚上动身。圣诞节清晨，天亮前他截住了她。两人约好去一座大圣公会教堂参加早堂礼拜，乔安娜新近受聘在那里担任独唱。他正在昏暗的门厅里等她。

===Original===
“Good! There you are, Peter. We must fly.”

“Not until you’ve told me you love me.”

“I love you, Peter. Come on.”

“No, sir, put your little arms around my neck. So. Now say, ‘Dear Peter, I love you and I’m going to marry you.’ ”

===Chinese===
「好！你可来了，彼得。我们得赶紧走。」

「先对我说你爱我，不然别想走。」

「我爱你，彼得。走吧。」

「不行，长官，把你的小胳膊搂上我的脖子。对，就这样。现在说：『亲爱的彼得，我爱你，我要嫁给你。』」

===Original===
“Oh, I can’t say that. Let me go, Peter.”

“Not one step.” He held her so close that she had to poise herself against him, breathlessly, exquisitely. A clock in the house boomed five.

“Peter, ask me tonight.”

“I’m asking you now. Answer me this minute, Joanna. Not one step will we stir till you do.” He shook her gently. “Say it, darling.”

She still had her arms around his neck. “Dear Peter,” she began, her voice breaking a little, “I love you and I’m going to marry you.”

===Chinese===
「噢，那个我可说不出。放开我，彼得。」

「一步也不放。」他把她搂得那么紧，她只得贴着他稳住身子，气喘吁吁，美妙至极。屋里一座钟当当敲了五下。

「彼得，今晚再问吧。」

「我现在就在问。这就回答我，乔安娜。你不答应，我们一步也不挪。」他轻轻摇了摇她。「说出来，亲爱的。」

她的双臂还搂着他的脖子。「亲爱的彼得，」她开口道，声音微微发颤，「我爱你，我要嫁给你。」

===Original===
“You’ve got a smudge on your face,” he told her solemnly.

She burst into hysterical tears at that. “I never thought I’d become engaged with a smudge on my face.”

“I know you didn’t. I’ll try to overlook it.” He got down on his knees and kissed her hands. “Darling Joanna, I’ll love you always.”

Between them, they wiped away the traces of the smudge and of her tears. Then they found their way out, and walked through the dark silent streets singing “Joy to the World,” like a pair of Christmas waifs.

===Chinese===
「你脸上有块灰印。」他郑重其事地告诉她。

她闻言顿时歇斯底里地哭起来。「我怎么也没想到，自己脸上带着一块灰印订了婚。」

「我知道你没想道。这块灰嘛，我尽量不计较。」他跪下去，吻了吻她的双手。「亲爱的乔安娜，我会永远爱你。」

两人一起擦去了灰印和泪痕。随后他们摸索着出了门，穿过黑暗寂静的街道，一路唱着《普世欢腾》（Joy to the World），像一对圣诞夜的小流浪儿。

===Original===
The lovers found it hard to see each other. There were too many things going on for that. Peter could have found time, but Joanna, he realized with a pang, seemed to think of nothing but her dance. When she wasn’t at a party, or dressing, she was at a rehearsal. The affair for the Day Nursery was to come off New Year’s Eve.

Monsieur Bertully’s seven pupils danced, swayed, pirouetted. Their slim silken limbs flashed and twinkled through a series of poses and groups until one thought of an animated Greek frieze. At the end the seven girls appeared as school children. Joanna as their leader was teaching them a game. Peter watched her flashing in a red dress across the stage, dancing, leaping, twirling. The orchestra struck up something vaguely familiar. Why, it was Joanna’s old dance, “Barn! Barn!”

She swayed, she balanced, she stamped her foot.

> “Stay back, girl, don’t you come near me!”

===Chinese===
情侣俩难得见上一面。要办的事太多，容不得他们相聚。彼得倒抽得出时间，可乔安娜——他痛苦地意识到——似乎满脑子只有她的舞蹈。她不是在赴会，就是在梳妆，再不就是在排练。为日间托儿所办的那场义演，定在除夕举行。

贝图利先生的七名学生舞着、摆着、旋着。她们纤细的、绸缎般的肢体在一连串造型与队形中闪动流转，让人想起一幅活起来的希腊浮雕饰带。最后，七个姑娘装扮成小学生的模样。乔安娜领着头，正教她们做一个游戏。彼得望着她一身红裙在台上闪过，舞蹈、腾跃、旋转。乐队奏起一支隐约耳熟的曲子。咦，那不正是乔安娜的老舞《谷仓！谷仓！》（“Barn! Barn!”）吗？

她摇曳，她定住身形，她跺了跺脚。

> 「退后，姑娘，你别挨近我！」

===Original===
Miss Sharples was there with a group of Greenwich Village folks, Helena Arnold told them afterwards.

Peter had to leave on New Year’s Day. It was bitterly cold and the Marshalls had dinner guests, but Joanna went to the station with him. She didn’t cry this time, Peter noticed. She didn’t tell him that it was because of the pain raging at her heart.

“I’ll have to get used to his leaving me,” she told herself stubbornly. “I’ve got it to stand, for years and years. Talking about it won’t do any good.”

She had fixed up a box of delicious sandwiches and other goodies for him, and there was a little letter in the box. But Peter didn’t know that, so in spite of her wan face he felt aggrieved as he stepped on the train, for she had barely pressed his hand and her lips were cold.

She cried herself into a headache on her way back.

===Chinese===
后来海伦娜·阿诺德告诉他们，夏普尔斯小姐也到场了，还带着一群格林尼治村的人。

彼得元旦就得动身。天气冷得刺骨，马歇尔家又来了吃饭的客人，可乔安娜还是送他去了车站。彼得注意到，这次她没有哭。她没有告诉他，那是因为她心口正翻腾着剧痛。

「我得习惯他的离开，」她倔强地对自己说。「这滋味我要忍受一年又一年。说来说去也没有用处。」

她给他备了一盒美味的三明治和其他点心，盒子里还放了一封短信。可彼得并不知道，所以尽管她脸色苍白，他上车时心里还是觉得委屈，因为她几乎没握他的手，嘴唇也是冷的。

回去的路上，她把自己哭出了头痛。

===Original===
It was bitter in Philadelphia, too. Peter got off the train at West Philadelphia. He would call on some of the boys on Sansom Street.

“They’re all out I think,” the landlady, Mrs. Larrabee, told him. She gave him a friendly smile. “You can run up, though, and see.” She was right, they were out, but the rooms were warm and comfortable.

“I think I’ll stay up here and thaw out,” he called down.

He sat in a comfortable chair, smoked a cigarette or two, read a few pages in a novel. Then he remembered Joanna’s box, and opened it. There was the letter on top.

===Chinese===
费城也一样天寒地冻。彼得在西费城下了火车。他要去桑瑟姆街拜访几个同学。

「我看他们都出去了，」房东拉腊比太太（Mrs. Larrabee）告诉他，友好地朝他笑了笑。「不过你可以跑上去瞧瞧。」她说得没错，人都出去了，可房间里温暖舒适。

「我想我就在这儿待着，暖和过来再说。」他朝楼下喊道。

他坐进一把舒服的椅子，抽了一两支烟，读了几页小说。然后他想起乔安娜的盒子，便打开了它。那封信正摆在最上面。

===Original===
> “**Dear Peter**,” he read, “isn’t it awful to have to separate this way? I have a secret I was saving for you. I’m to sing in Philadelphia very shortly. Aren’t you glad? I love you, Peter.
> “Jan.”

===Chinese===
> 「**亲爱的彼得**，」他念道，「这样分开真是难受，对不对？我有一个一直替你攒着的秘密。我不久就要来费城唱歌了。你高兴吗？我爱你，彼得。
> 「小乔。」

===Original===
His spirits went up, up.

“Good night,” he called to Mrs. Larrabee. “Happy New Year.”

It wasn’t so cold after all, he thought. Anyway, it wouldn’t do him any harm to stretch his legs a bit. He’d swing across town through the University grounds and take a car on Spruce Street.

The car jolted down over the bridge, turned one corner into a dingy side street, then another, slid ponderously into Lombard Street. It stopped to let the Twentieth Street car go by. Idly, Peter glanced out of the window. On the corner stood a woman, neatly, even carefully dressed. Something about her dejected pose made Peter look at her closely. She turned just then, and the street light fell full on an old-gold, oval face, haggard and disillusioned. Peter saw it was Maggie Ellersley.

===Chinese===
他的精神腾腾地往上升。

「晚安，」他向拉腊比太太喊道。「新年快乐。」

到底也没那么冷，他想。反正活动活动腿脚对他没什么坏处。他要横穿城区，从大学校园里过，到斯普鲁斯街去搭电车。

电车颠簸着下了桥，拐进一条灰扑扑的小街，又拐了一个弯，笨重地滑入隆巴德街。车子停下来，给二十街的电车让道。彼得百无聊赖地朝窗外瞥了一眼。街角站着一个女人，衣着整洁，甚至可以说一丝不苟。她那垂头丧气的姿态让彼得禁不住细看她。恰在此时她转过身来，路灯正正照在一张古金色的鹅蛋脸上——憔悴，幻灭。彼得认出来了：那是玛吉·埃勒斯利。

## XIV / 十四

===Original===
Poor Maggie! How relentlessly and completely had her illusions flown!

She had enjoyed the ride to Atlantic City. Her husband had surrounded her with magazines, fruit, candy, even books. She had had a wonderful dinner and when they got to Atlantic City, he took her to a very respectable, clean boardinghouse. It was nice to be protected, she realized that.
