And, when, the day after they were married, he gave her seventy-five dollars, and told her to send part of it to her mother, her spirits, which had not yet recovered from the shock of the past two days, rose considerably.

She thought Mr. Neal remarkably kind and gentle. And he was always clean. On the whole, while she was not the least bit in love with him, she considered he did pretty well, though she did wish he knew a little more about English grammar. His deliberate incorrectness made her ashamed of him and because he was so kind to her, this feeling on her part made her a little ashamed of herself.

He was the soul of generosity. Besides giving her money, he had taken her to two of the best stores, and bought her whatever she wanted. He would have liked to buy her a complete outfit, but the prices made her demur.

===Chinese===
可怜的玛吉！她的种种幻梦，竟被扫荡得这般无情、这般干净！

去大西洋城（Atlantic City）的一路，她坐得很舒心。丈夫用杂志、水果、糖果，甚至书本把她团团围住。她吃了一顿美妙的晚餐，抵达大西洋城后，他又带她住进一家体面干净的寄宿公寓。有人庇护终归是好的，这一点她心里明白。而婚后第二天，他递给她七十五美元，叫她寄一部分给母亲，她那颗还没从两天来的打击里缓过劲的心，顿时振奋了许多。

她觉得尼尔先生出奇地和气、温存。而且他总是干干净净的。总的来说，尽管她对他没有一丝一毫的爱意，却也认为他够不错的了，只是她的确希望他对英语语法能多懂一点。他张口闭口故意不讲语法，叫她替他害臊；又因为他待她那么好，这份嫌弃倒让她有点儿替自己害臊。

他慷慨到了家。除了给她钱，还带她上两家最好的铺子，她要什么便买什么。他本想给她置办一整套行头，只是那价钱让她缩了手。

===Original===
“Wait till we get to New York again. We can do better there.” But she did let him buy her a few things: There were a blue silk dress, a white satin skirt, two or three smart, delicately tinted blouses, a wonderful wrap, light but warm; tan and white shoes and stockings.

Atlantic City was a revelation to her. She had literally never been out of New York City, except once to a funeral in Brooklyn in company with the lugubrious Mis’ Sparrow. This fairyland by the sea with its colored lights, its human kaleidoscope, its boardwalk, its shops! She did not know the world held such as these.

But she was more interested in the Atlantic City that lay on the north side of Atlantic Avenue. There were many cottages here, a score of restaurants, a good drug store, all of them patronized by colored people. They were the kind of people Maggie wanted to know, she could see that at a glance. In the restaurant which she and her husband most frequented, she sat and watched the happy, laughing faces. They were like one big family although they came from Washington, Philadelphia, and Baltimore. She realized then how completely she had depended on the Marshalls and their immediate entourage. Cut off from them, she had no way of meeting these people, she possessed no background.

===Chinese===
「等咱们再回纽约去买，那儿能置办得更好。」不过她还是让他买了几样东西：一条蓝色绸裙，一条白色缎裙，两三件色调淡雅的俏丽衬衫，一件妙极了的披风外衣，轻而保暖；还有棕白两色的鞋子和长袜。

大西洋城在她眼前敞开了一个新世界。她实实在在从没出过纽约城，只有一回，跟着一脸哭丧相的斯派罗太太去布鲁克林（Brooklyn）参加了一场葬礼。这座海边的仙境，五彩的灯，人影的万花筒，木板步道，鳞次的铺子！她不知道世上竟有这样的地方。

但她更中意的，是大西洋大道（Atlantic Avenue）以北的那一片大西洋城。那里有许多小屋，二十来家餐馆，一家像样的药房，光顾的全是有色人种。这些人正是玛吉想要结交的，她一眼就看出来了。在她和丈夫最常去的那家餐馆里，她坐着端详那些快活、欢笑的脸。他们虽来自华盛顿、费城和巴尔的摩（Baltimore），却像是一个大家庭。她这才明白，自己从前对马歇尔一家和他们身边那个小圈子依赖得有多深。一旦离开他们，她便无从结识这些人，她毫无自己的根基。

===Original===
Some of the visitors seemed to know others hailing from the most remote places. One woman said, “Oh, there’s Annie Mackinaw, she’s been in San Francisco for five years you know, I must speak to her.” Surely, Maggie thought, her husband must have met some of these people somewhere. But although an occasional man nodded to him, even came up and spoke, not one brought over his wife or daughters. The women looked at Maggie, a little curiously; once she thought as she passed a large party at a table that they stopped talking with that queer suddenness which made her sure they were discussing her. They looked at her clothes, appraising them, but she could never catch their direct, gaze.

She sought to find solace in the theaters, of which she was very fond. This was an opportunity, plenty of leisure and a willing companion ready and able to take her whenever and wherever she wished. But Atlantic City theaters make no secret of their unwillingness to serve colored patrons. After being told at the ticket office that there were no more balcony seats, only to see them calmly handed out to the next white person in line; after enduring an evening in the poorly ventilated gallery with a feeling of resentment rankling in her breast; above all after seeing how these mischances awoke her husband’s passionate but futile anger, she desisted. He had a terrible, devastating temper, which left her speechless and cowering even though it was not directed toward her. Better do without the theater forever, she thought, than be the cause of awakening his savage wrath.

She returned to her survey of the colored visitors. Her husband found some friends and went off on mysterious trips with them, from which he returned amiable and pleasant and usually with some small gift for her. In his absence she sat on the piazza watching happy groups go by, or sat alone in the pavilion far down the boardwalk, where the colored people bathed. In time she came to know the characteristics of certain groups, could even tell from what city they came.

===Chinese===
度假的客人里，有些人似乎认得从天南地北来的别的人。有个女人说：「哟，那不是安妮·麦基诺（Annie Mackinaw）嘛，她在旧金山（San Francisco）待了五年了，你知道吧，我得去跟她打个招呼。」玛吉心想，她丈夫想必也在什么地方认识这些人中的一些。可是虽然偶尔有男人朝他点头，甚至走上前来搭话，却没有一个人把自己的太太或女儿领过来见她。女人们打量玛吉，带着一点好奇；有一回她从一大桌人旁边经过，觉得他们的谈话停得那么突兀古怪，让她确信方才议论的正是她。她们打量她的衣裳，估着料子成色，可她怎么也捉不住她们正眼相看的一瞥。

她想从戏院里寻些安慰，她是顶爱看戏的。眼下正是机会，闲工夫多的是，还有个殷勤的同伴，随她什么时候、去哪儿都乐意奉陪。可是大西洋城的戏院毫不掩饰它们不愿招待有色人种看客。在售票口被告知楼座的票卖完了，眼睁睁看着那些票平平静静地递给队伍里下一个白人；在通风恶劣的顶层楼座里熬过一个晚上，胸中郁着一腔怨愤；尤其是眼见这些倒霉事激得丈夫怒火中烧却又无处发泄——此后她便作罢了。他发起脾气来骇人可怕，即便那火头不是冲着她来的，也吓得她说不出话、缩成一团。她想，宁可一辈子不上戏院，也别去惹他那种凶暴的雷霆之怒。

她回过头来，继续观察那些有色人种的游客。丈夫交上了几个朋友，同他们一道出去办神秘的事，回来时总是和颜悦色，照例还给她捎点小礼物。他不在时，她就坐在游廊上看一队队快活的人走过，或是独自坐在木板步道尽头远远的凉亭里——有色人种都在那一带下水游泳。日子久了，她认得了某些人群的特点，甚至说得出他们是从哪个城市来的。

===Original===
Philadelphians were not as a rule as strikingly dressed as the folks, say, from Washington, but they had a better time. They seemed bound by some kind of tie, family, perhaps—which made it possible for them to group together incongruously but with evident enjoyment. Old women and young girls, young girls and elderly men, young men and almost middle-aged women, laughed and bathed and gossiped like brothers and sisters. These were the hardest to approach; it was impossible to invade their solidarity. They made the status of the outsider very clear.

The Baltimore people were somewhat like these, only gayer. They were clannish, too, but more willing to let down bars. Clearly they were a cross between the Philadelphians and the gay Washingtonians who played about in very distinct groups, superb in their fashionable clothes and their deep assurance.

Maggie’s landlady introduced her to one girl, a Miss Talbert from Philadelphia, who came up on the piazza one day to inquire for a former boarder. She was brown, not pretty, rather plainly but well dressed, with a beautiful manner. An atmosphere of niceness hung about her.

===Chinese===
费城人一般来说穿得不像华盛顿人那么惹眼，却玩得更尽兴。他们之间仿佛系着某种纽带，也许是亲族——这使他们能够错错落落地聚在一处，又显然乐在其中。老太太和大姑娘，大姑娘和老翁，小伙子们和将近中年的妇人，像兄弟姐妹一样又说又笑、下水游泳、闲话家常。这些人最难接近；他们抱成一团，外人插不进去。他们把「外人」的身份摆得清清楚楚。

巴尔的摩人和这些人有些相像，只是更快活些。他们也抱团，却更肯放下门槛。他们显然介于费城人和快活的华盛顿人之间——后者成群结伙地游乐，界线分明，衣着入时，气定神闲，神气十足。

玛吉的房东太太给她引见了一位姑娘，是费城来的塔尔伯特小姐（Miss Talbert），有一天上游廊来打听一位从前往过的房客。她肤色深棕，算不上漂亮，衣着素净却很考究，仪态美好。她周身笼罩着一种娴雅的气息。

===Original===
She acknowledged the introduction pleasantly. “You’re from New York, Mrs. Neal—I wonder if you know my cousin Sylvia Marshall?”

Maggie could have jumped for joy. “She’s my best friend.”

Things went a little better, then. Miss Talbert asked her to go in bathing, introduced her to a few people, beckoned her over to her table at lunch. But she and her party were staying for only three days more, and Maggie was almost as badly off as ever when she left.

===Chinese===
她客客气气地领受了这番引见。「您是从纽约来的吧，尼尔太太——不知您认不认识我表姐西尔维娅·马歇尔？」

玛吉简直要欢喜得跳起来。「她是我顶好的朋友。」

这下子情形好了一点。塔尔伯特小姐约她一同下水游泳，给她引见了几个人，午饭时又招手让她到自己桌上同坐。可她们一行再住三天就要走了，她一离开，玛吉几乎又落回先前那种无处结交的窘境。

===Original===
Her husband took her down to the pavilion the next day, and left her there. A sharp-faced old woman wearing a plain sad-colored dress and a formidable false front, beckoned to her.

“What does your husband do?” she asked the girl, looking at her over sloping glasses.

Maggie, confused, said he was in the motor-business. The old woman turned incredulously away.

===Chinese===
第二天丈夫带她下到凉亭那边，把她一个人留在那儿。一个脸相刻薄的老妇人朝她招手，穿一身暗淡的素色衣裳，头上顶着一大副叫人望而生畏的假发帘。

「你丈夫是做什么的？」她从溜到鼻尖的眼镜上方打量着姑娘，问道。

玛吉一时慌乱，说他是做汽车生意的。老妇人满脸不信地转过身去。

===Original===
She determined to ask her husband about his work. But he gave her no satisfaction.

“You wouldn’t understand it. Too much explaining to it. I make money enough for you, don’t I, girl?” He laid a heavy hand on her frail shoulder.

He thought he’d go to Philadelphia to live. “Feller told me of some good prospects there. We’ll just room for a while. If we don’t like it, we can go back to New York.”

===Chinese===
她打定主意问问丈夫他干的行当。可他没给她一个痛快的答复。

「你听不懂的。要解释的太多了。我给你挣的钱够花了，不是吗，丫头？」他把一只沉甸甸的手搭在她纤弱的肩上。

他想到费城去住。「有个伙计跟我说，那儿有些好门路。咱们先租间房住一阵。要是不喜欢，再回纽约去。」

===Original===
She was satisfied. She didn’t want to return to New York, she realized. Her mother could make out with the money which, Neal had assured her, she could send regularly. And it made her sick to think of the Marshalls.

Without regrets she mounted the train with him one day and went to the big, sprawling city. Its size, its long stretches of streets appalled her. The awful silence which seemed to descend over the town when she got below Walnut Street frightened her. One could be very lonely here, no doubt.

The “rooming” of which her husband had spoken proved to mean the second floor of a house in South Fifteenth Street. There were three rooms and a bath. She liked this because it gave her something to do. She kept them clean, arranged and rearranged the charming furniture which Neal gave her, and prepared their simple meals.

===Chinese===
她满意了。她明白自己并不想回纽约。尼尔担保过，她可以按月寄钱，母亲靠这些钱过得下去。而一想起马歇尔一家，她就心里发堵。

一天，她毫无留恋地随他登上火车，来到这座四下铺展的大城。城的规模，那一条条望不到头的长街，都叫她心惊。到了胡桃街（Walnut Street）以南，全城仿佛罩下来一层可怕的沉寂，让她害怕。在这里，人无疑会非常孤单。

丈夫所说的「租间房住」，原来是南十五街（South Fifteenth Street）上一所房子的二楼。三个房间，一间浴室。她喜欢这地方，因为它让她有事可做。她把屋子收拾得干干净净，把尼尔给她买的漂亮家具摆了又摆，再做好他们简单的饭菜。

===Original===
It was the first time she had had a really attractive setting. And she was soothed, bewitched by its effect. Her rather simple plan of life contained, it must be remembered, only three ideas—comfort, respectability, and love. This last had been added to her list very recently. She would have married Philip any time during the last five years without loving him, for the sake of the security which he could have brought her. So it is not strange, then, that she and Neal sailed their little craft so smoothly. It is true that marriage did not in reality prove as interesting and picturesque as she in common with most girls had conceived it to be. But marriage was marriage, and she must make the best of it. Neal was still kind, almost fatherly, very generous, clean, and, as far as she could see, had no bad habits. He smoked one cigar after each meal, and almost never drank.

===Chinese===
这是她头一回有了一个真正称心的环境。这环境抚慰了她，把她迷住了。要知道，她那套颇为简单的人生规划拢共只有三条——舒适、体面和爱情。最后这一条，是近来才添进单子里的。过去五年里的任何时候，她都可以不爱菲利普而嫁给他，为的是他能带来的那份安稳。如此说来，她同尼尔把这条小船撑得这般平稳，也就不足为怪了。诚然，婚姻实际上并不像她同大多数姑娘一同设想的那样有趣、那样如画。可婚姻就是婚姻，她只能随遇而安、尽力过好。尼尔依旧和气，近乎慈父，出手大方，干干净净，而且就她所见，没有半点坏习气。他每顿饭后抽一支雪茄，几乎滴酒不沾。

===Original===
“Can’t afford it in my business,” she heard him say often. His business! If only he hadn’t been so mysterious about that. Still it must be all right. Men called on him pretty often and he would see them in the middle room, which Maggie had turned into a restful living room. Certainly he made plenty of money.

She had comfort then and she did not feel the lack of love. Occasionally it occurred to her, it would be nice to be performing some of her housewifely duties for Philip. She thought he would enjoy doing some of them with her. But perhaps that was because he was young. Things seemed to change so when one became old—at least elderly. And she did not think Philip would have been out as much as Neal.

Her passion, however, was for respectable company—for more than that if she had but known it. She wanted friends, impeccable young women with whom she could talk over things, and exchange patterns and recipes, or go to the matinée. Once she met Miss Talbert on Christian Street. The girl greeted her kindly but a bit doubtfully, spoke about the weather. Then came the query:

===Chinese===
「干我这行，喝不起。」她常听他这么说。他那一行！只可惜他对这一行讳莫如深。不过总归是正当的。常有男人来找他，他就在中间那间屋里见客——玛吉已把它收拾成一间清幽的客厅。他挣的钱固然是多的。

这样她有了舒适，也不觉得缺什么爱了。偶尔她会想到，若是替菲利普做这些主妇的活计，该有多好。她想，他准乐意同她一起干几样。不过这也许是因为他年轻。人一上了年纪——至少是不再年轻——事情似乎就全变了。而且她想，菲利普总不会像尼尔这样老在外头跑。

然而，她心里渴慕的是体面的交往——倘若她自己明白，渴慕的其实远不止于此。她想要朋友，想要无懈可击的年轻女子，能同她们谈谈心事，交换纸样和食谱，或是一同去看日场演出。有一回她在克里斯蒂安街（Christian Street）碰见塔尔伯特小姐。姑娘客客气气地同她打招呼，却带着几分迟疑，扯了几句天气。接着来了那句问话：

===Original===
“What did you say your husband’s name was, Mrs. Neal?”

“Why Neal, of course, oh, Henderson, Henderson Neal.”

Miss Talbert looked at her a little sadly, exchanged a few more banalities, and went on her assured way.

“I did hope she’d ask me to call,” Maggie murmured. “How am I ever to get to know anybody in this great town?”

===Chinese===
「您方才说您丈夫叫什么名字来着，尼尔太太？」

「自然是尼尔呀，哦，是亨德森，亨德森·尼尔。」

塔尔伯特小姐略带怜悯地看了她一眼，又客套了几句淡话，便从容自信地走她的路去了。

「我原先还指望她会请我去串门呢。」玛吉喃喃道。「在这座大城里，我到底怎样才能结识人呢？」

===Original===
On the floor above her lived a girl and her brother, Annie and Thomas Mason. The brother played and the girl sewed and kept house. Once Annie got a letter of Neal’s by mistake and brought it down to Maggie. She was in her living-room trying to shorten a skirt when Annie tapped.

She stepped to the door. “Oh, come in.”

Miss Mason came in, nothing loth. “I got your husband’s letter by mistake. He’s Mr. H. Neal ain’t he?” She held out the letter glancing about the room. “You’ve fixed it up real pretty here. The last roomers kept the place looking so bad. You going to stay long?”

Maggie didn’t know. She was transported at the sight of the pleasant-voiced friendly girl and the North Pennsylvania accent which carried with it something very wholesome and grateful.

===Chinese===
她楼上住着一对兄妹，安妮·梅森和托马斯·梅森（Annie and Thomas Mason）。哥哥以奏乐为业，妹妹做针线、操持家务。有一回安妮错拿了一封尼尔的信，便给玛吉送下楼来。她正在客厅里改短一条裙子，安妮敲了门。

她应声走到门边。「噢，进来吧。」

梅森小姐并不推辞，走了进来。「我错拿了您先生的信。他是 H·尼尔先生，对吧？」她递出信，眼睛在屋里四处打量。「你们把这儿拾掇得真漂亮。先前那两家房客把屋子弄得不像样。你们要长住吗？」

玛吉不知道。这姑娘嗓音悦耳、待人亲热，一口宾夕法尼亚北部的口音里透着某种格外淳厚、格外暖人的东西，玛吉简直欣喜若狂。

===Original===
Miss Mason was frankly curious. “You here alone all day? What do you do while your husband’s to work?”

“Oh, clean, and sew and—and nap,” Maggie laughed a little. “Don’t you want to come to see me sometime, now, this afternoon?”

Miss Mason thought she “might’s well, your room seems bigger’n mine ’cause we’ve got a piano and you’ve got a table there. Say, s’pose I was to bring my sewing down, and I could help you even off your skirt.”

===Chinese===
梅森小姐毫不掩饰她的好奇。「你成天一个人在家？你丈夫上班去了，你做些什么呀？」

「噢，收拾屋子，做针线，还有——还有睡午觉。」玛吉轻轻笑了。「你哪天不想来我这儿坐坐吗？就今天下午，好不好？」

梅森小姐想了想，「去也行，你们屋比我们的大，我们那儿摆了架钢琴，你们这儿摆的是张桌子。哎，要是我把针线活儿带下来做，兴许还能帮你把裙子边弄平齐呢。」

===Original===
After that they spent a great deal of time together. They walked in the quiet autumn evenings down dingy Fifteenth Street, past the hideousness of Washington Avenue, down, down the stretch of unswerving street to Tasker or Morris, through to Broad Street which is really Fourteenth. They sauntered back arm in arm under young but fading trees, past the hurry of flying automobiles, under the soft silver of the street lights. Then they turned up Catherine Street, stopped at the bakery for ice-cream or a bag of cakes and so to the house to bed.

It was a pleasant, almost a bucolic friendship. Both girls had rather simple tastes. Sometimes they went further up Broad Street to the theaters, choosing the ones where they met with the least discrimination. Once Maggie took Annie to the Academy of Music. They stood in line for their seats and Maggie looked at the billboards. One of them read:

> Coming! 

 The Philadelphia Orchestra 

 Mr. Hubert Sanderson 

 Conductor 

 December 27th, 1910 

 Mr. Thomas Morse 

 will present 

 Miss Joanna Marshall 

 mezzo-soprano of New York

===Chinese===
打那以后，她们俩常在一块儿消磨时光。在静谧的秋夜，她们顺着灰扑扑的十五街往下走，绕过华盛顿大道（Washington Avenue）那一片丑陋，沿着笔直的长街一直走、一直走到塔斯克街或莫里斯街（Tasker or Morris），再穿到布罗德街（Broad Street）——其实就是十四街。回来时她们挽着臂闲逛，走过一排还年轻却已凋零的树下，身边汽车飞驰匆匆，头顶街灯洒下柔和的银光。然后拐上凯瑟琳街（Catherine Street），在面包店门口停一停，买个冰淇淋或一纸袋点心，就回屋睡觉。

这是一段愉快的、几乎称得上田园牧歌式的友谊。两个姑娘的趣味都不复杂。有时她们沿着布罗德街再往上走，去那些戏院，专挑受歧视最轻的几家。有一回玛吉带安妮去音乐学会剧院（Academy of Music）。她们排队等着买座位的票，玛吉望着广告牌。其中一块上写着：

> 即将上演！ 

 费城管弦乐团 

 休伯特·桑德森先生 

 指挥 

 1910 年 12 月 27 日 

 托马斯·莫尔斯先生 

 呈献 

 乔安娜·马歇尔小姐 

 纽约女中音

===Original===
She turned away, a little sick.

Maggie usually paid for their outings. Annie’s brother made a pretty fair salary, his sister told Maggie, for he played at private dances for wealthy white people in West Philadelphia, Rosemont, Sharon, Chestnut Hill and various other suburbs.

“But he don’t give me much ’cause he wants to leave the country for good sometime. I keep house for him and he pays for the lodgings and for most of our food. I make what little extra I can by taking in plain sewing. Your husband’s right openhanded, ain’t he?”

“Yes,” said Maggie heartily. “He’s very generous and very kind.” She wanted to change the subject, for Annie was inquisitive—one never knew what she’d ask next.

===Chinese===
她转过脸去，只觉一阵恶心。

出门的花销照例是玛吉付。安妮告诉玛吉，她哥哥薪水相当不错，因为他常去西费城（West Philadelphia）、罗斯蒙特（Rosemont）、莎伦（Sharon）、栗树山（Chestnut Hill）以及各处郊外，给有钱的白人的私人舞会奏乐。

「可他没给我几个钱，因为他还想有朝一日永远离开这个国家呢。我替他管家，房钱和大部分吃食都归他出。我接些素活计缝缝，多少再挣一点。你先生出手可真大方，是不是？」

「是啊，」玛吉由衷地说，「他非常大方，非常和气。」她想换个话题，因为安妮爱打听——谁也说不准她下句要问什么。

===Original===
“Funny, ain’t it,” pursued Annie, her mouth full of pins—she was at her everlasting sewing, turning up the hem of a bathrobe—“I ain’t never seen him yet, no, nor Tom neither.”

“Well, you will. Come and walk up to South Street with me. I want to get some postal cards.”

It was an aimless existence, but it had its points. Her mother was comfortable, she herself had ease, a husband and a companion.

===Chinese===
「也真怪，是不是，」安妮接着说，嘴里衔满了大头针——她那永远做不完的针线活，这会儿正给一件浴袍翻折下摆——「我到现在还没见过他呢，真的，汤姆也没见过。」

「会见着的。跟我一块儿上南街（South Street）走走吧。我想去买几张明信片。」

这是一种漫无目的的日子，倒也有它的好处。母亲衣食无忧，她自己过得安适，有丈夫，还有个伴儿。

===Original===
She went out to market one chilly November morning and came back later than she expected. She had scarcely got in before Annie appeared, an unusual flush on her yellow, freckled cheeks. Annie had reddish, crinkled hair, which she wore brushed stiffly back from her high forehead into a hard, ungraceful knob; “rhiny” hair, Maggie knew Sylvia and the boys would call it. She could imagine how they would talk about Annie in their pleasant, unmalicious way. Joanna would strike her attitude and imitate her accent. Annie broke into these reminiscences.

===Chinese===
一个清冷的十一月早晨，她出门上市场，回来得比预想的晚。她前脚刚进门，安妮就来了，黄黄的、有雀斑的脸颊上泛着一团少见的红晕。安妮有一头微红的鬈发，从高高的额前板板地梳到脑后，绾成一个硬邦邦、不好看的髻；玛吉知道，西尔维娅和那帮男孩准会管这叫「硬翘翘」（rhiny）的头发。她想象得出，他们会用那种和和气气、不带恶意的腔调怎样谈论安妮。乔安娜还会摆出她的架势，学她的口音。安妮打断了她的这些追忆。

===Original===
“I been down here two or three times a’ready. Kind o’ rawish like.”

“Yes, I think it’s going to rain. I’ll light the gas-heater and we can sit here and thaw out. I enjoy a chilly day if it’s warm inside.”

“Kind o’ that way myself.”

“Oh, you said you’d been here before. Want to see me about anything special?”

===Chinese===
「我下来过两三回了。天真有点儿阴冷兮兮的。」

「可不是，我看要下雨了。我把煤气炉点上，咱们坐在这儿暖和暖和。只要屋里是暖的，我还挺喜欢冷天呢。」

「我也差不多是这个脾气。」

「哦，你刚才说你想来找我？有什么特别的事吗？」

===Original===
“Oh, aimed I’d come set with you a spell. Me and Tom—now—we saw your husband last night.”

“That so? Where? How’d you guess it was he?”

“Near Bainbridge Street, then we watched him come in here. Why, Tom knowed him a’ready. I didn’t know his name was Henderson. I’d heard of him before myself.”

===Chinese===
「哦，就打算来陪你坐一会儿。我跟汤姆——对了——昨晚看见你丈夫了。」

「是吗？在哪儿？你怎么认出是他？」

「班布里奇街（Bainbridge Street）附近，后来我们眼看着他走进这儿来。嗐，汤姆早就认得他。我原先不知道他叫亨德森。我自个儿从前也听说过他。」

===Original===
Outside a steady soaking rain had begun to fall in the gray somberness of the November afternoon. The gas-heater cast a ruddy oblong of light on the white ceiling. Maggie, who had been straightening out a paper pattern, crossed the room and threw her slight figure on the couch, huddling close against the wall. She shivered a little in the luxurious warmth.

===Chinese===
外头，十一月的午后灰暗阴沉，一场透雨已经稳稳地落了下来。煤气炉在白色天花板上投出一方长方的红光。玛吉本来在抚平一张纸样，这时穿过屋子，把自己瘦小的身子往长沙发上一放，紧挨着墙缩着。在这一片奢适的暖意里，她微微打了个哆嗦。

===Original===
“Isn’t it grand to be indoors? Where did you ever hear of my husband?”

She was becoming drowsy and did not notice at first that Annie had not answered her. When she did, she looked up suddenly to catch the girl’s doglike brown eyes fixed wistfully on hers.

“What’s the matter Annie?”

===Chinese===
「待在屋里多美啊。你到底是在哪儿听说我丈夫的？」

她渐渐困倦起来，起初没留意安妮没有答话。等她觉察了，猛一抬头，正撞见姑娘那双小狗似的棕色眼睛，怔怔地、哀恳地望着她的眼睛。

「怎么了，安妮？」

===Original===
“Nothing.”

“Oh, but there is. Are you sick? Has Tom been unkind to you?”

“Oh, it isn’t me. It’s you! Oh, Maggie, how could you?”

“What about me? How could I what?”

===Chinese===
「没什么。」

「噢，一定有事。你病了吗？汤姆待你不好？」

「噢，不是我。是你！噢，玛吉，你怎么能呢？」

「我怎么了？我怎么能什么？」

===Original===
“Marry him?”

“Marry whom? my husband—why shouldn’t I?”

“Didn’t you know?”

“For God’s sake speak up, Annie Mason. What is it you know about him? Has he got another wife? Is he an escaped convict?”

===Chinese===
「嫁给他呀？」

「嫁给谁？嫁给我丈夫——我为什么不该嫁？」

「你原来不知道？」

「看在上帝份上，你就直说吧，安妮·梅森。你知道他什么事？他另有老婆？他是个越狱的罪犯？」

===Original===
“He’s a gambler.”

“A what?”

“A gambler. Tom knows him well. And I guess I musta saw him when I was a little girl. He used to live up around Stroudsburg. They run him out of town.”

“I’ll never believe it.” But in her heart she did. That money—why, of course, his long hours, especially at night, his reticence—all this combined to make her recognize the truth.

===Chinese===
「他是个赌徒。」

「一个什么？」

「一个赌徒。汤姆跟他很熟。我想我小时候八成也见过他。他从前就住在斯特劳兹堡（Stroudsburg）那一带。是人家把他撵出城去的。」

「我绝不相信。」可她心里是信的。那些钱——可不是嘛，他那长长的外出钟点，尤其是夜里的，他的讳莫如深——这一切凑在一起，叫她认下了这个真相。

===Original===
“You poor thing. Of course you don’t want to believe it. That’s what I said to Tom. I said, ‘That poor thing, she’s got no notion of it.’”

It was intolerable, such pity! “Where is your brother, Annie?”

“Who, Tom! Prob’ly upstairs, he don’t go out to rehearsal till four.”

“Tell him to come here.”

===Chinese===
「你多可怜呀。你当然不愿意信。我跟汤姆就是这么说的。我说：『那可怜的人儿，她一点儿都不知道呢。』」

这样的怜悯，叫人无法忍受！「你哥哥呢，安妮？」

「谁，汤姆吗！多半在楼上，他要到四点才出去排练。」

「叫他到这儿来一趟。」

===Original===
Annie went out, whimpering a little, twisting her fingers in the folds of her white apron. She came back followed by a tall thin young man, dark, with kind, soft brown eyes. Maggie noticed that the hair in front of his ears was unshaven to form flat side-whiskers. “Siders” the boys used to call them. They had teased Sandy about them, for he had affected them in his college days.

She was standing by the table holding the envelope of the paper pattern in her hand. “Mr. Mason, what’s this you know about my husband?”

“Annie shouldn’t have told you, ma’am,” he said abjectly. “It was none of her business.”

===Chinese===
安妮出去了，轻声抽噎着，手指绞着白围裙的褶子。她回来时身后跟着一个瘦高个儿的年轻人，肤色黝黑，一双温和的棕色眼睛。玛吉注意到他耳前的头发没有剃掉，留成了两片平贴的颊须。小伙子们从前管这个叫「连鬓儿」（siders）。他们还拿这个取笑过桑迪，因为他上大学那阵子偏爱留这个。

她站在桌旁，手里攥着那只纸样袋。「梅森先生，你知道我丈夫的什么事？」

「安妮不该告诉您的，太太。」他低声下气地说。「这不关她的事。」

===Original===
“Well, she has. Sit down, please, and tell me all you know.”

“I’d rather stand, thank you, ma’am. Well if I must. Even when I was a little boy, Henderson Neal was knowed to be a cardsharp. There wasn’t nobody could stand against him. Used to wait for the men on a Saturday night, white and colored. He’d meet ’em in the bar and treat, and then ask ’em in on a little game. And they’d play, till they was cleaned out. Then he’d give ’em another drink, and clap ’em on the back. Perhaps he’d hand ’em back a dollar. ‘Better luck next time old man!’ And they’d come back the next Saturday night, the poor fools. Some of them blowed their brains out, they got so far back in their debts.”

She was tearing the envelope into bits, but her voice was steady. “You’re sure of this?”

===Chinese===
「可她已经说了。请坐吧，把你所知道的都告诉我。」

「我情愿站着，谢谢您，太太。好吧，非说不可的话。我还是个小孩子的时候，亨德森·尼尔就是出了名的牌局老千。当年没有一个人斗得过他。他惯常在星期六晚上候着那些男人，白人黑人都有。他在酒馆里迎上他们，请他们喝酒，然后邀他们入局玩个小牌。他们就玩呀玩的，直到输得精光。这时他再请他们喝一杯，拍拍他们的肩膀。兴许还塞还给他们一块钱。『老伙计，下回运气好点！』到了下个星期六晚上，那些可怜的傻瓜又回来了。有些人债台筑得太高，干脆拿枪崩了自己的脑袋。」

她正把那只纸样袋撕成碎片，声音却是平稳的。「这些你都确信？」

===Original===
“My uncle was one of them that killed theirselves. They was a colored minister come to Stroudsburg and he run him out of town. Then he crossed over to Phillipsburg, then down to Trenton. They made things too hot for him there, too. Then he got in with a white saloon-keeper in the mining districts in Pennsylvania. Finally things got too hot for him and he left the country for a while, was servant to an actor. He come back in about five years with another name.”

“An alias,” murmured Annie who read the papers.

“But pretty soon he started out again under his own name. You see he got some political protection in New York, and I guess he’s got the same here. Most people know about him a’ready. I’m sorry I had to tell you, ma’am.”

===Chinese===
「我叔叔就是那些寻了短见的人里头的一个。后来有位有色人种的牧师来到斯特劳兹堡，把他撵出了城。他又窜到菲利普斯堡（Phillipsburg），再折到特伦顿（Trenton）。在那儿人家也叫他混不下去了。后来他搭上了宾夕法尼亚矿区一个开酒馆的白人。末了那儿也待不住了，他出国躲了一阵，给一个演员当仆人。大约五年后回来，换了个名字。」

「化名。」爱看报纸的安妮低声说。

「可是没过多久，他又用回自己的本名干起来了。您瞧，他在纽约攀上了政治靠山，我琢磨在这儿也一样。大多数人早知道他的底细了。很抱歉我不得不告诉您这些，太太。」

===Original===
“Yes, yes, I’m sure. Would—would you mind leaving me now? You, too, Annie—please.”

She didn’t lie down and moan and cry as she had done—was it less than six months ago?—when she received Joanna’s letter. That was child’s trouble compared to this. She had wanted so to be decent, and she was a gambler’s wife. God! how funny!

Now she must think, she must think. Oh, what was she to do? Leave him, she knew that. But afterwards? She had no money. He had given her her very clothes. Her old ones were at her mother’s. Her mother!

===Chinese===
「是，是，我相信。现在——现在你们能不能让我一个人待着？你也一样，安妮——求你了。」

她没有像上一回那样——那是不是还不到半年以前的事？——收到乔安娜的信时，倒在地上呻啕痛哭。比起这一回，那还算小孩子家的烦恼。她那么想做个体面人，到头来却做了赌徒的妻子。天哪！多么可笑！

现在她得想一想，她得想一想。唉，她该怎么办？离开他，这个她知道。可往后呢？她没有钱。她身上穿的每一件衣裳都是他给的。旧衣裳都在母亲那儿。母亲！

===Original===
“Poor Mamma!” she said again as on a former occasion. “What a hell her life’s always been!”

No wonder those people, those men in Atlantic City who knew him didn’t introduce their women folks to her.

“I suppose they thought ‘You thief! Dressing that girl on other men’s money!’”

===Chinese===
「可怜的妈妈！」她又说了一遍，同从前那回一样。「她这一辈子过的都是什么地狱般的日子啊！」

难怪大西洋城那些人——那些认得他的男人们——不把他们的女眷引见给她。

「我猜他们是这么想的：『你这个贼！拿别的男人的钱给这丫头买穿戴！』」

===Original===
Pretty soon he’d be home for dinner. She heard him presently coming up the stairs. There! He had stepped on the creaky one. That meant he was—now—just outside the door. He stepped in.

“Nice and warm in here.”

She barely allowed him time to take off his overcoat. “Henderson, I know how you make your money. You’re a gambler.”

He didn’t deny it. “Who told you that?”

===Chinese===
一会儿他就要回家吃晚饭了。不多时她听见他上楼来了。听！他踩上了那级吱呀作响的楼梯。这就是说，他——此刻——就在门外了。他跨进门来。

「这儿可真暖和。」

她几乎没容他脱下大衣。「亨德森，我知道你的钱是怎么挣来的了。你是个赌徒。」

他没有否认。「谁告诉你的？」

===Original===
“The nephew of that man, that Mr. Mason (she hazarded the name) who shot himself in Stroudsburg.”

“Where’d you see him?”

“What difference does that make? And I’ve been living like a queen off stolen money. I want you to know I’m leaving you this instant.”

He caught her by the arm. “Don’t be a fool, Maggie!”

===Chinese===
「那个人，那个在斯特劳兹堡开枪自尽的梅森先生（她赌着猜了这个姓）的侄子。」

「你在哪儿见到他的？」

「那有什么要紧？我一直像王后一样过着日子，花的却是偷来的钱。我告诉你，我这就要离开你。」

他一把抓住她的胳膊。「别犯傻了，玛吉！」

===Original===
She could see the blood mounting, as his temper rose, shadowing his dark face.

“That’s what I’m trying to do—stop being a fool.”

“Where will you go, how can you live? Off my money? You’ve none of your own.”

“I’ll make some.”

===Chinese===
她眼见血往头上涌，随着他的火气升起，在他黝黑的脸上蒙上一层阴翳。

「我正是想这么办——不再犯傻。」

「你要去哪儿？你靠什么活？靠我的钱？你自己一个子儿都没有。」

「我会去挣。」

===Original===
“I’ll never let you go. I’ll kill you first.” He crushed both slender wrists in his brutal hand and she went ashen with pain.

“I wish you would kill me.”

He flung her away from him then and she leaned back against the wall, breathing hard.

“I suppose you’ll go back to that man, that fine gentleman that didn’t want you.”

===Chinese===
「我绝不放你走。先杀了你再说。」他用那只粗暴的手死死攥住她两截纤细的手腕，她疼得面如死灰。

「我倒巴不得你杀了我。」

他于是把她狠狠一推，她仰面靠在墙上，喘着粗气。

「我猜你要回到那个男人身边去，回到那位不肯要你的漂亮绅士身边去。」

===Original===
“Isn’t it likely he’d want me now? I was a nice girl then, not the wife of a gambler.”

He broke down suddenly at that, sank in a chair, buried his head in his hands.

“What do you want me to do?”

“I want you to let me go.” Her voice was hard.

===Chinese===
「他现在还会要我吗？那时候我是个清白姑娘，可不是赌徒的老婆。」

这句话顿时把他击垮了，他跌坐进椅子里，双手抱住了头。

「你要我怎么办？」

「我要你放我走。」她的声音冷硬。

===Original===
He lifted a wretched face. “You wouldn’t stay even if I was to do something else—something decent?”

But she couldn’t forgive him for dragging her into this abyss, this slough of degradation.

“You couldn’t change now, and anyway I wouldn’t live with you.”

To her amazement he got up, took his hat and coat and started for the door.

“I’ll go. You’re not the one to be turned out. You know I pay for these rooms a quarter in advance. This here’s the beginning of the second quarter. There’s some money in the top bureau drawer.”

===Chinese===
他抬起一张凄惨的脸。「就算我改行去干别的——干点正经的——你也不肯留下了？」

可她无法原谅他把她拖进了这个深渊，这滩堕落的泥沼。

「你现在改不了了，再说，我也不愿再跟你过。」

叫她吃惊的是，他站起身，拿起帽子和大衣，朝门口走去。

「我走。被扫地出门的不该是你。你知道这些屋子我是按季度预付的房钱。这刚是第二季度的开头。五斗柜最上层的抽屉里放了些钱。」

===Original===
“I don’t want the money. Take it with you.” She got it and stuffed a handful of bills—yellow ones—in the pocket of his overcoat. “I don’t want your rooms, either.”

“You’ll have to keep them. You’ve no money and you’ve no place to go. You ain’t got a friend in Philadelphia, and you can’t walk to New York. If you walk around the streets long enough, you’ll find there’s worse things can happen than being a gambler’s wife.” He straightened up. “If you don’t promise me to stay, I’ll tag around after you everywheres you go.”

“If I stay—for a while—will you promise me not to come back?”

“I promise.”

“Pooh, the promise of a gambler!” She hated him.

“I’ll show you. Best not to try me too far though, Maggie.”

“Well, are you going?”

===Chinese===
「我不要那钱。你带上。」她把钱取来，抓起一把钞票——黄票子——塞进他大衣口袋。「你的屋子我也不要。」

「你非留着不可。你没有钱，也没有地方可去。你在费城一个朋友都没有，纽约又走不到。要是在街上逛得久了，你就会发现，比做赌徒的老婆更糟的事有的是。」他挺直了身子。「你要是不答应留下，我就跟着你，你走到哪儿我跟到哪儿。」

「要是我留下——留一阵子——你能不能向我保证不回来？」

「我保证。」

「呸，赌徒的保证！」她恨他。

「我会证明给你看。不过玛吉，最好别把我逼得太狠。」

「怎么样，你走不走？」

===Original===
He walked out, closing the door very quietly after him. She had not shed a tear, she did not now. Instead she sat, with her brow wrinkled, trying to recall something.

“Oh, yes,” she sprang up and rushed to the closet, pulling with nervous, shaking fingers at the garments hanging there. In the pocket of her little poplin suit, the suit in which she was married, she found what she was looking for.

===Chinese===
他走了出去，随手把门极轻地带上。先前她没掉一滴泪，此刻也没有。她只是坐在那里，眉头紧锁，极力回想着一件什么事。

「哦，对了。」她一跃而起，奔向衣柜，用紧张发抖的手指去翻里面挂着的衣裳。在她那套小小的府绸套装——她结婚时穿的那套——的口袋里，她找到了要找的东西。

===Original===
It was an oblong business card, slightly soiled around the edges. She had come across it in Atlantic City and for some reason had kept it. Across the front ran a neat superscription

> **Madame Harkness** 

 Hair Culturist 

 270 West 137th Street 

 New York City

Her glance dropped to the left-hand corner. Yes, she was right, there it was: Branch offices—Washington, DC, 1307 U Street, N. W.; Baltimore, 1816 Druid Hill Avenue; Philadelphia, 2021 South Street.

===Chinese===
那是一张长方形的名片，四边微微有些脏了。她是在大西洋城偶然得到它的，不知怎么就留了下来。正面横着一行端正的题名

> **哈克尼斯夫人（Madame Harkness）** 

 护发专家 

 西 137 街 270 号 

 纽约市

她的目光落到左下角。没错，她想得对，就在那儿：分店——华盛顿特区西北区 U 街 1307 号；巴尔的摩德鲁伊德山大道（Druid Hill Avenue）1816 号；费城南街 2021 号。

===Original===
She sat all night brooding wide eyed over the purring gas-stove. In the morning she made herself tidy and walked up to Twentieth and South.

===Chinese===
她对着嗡嗡低鸣的煤气炉，睁着眼睛思前想后枯坐了一整夜。早晨，她把自己拾掇齐整，朝二十街与南街口走去。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
One of the mysteries of the ages will be solved with the answer to the question: Why do men consider women incalculable? Peter had been hurt by Joanna’s indifference again and again, she had refused a dozen times to marry him, she had scolded him, teased him, slighted him. Yet she had always come back to his eager arms. In spite of this he had been unable to see in her attitude at Christmas and in the unkind letter which she had written the logical outcome of her earlier acts﻿—all of which by enduring he had tacitly endorsed.

He read the letter in a maze of anger and wounded pride. Before he knew it he had caught up his cap and started for Maggie’s house. By the time the long, yellow, crawling car had jolted him over the uneven reaches of Lombard Street and set him down at Fifteenth he was in a fever of bitterness, resentment and self-pity. Maggie hardly knew him when he entered her little sitting-room.

===Chinese===
亘古谜团之一，一旦回答了下面这个问题便会告破：男人为什么认为女人不可捉摸？彼得被乔安娜的冷淡伤了一回又一回，她十几次拒绝嫁给他，骂过他、逗弄过他、冷落过他。可她总会回到他热切张开的怀抱里。尽管如此，他却始终没能看出来，她在圣诞节时的态度、她写来的那封狠心的信，正是她先前种种作为的必然结果——那些作为，他都一一忍了下来，也就等于默默认可了。

他满腔怒火、自尊受创，昏头涨脑地把那封信读了一遍。不知不觉间，他已抓起帽子，动身往玛吉家去。等那辆长长的、黄澄澄、慢吞吞的电车载着他，在隆巴德街高低不平的一路上颠簸到十五街口把他放下，他心里已经烧起一团又苦、又怨、又自怜的火。走进她那间小客厅时，玛吉几乎认不出他来。

===Original===
“Oh, Peter,” she went up to him swiftly, “something awful has happened.”

He showed her the letter, striding up and down the room as she read it.

She lifted her head to say to him: “She doesn’t mean it; you know Joanna, always making a mountain out of a molehill.”

Instead she heard herself saying: “How could she possibly write such things to you﻿—you’ve always been so kind.”

===Chinese===
「哎呀，彼得，」她快步迎上前去，「出大事了。」

他把信递给她看，她读信的当口，他在屋里大步踱来踱去。

她抬起头，本想对他说：「她不是当真的；你了解乔安娜的为人，她总是小题大做。」

出口的却是：「她怎么可能给你写这样的话——你一向待她那么好。」

===Original===
“Too kind,” he muttered. “I tell you what, Maggie, Joanna’s got no heart, she’s all head, all ideas and if you don’t see and act her way, she’s got no use for you.”

“I do think she thinks herself a lot better than anyone else,” Maggie said slowly, remembering Joanna’s letter to her about Philip.

“Well, she is, you know,” he put in unexpectedly. “Oh, Lord, what am I going to do without her!”

Genuinely touched, she sat down on the little box-couch beside him and slid her arm around his shoulder. “After all, you’ve still got me, Peter.”

===Chinese===
「就是太好了，」他咕哝道，「我跟你说吧，玛吉，乔安娜没有心，她全是脑子、全是主张，你要是不按她的眼光看事、照她的路子行事，她就用不着你了。」

「我倒真觉得，她自以为比谁都强得多。」玛吉慢吞吞地说，心里想起乔安娜写给她谈菲利普的那封信。

「这个嘛，她确实是比谁都强，你是知道的。」他出其不意地插了一句。「唉，天哪，没有她我可怎么好！」

她真心动了情，在他身旁那只小巧的箱柜式沙发上坐下，一条胳膊揽上他的肩膀。「说到底，你还有我呢，彼得。」

===Original===
He looked up at her, feeling the surge of a new idea in his heart. If he could only punish Joanna﻿—no not punish exactly, you couldn’t punish her, she was always too remote for that﻿—but shock her, let her see, as his boyhood’s phrase would have had it, that she was not the only pebble on the beach. Besides, what a revenge to cut loose altogether from the influence of her ideals and ally himself with one whom she would have characterized as having no ideals at all.

Before the thought was even shaped in his brain he was speaking:

“Of course I always have you, Maggie. How﻿—how would you like to spend your future with me?”

“What do you mean, Peter?”

===Chinese===
他抬眼望她，心头涌起一个新念头。要是能惩罚一下乔安娜就好了——不，说惩罚也不尽然，你没法惩罚她，她永远太超然，够不着——那就震她一震，让她瞧瞧，照他少年时代那句口头禅的说法，海滩上的石子可不止她一颗。再说，彻底斩断她那些理想对自己的影响，去同一个在她口中根本毫无理想可言的人结盟，这是何等痛快的一场报复。

这念头还没在脑子里成形，话已出了口：

「我当然一直都有你，玛吉。你——你可愿意同我共度往后的日子？」

「你这是什么意思，彼得？」

===Original===
“I mean, Joanna’s chucked me. You and I get along famously, you’ve got your divorce from Neal. Why not marry me?”

It was plain that though surprised she liked the idea. She saw herself suddenly transformed in this inhospitable snobbish city from Maggie Neal, alone and *déclassée*, into Mrs. Peter Bye, a model of respectability.

That he had no money, no accepted means of making a livelihood she understood would mean nothing. He was a Bye and she as his wife could go anywhere. She would show Alice Talbert! And afterwards when he got his degree!

But because she had once loved Philip she could judge what Peter might mean to Joanna. To her credit she hesitated.

===Chinese===
「我是说，乔安娜把我甩了。你我相处得这么融洽，你跟尼尔的婚也离了。干吗不嫁给我？」

明眼人一看便知，她虽觉意外，心里却喜欢这个主意。她仿佛看见自己在这座冷漠势利的城市里骤然变了身份：不再是孤零零、落魄失级（*déclassée*）的玛吉·尼尔，而成了彼得·拜太太——规矩体面的典范。

他没有钱，也没有什么拿得出手的正当营生，她明白这些都无关紧要。他是拜家的人，她做了他的妻子，什么样的场合都去得。她要让艾丽斯·塔尔伯特瞧瞧！还有，等他日后拿了学位！

但正因为她曾爱过菲利普，她掂量得出彼得对乔安娜意味着什么。难得的是，她还迟疑了一下。

===Original===
“Joanna probably doesn’t mean to let you go, Peter, she’s just angry and disappointed. She takes things harder than Sylvia or I. You know she really cares about you, and so do you about her.”

But he assured her that he did not. “She’s too exacting. Now there’s one thing about you, Maggie﻿—maybe it’s because you’ve already been married﻿—you know how to treat a man. Joanna makes you feel as though you were in a straitjacket all the time. I always feel ordinary when I’m with you.”

Neither of them noticed the doubtfulness of the compliment. In the end she accepted him. After all, she owed nothing to Joanna, who certainly had not considered her. How surprised she would be to think that Peter could so quickly find solace in her﻿—Maggie’s﻿—arms! And Joanna should learn, too, that he could become a success without everlastingly being pushed and prodded.

Hard on this thought came another. “Peter, you won’t have to work so hard now to get through school. I’ll help you. You know I’m doing very well with the hair-work.”

===Chinese===
「乔安娜多半不是真要放你走，彼得，她只是一时生气、一时失望。她比西尔维娅和我都更容易把事情往心里去。你知道她真在乎你，你也真在乎她。」

可他一口咬定并非如此。「她太苛求了。你呢，玛吉，有一样好处——兴许是因为你结过一回婚——你知道怎么对待男人。乔安娜让你觉得仿佛时时刻刻穿着拘束衣。跟你在一起，我总觉得自己就是个平平常常的人。」

两人谁也没留意这句恭维话里的可疑之处。到头来她还是接受了他。说到底，她不欠乔安娜什么，乔安娜也压根没为她着想过。一想到彼得竟能这么快就在她——她玛吉的——怀抱里寻得慰藉，乔安娜该会多么吃惊！乔安娜也该领教领教：不必没完没了地又被推又被撵，他照样能出人头地。

这个念头刚落，紧跟着又冒出一个。「彼得，如今你把书念完，就不必再那么拼命苦干了。我来帮你。你知道的，我做头发这行营生做得挺好。」

===Original===
He dismissed the theme airily, one hand on her shoulder, the other fumbling for a cigarette.

“Oh, I’m going to give medicine up. I’ll just keep on with Tom and the music. Heavens, it’s so nice to know you won’t mind, Maggie. Can’t think why I’ve stuck to the old school as long as I have, when here I am all set with this nice easy job to my hand. Might as well get along with as little trouble as possible. The world owes me a living.”

===Chinese===
他一手搭在她肩上，另一只手摸摸索索去掏香烟，轻飘飘地把话头岔开了。

「噢，我打算把医学撂下不学了。就跟汤姆一块儿干音乐这行得了。天哪，知道你不会介意，可真好，玛吉。真想不通我干吗在老学堂里硬熬这么久，手边现成摆着这么份称心省力的差事。往后还是怎么省事怎么来吧。这世界本就欠我一碗饭吃。」

---

===Original===
Afterwards, back in his room with the green iron bedstead and the Bye Bible, he felt a difference, a sense of let-downness. He threw himself across the bed and groaned, “Joanna, how could you?”

She could, that was evident. He was stupefied at the turn in his affairs. Five hours ago he had expected some day to be a physician and to marry Joanna Marshall. Now it seemed that he was going to be a musician and marry Maggie Neal.

“It isn’t true,” he told himself, fiercely. But it was true. There on the dresser were some cookies wrapped up in a red and white fringed napkin, Maggie’s gift when he left her.

===Chinese===
后来，回到自己那间摆着绿铁床架和拜家圣经的屋子，他觉出一种异样，一种泄了气似的失落。他一头横倒在床上，呻吟道：「乔安娜，你怎么能这样？」

她能，这是明摆着的。他让自己事情的这一转折惊得目瞪口呆。五个钟头以前，他还指望有朝一日当上医生、娶了乔安娜·马歇尔。如今看来，他是要当音乐家、娶玛吉·尼尔了。

「这不是真的，」他狠狠地对自己说。可这是真的。梳妆台上正摆着一包饼干，用一条红白相间的流苏餐巾裹着，是他离开她时玛吉送的礼物。

===Original===
“I made them for you, hoping you would come in. Now you’ll be in often, often, won’t you? Oh, Peter, I’ll be good to you. I’ll be as unlike Joanna as possible.” He did not want her to be unlike Joanna. In fact, he did not want her at all.

He might as well take her, though, for Joanna did not want him. That was it, no matter how many women he unaccountably married, Joanna might be shocked but she would never really care. Or suppose she did care a little while, she would soon forget it with her singing and dancing. Still, he supposed he must tell her. He would write her a gay, mocking letter. “I hope you’ll be as happy with your art as I feel I shall be with Maggie. She suits me perfectly.”

===Chinese===
「这是我特意给你做的，盼着你会进来坐坐。往后你可要常来、常来了，好不好？唉，彼得，我会好好待你的。我会尽量做到处处跟乔安娜不一样。」他不要她跟乔安娜不一样。说实在的，他压根就不要她。

不过，他不妨娶了她，反正乔安娜不要他。就是这么回事：不管他莫名其妙地娶了多少个女人，乔安娜也许会震惊，但绝不会真正放在心上。就算她真在意上一小阵子，唱唱歌、跳跳舞，也就很快抛在脑后了。不过，他想总得让她知道。他要给她写一封欢快而带讥诮的信：「但愿你跟你的艺术过得快活，就像我料想自己跟玛吉会过得那样快活。她跟我再般配不过了。」

===Original===
After he had littered his desk and the floor beside it vainly with a veritable snowstorm of torn bits of paper, he let his head drop on his lean brown hands and went to sleep. Perhaps it would not be exact to say he cried himself to sleep, but there were certainly tears that burnt and scalded behind his eyelids.

His landlady complained of the torn paper the next morning. “ ’Tisn’t as though you didn’t have a nice wastepaper basket ready and waitin’, Mr. Bye.” As she finished speaking she handed him Joanna’s letter containing Goethe’s poem. The tenderness, the real love that blazed in the beautiful lines overwhelmed him. He could not tell her the truth after a letter like that. So he wrote her, postponing but hinting, he fondly believed, at the news which he must soon break to her. A month later, finding himself still unequal to the task, he wrote to Sylvia.

===Chinese===
他把书桌和桌边的地板徒然撒满了碎纸片，简直下了一场纸屑的暴风雪；随后他把头垂在瘦削的棕色双手上，睡着了。若说他哭着哭着睡着了，也许并不确切，但眼皮底下确实有泪水在灼灼滚烫地烧着。

第二天早上，女房东抱怨那些碎纸。「又不是没给您备着一只好好的废纸篓，现现成成地等着您用呢，拜先生。」她话音刚落，便递给他乔安娜的来信，信里抄着歌德（Goethe）的那首诗。美丽的诗行里灼灼燃烧着的柔情，那份真爱，将他彻底压倒了。接到这样一封信之后，他没法把实情告诉她了。于是他给她回信，一面把这事往后拖，一面又——他一厢情愿地以为——暗示了他不久便不得不向她挑明的那个消息。一个月后，发觉自己仍旧开不了这个口，他便写信给西尔维娅。

## XV / 十五

===Original===
Sylvia was arranging the smallest birthday cake in the world. It bore one very small candle and it was for the very small baby who, propped up in a high chair, sat and watched the birthday proceedings with round solemn eyes. A three-year-old youngster, whose nose just rose above the edge of the table, watched, too, with eyes no less round and far more interested.

“Look at the darlings!” said Sylvia. “They know just what their mother’s doing. Aren’t my children intelligent, Brian?”

“What you mistake for intelligence is hunger, much more likely,” laughed her husband. “I’ve seen Roger look that way before when there wasn’t any birthday cake, but when there certainly were eats.”

“You watch them,” said Sylvia, “and I’ll see if mother and father are ready to come. I had a telegram from Joanna this afternoon, so I know she can’t make it.” Her voice floated up to him as she ran down the back stairs.

The five years of Sylvia’s married life had brought their changes to the Marshall household. Mrs. Marshall had insisted on Sylvia’s and Brian’s remaining with them.

“Else we’d be lonely,” she complained, “what with Sandy gone for good, and Philip and Joanna everlastingly ‘on the road,’ as they express it.”

===Chinese===
西尔维娅正在摆弄世界上最小的生日蛋糕。蛋糕上插着一支很小的蜡烛，是给一个很小的娃娃的——那娃娃被安在一张高脚椅里，坐着一双圆滚滚的庄重眼睛，瞧着过生日的种种排场。一个三岁的小家伙，鼻尖刚刚够到桌沿，也在瞧，眼睛一样圆，兴味却浓得多。

「瞧这俩小宝贝！」西尔维娅说，「妈妈在干什么，他们全知道。我的孩子多聪明，是不是，布莱恩？」

「你当是聪明的地方，多半是肚子饿了。」她的丈夫笑道，「我见过罗杰（Roger）在没有生日蛋糕、却千真万确有吃食的时候，也是这副神情。」

「你看着他们吧，」西尔维娅说，「我去看看爸爸妈妈预备好了没有。今天下午我接到乔安娜的电报，知道她来不了了。」她跑下后楼梯，声音飘上来传到他耳边。

西尔维娅婚后这五年，给马歇尔家带来了种种变化。马歇尔太太执意要西尔维娅和布莱恩留在家里跟他们一起住。

「不然我们就太冷清了，」她抱怨道，「桑迪一去不回头，菲利普和乔安娜又一年到头‘在路上跑码头’，用他们自己的话说。」

===Original===
Alexander and Helena Arnold, after seeing each other constantly and unresponsively for ten years, suddenly fell completely in love on that night of the Pierce Day Nursery dance. Sandy proved himself an impulsive wooer, for he won Helena’s consent and would have married her before Sylvia’s and Brian’s wedding came off.

“Gracious, don’t spoil my thunder,” Sylvia had begged him aghast.

“Well, I’m the oldest,” Sandy had retorted. “It’s really my place to marry first.”

Helena, unaware of all this, announced that she wanted to be bridesmaid at Sylvia’s wedding, so Alec must wait till after. “Think of all the extra clothes I can get. Besides, I couldn’t possibly finish my trousseau before.”

The two had married the June following Sylvia’s wedding and had moved into a house of their own. The household had hardly become adjusted to Alexander’s absence, when Philip started on his long tours which kept him away from home a good part of the year.

He had been graduated from Harvard, with honors and with his coveted Phi Beta Kappa key. He had come home, happy though not as radiant, Joanna thought for one, as in the old days. Then he had evolved his new scheme. He proposed that an organization be started among the colored people which should reach all over the country.

===Chinese===
亚历山大和海伦娜·阿诺德，彼此见了十年面、十年心如止水，却在皮尔斯日间托儿所（Pierce Day Nursery）舞会的那一晚，忽然整个儿坠入了爱河。桑迪证明了自己是个说干就干的求婚者，他赢得了海伦娜的应允，本来还要抢在西尔维娅和布莱恩的婚礼之前把婚结了。

「天哪，别抢我的风头。」西尔维娅曾惊慌失措地求他。

「嗐，我年纪最大，」桑迪反驳道，「论理也该我先结婚。」

海伦娜对这一切毫不知情，只宣布她要在西尔维娅的婚礼上当女傧相，所以亚历克必须等婚礼过后再办。「想想我能多添多少衣裳。再说，那之前我的嫁妆也无论如何备不齐。」

两人在西尔维娅婚礼后的那年六月成了婚，搬进了一栋自己的房子。家里人刚习惯亚历山大的离开，菲利普又开始了他那漫长的巡回，一年里大半时间都不在家。

他从哈佛毕业了，带着荣誉，也带着他梦寐以求的优等生荣誉学会的钥匙。他回了家，很快活，可在乔安娜看来——至少她是这么想的——已不像旧日那样神采焕发。接着他酝酿出了他的新计划。他提议在有有色人种中间发起一个组织，让它的触角伸遍全国。

===Original===
“White and colored people alike may belong to it,” said Philip, his eyes kindling to his vision, “but it is to favor primarily the interests of colored people. No, I’m wrong there,” he corrected himself. “It is to favor primarily the interests of the country.”

He was speaking to a group of both white and black enthusiasts. “How shall we start it?” someone asked.

They all liked the plan. He had his project well mapped out, for he had thought of little else for the past three years. There were to be a national board and a national office, supported by local boards and membership. There would be need of organized publicity; he might suggest a magazine or a weekly newspaper. A huge campaign must be got underway, an effort at nationwide support.

“Its objects will be,” he enumerated them on his long brown fingers, “the suppression of lynching and peonage, the restoration of the ballot, equal schools and a share in civic rights.”

“A large order,” said Barney Kirchner, Philip’s classmate, “but I like it. I’ll get my uncle behind it.” Barney was wealthy in his own right, but his uncle, an Austrian Jew, had built up an immense fortune which had since supported many a notable cause.

The little nucleus worked well. From that meeting grew up all that Philip predicted, rather weak and tottering at first, but the five years had seen the awakening of a great racial consciousness. There were still tremendous possibilities almost untouched.

The organization had a magazine, *The Spur*, of which Philip was editor. But he was constantly called to exercise his vision and judgment in the field. His observation, his constant scrutiny of his own people helped him here, but he was the born organizer in any event.

===Chinese===
「白人和有色人种都可以加入，」菲利普说，眼睛为他的憧憬灼灼发亮，「但它首先要为有色人种的利益着想。不，这话说错了，」他纠正自己，「它首先要为这个国家的利益着想。」

他是对着一群白人和黑人的热心之士说这番话的。「我们怎样着手呢？」有人问。

大家都喜欢这个计划。他把全盘方案筹划得妥妥帖帖，因为过去三年里他满脑子想的就是这个。要设一个全国理事会和一个全国总部，由各地分会和会员来支撑。需要有组织地展开宣传；他可以办一份杂志，或者一份周报。还得发起一场大张旗鼓的运动，争取举国的支持。

「它的宗旨将是，」他扳着修长的棕色手指一条条数，「取缔私刑和劳役偿债制，恢复选举权，平等的学校教育，以及分享公民权利。」

「胃口不小啊，」菲利普的同班同学巴尼·基希纳（Barney Kirchner）说，「不过我喜欢。我去把我伯父拉来撑腰。」巴尼自己就颇有家产，而他那位伯父——一个奥地利犹太人——更是攒下了一份巨大的家业，此后资助过许多了不起的事业。

这个小小的核心干得很出色。从那次会议里生长出菲利普所预见的一切，起初虽然弱小踉跄，但这五年间，一场伟大的种族觉醒已然到来。还有巨大的可能性几乎未曾触动。

这个组织办了一份杂志《马刺》（The Spur），菲利普任主编。但他不断被召到各地，去施展他的眼力和判断。他对本族人民的观察、他那不停的审视在这里帮了他，不过无论如何他都是天生的组织者。

===Original===
Joanna already started on her concert tours, often met him on the “road.” Sometimes they were booked at the same place for the same night. Each was the other’s supporting attraction.

“Oh, is this Mr. Marshall?” Joanna would gush when he met her train. She put an imaginary lorgnette to her eye. “Any relation to the eminent Miss Joanna Marshall, the world-famous mezzo?”

“Never heard of her. Haven’t the least idea who she is. Come along, Silly. Now, Joanna, do be on time and don’t stop to primp. Mind, I won’t wait for you a minute.”

“Not the littlest, teeniest one?” It was hard to say which was prouder of the other.

Joanna was in fine feather in those days. She had youth and a certain grave beauty which did not strike the observer at first as did Sylvia’s or even poor Maggie’s. But it grew on one and remained. Young men, though they liked to be seen with a star, were a little afraid of her queenliness, her faint condescension. She took herself so seriously! Her own folks and Peter often teased her about this, but they adored it in her. And she, in turn, adored her little fame, the footlights, the adulation. Even the smallest church in the quietest backwoods, with a group of patient dark faces peering at her out of the often smoky background, had its appeal. At such times, strange to say, she was at her best, gave of her finest. She would come on the stage, trailing clouds of glory, and lean toward them﻿—a rosy brown vision. In some misty colorful robe of Sylvia’s designing, her thick crinkling hair piled high on her head as the Spanish woman had taught her, she seemed to say:

“I am no better than you. You are no worse than I. Whatever I am, you, in your children, may be. Whatever you are, I in my father have been.”

She was absolutely sincere in her estimation of her art, or of any art. It was only in its relation to the other things of life that she lost her vision and sense of proportion.

===Chinese===
乔安娜那时已开始她的巡回音乐会，常在「路上」与菲利普相遇。有时两人被约在同一地方、同一晚上演出，互为对方的助演。

「哟，这位是马歇尔先生吗？」乔安娜去车站接他时会这样拿腔拿调，举起一支想象中的长柄眼镜举到眼前。「与那位大名鼎鼎的乔安娜·马歇尔小姐、举世闻名的女中音，可有亲缘？」

「从没听说过。半点也不知道她是何许人。走吧，小傻瓜。听着，乔安娜，千万要准时，别停下来梳妆。当心，我可是连一分钟也不等你的。」

「连最小最小的一分钟也不等吗？」很难说两人中哪一个更以对方为荣。

那段日子乔安娜正春风得意。她有青春，还有端庄的美，初见之下不像西尔维娅、甚至不像可怜的玛吉那样夺人眼目。可这美会渐渐沁进人心里，并且常驻不走。年轻人虽然乐于被人看见同一位明星在一起，却有点怕她那股女王气派、那点淡淡的居高临下。她把自己看得太重了！自家人和彼得常拿这个取笑她，可他们又偏偏爱她这一点。而她呢，反过来也爱她那点小小的名声，爱脚灯，爱众人的捧场。哪怕是最偏僻乡野里最小的一座教堂，一群耐心的黝黑面孔从多半烟熏火燎的背景里凝望着她，也有它的动人之处。说来奇怪，每逢这种时候，她总是发挥得最好，献出她最美的一切。她会走上舞台，曳着一身荣耀的光云，向他们俯下身去——一个玫瑰棕色的幻影。身上是西尔维娅设计的雾蒙蒙、色彩明媚的长袍，浓密卷曲的头发按那位西班牙妇人教的高高堆在头顶，她仿佛在说：

「我并不比你们好。你们也并不比我差。今天的我，你们的儿女将来都可以做到。今天的你们，我在我父亲身上经历过。」

她对自己这门艺术、对任何艺术的估量，都是绝对真诚的。只是关系到人生的其他方面时，她才会失去眼光和分寸感。

===Original===
She liked most to go to Philadelphia, where she was in great favor. There she had had three great triumphs, once in Association Hall, twice at the Academy of Music. Both she and Peter had thrilled when she came from the Academy the second time. She sent her flowers and her stage-gown home in the car of a friend, while she and Peter were whirled in a taxi out to Fairmount Park.

They had driven to the Green Street entrance, and then dismissing the cab had walked around the drive, up the steps, in front of the mansion and on to Lemon Hill. It was one of those last, warm, almost hot nights of Indian Summer. The slopes of the park lay deserted before them, deep in velvety shadow, with here and there a gold patch bright as day under the watching arc-light.

They sat down on the dry, short grass. “Like that other evening in Morningside, long, long ago. How long, Joanna?”

“Oh, ages! How’d I sing, Peter?”

“Divinely. You looked like an angel, Janna. No, not an angel, more like a siren in that yellow dress. Where’d you get it, dearest?”

“Yellow nothing! That was orange﻿—deep, deep orange. Sylvia planned it out for me. Isn’t she a genius? Through me she certainly is teaching these colored people how to dress. We will not wear these conventional colors﻿—grays, taupe, beige﻿—poor boy, you don’t know what they are, do you? They’re all right for these palefaces. But colored people need color, life, vividness.”

===Chinese===
她最喜欢去费城，那里极捧她的场。她在那里赢得过三场大胜：一次在协会会堂（Association Hall），两次在费城音乐厅（Academy of Music）。第二次从音乐厅下来时，她和彼得两人都激动不已。她把鲜花和演出礼服托朋友的汽车送回家，自己和彼得坐上一辆出租车，一路飞驰到费尔芒特公园。

车到格林街（Green Street）入口，他们打发走了车，沿着车道散步，拾级而上，走过大厦门前，一路走到柠檬山（Lemon Hill）。那是印第安夏（Indian Summer）最后的几个夜晚，暖得几乎发热。公园的坡地空无人迹地铺展在他们面前，深深浸在丝绒般的暗影里，偶有一片金黄，在弧光灯的注视下亮得如同白昼。

他们在干爽的短草上坐下。「就像很久很久以前，在晨边公园的那个晚上。有多久了，乔安娜？」

「噢，一百年啦！我唱得怎么样，彼得？」

「如闻仙乐。你像个天使，小乔。不，不是天使，穿了那身黄衣裳，更像一个海妖。哪儿弄来的，亲爱的？」

「什么黄衣裳！那是橙色的——深深的、深深的橙色。西尔维娅替我设计的。她是不是天才？借着我，她真正教会了这些有色人种怎么穿衣裳。我们再不要穿那些老气横秋的颜色了——灰色、灰褐色、米色——可怜的 孩子，你还不知道这些是什么颜色吧？那些颜色留给这些白面孔穿正合适。有色人种需要的是色彩、生命、鲜明。」

===Original===
“George! I guess you’re right. How’d you come to think of it?”

“I didn’t. It was Sylvia. I started out in a white dress. You should have seen me looking like an icebergish angel.”

“You are one, you know Janna.”

“Which? Iceberg or angel?”

“Both. One makes me adore you, the other says ‘hands off.’ ”

“Not a bad thing, do you think, considering all the men I meet?”

“I hate them. Sure you don’t like any of ’em better than me?”

“No, dear, I like you best.”

“ ‘No, dear, I like you best,’ ” he mimicked. “For God’s sake, Jan, can’t you say, ‘Peter, I love you always’? Say it.”

She hesitated, sighed a little. “Peter I love you.”

“Why’d you leave off ‘always’?”

“Dear little boy, how can I say it? I do when I think of it. But, Peter, I have so much to think about﻿—my tour, my booking, you know, my lessons in French and Italian, my dancing. I still keep that up; I’d really rather do that than sing. Dancing makes me﻿—”

“Oh, damn the dancing!”

“Why, Peter!” She looked at his flushed face in amazement.

===Chinese===
「嚯！我想你说得对。你怎么想起来的？」

「不是我，是西尔维娅。我起初是穿白纱裙出场的。你真该瞧瞧我那副冰山天使的德性。」

「你现在也还是啊，你知道吗，小乔。」

「哪一个？冰山，还是天使？」

「都是。一个让我爱你爱得要命，另一个却在说『莫挨』。」

「这不算坏事吧，你想想我见过的这么多男人？」

「我恨他们。你敢肯定不比喜欢我更喜欢他们当中的哪一个？」

「不喜欢，亲爱的，我最喜欢你。」

「『不喜欢，亲爱的，我最喜欢你。』」他学她的腔调，「看在老天爷份上，小乔，你就不能说『彼得，我永远爱你』吗？说出来。」

她迟疑了一下，轻轻叹了口气。「彼得，我爱你。」

「你怎么把『永远』省掉了？」

「亲爱的小男孩，教我怎么说法呢？想起来的时候，我是爱的。可是彼得，我要想的事太多了——我的巡演，我的档期，你知道的，我的法语课、意大利语课，还有我的舞蹈。我还没丢下它呢；说实在的，我宁可跳舞也不愿唱歌。跳舞让我——」

「噢，让舞蹈见鬼去吧！」

「哎呀，彼得！」她愕然望着他涨红的脸。

===Original===
“Hang it all, talking to me about dancing, when I’m talking to you about love﻿—*love*, Joanna﻿—and there’s nothing to keep us from getting married. Some fellows and girls ball their lives up so they can’t ever get them straightened out. But here we are ‘all set’ as the fellows say. And you talk to me about dancing! Suppose I were to talk to you about *Materia Medica*!”

“I think it would be a good thing if you would.”

He was honestly aggrieved at that.

She leaned over and kissed him. “See how brazen I am. That’s the second time I’ve given you a kiss. Oh, Peter, you big baby!”

“Dear Janna, I love you so! Great Scott! aren’t girls funny! You can’t guess how hard it is for me to be letting all these stupid years go by. Sometimes I’ve half a mind to chuck it all.”

“You’d never get me then.”

“I don’t suppose I would. Well, I have you now.”

“Dear Peter, we must be going home. Cousin Parthenia will rave.”

“Pshaw, she knows you’re with me. Love me, darling?”

“You know I do, you dear, dear boy.”

===Chinese===
「岂有此理，我跟你谈的是爱情，你却跟我谈跳舞——爱情啊，乔安娜——再说，已经没有什么能拦着咱们结婚了。有些小伙子跟姑娘把自己的日子搅成一团乱麻，一辈子也理不出个头绪。可咱们俩呢，用小伙子们的话说，是『万事俱备』。你倒跟我谈跳舞！换了我跟你谈《药物学》（Materia Medica），你怎么想！」

「你要真跟我谈，我倒觉得是件好事。」

这话让他当真委屈起来。

她俯身吻了吻他。「瞧我多不知羞。这是我主动给你的第二个吻了。噢，彼得，你这个大小孩！」

「亲爱的小乔，我多么爱你！天哪！姑娘家真是怪透了！你猜不出让这些蠢年头一年年白白溜走，对我有多难受。有时候我真想干脆把这一切统统扔下。」

「那你可就永远得不到我了。」

「我想也是。好在现在你在我手里。」

「亲爱的彼得，咱们该回家了。帕瑟尼娅（Parthenia）表姐要念叨了。」

「嗐，她知道你跟我在一起呢。爱我吗，亲爱的？」

「你知道我爱，你这个亲亲爱爱的宝贝。」

===Original===
“Come, sit up on the bench. There, that’s it.” He knelt before her. “Know what I’m going to give you tonight?” He felt in his pocket. “Like it, Janna?”

He showed her a ring, a tiny gold chased ring, whose facets gleamed like diamonds.

“Peter, it’s too beautiful. Oh, I love you for it.”

He slipped it on her finger, got up and sat beside her, kissing her little cold hands. She leaned against his shoulder﻿—he put his arm about her. A poignant sweetness seemed to flood in on them out of the solemn, mellow night.

Peter was the first to stir. “I must get you home, darling. Oh, Joanna, aren’t you too happy? I wonder if we wouldn’t be better off if we were resting like this, our arms close about each other, in our grave.”

The inevitable separation came the next day. Joanna was cold, almost indifferent. It was the way she had taught herself to endure pain. She hated always to leave Peter, particularly if she were returning to New York. The excitement of visiting other places healed her loneliness. Sometimes she wished she weren’t going to see Peter for these brief visits which lacerated her so.

===Chinese===
「来，坐直了靠在长椅上。对，就这样。」他在她面前跪下。「知道今晚我要送你什么吗？」他伸手到口袋里摸。「喜欢吗，小乔？」

他拿给她看一枚戒指，一枚小小的金质雕花戒指，棱面上闪着钻石似的光。

「彼得，它太美了。噢，为了它我也爱你。」

他把戒指套上她的手指，起身挨着她坐下，吻着她一双冰凉的小手。她偎在他肩上——他伸臂搂住她。一种锥心的甜美，仿佛从那庄严、温存的夜色里漫过来，将他们淹没。

彼得先动了动。「我得送你回家了，亲爱的。噢，乔安娜，你是不是幸福得不行？我在想，要是咱们就这样相拥相偎、长眠在坟墓里，是不是反倒更好。」

第二天，不可避免的离别到来了。乔安娜冷淡得近乎漠然。这是她教给自己熬受痛苦的法子。她一向怕离开彼得，尤其是要回纽约的时候。走访别处的新鲜兴奋会治愈她的孤寂。有时她真希望不必去赴这些短暂的会面——它们把她割得太疼。

===Original===
Unfortunately her lover did not understand this. “How can she melt like she did last night and then leave me so cool and composed this morning?” he wondered, staring dejectedly after the departing express. He had not ridden to West Philadelphia with her because he had to be at a hospital at Sixteenth Street at one o’clock and it was now noon.

“She used to cry when we separated.” He stood uncertainly a moment on the corner of Fifteenth and Market. “Guess I’ll go over to that little Automat on Juniper Street and snatch a mouthful. I won’t feel like eating after I see Carpenter start in on that slashing. Golly, what a steady hand he has.”

He walked through the City Hall Arcade to Juniper Street, crossed in front of Wanamaker’s and forced a passage through the teeming little byway.

The Automat was crowded. “Have to eat standing,” he thought, drawing a glass of water and seizing a knife and fork. “No, there’s an empty table.” He collected his food and began to eat.

Someone put a plate on the table beside him, rested a hand there a moment. Peter glanced at it.

“Colored. What a nice hand! Ought to have a peach of a face to match that.”

===Chinese===
可惜她的恋人不懂这个。「她怎么能昨夜那样柔情似水，今早离开我时却又这般冷静自持？」他怅然望着远去的快车，心里纳闷。他没有陪她去西费城，因为一点钟得到十六街的一家医院，而此刻已是正午。

「从前分别的时候她总要哭的。」他在第十五街与市场街口的街角上迟疑地站了片刻。「想起来了，我去朱尼珀街（Juniper Street）那家小自动餐馆（Automat）随便扒两口吧。看完卡彭特动那台大手术，我就吃不下东西了。乖乖，他那双手可真稳。」

他穿过市政厅拱廊走到朱尼珀街，从沃纳梅克百货公司（Wanamaker's）门前穿过，又从熙熙攘攘的小路上挤了过去。

自动餐馆里人满为患。「只好站着吃了。」他一边想，一边取了杯水，抓起一副刀叉。「不对，那儿有张空桌。」他端着食物坐下来吃了。

有人把一只盘子放在他旁边的桌上，一只手在桌上支了片刻。彼得瞥了那手一眼。

有色人种的手。多好看的一只手！该配上一张桃子似的俏脸才对。

===Original===
He looked up. “Maggie Ellersley! I had heard you lived here. I thought I saw you once, why﻿—four years ago﻿—one New Year’s night on Twentieth Street. You’ve been here ever since?”

“Yes, Peter. Oh, it’s so nice to see you!”

“Isn’t it, though! I mean isn’t it great to see somebody from home? I’ve just seen Joanna off.”

Her face stiffened at that. But he was busy looking at his watch.

“Ten minutes more! Look here, Maggie, what’d you drop us all that way for? How’s your husband?”

She answered his second question. “I haven’t any.”

He glanced at her apologetically, ashamed of his levity. “Is he dead?”

“No,” said Maggie woodenly. “I’ve left him!”

“Oh!” he was embarrassed. “I’m sorry, Maggie. Got to run now. When may I see you again?”

His engaging manner brought back the old days. “Peter, you aren’t ashamed of me?”

“My dear girl!” He was younger than she and for that reason he adopted a paternal air, patting her on the shoulder. “How can you ask that?”

“Would you come to see me tonight, Peter? Come to dinner?”

“Try me. What’s the address?”

She gave it to him. “That’s Fifteenth and Fitzwater.”

“Yes, I know. I’ll see you at six sharp. Until then, Maggie.” He bared his curly head and flashed out the side door.

===Chinese===
他抬起头来。「玛吉·埃勒斯利！听说你住在这儿。我想我见过你一次——什么时候来着——四年前吧，一个新年的夜晚，在二十街。你打那以后一直在费城？」

「是的，彼得。噢，见到你真好！」

「可不是嘛！我是说，见到一个家乡来的人多好！我刚送走乔安娜。」

一听这话，她的脸僵住了。可他正忙着看表。

「只剩十分钟了！我说，玛吉，你当年怎么那样把我们全都甩了？你丈夫好吗？」

她回答了他后面的一个问题。「我没有丈夫。」

他歉然看了她一眼，为自己的轻浮惭愧。「他去世了？」

「没有，」玛吉呆板地说，「我离开他了！」

「噢！」他窘住了。「对不起，玛吉。我得赶紧走了。什么时候能再见到你？」

他那讨人喜欢的做派带回了旧日时光。「彼得，你不为我害臊吗？」

「我亲爱的姑娘！」他比她年轻，因此摆出一副老大哥的架势，拍拍她的肩。「你怎么能这样问？」

「今晚你来看看我好吗，彼得？来吃晚饭？」

「你还用问。地址在哪儿？」

她把地址告诉了他。「在十五街和菲茨沃特街口。」

「好，我知道那地方。六点整见。回见，玛吉。」他摘了一下他那长着鬈发的头，一闪身从边门出去了。

===Original===
He tapped at her door at six.

“I didn’t hear you ring,” said Maggie. “Come in. This *is* nice, Peter.”

“I should say so. Jolly little place you’ve got here.” He settled back on the couch, stretched out his long legs. “All these years I’ve been tramping about Philadelphia, a poor homeless beggar, when I might have been coming to see you. How long have you been alone, Maggie?”

“Four and a half years.”

“Four and a half years! Why that’s﻿—look here, how long have you been married?”

“Five years last June. I left him almost right away, or rather he left me.”

“Deserted you, you mean?”

“No, no, not that. He wanted to stay. I﻿—I couldn’t let him.” She told him all about it. “Peter, think of it, I’d married a gambler, a common gambler. And I’d wanted so to be decent!” She wept painfully.

He put his arm about her slender shoulders. “There, there now, Maggie.”

“It’s the first time I’ve shed a tear about it. Seeing you, someone out of the old happy days, upset me. Sit here, Peter.”

===Chinese===
六点整，他敲响了她的门。

「我没听见你按铃啊。」玛吉说，「进来吧。这可真太好了，彼得。」

「可不是嘛。你这小地方真够惬意的。」他往沙发上一靠，舒展开两条长腿。「这些年来我一直在费城四处流浪，活像个无家可归的叫花子，其实满可以来看看你的。你独自一个人多久了，玛吉？」

「四年半。」

「四年半！那不就是——我问你，你结婚多久了？」

「到今年六月满五年。我差不多一结婚就离开了他，或者不如说，是他离开了我。」

「你是说，他抛弃了你？」

「不，不，不是那样。他想留下。是我——我不能让他留。」她把事情原原本本告诉了他。「彼得，你想想，我嫁了个赌徒，一个不折不扣的赌徒。而我当初多么想做个体面人啊！」她痛苦地哭泣起来。

他搂住她瘦削的肩头。「好了，好了，玛吉。」

「这是我头一回为这事掉眼泪。见了你——见了旧日快活时光里来的人——我心里撑不住了。坐这儿，彼得。」

===Original===
“They were wonderful days, weren’t they? Remember what a bunch we were? And now we’re scattered everywhere. Joanna and Philip romping all over the country; Sylvia and Brian married; Sandy too, did you know it?”

“Yes, I read of it in the *Amsterdam News*.”

“You and I here. Harry Portor﻿—do you remember him?”

“Ye‑es, big square fellow, wore glasses. He used to go skating with us, didn’t he?”

“Yes, that’s the fellow. He studied medicine, too, at Harvard. Went to Washington as intern in the Freedmen’s Hospital. I haven’t seen him for ages. What’d you leave us for so suddenly, Maggie?”

She couldn’t tell *him*, of all people, about Joanna.

“Oh, I don’t know, girls are crazy, I think. Well, I’m not complaining. I’m better off than I’ve ever been. That Madame Harkness﻿—you know whom I mean?”

“The hair-woman﻿—what about her?”

“She’s made me supervisor of three of her branch stores, here in Philadelphia, Baltimore, and Washington, DC. I have my little home here, my salary’s good. I make more than enough to live on. My mother doesn’t have to do anything if she doesn’t want to. And above all, I’m practically free.”

“How do you mean free?”

“I’m suing for a divorce. Lawyer Talbert has my case.”

===Chinese===
「那些日子多美啊，是不是？记得咱们当年是多大的一伙吗？如今散得到处都是。乔安娜和菲利普满天下跑；西尔维娅和布莱恩结了婚；桑迪也结了，你知道吧？」

「知道，我在《阿姆斯特丹新闻》（Amsterdam News）上看到的。」

「就剩你我在这儿。哈里·波托尔——你还记得他吗？」

「记——记得，方肩膀的大个子，戴眼镜。从前常跟咱们一块儿去滑冰，是吧？」

「对，就是他。他后来也学的医科，在哈佛。去了华盛顿，在自由人医院（Freedmen's Hospital）当实习医生。我好些年没见过他了。你当年为什么那么突然地离开我们，玛吉？」

偏偏对他，她不能说乔安娜的事。

「噢，我也说不上，姑娘家发起疯来，大约就是那样吧。嗐，我没什么可抱怨的。我过得比哪会儿都好。那位哈克尼斯夫人——你知道我说的是谁吧？」

「做头发生意的那个女人——她怎么了？」

「她让我当了三家分店的监理，就在这儿费城，还有巴尔的摩和华盛顿。我有自己这个小家，薪水也不坏，挣的钱过日子绰绰有余。我母亲要是不想干活，就什么也不用干。最要紧的是，我差不多算自由了。」

「怎么个自由法？」

「我正起诉离婚。塔尔伯特（Talbert）律师接了我的案子。」

===Original===
“Oh, Mrs. Marshall’s cousin. Have you ever seen your﻿—Mr. Neal since he left?”

“About once a year. I hadn’t seen him for a long time though, until he came here six weeks ago, just before I started divorce proceedings.” Her face changed at the thought of it.

“He didn’t threaten you, Maggie?”

“Yes and no. In his way he cares about me, though not as much as for his gambling. He’s﻿—he’s got it in his head that I care about somebody else, and every now and then he writes me a threatening letter. That’s why he came to see me this last time.”

“You oughtn’t to let him in.”

“Oh, I have to. This Mrs. Davis, from whom I rent these rooms, doesn’t know there’s any trouble, she thinks he’s a steward on a boat, and I never have told her differently. She thinks I’m with him when I go away on these trips. Last time he was here, he stayed half the night right on that couch. He had a wretched cold, and it was raining!”

“I should think you’d have been afraid.”

“That’s why I let him stay. He’d been harboring such jealous thoughts toward me. He﻿—he has an idea that I like another man. And he has a terrific temper. You can’t imagine how it smolders and sulks. He wasn’t so bad about my sending him away, but since he’s had this suspicion I’ve really been afraid. I expect he’ll be really violent some day.”

===Chinese===
「哦，马歇尔太太的那位表亲。他走以后，你见过你那位——尼尔先生吗？」

「一年见一回吧。不过我有好长时间没见他了，直到六个星期前他来这儿，就在我着手办离婚之前。」想起这事，她的脸色变了。

「他没威胁你吧，玛吉？」

「也算有，也算没有。按他的方式，他是在乎我的，虽然远不如在乎他的赌。他——他认定我心里还有别的男人，隔三差五就给我写封恐吓信。上一回他就是为这个来看我的。」

「你不该放他进来。」

「唉，不得不放。我租这几间屋子的房东戴维斯太太（Mrs. Davis）不知道出了乱子，她当他是船上的一名乘务员，我也从来没有说破。我出这些远门的时候，她还当是陪他去的。上回他来，就在那张沙发上坐了半夜。他得了很重的感冒，外头又下着雨！」

「要我说，你该害怕的。」

「所以才留他住下呀。他心里一直转着那些嫉妒的念头。他——他认定我喜欢上了别的男人。他脾气又暴烈。你想不到那火气是怎么阴着、闷着的。我打发他走，他倒还不怎么闹；可自打起了这个疑心，我是真怕了。我看总有一天他要动真格的暴力。」

===Original===
“Well, Great Scott, won’t my coming to see you be dangerous? I was just thinking what good times we’d have.”

“We will. No, you’re all right. He wouldn’t be interested in you after he once knew who you were. And there’s Thomas Mason upstairs; he’s not bothered about him either, though Tom and his sister are in here all the time.”

Peter pushed his chair back. “That was a mighty good dinner, Maggie. Mind if I smoke?” He lit a cigarette. “Well, you’ve had hard luck, haven’t you? But never mind, it’s bound to break even, sooner or later. That’s what I keep saying to myself.”

“You in trouble too, Peter? I’ve been running on so about my affairs. Tell me about yours. Studying the way you have to must be an awful strain.”

He noticed gratefully how quick and ready was her sympathy. That was just it. Studying itself wasn’t so bad, working wasn’t bad. But the combination, the struggle to make ends meet, his few social obligations, and color!

“Why, it’s awful. I’m on the rack all the time.”

“If you could stop for a year or so and take a little rest, do something entirely different.”

He glanced at her, amused but touched. “Joanna ought to hear you say that. She’d faint away. She can’t understand anybody’s wanting to let up.”

===Chinese===
「那么，天哪，我来看你岂不危险了？我还正想着咱们该多玩几个痛快呢。」

「该玩还是玩。不，你没事。他一旦知道了你是谁，就不会对你起意。楼上还住着个托马斯·梅森（Thomas Mason）呢；他对人家也不找麻烦，虽然汤姆和他妹妹整天泡在我这儿。」

彼得把椅子往后一推。「这顿饭太香了，玛吉。我抽支烟你不介意吧？」他点上一支烟。「唉，你运气是不好，是不是？不过别往心里去，早晚总会扯平的。我就老是这样劝自己。」

「你也遇到难处了吗，彼得？我只顾絮絮叨叨讲自己的事。跟我说说你的吧。像你这样念书，一定苦透了。」

他感激地注意到，她的同情来得多么迅捷、多么恳切。正是这话。念书本身不算苦，干活也不算苦。可这几样加在一起，加上拆东墙补西墙的挣扎，加上那点人情来往，再加上肤色，就苦透了！

「唉，苦透了。我一天到晚像受着刑。」

「要是你能歇上一两年，好好休整休整，干点完全不一样的事，那多好。」

他瞥了她一眼，又好笑又感动。「这话该让乔安娜听听。她准得晕过去。她怎么也想不通，居然有人愿意松劲。」

===Original===
Maggie said with a faint bitterness that you must always be top notch for Joanna.

“I should say so. Here, I’ll help you with the dishes. Well﻿—if you really don’t want me.” She washed and wiped so fast that the room seemed cleared by magic. It had turned cooler and Maggie lit her little gas-stove.

Peter smoked and relapsed into a moody silence, which he broke now and then with an account of his struggles. His Uncle Peter had died during his third year and the house had been inherited by his daughter, Mrs. Boyd. Of course he couldn’t expect anything of her. Her father was only his great-uncle, and she had her own children to look after. He had moved to Mrs. Larrabee’s in West Philadelphia, with some of his fraternity brothers. Somehow his money sped. His books were expensive, the cost of his instruments pure robbery.

“I do what playing I can, but I confess I’m up against it,” he ended ruefully.

“Lots of the boys do waiting, don’t they?” asked Maggie. “Why don’t you do that?”

He just couldn’t, he told her.

“I never could endure standing around ‘grand white folks.’ ” Both of them smiled at the childhood’s phrase. “ ‘Yes, sir, thank you﻿—Oh, no, sir.’ Then some lazy white banker, or some fat white woman that never did a day’s work in her life, puts a hand in a pocket and offers you a dime. God, how I hate it! I did it once at Asbury Park, Phil did, too. We both said, ‘Never again!’ ”

===Chinese===
玛吉带着一丝淡淡的辛酸说，在乔安娜跟前，你就得永远是第一等。

「可不是嘛。来，我帮你洗碗。嗐——你要真不肯让我插手。」她洗碗擦碗手脚飞快，一转眼屋子像被施了魔法似的收拾干净了。天转凉了，玛吉点起她的小煤气炉。

彼得抽着烟，又坠入郁郁的沉默，只是偶尔打破沉默，讲讲自己的挣扎。他的叔父彼得在他三年级那年去世了，房子由老人家的女儿博伊德太太（Mrs. Boyd）继承了。他自然不能指望她什么。她父亲不过是他的叔祖父，她又有自己的儿女要照看。他搬去了西费城的拉腊比太太（Mrs. Larrabee）家，跟几个兄弟会的弟兄住在一起。不知怎么的，钱总是飞快地溜走。他的书昂贵，器械的价钱更是不折不扣的拦路抢劫。

「我能弹琴的地方都去弹，可是说实话，我实在撑不住了。」他末了苦着脸说。

「好多男孩子不是都去伺候宴席吗？」玛吉问，「你怎么不去呢？」

他告诉她，他实在干不了那个。

「我从来受不了站在那些『白人老爷太太』跟前听差。」两人都为孩子话笑了。「『是，老爷，谢谢——噢，不敢当，老爷。』然后哪个懒洋洋的白人银行家，或是哪个一辈子没做过一天活计的白人胖太太，把手伸进口袋，赏你一枚一角的银币。天哪，我恨透了！我在阿斯伯里帕克干过一回，菲尔也干过。我们俩都说：『再也不干了！』」

===Original===
“Where do you play?”

“At different dance-halls. They don’t pay as well here as in New York, though. What’s that, Maggie?”

A thin stream of music, played on a violin, floated down to them.

“That’s good fiddling. Is it in this house?”

“Yes. It’s Tom Mason, the man I told you about. The very thing for you! He makes barrels of money. Come on, Peter.”

She led him, bewildered, up to the third floor, tapped on a door and was admitted to a room much like the one she had just left. A young woman with red crinkled hair and a yellow freckled face sat sewing on a white apron. The young man who let them in had been putting some resin on his bow. Against the wall stood a battered, timeworn piano.

“Hello, Annie,” said Maggie. “Hello, Tom. This is my friend Mr. Bye. I’ve brought him up to hear you play.”

“But I can’t, Miss Maggie. I’ve no accompanist.” He turned soft brown eyes upon her. “Unless your friend here plays the piano.”

“Well, I do admit to tickling the ivories occasionally,” laughed Peter. “Let’s see your score.”

===Chinese===
「你在哪儿弹呢？」

「各家舞厅都弹。不过这儿的报酬不如纽约。那是什么声音，玛吉？」

一缕细细的琴声，是小提琴拉的，飘下楼来。

「拉得真不赖。就在这幢房子里？」

「是啊。是汤姆·梅森，就是我刚才跟你说的那个人。这不正对路子嘛！他挣的钱可海了去了。走吧，彼得。」

她领着满头雾水的他上了三楼，敲敲门，被让进一间跟她刚才那间差不多的屋子。一个长着红色鬈发、黄褐雀斑脸的年轻姑娘坐在那儿缝一条白围裙。开门让他们进来的年轻人刚才正在给琴弓上松香。靠墙立着一架破旧的老钢琴。

「哈罗，安妮。」玛吉说，「哈罗，汤姆。这是我朋友，拜先生。我带他上来听听你演奏。」

「可我拉不成啊，玛吉小姐。我没有伴奏。」他把柔和的棕色眼睛转向她。「除非你这位朋友会弹钢琴。」

「嗯，我倒承认，偶尔也摆弄摆弄琴键。」彼得笑道，「曲谱拿来瞧瞧。」

===Original===
He sat down to the piano, ran his brown limber fingers over the keys, and began to play the accompaniment to a typical syncopated melody, accenting the time with staccato nods of his well-shaped head.

“Oh, great, that’s great!” cried Tom after a few minutes. “Wait till I get my violin.”

Together they made some wonderful sounds. “Play that passage again, will you?” Tom pointed it out with his bow.

“That’s the best accompanist you’ve ever had, isn’t it, Tom?” Annie asked.

“I should say so. Don’t suppose you’d ever consent to doin’ this sort of thing in public, Mr. Bye?”

“That depends on the price and the hours,” said Peter.

Tom told him about himself. He played, had all the work he could do, for the wealthy folks of the town and suburbs. The pay was first-rate. Only he had never been able to keep a good accompanist.

“They’re so do-less,” he complained. “What’s your regular line?”

Peter explained that he was a student.

Mason liked that. “Then you’d be workin’ because you’d really need the fun’s. Nothin’ like having a purpose. Do you out to Sharon Hill with me tomorrow think you could go night and play that? There’d be a few other odds and ends. Though them white folks don’t let me play nothin’ much but that, once I get started. You might drop in for an hour tomorrow and take a peep at the others. You can do them easy, if you can read that.” He pointed to the piece they’d already played.

“Honey-Babe,” declaimed Peter. “Well, Mr. Mason, if we can come to terms, I’m your man.”

Mason took him aside then, and whispered a few words.

“All right,” Peter told him, shaking hands. “That listens pretty. See you tomorrow, say at four. Good night folks. You coming too, Maggie?”

===Chinese===
他在钢琴前坐下，棕色灵巧的手指在琴键上跑了一遍，开始为一支典型的切分旋律伴奏，随着节拍，那颗轮廓漂亮脑袋一顿一顿地点着。

「噢，妙极了，太妙了！」几分钟后汤姆喊道，「等我拿小提琴来。」

两人合奏出了极美妙的声音。「把那一段再弹一遍，行吗？」汤姆用琴弓指给他看。

「这是你请过的最好的伴奏了吧，汤姆？」安妮问。

「可不是。拜先生，你想你总不肯干这种抛头露面的营生吧？」

「那得看价钱和钟点。」彼得说。

汤姆跟他讲了自己的情形。他拉琴，活计多得接不完，主顾是本城和郊外的阔人。报酬头一等。只是他一直留不住一个好的伴奏。

「他们一个个都死气沉沉不顶事。」他抱怨道，「你是干哪一行的？」

彼得解释说自己是学生。

梅森听了正中下怀。「那你是真缺钱才肯干活的了。干什么都不如心里有个奔头。明晚跟我去沙伦希尔（Sharon Hill）一趟怎么样，你看你能去弹那个吗？还有几样零碎的曲子。不过那帮白人只要我一开头，别的什么都不大让我弹。你明儿可以过来一个钟头，先瞄一眼旁的曲子。你既然能看谱弹那个，这些闭着眼睛都拿得下。」他指了指他们刚弹过的那支曲子。

「《蜜糖宝贝》（Honey-Babe）。」彼得念道。「好吧，梅森先生，只要条件谈得拢，我就是你的人。」

梅森把他拉到一边，咬了几句耳朵。

「行啊。」彼得跟他握握手说，「听着不赖。明儿见，就定四点吧。晚安二位。你也回去吗，玛吉？」

===Original===
Downstairs he stopped at the landing. “Maggie, you jewel! How well you’ve managed! No, I won’t come in. You see what was worrying me most was my operating set. The price of those little steel knives and forceps is going to touch the sky pretty soon. Wow! This confounded war is taking everything across seas. Fellow told me to get my order in before Christmas even if I didn’t pay for them till next year. But where was I going to raise all that money? Now the way looks clearer.”

“I’m so glad, Peter.”

“It’s me that’s glad, Maggie. Best thing in the world for me that I met you today. Such a piece of fortune! Cheer up, child! Perhaps we’ll bring each other luck!”

===Chinese===
下到楼下，他在楼梯口站住了。「玛吉，你真是个活宝！你安排得多妥帖！不啦，我不进去了。你知道吗，最愁坏我的是我的那套手术器械。那些小小的钢刀和镊子，价钱眼看就要涨上天了。哇！这场该死的战争把什么都运过海去打仗了。有人劝我赶在圣诞节前把订单下好，哪怕明年再付钱也行。可我上哪儿去凑那一大笔钱呢？这下子路子可亮堂多了。」

「我真替你高兴，彼得。」

「高兴的是我才对，玛吉。今天遇上你，是天下顶顶好的事。这么好的运气！高兴起来吧，丫头！说不定咱们还能给彼此带来好运呢！」

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
The house on South Fifteenth Street saw Peter often after that. Mason could have given him work every night if he had wanted it. As it was he gave him enough to cause him to come for rehearsals three and four times a week. Usually Peter terminated his practice with a visit to Maggie, who got home regularly at five-thirty when she was in town.

She appreciated Peter’s company, for she had been very lonely in this big city with its impregnable social fortresses. “It’s a wonder you come to see me so often, Peter,” she told him wistfully. “Being a Bye gives you the entrance everywhere among the oldest of these ‘old Philadelphians.’”

“Yes,” said Peter cheerfully, “but home-folks are best. And then you make it so pleasant for me, Maggie. Why, I’ve never eaten in my life anything so wonderful as that dinner Sunday. You certainly have the knack of making a fellow feel comfortable.”

She was proud to have him there, he was so handsome and charming, but much more than that, so clearly a personage. She enjoyed being seen with him. He took her out occasionally to the park, to the theaters on Broad Street, once to a bazaar given by some fine ladies at the Y.M.C.A. on Christian Street. She recognized some of the women as among those whom she had seen at Atlantic City. The startled stare of Alice Talbert, who happened to be there that evening, afforded her endless satisfaction. Maggie realized she spoke to her with a sort of wondering respect.

“Wonder what she thought,” she said to herself. “Well, she can think anything she pleases.” She had not forgotten Miss Talbert’s cool reception when she called at Lawyer Talbert’s office on the corner of Fifteenth and Lombard. Alice was her father’s secretary. She was quite remote on seeing Maggie, until she learned that the latter’s business was with the lawyer.

===Chinese===
从那以后，南十五街的那栋房子里便常常见得到彼得的身影。梅森要是想给他活儿，每晚都给得出；实际上他给的活儿，也够他一周来排练三四回。彼得通常一练完便去看玛吉——她在城里的时候，总是五点半准时到家。

有彼得作伴她很受用，因为在这座社交壁垒固若金汤的大城市里，她一直十分孤单。「彼得，你肯这样常来看我，真是难得。」她怅然地对他说。「姓拜这个姓，在这些『老费城人』（old Philadelphians）里最老牌的圈子里，你处处都进得了门。」

「是啊，」彼得高高兴兴地说，「可自家人最好。再说，你把这儿弄得这么让我舒坦，玛吉。嗬，我这辈子还没吃过像礼拜天那顿饭那么好的东西。你真有一手，能叫人浑身自在。」

有他在座她觉得体面：他生得那么英俊、那么讨人喜欢，而更要紧的是，他分明是个人物。她乐意让人瞧见自己同他走在一起。他偶尔带她出去，去公园，去布罗德街的几家戏院，还去过一回克里斯蒂安街基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）里几位阔太太办的义卖会。她认出其中几位太太，正是在大西洋城见过的。那天晚上艾丽斯·塔尔伯特（Alice Talbert）恰好在场，她那惊愕的凝视，让玛吉受用不尽。玛吉察觉，她同自己说话时，带着一种啧啧称奇的敬意。

「不知她心里怎么想。」她自忖道。「哼，她爱怎么想就怎么想。」她没有忘记，自己上一回到第十五街与隆巴德街（Lombard Street）街口的塔尔伯特律师事务所时受到的那番冷淡接待。艾丽斯是她父亲的秘书。一见玛吉，她疏远得很，直到得知后者要办的事正是找律师本人，这才另当别论。

===Original===
Peter was making money these days, real money he told Maggie.

“I’m better off financially than I’ve ever been in my life. Why, I could make a real living at this sort of thing. Mason’s got a wonderful clientele!” As usual he was lounging in Maggie’s little living-room, smoking, watching her move about in her sober house-dress, arranging her accounts and orders. She had bought a little typewriter and had learned to use it. Peter was surprised to find her so methodical. He realized that she would have been a great help to Philip.

He felt a little guilty about coming to Maggie’s so often. “But it’s so confoundedly uncomfortable in my room. Of course I could do better now, but it’s a lot of trouble to move. It’s way up at the top of the house, clean enough, but with just a few sticks of furniture in it, a green iron-bed﻿—ugh!﻿—some books and the Bye family Bible. Don’t know why I lugged that along with me. I never look in it. Well, so long, Maggie, see you tomorrow or next day.”

“All right, Peter. You’re sure you won’t have me fix a cup of cocoa for you before you go? You poor, neglected boy! Two buttons off that overcoat. Bring it in the next time you come and I’ll put them on for you. I’ll find some that will match up here on South Street.” He said he could attend to it himself, but she told him no, that wasn’t a man’s job.

“You certainly are some girl!” He took her hand in his for a moment. “I’ll bring it with bells. Here, turn me out. I’ve got to get up at six tomorrow morning. Haven’t put my nose inside of Carter’s classes this week. Playing out so late with Mason puts me out of commission, you bet.”

===Chinese===
彼得这些日子在挣钱，他告诉玛吉，挣的是真金白银。

「论手头，我这辈子从没这么宽裕过。嗬，靠这个营生，我真挣得出一份像样的日子过。梅森手里有一大批好主顾！」他照例懒洋洋地窝在玛吉的小客厅里，抽着烟，看她穿着素色的家常衣裙在屋里走动，理账目、理订货单。她买了一台小小的打字机，也学会了使。彼得惊异于她这般有条不紊。他心里明白，她本可以给菲利普帮上大忙的。

常往玛吉这儿跑，他心里有点过意不去。「可我那间屋子实在憋屈得要命。如今我自然可以住得好些，只是搬家太麻烦。屋子在这栋房子的顶上头，倒够干净，就是统共几件家什，一张绿铁床——呸！——几本书，还有拜家的家传《圣经》。不知道我干吗把它一路拖来。我从来就不翻。好啦，回见，玛吉，明儿见或者后儿见。」

「好吧，彼得。走之前真不用我给你冲杯可可吗？你这个可怜的、没人疼的孩子！大衣上掉了两颗扣子。下回来把它带来，我给你钉上。南街这头我准能找到配得上的。」他说自己会弄，她却说不行，那不是男人干的活儿。

「你可真是个好姑娘！」他握着她的手握了片刻。「我一定敲锣打鼓地把它带来。行了，送我出去吧。明早六点我就得起床。这个礼拜卡特教授的课，我连鼻子都没伸进去过。跟梅森在外头演到那么晚，真把我累趴下了，可不是嘛。」

===Original===
“Carter, Carter, that’s the Professor of Surgery, isn’t it?”

“No! no! That’s Davenant. I never miss one of his classes. Eat it up in gobs. The old boy’s fond of me. Says I’m his pet carver. Wanted to take me to see an operation in a private hospital last week﻿—white of course﻿—but Carter interfered. ‘Not the place for Bye, Dr. Davenant,’ he said. I hate him with his confounded hypocritical patronage. I’d like to chuck him in a minute.”

Her sympathy was instant.

“Well, why don’t you, Peter? After all, your music really is in good shape. All this steady practice these long years must count for something. Tom says you’re a wonder. He’d like to go into partnership with you, I’m sure. He says there’s heaps of money in it.”

“Oodles! Absolutely! But nothing doing, Maggie. Too mediocre for Miss Joanna Marshall. But she deserves the best, she’s the best herself,” he added in quick loyalty. “Well, that was a false start I made before, wasn’t it? I’m really going this time. Mr. Peter Bye, exit this way.”

===Chinese===
「卡特，卡特，那位是外科学教授，对吧？」

「不！不！那是达文南特（Davenant）。他的课我一堂不落。大口大口地听，恨不得全吞下去。那老头儿喜欢我。说我是他手底下最得宠的操刀手。上礼拜他想带我去一家私立医院看手术——白人的医院，当然——让卡特拦下了。『那地方不是拜该去的，达文南特医生，』他说。他那一套虚伪透顶的恩主做派，我恨透了。恨不得立马甩了他。」

她的同情来得飞快。

「那你怎么不甩呢，彼得？说到底，你的音乐功底是真的拿得出手。这么多年坚持不断的练习，总该算点分量吧。汤姆说你是个人才。他准愿意同你合伙。他说这一行里有大把的钱好赚。」

「多得很！一点儿不假！可是不行，玛吉。对乔安娜·马歇尔小姐来说，太平庸了。不过她配得上最好的，她自己就是最好的，」他紧接着出于一片忠心补了一句。「好啦，刚才那趟算白起了个头，是不是？这回我真走了。彼得·拜先生，请由这厢退场。」

===Original===
He walked up to Lombard Street, thinking. “That girl can certainly see along with you. Nice to meet someone with a disposition like that. Of course I’d rather be a surgeon. But I’m tired of this everlasting digging. I’ve been nothing but a slave for nearly seven years. And poor as the deuce in the bargain. Good Lord, when I think of all the money I might have made out of you!” He looked at his fine slender hands with their firm square-tipped fingers.

“Ideal surgeon hands,” Doctor Davenant had told his assistant.
